\begin{abstract}
This article is the human-readable exposition of the formally verified Fabius-function
development in the \texttt{ProveIt} repository.  It keeps three related functions separate:
the bounded distribution function $F$, Rvachev's even compactly supported bump $\RvachevUp$, and
the signed global extension $\FabiusGlobal$.  Their construction and uniqueness, differential
and refinement laws, strict shape properties, optimal Lipschitz constants, derivative
bounds, exact dyadic-cell derivative value distributions, effective flatness, and precisely
formalized analytic and non-elementarity results are presented together with direct pointers
to their Lean proofs.

The probabilistic part identifies $F$ and $\RvachevUp$ with exact probability expressions for an
infinite weighted sum of independent uniform coordinates.  Finite atomic convolution laws,
weak convergence, half-weighted step approximants, and centered uniform splines give proved
finite models and quantitative approximation results.  At the reusable level, arbitrary
absolutely summable vector weights now have probability, head--tail, reflection,
continuous-linear naturality, operator-affine recursion, and exact support-as-range laws,
while every nontrivial absolutely summable real-weight law is absolutely continuous with
respect to Lebesgue measure and has null singletons, and arbitrary signed real weights have
exact min/max interval ranges and topological supports.  A named geometric-uniform
specialization supplies the weights \((1-q)q^n\), their series and law, affine self-similarity
and reflection, absolute continuity and null singletons for \(|q|<1\), support equal to the
series range for \(|q|<1\), exact unit-interval support for \(0\le q<1\), and uniqueness
among all probability laws satisfying the same affine independent-copy equation.  The
uniqueness theorem includes \(q=0\) and negative \(q\), and assumes no support, density, or
moment condition.  Its named CDF is
continuous and reflection-symmetric for \(|q|<1\), has the exact exterior tails for
\(0\le q<1\), and, for \(0<q<1\), is \(C^\infty\) with an explicit nonnegative,
compactly supported \(C^\infty\) Radon--Nikodym density; the half-base CDF and density recover
the bounded Fabius function and the affine Rvachev bump exactly.  A support-free quantile
theorem transports uniform measure through
the clamped Fabius inverse and gives Banach-valued quantile substitution.  The analytic part develops the
formal sinc and cosine products, Fourier inversion and Poisson summation, exact moment
recurrences, and the Fourier--Legendre expansion.  For each fixed complex strict
contraction, the infinite q-Pochhammer symbol is
also proved entire in its symbol variable, with its exact factor-zero and
reciprocal-power zero descriptions and analytic order one at every zero.  The arithmetic
part gives reciprocal-power
values, Thue--Morse formulas for arbitrary dyadic arguments, executable binary reduction,
two-adic information, exact half-base values, and denominator-free arbitrary-base
$q$-binomial and geometric-interpolation identities present in Lean.  A compact-rectangle
inverse-pair theorem integrates arbitrary absolutely-continuous outer functions without a
clock-measurability hypothesis.  Finite formal-series filters package arbitrary-node
moment exactness and the complete denominator-free Gaussian residual on geometric nodes;
their analytic companion gives the same head--tail law for unconditionally summable
weighted sampled coefficient series, without a convergence obligation at zero-weight nodes.

A separate activation layer writes \(h\) for the continuous totalization of
\(\tanh(x)/x\), with \(h(0)=1\), and turns \(p(x)=1-h(x)\) into an exact
square-summable calculus.  It proves the sharp
punctured limit \(p(at)/t^2\to a^2/3\) for every real scale, genuine summability and a
coefficient-sharp Tannery limit for arbitrary square-summable real weights, and the exact
normalized geometric coefficient \((1-q)/(3(1+q))\) for \(\lvert q\rvert<1\).  Its dyadic
value is \(1/9\).  The same layer identifies the totalized quotient with the divided slope
of \(\tanh\) at zero and proves the finite jet
\(p(x)=x^2/3-2x^4/15+17x^6/315-62x^8/2835+O(x^{10})\).
These are deterministic analytic statements; no Bernoulli active-count expectation is
inserted into them.

Repeated integration is developed once, before its Fabius specialization, as an oriented
normalized Volterra calculus whose algebra takes values in an arbitrary real normed space.
For continuous inputs in a complete target, literal iteration and the Cauchy kernel are
identified exactly; strict FTC, additive composition of orders, Taylor reconstruction, and
finite polynomial commutators then give closed forms for every integer-order primitive of
the signed extension and every natural-monomial weight.  A separate Gamma-normalized
operator supplies the positive-real Riemann--Liouville kernel over arbitrary real normed
spaces on ordered endpoints and agrees exactly with the normalized integer operator at
every positive natural order.  For continuous inputs in a complete target, two positive
real orders compose additively on every ordered compact interval.  Positive affine
covariance, Gamma-normalized order raising from an interior right derivative, and the first
exact fractional shifts of the signed, bounded, and folded Fabius functions are also proved
on their stated ordered domains.

The endpoint chapters report only the negative-Laplace decompositions, periodic correction,
lower-Lambert formulas, saddle estimates, and correction terms that have named proved
counterparts in the formal source.  Plausible extensions, numerical observations,
conditionally convergent boundary filters, sinc-specific generic-$q$ acceleration,
complex-order and iterated fractional formulas, and other arguments not yet represented by
an exact Lean theorem are kept in the companion volume under
\path{docs/semi-formalized-research-frontiers/}, not in this primary exposition.
\end{abstract}

\newpage


\section{Historical note and scope}
\label{sec:history}

The probability distribution now attached to the name of Fabius is older than Fabius's
two-page note.  It occurs in the 1935 work of Jessen and Wintner on infinite convolutions
of independent random variables \cite{jessen1935}, was
rediscovered several times, and entered a different mathematical culture through the
compactly supported ``finite function'' of V.\,A.~Rvachev.  Fabius used it in 1966 as a
particularly economical probabilistic construction of a nowhere analytic
$C^\infty$ function \cite{fabius1966}.  Independently, and for entirely different reasons,
the Rvachevs introduced the same object in 1971 as the canonical compactly supported
solution of a functional--differential equation \cite{rvachev1971}, and V.\,A.~Rvachev
later developed it into the theory of \emph{atomic functions} used in approximation
theory and numerical analysis \cite{rvachev1973,rvachev1979,rvachev1990}.  Rvachev's
normalization emphasizes the even compactly
supported bump, whereas the Fabius normalization emphasizes its cumulative distribution
function.  Arias de Reyna's 1982 paper gave a systematic treatment of the compactly supported
function, including its Fourier product, probability interpretation, partitions of unity,
derivatives, and dyadic values; an English translation appeared in 2017
\cite{arias1982}.  His later arithmetic paper organized the exact rational sequences and
denominator questions \cite{arias2018}.  Haugland's note \cite{haugland2016} gives a
practical algorithm for evaluating $F$ at dyadic rationals, closely related to the
bit-recursion of \Cref{sec:dyadic}.

The present article is not merely a paraphrase of those papers.  It follows the proof
dependency graph of the Lean development in the \texttt{ProveIt} repository
\cite{proveit}.  That development first constructs the bounded distribution function, then
derives the Rvachev and signed-global forms, and finally builds the arithmetic and asymptotic
layers on top.  This order has two advantages.  It prevents identities valid for the signed
extension from being silently applied to the bounded distribution, and it makes the exact
dyadic algorithms independent of any numerical approximation.

The formalization also settles several questions that are only implicit, conjectural, or
incorrectly normalized in the source material.  Most importantly, the sharp endpoint
asymptotic contains a nonconstant periodic function of an exact lower-Lambert phase.  The
periodic term is not a cosmetic improvement: every nonzero Fourier mode is explicit and
nonzero, and an elementary formula without it fails at the error scale usually claimed.
The development further proves a full inverse-Lambert expansion, establishes an exact
bit-recursive evaluator for every dyadic rational, and supplies an absolutely and uniformly
convergent Fourier--Legendre expansion of $\RvachevUp$.

Arias de Reyna's arithmetic paper \cite{arias2018} enumerates the independent occurrences of
this function in chronological order, and two of them deserve mention beside Jessen--Wintner,
Fabius, and the Rvachevs.  In 1981 G.\,Kh.~Kirov and G.\,A.~Totkov arrived at it in a study
of the distribution of the zeros of the derivatives of the function they wrote $\lambda(x)$
(\emph{Godishnik Vissh. Uchebn. Zaved. Prilozhna Mat.} \textbf{17} (1981), no.~4, 157--165,
in Bulgarian with English and Russian summaries); related dyadic derivative phenomena are
represented in the formal Taylor and exact-evaluation APIs used below.  In
1985 R.~Schnabl gave a further independent construction, ``Über eine $C^\infty$-Funktion,''
in \emph{Zahlentheoretische Analysis}, Lecture Notes in Mathematics~1114, Springer, Berlin
(1985), 134--142.  Neither paper is used in the proofs below, which is why their data are
given here rather than in the bibliography; they are recorded because a reader tracing the
function through the literature will meet both names.

Our convention is fixed throughout:

\begin{itemize}
\item $F$ is the bounded cumulative distribution function, equal to $0$ on
      $(-\infty,0]$ and to $1$ on $[1,\infty)$;
\item $\RvachevUp$ is the even compactly supported bump on $[-1,1]$;
\item $\FabiusGlobal$ is the signed global extension satisfying
      $\FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x)$ on all of $\RealNumbers$.
\end{itemize}

All logarithms are natural logarithms.  We write $L=\log2$ in the asymptotic sections,
$\BinaryDigitSum(n)$ for the sum of the binary digits of $n$, and
$\SincRad z=\sin z/z$ with the removable value $1$ at the origin.



\subsection{A guide to the main results}
\label{sec:aud-roadmap}

The exposition follows the dependency order of the formal development.  The opening chapters
separate the bounded function \(F\), the bump \(\RvachevUp\), and the signed extension
\(\FabiusGlobal\); construct the canonical function; and record the exact uniqueness and shape
theorems.  The probability chapter then gives the weighted-uniform representation, the sharp
square-summable activation calculus and its finite Taylor jet, and three finite approximations:
atomic convolution
measures, half-weighted step functions, and centered uniform splines.  It also isolates the
arbitrary-weight distribution laws and the
support-free and compact inverse-CDF transport theorems before specializing them back to the Fabius
quantile.

The Fourier and global-extension chapters collect the proved product, inversion, Poisson,
derivative, normalized repeated-integration, exact dyadic-cell distribution, bound, and
flatness APIs.  The repeated-integration layer is first proved for arbitrary continuous
Banach-valued inputs and polynomial weights, then specialized to the signed and bounded
Fabius functions.  These chapters lead to the exact
analytic locus of \(F\), the proved nonanalyticity regions for \(\RvachevUp\) and \(\FabiusGlobal\),
the totalized inverse on \([0,1]\), and the formal non-elementarity results.  The moment and
arithmetic chapters then derive the rational recurrences, reciprocal-power identities, path
and composition formulas, parity and two-adic statements, exact dyadic evaluation, binary
reduction, and the specialized half-base \(q\)-binomial formulas.

The later analytic chapters summarize the formal endpoint machinery: the exact
negative-Laplace product and logarithmic decomposition, the periodic Fourier correction,
the lower-Lambert coordinate, the proved saddle asymptotics and correction coefficients, and
the rigorous obstructions to deleting the periodic term.  The Fourier--Legendre, summation,
and computability chapters finish the proved story.  The final module guide names the Lean
declarations supporting each retained theorem.

A statement appears here only when its complete mathematical content has an exact proved Lean
counterpart.  Material removed from earlier drafts because that test is not yet met is
preserved in the research-frontier volume.  Thus the division between this document and
that volume is a proof-status boundary, not a judgment about mathematical plausibility
or importance.

\begin{boxedremark}
\textbf{One formal object, three global functions.}
The bounded \(F\) is the cumulative distribution function used for probabilities and
dyadic values.  The compactly supported \(\RvachevUp\) is obtained by folding \(F\), and its Fourier
transform has the proved sinc and cosine product descriptions.  The signed
\(\FabiusGlobal\) is the global Thue--Morse continuation and satisfies the pure dilation
equation on all of \(\RealNumbers\).  Domains and normalizations are stated explicitly whenever a
theorem passes from one presentation to another.
\end{boxedremark}

\section{Three functions that must not be conflated}
\label{sec:three-functions}

The literature often uses the same letter $F$ for three related, but globally different,
functions.  That economy of notation is harmless in a local calculation and dangerous in a
long proof.  We therefore begin by separating them.

\subsection{The bounded Fabius distribution function}

\begin{definition}[Bounded Fabius function]
A function $F:\RealNumbers\to[0,1]$ is called a \emph{bounded Fabius function} if
\begin{align}
 F(x)&=0 &&(x\le 0), \label{eq:F-left}\\
 F(x)&=1 &&(x\ge 1), \label{eq:F-right}\\
 F(1-x)&=1-F(x) &&(0\le x\le 1), \label{eq:F-symmetry}\\
 F'(x)&=2F(2x) &&(0\le x\le \tfrac12), \label{eq:F-diffeq}
\end{align}
and $F$ is infinitely differentiable on $\RealNumbers$.
\end{definition}

The derivative equation on the right half follows from symmetry:
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2F(2-2x),\qquad \tfrac12\le x\le 1.                 \label{eq:F-diffeq-right}
\end{equation}
In particular $F'(x)>0$ for $0<x<1$, once positivity in the interior is known.  Thus $F$
is a genuine cumulative distribution function, not merely a smooth interpolation between
$0$ and $1$.

Because $F$ is constant on both exterior half-lines and is $C^\infty$, every derivative of
positive order vanishes at both endpoints:
\begin{equation}
 F^{(m)}(0)=F^{(m)}(1)=0\qquad(m\ge1).                    \label{eq:flat-endpoints}
\end{equation}
This flatness is the source of both its extraordinary endpoint decay and its failure to be
analytic there.

\subsection{Rvachev's compactly supported bump}

\begin{definition}[Rvachev up-function]
Given $F$, define
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=
 \begin{cases}
   F(x+1),&x\le0,\\
   F(1-x),&x>0.
 \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:up-fold}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=F(1-|x|).                                          \label{eq:up-absolute}
\end{equation}
Here the right side is interpreted using the globally bounded function $F$.
\end{definition}

It follows immediately that $\RvachevUp$ is even, nonnegative, supported on $[-1,1]$, and
$\RvachevUp(0)=1$.  On the unit interval the two functions are simply translates:
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\RvachevUp(x-1),\qquad 0\le x\le1.                          \label{eq:F-up-translate}
\end{equation}
The graph of $F$ is an infinitely flat sigmoid; the graph of $\RvachevUp$ is its symmetric fold.

The primary exposition omits sampled plots: their decimal coordinates are not themselves
Lean declarations.  Exact point evaluation is developed below through rational dyadic
formulas and executable evaluators.

\subsection{The signed global extension}

Let $\BinaryDigitSum(n)$ denote the sum of the binary digits of $n$, and put
\begin{equation}
 \varepsilon_n=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}.                             \label{eq:tm-sign}
\end{equation}
The sequence $(\varepsilon_n)$ is the $\{\pm1\}$ form of the Thue--Morse sequence
\cite{allouche2003}.

\begin{definition}[Signed extension]
Define
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}\varepsilon_n\,
   \RvachevUp(x-2n-1).                                            \label{eq:global-extension}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

At a fixed $x$ only finitely many summands can be nonzero, so this is a locally finite sum.
On every block $[2b,2b+2]$ only the $b$-th translate can be nonzero
(and at the endpoints it too vanishes):
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\varepsilon_b\RvachevUp(x-2b-1),
 \qquad 2b\le x\le 2b+2.                                  \label{eq:single-block}
\end{equation}
It vanishes for $x\le0$, and it agrees with the bounded $F$ for every $x\le1$
(both functions vanish on $(-\infty,0]$).  It does
\emph{not} remain in $[0,1]$ to the right of that interval.  This is the global object that
satisfies the cleanest differential equation:
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).             \label{eq:global-diffeq}
\end{equation}
Arias de Reyna's arithmetic paper uses this extension.  Statements about its values outside
$[0,1]$ must therefore not be transferred blindly to the bounded cumulative distribution.

\begin{boxedremark}
\textbf{Notation used in this article.}
$F$ always means the bounded distribution function; $\RvachevUp$ always means the compactly
supported even bump; and $\FabiusGlobal$ always means the signed global extension.  We have
$F=\FabiusGlobal$ throughout $(-\infty,1]$ (hence on $[0,1]$), but globally they are different
functions.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Exact shape: positivity, bijectivity, and unimodality}
\label{shape:basic}

The three defining equations \eqref{eq:F-left}--\eqref{eq:F-diffeq} already determine the
qualitative picture, but the statements that are actually used later are the sharp ones: not
$F\ge0$ but $F(x)>0$ exactly for $x>0$; not $\RvachevUp\le1$ but $\RvachevUp(x)=1$ exactly at the origin.
We record them here, in the form of equivalences, because the strict versions are what make
the convexity and Lipschitz theorems below sharp.

Everything rests on a single formula that merges \eqref{eq:F-diffeq} and
\eqref{eq:F-diffeq-right} into one identity valid on the whole line.

\begin{lemma}[Unified derivative formula]
\label{thm:shape-unified-derivative}
For every real $x$,
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1).                                        \label{eq:shape-unified-derivative}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:up-absolute}, $\RvachevUp(2x-1)=F\bigl(1-|2x-1|\bigr)$.  For $0\le x\le\tfrac12$ one has
$|2x-1|=1-2x$, so $\RvachevUp(2x-1)=F(2x)$ and \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} is
\eqref{eq:F-diffeq}.  For $\tfrac12\le x\le1$ one has $|2x-1|=2x-1$, so $\RvachevUp(2x-1)=F(2-2x)$
and \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} is \eqref{eq:F-diffeq-right}.  For $x<0$ or $x>1$ one
has $|2x-1|>1$, hence $1-|2x-1|<0$ and $\RvachevUp(2x-1)=0$ by \eqref{eq:F-left}, while $F'(x)=0$
because $F$ is constant on each exterior half-line by \eqref{eq:F-left} and
\eqref{eq:F-right}.
\end{proof}

Since $\RvachevUp\ge0$, formula \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} shows at once that $F$ is
nondecreasing on all of $\RealNumbers$.  It is also convenient to note that the reflection symmetry
\eqref{eq:F-symmetry}, stated on the unit interval, in fact holds globally:
\begin{equation}
 F(1-x)=1-F(x)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).                             \label{eq:shape-symmetry-global}
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $x<0$ both sides equal $1$ by \eqref{eq:F-left} and \eqref{eq:F-right}, and for
$x>1$ both sides equal $0$.
The evenness of \(\RvachevUp\) in \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} also gives the global
first-jet reflection law
\begin{equation}
 F'(1-x)=F'(x)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers),                              \label{eq:shape-derivative-reflection}
\end{equation}
formalized as \lean{deriv_fabiusReal_one_sub} in \path{Differential.lean}.

\begin{theorem}[Positivity, strict monotonicity, bijectivity]
\label{thm:shape-bijection}
The bounded Fabius function satisfies the following.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $F(x)>0$ if and only if $x>0$.
\item $F(x)<1$ if and only if $x<1$.
\item $F'(x)>0$ for $0<x<1$, and $F$ is strictly increasing on $[0,1]$.
\item $F$ maps $[0,1]$ bijectively onto $[0,1]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The heart of the matter is the following doubling step: if $0\le x\le\tfrac12$ and $F(x)=0$,
then $F(2x)=0$.  To see it, observe that $F\ge0$ everywhere, so a zero of $F$ is a global
minimum of a function differentiable on all of $\RealNumbers$; Fermat's rule gives $F'(x)=0$, and
\eqref{eq:F-diffeq} turns this into $2F(2x)=0$.

Suppose now that $F(x)=0$ for some $x>0$.  Since $2^nx\to\infty$, there is a smallest
integer $n\ge0$ with $2^nx\ge\tfrac12$.  For $0\le m<n$ we have $2^mx<\tfrac12$, so the
doubling step applies at $2^mx$ and propagates the vanishing: from $F(x)=0$ we obtain
$F(2^mx)=0$ for every $m\le n$, and in particular $F(2^nx)=0$.  But $F$ is nondecreasing and
$2^nx\ge\tfrac12$, so $F(2^nx)\ge F(\tfrac12)=\tfrac12$, where the midpoint value comes from
\eqref{eq:F-symmetry} at $x=\tfrac12$.  This contradiction proves that $F$ has no zero on the
positive half-line; combined with \eqref{eq:F-left} it proves (i).

Assertion (ii) follows from (i) and \eqref{eq:shape-symmetry-global}: $F(x)<1$ if and only if
$F(1-x)>0$, that is, if and only if $1-x>0$.

For (iii), let $0<x<1$.  Then $2x-1\in(-1,1)$, so $1-|2x-1|\in(0,1]$, and (i) gives
$\RvachevUp(2x-1)=F(1-|2x-1|)>0$.  Now \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} yields $F'(x)>0$.  A
continuous function on $[0,1]$ whose derivative is positive throughout the interior is
strictly increasing on $[0,1]$.

For (iv), $F$ is continuous with $F(0)=0$ and $F(1)=1$, so the intermediate value theorem
makes $F([0,1])$ contain $[0,1]$; since $F$ takes values in $[0,1]$, the image is exactly
$[0,1]$.  Strict monotonicity from (iii) gives injectivity.
\end{proof}

The fold \eqref{eq:up-absolute} now transfers the whole picture to Rvachev's bump, and it
does so in strict form.

\begin{theorem}[Strict unimodality of $\RvachevUp$]
\label{thm:shape-unimodal}
Rvachev's function satisfies the following.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\RvachevUp$ is strictly increasing on $[-1,0]$ and strictly decreasing on $[0,1]$.
\item $\RvachevUp(x)=1$ if and only if $x=0$; the maximum value $1$ is attained at the origin and
      nowhere else.
\item $\RvachevUp(x)=0$ if and only if $x\notin(-1,1)$.  Thus $\RvachevUp$ is nonzero exactly on
      $(-1,1)$, and $\SupportOperator\RvachevUp=[-1,1]$.
\item $\RvachevUp'(x)>0$ for $-1<x<0$ and $\RvachevUp'(x)<0$ for $0<x<1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:up-absolute}, $\RvachevUp(x)=F(1-x)$ for $x\ge0$.  As $x$ increases through $[0,1]$ the
argument $1-x$ decreases through $[0,1]$, on which $F$ is strictly increasing by
\cref{thm:shape-bijection}(iii); hence $\RvachevUp$ is strictly decreasing on $[0,1]$, and evenness
gives (i) on $[-1,0]$.

For (ii), $\RvachevUp(x)=F(1-|x|)$ and \cref{thm:shape-bijection}(ii) say that $\RvachevUp(x)<1$ unless
$1-|x|\ge1$, that is, unless $x=0$; and $\RvachevUp(0)=F(1)=1$.  For (iii), $\RvachevUp(x)>0$ if and only
if $1-|x|>0$ by \cref{thm:shape-bijection}(i), and $\RvachevUp\ge0$, so the zero set is exactly the
complement of $(-1,1)$; the closed support is the closure $[-1,1]$.

For (iv), the identity $\RvachevUp(x)=F(1-x)$ holds on the whole open half-line $x>0$, hence may be
differentiated there: $\RvachevUp'(x)=-F'(1-x)$, which is negative for $0<x<1$ by
\cref{thm:shape-bijection}(iii).  Evenness gives $\RvachevUp'(x)=-\RvachevUp'(-x)$, so $\RvachevUp'>0$ on
$(-1,0)$.
\end{proof}

Parts (i) and (iii) are the classical description of the bump \cite{rvachev1990,arias1982};
the strict forms, and the equivalences in \cref{thm:shape-bijection}, are exactly what the
formal development records.

\subsection{Convexity and the maximal slope}
\label{shape:convexity}

The derivative formula transfers the monotonicity of \(\RvachevUp\) to the derivative of \(F\).

\begin{theorem}[Convexity, concavity, and the maximal slope]
\label{thm:shape-convexity}
The derivative \(F'\) is nondecreasing on \((-\infty,\tfrac12]\) and nonincreasing on
\([\tfrac12,\infty)\).  It is strictly increasing on \([0,\tfrac12]\) and strictly decreasing
on \([\tfrac12,1]\).  Consequently \(F\) is convex on
\((-\infty,\tfrac12]\), concave on \([\tfrac12,\infty)\), strictly convex on
\([0,\tfrac12]\), and strictly concave on \([\tfrac12,1]\).  Moreover
\begin{equation}
 F'(\tfrac12)=2=\max_{x\in\RealNumbers}F'(x),                       \label{eq:shape-max-slope}
\end{equation}
and equality \(F'(x)=2\) holds exactly at \(x=\tfrac12\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The derivative monotonicity statements are
\lean{monotoneOn_deriv_fabiusReal},
\lean{antitoneOn_deriv_fabiusReal},
\lean{strictMonoOn_deriv_fabiusReal_Icc}, and
\lean{strictAntiOn_deriv_fabiusReal_Icc}.  The four convexity statements are
\lean{convexOn_fabiusReal_Iic_half},
\lean{concaveOn_fabiusReal_Ici_half},
\lean{strictConvexOn_fabiusReal_firstHalf}, and
\lean{strictConcaveOn_fabiusReal_secondHalf}.  Finally
\lean{deriv_fabiusReal_half},
\lean{deriv_fabiusReal_eq_two_iff}, and
\lean{isGreatest_deriv_fabiusReal} prove the exact maximal-slope statement.  All are in
\path{Convexity.lean}.
\end{proof}

The same module records the complete pointwise curvature profile.  For every
\(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 F''(x)=8\bigl(\RvachevUp(4x-1)-\RvachevUp(4x-3)\bigr),                \label{eq:shape-second-derivative}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{align}
 F''(x)>0&\iff 0<x<\tfrac12,\notag\\
 F''(x)<0&\iff \tfrac12<x<1,\notag\\
 F''(x)=0&\iff x\notin(0,1)\ \hbox{or}\ x=\tfrac12.    \label{eq:shape-curvature-loci}
\end{align}
The derivative-level statement and its pointwise corollary are
\lean{deriv_fabiusReal_hasDerivAt} and \lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal}; the
three equivalences are \lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_pos_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_neg_iff}, and
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_eq_zero_iff}, with
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_half} naming the midpoint specialization.

\subsection{The optimal Lipschitz constant}
\label{shape:lipschitz}

The bound $F'\le2$ of \eqref{eq:shape-max-slope} is a Lipschitz estimate, and because the
bound is attained the estimate cannot be improved.

\begin{theorem}[Two is the least Lipschitz constant]
\label{thm:shape-lipschitz}
Both $F$ and $\RvachevUp$ are $2$-Lipschitz on $\RealNumbers$:
\begin{equation}
 |F(x)-F(y)|\le2|x-y|,\qquad |\RvachevUp(x)-\RvachevUp(y)|\le2|x-y|\qquad(x,y\in\RealNumbers).
                                                          \label{eq:shape-lipschitz}
\end{equation}
Moreover $2$ is the least admissible constant for each: if $K\ge0$ is such that
$|F(x)-F(y)|\le K|x-y|$ for all $x,y$, or such that $|\RvachevUp(x)-\RvachevUp(y)|\le K|x-y|$ for all
$x,y$, then $K\ge2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:shape-unified-derivative} and $0\le\RvachevUp\le1$ we have $0\le F'\le2$ on all of
$\RealNumbers$, so the mean value theorem gives the first estimate in \eqref{eq:shape-lipschitz}.  The
map $x\mapsto1-|x|$ is $1$-Lipschitz, so the second estimate follows from the first through
the fold \eqref{eq:up-absolute}.

For optimality, let $K$ be admissible for $F$.  Dividing $|F(x)-F(\tfrac12)|\le K|x-\tfrac12|$
by $|x-\tfrac12|$ and letting $x\to\tfrac12$ gives $K\ge|F'(\tfrac12)|=2$ by
\eqref{eq:shape-max-slope}.  For $\RvachevUp$ the same limit at the same point works: the proof of
\cref{thm:shape-unimodal}(iv) gives $\RvachevUp'(\tfrac12)=-F'(\tfrac12)=-2$, so $K\ge2$.
\end{proof}

The two extremal points are the same point in the two pictures: $\tfrac12$ is the centre of
the sigmoid and the midpoint of the decreasing half of the bump, and the fold sends one to
the other.

\begin{corollary}[Linear majorants at the endpoints]
\label{thm:shape-majorants}
For all admissible arguments,
\begin{align}
 F(x)&\le2x                       &&(x\ge0),   \label{eq:shape-majorant-left}\\
 1-F(x)&\le2(1-x)                 &&(x\le1),   \label{eq:shape-majorant-right}\\
 \RvachevUp(x)&\le2\bigl(1-|x|\bigr)     &&(|x|\le1). \label{eq:shape-majorant-up}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Applying \eqref{eq:shape-lipschitz} with $y=0$ and using $F(0)=0$ gives
\eqref{eq:shape-majorant-left}.  Replacing $x$ by $1-x$ there and using
\eqref{eq:shape-symmetry-global} gives \eqref{eq:shape-majorant-right}.  Substituting
$1-|x|\ge0$ into \eqref{eq:shape-majorant-left} and using \eqref{eq:up-absolute} gives
\eqref{eq:shape-majorant-up}.
\end{proof}

The content of \eqref{eq:shape-majorant-left} lies on $[0,\tfrac12]$; for $x\ge\tfrac12$ it is
weaker than $F\le1$.  Its value is that it holds with no hypothesis at all on the size of
$x$, so it can be used as a linear majorant in any estimate near the left endpoint, and
\eqref{eq:shape-majorant-up} says that the bump vanishes at least linearly at both ends of
its support.  These majorants and the optimality of the constant $2$ are contributions of the
formal development; the plain $2$-Lipschitz bound is used implicitly throughout
\cite{arias1982,rvachev1990}.

\section{Existence and uniqueness by a contraction}
\label{sec:existence}

The most economical construction of $F$ is a Banach fixed-point argument.  It has an
additional advantage: the contraction estimate later gives a clean uniqueness theorem,
without presupposing Fourier analysis or probability.

\subsection{The admissible function space}

Let $I=[0,1]$ and let $C(I)$ carry the supremum norm.  Define $\mathcal A\subset C(I)$ by
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A=\{f\in C(I):0\le f\le1,
   \ f(1-x)=1-f(x)\}.                                     \label{eq:admissible-space}
\end{equation}
The set $\mathcal A$ is closed in the Banach space $C(I)$, hence complete.  It is nonempty;
for example $f(x)=x$ belongs to it.

Extend $f\in\mathcal A$ to a continuous function $\widetilde f$ on $\RealNumbers$ by clamping the
argument to $I$:
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde f(t)=
 \begin{cases}
  f(0),&t\le0,\\
  f(t),&0\le t\le1,\\
  f(1),&t\ge1.
 \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:clamped-extension}
\end{equation}
Symmetry gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1 f(t)\Differential t=\frac12.                              \label{eq:admissible-half-integral}
\end{equation}
Indeed, replacing $t$ by $1-t$ and adding the two integrals gives $2\int_0^1f=1$.

\subsection{The integral transform}

For $f\in\mathcal A$, define $Tf$ by
\begin{equation}
 (Tf)(x)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\int_0^{2x}\widetilde f(t)\Differential t,
       &0\le x\le\tfrac12,\\[1.2ex]
  \displaystyle 1-\int_0^{2-2x}\widetilde f(t)\Differential t,
       &\tfrac12<x\le1.
 \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:fixed-transform}
\end{equation}
The two definitions agree at $x=1/2$ by
\eqref{eq:admissible-half-integral}.  Positivity and the upper bound $f\le1$ show that
$0\le Tf\le1$, and a direct substitution shows $(Tf)(1-x)=1-(Tf)(x)$.  Hence
$T:\mathcal A\to\mathcal A$.

The central estimate is stronger than the naive integral bound.

\begin{lemma}[Contraction estimate]
For $f,g\in\mathcal A$,
\begin{equation}
 \|Tf-Tg\|_\infty\le\frac12\|f-g\|_\infty.                \label{eq:contraction}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $h=f-g$.  Since both $f$ and $g$ have integral $1/2$,
$\int_0^1h=0$.  For $0\le y\le1$ we may therefore estimate the primitive in two ways:
\begin{align*}
 \left|\int_0^y h(t)\Differential t\right|
 &\le y\|h\|_\infty,\\
 \left|\int_0^y h(t)\Differential t\right|
 &=\left|\int_y^1h(t)\Differential t\right|
 \le(1-y)\|h\|_\infty.
\end{align*}
Consequently
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\int_0^yh(t)\Differential t\right|
 \le\min(y,1-y)\|h\|_\infty\le\frac12\|h\|_\infty.
\end{equation*}
Every value of $Tf-Tg$ is one of these primitives, possibly with a minus sign.  Taking the
supremum proves \eqref{eq:contraction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Existence of the bounded Fabius function]
The transform $T$ has a unique fixed point $f_\ast\in\mathcal A$.  Extending $f_\ast$ by
$0$ on $(-\infty,0]$ and by $1$ on $[1,\infty)$ produces the bounded Fabius function $F$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The space $\mathcal A$ is complete and $T$ is a strict contraction with constant $1/2$, so
Banach's fixed-point theorem gives a unique fixed point.  On $[0,1/2]$ its fixed-point
equation is
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\int_0^{2x}F(t)\Differential t.                               \label{eq:F-integral-equation}
\end{equation}
Differentiating gives $F'(x)=2F(2x)$.  The second half of
\eqref{eq:fixed-transform} supplies symmetry and the matching right-hand formula.  The
endpoint values are immediate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The differential equation for \texorpdfstring{$\RvachevUp$}{up} and smoothness}

Folding \eqref{eq:F-diffeq} and \eqref{eq:F-diffeq-right} yields the refinement-type
differential equation
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp'(x)=2\bigl(\RvachevUp(2x+1)-\RvachevUp(2x-1)\bigr)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).  \label{eq:up-diffeq}
\end{equation}
At $x=0$ the two one-sided derivatives glue because $\RvachevUp(1)=\RvachevUp(-1)=0$.  Once $\RvachevUp$ is
continuously differentiable, \eqref{eq:up-diffeq} bootstraps regularity: if $\RvachevUp\in C^r$,
then the right side is $C^r$, so $\RvachevUp\in C^{r+1}$.  Induction gives $\RvachevUp\in C^\infty$ and
then $F\in C^\infty$.

\begin{proposition}[Basic shape]
The functions $F$ and $\RvachevUp$ have the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $F$ is strictly increasing on $(0,1)$ and satisfies $0<F(x)<1$ there.
\item $\RvachevUp$ is even, strictly positive on $(-1,1)$, and zero outside $[-1,1]$.
\item $\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x=1$.
\item Every derivative of $\RvachevUp$ vanishes at $x=\pm1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $0<x\le1/2$, \eqref{eq:F-diffeq} gives $F'(x)=2F(2x)$.  Positivity follows first near
$1/2$ from $F(1/2)=1/2$, and then propagates toward zero by repeatedly doubling the
argument; symmetry handles the right half.  This proves strict monotonicity and, by
\eqref{eq:up-absolute}, positivity of $\RvachevUp$ in the interior of its support.  Finally,
\begin{equation*}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x
 =2\int_0^1F(t)\Differential t=1,
\end{equation*}
where the middle identity uses the fold and the last uses symmetry.  Flatness at the support
endpoints follows from smoothness and the fact that $\RvachevUp$ is identically zero just outside
the support.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The original Rvachev characterization}

The formal predicate \lean{IsOriginalFabius} packages the source characterization exactly.
For a function \(\varphi:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) and a scale \(k\), it requires \(C^\infty\)-smoothness,
topological support \([-1,1]\), strict positivity on \((-1,1)\), the normalization
\(\varphi(0)=1\), positivity of \(k\), and
\begin{equation}
 \varphi'(x)=k\bigl(\varphi(2x+1)-\varphi(2x-1)\bigr)
 \qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).                                           \label{eq:formal-original-rvachev}
\end{equation}
The positivity of the scale is redundant: the smart constructor
\lean{IsOriginalFabius.mk_of_derivative_law} derives it from the other hypotheses.

\begin{theorem}[Original characterization]
\label{thm:formal-original-characterization}
If \(\lean{IsOriginalFabius}\ \varphi\ k\), then
\begin{equation}
 k=2,\qquad \varphi=\RvachevUp.                                   \label{eq:formal-characterization-iff}
\end{equation}
Conversely, the canonical \(\RvachevUp\) satisfies this predicate at scale \(2\); in particular
there is exactly one original solution.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The scale is fixed by \lean{originalFabius_scale_eq_two}; the canonical witness is
\lean{canonical_isOriginalFabius}.  Uniqueness and the displayed characterization are
\lean{originalFabius_eq_canonical},
\lean{isOriginalFabius_iff_eq_canonical}, and
\lean{existsUnique_originalFabius} in
\path{OriginalUniqueness.lean}.
\end{proof}

The fold loses only the unused right tail of a bounded candidate.  Precisely,
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp_G=\RvachevUp_H
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G(t)=H(t)\ \hbox{for every }t\le1.                        \label{eq:exact-kernel-fold}
\end{equation}
Thus a bounded candidate is canonical exactly when its fold satisfies the original
characterization at scale \(2\) and its right tail is constantly \(1\).
These are the exact declarations
\lean{rvachevUp_eq_iff_eqOn_Iic_one},
\lean{isFabius_iff_isOriginalFabius_rvachevUp_and_rightTail}, and
\lean{isOriginalFabius_iff_existsUnique_isFabius} in
\path{OriginalUniqueness.lean}.

\section{The probability law behind the functions}
\label{sec:probability}

The fixed-point construction is analytic.  A probabilistic construction explains the same
self-similarity almost without calculation and makes monotonicity and positivity transparent.

\subsection{An infinite weighted sum of uniforms}

Let $U_0,U_1,\ldots$ be independent random variables, each uniformly distributed on
$[0,1]$, and define
\begin{equation}
 X=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{U_n}{2^{n+1}}.                \label{eq:random-X}
\end{equation}
The series converges absolutely for every outcome and satisfies $0\le X\le1$.  Removing the
first coordinate gives an independent copy $X'$ of $X$, and
\begin{equation}
 X=\frac{U_0+X'}2.                                         \label{eq:random-split}
\end{equation}
Reflecting every coordinate, $U_n\mapsto1-U_n$, preserves the product measure and sends
$X$ to $1-X$.

Let $H(x)=\Probability(X\le x)$ be the cumulative distribution function.  Conditioning on $U_0$
in \eqref{eq:random-split} gives
\begin{equation}
 H(y)=\int_0^1 H(2y-u)\Differential u.                               \label{eq:CDF-smoothing}
\end{equation}
For $0\le y\le1/2$, the integrand vanishes when $u>2y$, so
\begin{equation}
 H(y)=\int_0^{2y}H(t)\Differential t.                                \label{eq:CDF-left}
\end{equation}
Reflection gives $H(1-y)=1-H(y)$.  Thus the restriction of $H$ to $[0,1]$ is a fixed point
of the contraction $T$ from \cref{sec:existence}.

\begin{theorem}[Global probabilistic representation]
\label{thm:probability-representation-global}
For every \(x,y\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 F(x)
 &=\Probability\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{U_n}{2^{n+1}}\le x\right),
                                                               \label{eq:F-CDF}\\
 \RvachevUp(y)
 &=\Probability\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{U_n}{2^{n+1}}\le1-|y|\right).
                                                               \label{eq:up-CDF-global}
\end{align}
The second identity includes both tails: if \(|y|\ge1\), both sides vanish.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The product-space construction proves continuity and measurability of the uniformly
convergent coordinate sum, identifies its pushforward distribution, establishes the
head--tail self-similarity and the coordinate-reflection law, and invokes uniqueness of the
bounded Fabius fixed point.  The exact canonical specializations are
\lean{fabiusReal_eq_weightedSum_probability} and
\lean{rvachevUp_eq_weightedSum_probability_global} in
\path{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean}.  The same module makes the transition to the
generic law literal:
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedSumDistribution_eq_weightedUniformDistribution}
identifies the dyadic \lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedSumDistribution} with the
generic weighted-uniform law for \(w_n=1/2/2^n=2^{-n-1}\).  The additional bridges
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedCoordinateSum_eq_geometricUniformSeries_one_half}
and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedSumDistribution_eq_geometricUniformDistribution_one_half}
identify the dyadic series and law with the named geometric specialization at \(q=1/2\).
Its exact topological support is recorded by
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedSumDistribution_support_eq_Icc}.  The same
\path{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean} ownership now includes the almost-everywhere carried
support and restriction identity
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.ae_weightedSumDistribution_mem_Icc} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedSumDistribution_restrict_Icc}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Generic weighted laws and support-free quantile transport}
\label{sec:weighted-laws-quantile}

The probability mechanism does not depend on the dyadic weights.  Let
\(\Omega=[0,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}\), let \(\mathbf P\) be product Lebesgue measure, and let
\(\tau\omega=(\omega_1,\omega_2,\ldots)\).  For a real normed vector space \(E\)
equipped with a measurable structure and weights \(w:\NaturalNumbers\to E\), put
\begin{equation}
 S_w(\omega):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\omega_n w_n,\qquad
 \mu_w:=(S_w)_*\mathbf P,\qquad w_n^+:=w_{n+1}.             \label{eq:weighted-law-def}
\end{equation}
The series \(S_w\) and the pushforward \(\mu_w\) are totalized Lean definitions: they
exist syntactically even without summability or measurability.  The law statements below
add precisely the hypotheses that make those definitions analytically meaningful.

\begin{theorem}[Head--tail product law and arbitrary weighted distributions]
\label{thm:generic-weighted-law}
For every measurable map \(g:\Omega\to A\) into an arbitrary measurable space,
\begin{equation}
 \bigl(\omega\mapsto(\omega_0,g(\tau\omega))\bigr)_*\mathbf P
 =\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes g_*\mathbf P,                       \label{eq:generic-head-tail-map}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_{[0,1]}\) is Lebesgue measure restricted to the unit interval.

Now let \(E\) be a real Banach space with its Borel measurable structure.  If
\(\sum_n\lVert w_n\rVert<\infty\), then \(\mu_w\) is a probability measure.  The
weighted joint law is more general and independent of any parent sequence: for every separately chosen
\(v:\NaturalNumbers\to E\) with \(\sum_n\lVert v_n\rVert<\infty\),
\begin{equation}
 \bigl(\omega\mapsto(\omega_0,S_v(\tau\omega))\bigr)_*\mathbf P
 =\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes\mu_v.                               \label{eq:weighted-head-tail-map}
\end{equation}
Under full summability, with \(W=\sum_n w_n\) and
\(A_w(u,z)=u\,w_0+z\),
\begin{align}
 \mu_w&=(A_w)_*\bigl(\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes\mu_{w^+}\bigr),  \label{eq:weighted-law-split}\\
 (x\mapsto W-x)_*\mu_w&=\mu_w.                              \label{eq:weighted-law-reflection}
\end{align}
Thus every absolutely summable vector-weight law is centrally symmetric about \(W/2\).
If \(E=\RealNumbers\) and at least one coefficient \(w_n\) is nonzero, then \(\mu_w\) is absolutely
continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and every singleton is \(\mu_w\)-null.  No sign
condition is imposed on the weights, and the nonzero coefficient may occur at an arbitrary
index rather than at the head.
More generally, its topological support is the exact range of the continuous series map:
\begin{equation}
 \SupportOperator(\mu_w)=S_w(\Omega).                                   \label{eq:weighted-law-support-range}
\end{equation}
If \(E=\RealNumbers\), with no sign restriction, put
\[
 L_w=\sum_n\min\{w_n,0\},\qquad U_w=\sum_n\max\{w_n,0\}.
\]
Then cancellation and sign changes create no gaps:
\begin{equation}
 S_w(\Omega)=\SupportOperator(\mu_w)=[L_w,U_w].                         \label{eq:weighted-law-signed-support}
\end{equation}
If every \(w_n\ge0\), this becomes the full interval \([0,W]\), including the degenerate
case \(W=0\).  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 S_w(\Omega)=\SupportOperator(\mu_w)=[0,W],\qquad
 \mu_w([0,W])=1,\qquad \mu_w\bigl(\RealNumbers\setminus[0,W]\bigr)=0.  \label{eq:weighted-law-support}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

Equation \eqref{eq:generic-head-tail-map} is
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_head_tail_comp}.
Definition \eqref{eq:weighted-law-def} uses the existing
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries} and the new
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution}; its totalization boundary
is part of the latter definition's statement.  The probability and weighted head--tail
results are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.isProbabilityMeasure_weightedUniformDistribution} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_head_tailWeightedUniformSeries}.
The latter is the arbitrary-\(v\) statement \eqref{eq:weighted-head-tail-map};
the shifted choice \(v=w^+\) is made only in the subsequent split of \(\mu_w\).
Equations \eqref{eq:weighted-law-split}--\eqref{eq:weighted-law-reflection} are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_split} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_reflection}; the two exact
mass statements in \eqref{eq:weighted-law-support} are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_Icc} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_compl_Icc}.
Together these declarations give the probability, splitting, reflection, and carried-set
layer of \path{WeightedUniformSeries.lean}.

The exact-support upgrade is isolated in \path{WeightedUniformSupport.lean}.  Its ten
public declarations are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.isOpenPosMeasure_infinitePi},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_isOpenPosMeasure},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.support_map_eq_closure_range_of_continuous},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_constCoordinates},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_support_eq_range},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.range_weightedUniformSeries_eq_Icc_min_max},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_support_eq_Icc_min_max},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.range_weightedUniformSeries_eq_Icc},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_support_eq_Icc}, and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_support_eq_unitInterval}.
The first three establish open positivity for arbitrary products and identify the support of
a continuous pushforward with the closure of its range.  Compactness of the coordinate cube
then removes the closure in \eqref{eq:weighted-law-support-range}.  In the real case,
coordinates selecting each negative or positive coefficient attain the two endpoints, and
connectedness fills the interval in \eqref{eq:weighted-law-signed-support}; constant
coordinate paths give the especially transparent nonnegative specialization.

The same module proves a more structural layer before any choice of geometric weights.

\begin{theorem}[Naturality and operator-affine recursion]
\label{thm:weighted-law-naturality}
Let \(G\) and \(H\) be arbitrary measurable spaces, let \(g:\Omega\to G\) be measurable,
and let \(\Phi:[0,1]\times G\to H\) be measurable.  If
\[
 f(\omega)=\Phi\bigl(\omega_0,g(\tau\omega)\bigr)
 \quad\text{for \(\mathbf P\)-almost every \(\omega\)},
\]
then no separate measurability hypothesis on \(f\) is needed and
\begin{equation}
 f_*\mathbf P=\Phi_*\bigl(\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes g_*\mathbf P\bigr).
 \label{eq:ae-head-tail-recursion}
\end{equation}
There is also the immediate pointwise-recursion specialization.

Let \(E\) be a real Banach space, let \(F\) be a real normed space, and let
\(L:E\to F\) be continuous linear.  The target \(F\) need not be complete.  For
\(\sum_n\lVert w_n\rVert<\infty\), writing \((Lw)_n=L(w_n)\), one has
\begin{equation}
 L\bigl(S_w(\omega)\bigr)=S_{Lw}(\omega).
 \label{eq:weighted-series-naturality}
\end{equation}
If both spaces carry their Borel measurable structures, then
\begin{equation}
 L_*\mu_w=\mu_{Lw}.
 \label{eq:weighted-law-naturality}
\end{equation}
For scalar multiplication the pointwise identity
\begin{equation}
 S_{cw}(\omega)=cS_w(\omega)                              \label{eq:weighted-series-smul}
\end{equation}
holds in every real normed space, with no completeness or summability assumption; the
law-level identity \((x\mapsto cx)_*\mu_w=\mu_{cw}\) retains completeness, Borel, and
norm-summability hypotheses so that the totalized measure maps are genuine pushforwards.

Finally, let \(A:E\to E\) be continuous linear and suppose
\(w_{n+1}=A(w_n)\) for every \(n\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 S_w(\omega)=\omega_0w_0+A\bigl(S_w(\tau\omega)\bigr).
 \label{eq:weighted-operator-split}
\end{equation}
If \(E\) carries its Borel measurable structure, then also
\begin{align}
 A_*\mu_w&=\mu_{w^+},                                      \label{eq:weighted-shift-map}\\
 \mu_w&=(B_{A,w})_*\bigl(\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes\mu_w\bigr),
 \qquad B_{A,w}(u,z)=u w_0+A(z).                           \label{eq:weighted-operator-law}
\end{align}
The scalar case \(w_{n+1}=c w_n\) is a corollary of the operator statement with
\(A=c\,\mathrm{id}\).  Here \(c\in\RealNumbers\) is arbitrary: positivity and contraction are not
built into either theorem; norm summability of the displayed weights is the analytic
hypothesis.
\end{theorem}

Equation \eqref{eq:ae-head-tail-recursion} and its pointwise specialization are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_of_ae_head_tail_recursion} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_of_head_tail_recursion}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:weighted-series-naturality}--\eqref{eq:weighted-series-smul} are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_map} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_smul}; their law-level counterparts
are \lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_map} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_smul}.  Equation
\eqref{eq:weighted-shift-map} is
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_map_eq_shift}.
The pointwise and law recursions \eqref{eq:weighted-operator-split} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-operator-law} are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_split_of_shift_eq_map} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_split_of_shift_eq_map}; their
scalar corollaries are
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_geometric_split} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_geometric_split}.  These are
exactly the eleven public declarations in the generic naturality tranche.

The compatibility layer also proves the exact barycenter in every complete Borel real
normed space.  If \(\sum_n\lVert w_n\rVert<\infty\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_E x\,\Differential\mu_w(x)=\frac12\mathbin{\cdot}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_n.
 \label{eq:weighted-uniform-barycenter}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.integral_id_weightedUniformDistribution}.  It uses no
sign or order on the weights, no coordinatewise independence expansion, and no termwise
integration hypothesis: bounded-series integrability supplies the integral and central
reflection gives total weight minus the barycenter equal to the barycenter.

The word ``geometric'' in the final two declaration names denotes only the conditional
recursion \(w_{n+1}=cw_n\).  The named specialization is supplied separately, rather than
being hidden in these generic theorems.

The refactored weighted-law surface also exposes seven compatibility declarations:
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformSeries_smul_weights} in
\path{WeightedUniformSeries.lean}, together with
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_head_tail_function},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_isProbabilityMeasure},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_smul_weights},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformProduct_map_head_tail_weightedUniformSeries},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_unitInterval}, and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_compl_unitInterval} in
\path{WeightedUniformDistribution.lean}.  The two scalar compatibility identities use the
same totalized pointwise equality as \eqref{eq:weighted-series-smul}; at law level the
compatibility theorem is oriented as
\(\mu_{cw}=(x\mapsto cx)_*\mu_w\).  Those two unit-interval compatibility declarations are
carried-set statements; the separate exact support equalities are supplied by
\path{WeightedUniformSupport.lean}.

The same compatibility module contains a four-declaration real absolute-continuity tranche.
For an arbitrary s-finite measure \(\nu\) on \(\RealNumbers\) and \(a\ne0\),
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.uniformScaledAdd_absolutelyContinuous} proves that the image
of \(\lambda_{[0,1]}\otimes\nu\) under \((u,x)\mapsto ua+x\) is absolutely continuous with
respect to Lebesgue measure.  The weighted head-coordinate workhorse is
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_absolutelyContinuous_of_head_ne_zero}.
The sharp theorem
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_absolutelyContinuous} assumes
only norm summability and \(\exists n,\ w_n\ne0\), and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.weightedUniformDistribution_nullSingletonClass} packages
the resulting zero mass of every singleton as Mathlib's \texttt{NullSingletonClass}.
Together with the six compatibility declarations listed above and the barycenter theorem,
these are exactly the eleven public theorems, and there are no public definitions, in
\path{WeightedUniformDistribution.lean}.

The same measure language gives a clean inverse-CDF theorem without differentiating an
inverse.

\begin{theorem}[Support-free and compact inverse-CDF transport]
\label{thm:compact-inverse-cdf-transport}
Let \(\mu\) be an arbitrary probability measure on \(\RealNumbers\), with no support
restriction, and let \(Q:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) be almost-everywhere measurable for
\(\lambda_{[0,1]}\).  If, for every \(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 Q(y)\le x\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 y\le\operatorname{cdf}_{\mu}(x)
 \quad\text{for \(\lambda_{[0,1]}\)-almost every \(y\)},     \label{eq:quantile-map-support-free}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 Q_*\lambda_{[0,1]}=\mu.                                    \label{eq:quantile-map}
\end{equation}
Thus neither \(Q([0,1])\subseteq[0,1]\) nor compact support of \(\mu\) belongs to the
foundational theorem; null-set choices of quantile convention are immaterial.

There is also a pointwise compact specialization.  Suppose now that \(\mu\) is carried
almost everywhere by \([0,1]\), and:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(Q([0,1])\subseteq[0,1]\);
\item for every \(x,y\in[0,1]\),
      \[
       Q(y)\le x\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       y\le\operatorname{cdf}_{\mu}(x).
      \]
\end{enumerate}
Together with the same source-a.e. measurability, these hypotheses again imply
\eqref{eq:quantile-map}.  Both versions permit atoms.
\end{theorem}

The support-free theorem is exactly \lean{map_quantile_eq}; its compact specialization
is \lean{map_inverseCDF_volume_restrict_Icc}, both in
\path{QuantileTransport.lean}.  In both cases only the \emph{source measure} is
restricted to \([0,1]\).  The first theorem neither controls nor needs the values of
\(Q\) outside a source-full-measure set.

\begin{corollary}[Fabius quantile substitution]
\label{cor:fabius-quantile-transport}
Let \(I=\lean{fabiusInv}\) be the Fabius inverse obtained by first clamping its argument
to \([0,1]\), and let \(\mu_X\) be the dyadic weighted-sum distribution in
\eqref{eq:F-CDF}.  Then
\begin{equation}
 I_*\lambda_{[0,1]}=\mu_X.                                  \label{eq:fabius-quantile-map}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every strongly measurable map \(\Phi:\RealNumbers\to E\) into a real Banach
space,
\begin{equation}
 \int_{[0,1]}\Phi(I(y))\Differential y
 =\int_{\RealNumbers}\Phi(x)\,\Differential\mu_X(x).                            \label{eq:fabius-quantile-integral}
\end{equation}
No separate integrability hypothesis is imposed.  Lean's Bochner integral is totalized
to zero in the non-integrable case, and the measure-map theorem identifies the two
totalized integrals there as well.
\end{corollary}

The measure identity and Banach-valued integral identity are respectively
\lean{map_fabiusInv_restrict_Icc_eq_weightedSumDistribution} and
\lean{integral_comp_fabiusInv_restrict_Icc_eq_weightedSumDistribution} in
\path{QuantileTransport.lean}.  Together with the support-free and compact statements
above, they are exactly the four public declarations of that module.  The clamped
definition of \(I\) is global, but both
displayed transport identities deliberately use uniform measure restricted to
\([0,1]\).  They do not by themselves prove the two-function integration identity; the
separate absolutely-continuous theorem below supplies that full-interval result.  The
transport declarations assert neither complex Mellin continuation nor fractional-power
substitutions.

A separate subgraph-Fubini argument does provide the exact first-order weighted
inverse identity, without differentiating either clock.

\begin{theorem}[Weighted Fabius inverse layer cake]
\label{thm:weighted-fabius-inverse-layer-cake}
Let \(\phi\) be interval-integrable on \([0,1]\), with real or complex values, and
let \(\psi\) be interval-integrable there with values in a compatible Banach space.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1 \phi(u)\left(\int_0^{I(u)}\psi(x)\Differential x\right)\Differential u
 =\int_0^1\left(\int_{F(x)}^1\phi(u)\Differential u\right)\psi(x)\Differential x.
 \label{eq:weighted-fabius-inverse-layer-cake}
\end{equation}
If \(\Psi(0)=0\), \(\psi\) is continuous on \([0,1]\), and
\(\Psi'(x)=\psi(x)\) pointwise there, the inner primitive on the left may be
replaced by \(\Psi(I(u))\).
If instead \(\Psi:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) is merely absolutely continuous on \([0,1]\),
the same replacement is valid with \(\psi=\Psi'\) almost everywhere, for every
real or complex interval-integrable weight \(\phi\); no separate integrability
hypothesis on the right-hand side is needed.
\end{theorem}

The arbitrary-absolutely-continuous Fabius form is
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_comp_fabiusInv_of_absolutelyContinuousOnInterval}.
The explicit-primitive and pointwise-\(C^1\) forms are respectively
\lean{intervalIntegral_smul_intervalIntegral_fabiusInv} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_smul_comp_fabiusInv} in \path{InverseLayerCake.lean}.

The generic theorem does not require the primitive to vanish at the left endpoint.

\begin{theorem}[Absolutely-continuous inverse layer cake on a rectangle]
\label{thm:ac-weighted-inverse-layer-cake}
Let \(a\le b\) and \(c\le d\).  Suppose \(C,Q:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) map
\([a,b]\) into \([c,d]\) and \([c,d]\) into \([a,b]\), respectively, and
\[
 C(x)<u\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x<Q(u)
 \qquad(x\in[a,b],\ u\in[c,d]).
\]
For \(\mathbb K=\RealNumbers\) or \(\ComplexNumbers\), let
\(\phi:[c,d]\to\mathbb K\) be integrable and let
\(\Psi:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) be absolutely continuous on \([a,b]\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \int_c^d\phi(u)\bigl(\Psi(Q(u))-\Psi(a)\bigr)\Differential u
 =\int_a^b\Psi'(x)\left(\int_{C(x)}^d\phi(u)\Differential u\right)\Differential x.
 \label{eq:ac-weighted-inverse-layer-cake}
\end{equation}
When \(\Psi(a)=0\), the subtraction on the left disappears.  No measurability
hypothesis is imposed on either clock, and equality is allowed in either endpoint
pair.  Values of the clocks outside the two displayed intervals play no role.
\end{theorem}

The order-theoretic engine is
\lean{galoisConnection_Icc_restrict_of_lt_iff_lt}: the strict order equivalence
makes the two interval restrictions a Galois connection and hence monotone.  A
canonical clamped extension of the restricted inverse is therefore measurable.
Consequently, the generic explicit-primitive and pointwise-\(C^1\) theorems
\lean{intervalIntegral_smul_intervalIntegral_of_lt_iff_lt} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_smul_comp_of_lt_iff_lt} no longer assume clock
measurability.  The subtraction and normalized forms of
\eqref{eq:ac-weighted-inverse-layer-cake} are
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_comp_sub_of_lt_iff_lt_of_absolutelyContinuousOnInterval}
and
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_comp_of_lt_iff_lt_of_absolutelyContinuousOnInterval}.
The reusable arbitrary-s-finite-measure
engine is \lean{integral_smul_setIntegral_subgraph} in
\path{SubgraphFubini.lean}.  Finally,
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_fabiusInv_eq} is the real weighted-area specialization;
\lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusInv_eq_intervalIntegral_rvachevUp} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusInv_eq_one_half} identify the inverse area with the
unit-interval \(\RvachevUp\) area and with \(1/2\), respectively.

The inverse-order relation also supports a genuinely two-function
absolutely-continuous calculus.  Here the important reusable point is that
measurability of the clocks is a conclusion of the order structure on the
rectangle, not an extra hypothesis.

\begin{theorem}[Absolutely-continuous calculus for an inverse pair]
\label{thm:ac-inverse-pair}
Let \(a\le b\) and \(c\le d\).  Suppose \(C,Q:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) map
\([a,b]\) into \([c,d]\) and \([c,d]\) into \([a,b]\), respectively, and
\begin{equation}
 C(x)<u\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x<Q(u)
 \qquad(x\in[a,b],\ u\in[c,d]).                            \label{eq:ac-inverse-order}
\end{equation}
If \(A,B:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\), with \(A\) absolutely continuous on \([a,b]\) and
\(B\) absolutely continuous on \([c,d]\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_a^b A'(x)B(C(x))\Differential x
 +\int_c^d A(Q(u))B'(u)\Differential u
 =A(b)B(d)-A(a)B(c).                                      \label{eq:ac-inverse-pair}
\end{equation}
Moreover, every \(y\in[a,b]\) cuts out the smaller inverse rectangle
\([a,y]\times[c,C(y)]\), and
\begin{equation}
 \int_a^y A'(x)B(C(x))\Differential x
 +\int_c^{C(y)} A(Q(u))B'(u)\Differential u
 =A(y)B(C(y))-A(a)B(c).                                   \label{eq:ac-inverse-pair-to}
\end{equation}
No measurability, continuity, or differentiability assumption is imposed on
\(C\) or \(Q\).  The strict-order equivalence forces both restrictions to be
monotone; canonical clamped extensions are then measurable.  Values of the
two clocks outside their displayed compact intervals play no role.  Equality
of either endpoint pair is allowed.  The stated orientation is essential:
reversed endpoints require swapping the corresponding interval and its order
data first.
\end{theorem}

The complete and variable-upper statements are, respectively,
\lean{intervalIntegral_deriv_mul_comp_add_comp_mul_deriv_of_lt_iff_lt} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_deriv_mul_comp_add_comp_mul_deriv_to_of_lt_iff_lt} in
\path{InversePairIntegral.lean}.  Their proof combines
\lean{intervalIntegral_smul_intervalIntegral_of_lt_iff_lt} with the
fundamental theorem for absolutely-continuous functions; no derivative or
Jacobian formula for the clocks enters.

\begin{corollary}[Fabius inverse-pair calculus and area sum]
\label{cor:fabius-ac-inverse-pair}
For real functions \(A,B:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) that are absolutely continuous on
\([0,1]\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1 A'(x)B(F(x))\Differential x
 +\int_0^1 A(I(u))B'(u)\Differential u
 =A(1)B(1)-A(0)B(0).                                      \label{eq:fabius-ac-inverse-pair}
\end{equation}
For every \(y\in[0,1]\), the corresponding variable-upper identity is
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^y A'(x)B(F(x))\Differential x
 +\int_0^{F(y)} A(I(u))B'(u)\Differential u
 =A(y)B(F(y))-A(0)B(0).                                   \label{eq:fabius-ac-inverse-pair-to}
\end{equation}
In particular, taking both outer functions to be the identity gives the
inverse-graph area identity
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(x)\Differential x+\int_0^1I(u)\Differential u=1.                   \label{eq:fabius-inverse-area-sum}
\end{equation}
At a variable inverse endpoint \(u\in[0,1]\), the same specialization gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^u I(v)\Differential v
 =uI(u)-\int_0^{I(u)}F(x)\Differential x.                            \label{eq:fabius-inverse-area-to}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

The complete and variable-upper two-function statements are exactly
\lean{intervalIntegral_deriv_mul_fabiusReal_add_fabiusInv_mul_deriv} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_deriv_mul_fabiusReal_add_fabiusInv_mul_deriv_to}; the
two area statements are \lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusReal_add_fabiusInv_eq_one}
and \lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusInv_to_eq_mul_sub_intervalIntegral_fabiusReal}
in \path{InversePairIntegral.lean}.  These are proper-compact first-order
identities.  Reversed-endpoint, higher-order, improper-integral,
fractional-power, complex-valued arbitrary-AC, and complex-Mellin extensions
remain outside these declarations.

\subsection{The normalized geometric family}

The same product probability space supports a more general family without changing the
measure-theoretic argument.  For \(q\in\RealNumbers\) with \(\lvert q\rvert<1\), put
\begin{equation}
 w_n(q)=(1-q)q^n,
 \qquad
 X_q=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_n(q)U_n,
 \qquad
 \mu_q=\operatorname{law}(X_q).                            \label{eq:geometric-uniform-law}
\end{equation}
For every real \(q\), \(w_0(q)=1-q\) and \(w_{n+1}(q)=q\,w_n(q)\).  Under
\(\lvert q\rvert<1\), the weights are absolutely summable and have total mass one.  The
series is continuous and measurable on the full product space, and its law satisfies the
exact affine refinement
\begin{equation}
 \mu_q
 =\bigl(\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]\otimes\mu_q\bigr)
    \mathbin{\mathrm{map}}
      \bigl((u,x)\mapsto(1-q)u+qx\bigr).                   \label{eq:geometric-law-refinement}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, \(X_q\overset d=(1-q)U_0+qX_q'\), where \(X_q'\) is an independent copy.
The head coordinate and the shifted full series have product law.  Coordinate reflection
gives \(X_q(R\omega)=1-X_q(\omega)\) and
\(\mu_q\mathbin{\mathrm{map}}(x\mapsto1-x)=\mu_q\), where
\((R\omega)_n=1-\omega_n\).  Its barycenter is
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\RealNumbers}x\,\Differential\mu_q(x)=\frac12
 \qquad (|q|<1).                                           \label{eq:geometric-uniform-mean}
\end{equation}
This is the specialization
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.integral_id_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_one_half}
of \eqref{eq:weighted-uniform-barycenter}; it includes \(q=0\) and every negative
contraction and uses no positivity hypothesis.  For every \(|q|<1\), the law \(\mu_q\) is absolutely continuous
with respect to Lebesgue measure and has null singletons; no positivity hypothesis on \(q\)
is needed.  For every \(|q|<1\), the topological support of \(\mu_q\)
is exactly the range \(X_q(\Omega)\).  When \(0\le q<1\), every weight is nonnegative,
\(X_q(\omega)\in[0,1]\), \(\mu_q([0,1])=1\),
\(\mu_q(\RealNumbers\setminus[0,1])=0\), and \(\SupportOperator(\mu_q)=[0,1]\); the endpoint \(q=0\) is
included.  At \(q=1/2\), both
\(X_q\) and \(\mu_q\) are exactly the dyadic random series and law above.

The same affine equation admits a reusable uniqueness theorem far beyond this real
uniform-digit example.  Let \(D\) be a measurable space, let \(E\) be a complete,
second-countable real inner-product space with its Borel measurable structure, let
\(\rho\) be a probability measure on \(D\), and let \(d:D\to E\) be measurable.  For a
probability measure \(\lambda\) on \(E\), define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_{\rho,d,q}(\lambda)
   =(d_*\rho)*\bigl((x\mapsto qx)_*\lambda\bigr)
   =\bigl((u,x)\mapsto d(u)+qx\bigr)_*(\rho\otimes\lambda).
 \label{eq:affine-independent-copy-law}
\end{equation}
The second equality is the product-map form of convolution.  Thus
\(\mathcal A_{\rho,d,q}\) is precisely the law transformer for \(d(U)+qX\) when
\(U\sim\rho\), \(X\sim\lambda\), and the two inputs are independent.

\begin{theorem}[Contractive affine independent-copy law]
\label{thm:affine-independent-copy-uniqueness}
The transformer \(\mathcal A_{\rho,d,q}\) preserves probability measures, and its
characteristic function factors as
\begin{equation}
 \CharacteristicFunction{\mathcal A_{\rho,d,q}(\lambda)}(t)
 =\CharacteristicFunction{d_*\rho}(t)\,\CharacteristicFunction{\lambda}(qt).
 \label{eq:affine-independent-copy-charfun}
\end{equation}
If \(\lambda\) is a fixed point, then for every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \CharacteristicFunction{\lambda}(t)
 =\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\CharacteristicFunction{d_*\rho}(q^kt)\right)
    \CharacteristicFunction{\lambda}(q^nt).                 \label{eq:affine-charfun-finite-iterate}
\end{equation}
If \(|q|<1\), the transformer has at most one probability fixed point.  Equivalently, two
probability measures satisfying the product-map equation in
\eqref{eq:affine-independent-copy-law} are equal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The factorization is the convolution product rule followed by the characteristic-function
rule for real scalar multiplication.  For two fixed points, their common digit factor has
modulus at most one, so one recurrence step gives
\[
 \bigl|\CharacteristicFunction{\lambda}(t)-\CharacteristicFunction{\nu}(t)\bigr|
 \le
 \bigl|\CharacteristicFunction{\lambda}(qt)-\CharacteristicFunction{\nu}(qt)\bigr|.
\]
Iteration moves the argument to \(q^nt\).  Since \(|q|<1\), this tends to zero; continuity
of characteristic functions and their common value \(1\) at the origin force equality at
every \(t\), and characteristic-function extensionality gives \(\lambda=\nu\).  The proof
uses no support, density, absolute-continuity, or moment hypothesis on either fixed point or
on the arbitrary measurable digit law.  It includes \(q=0\) and negative \(q\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Characterization of the geometric uniform law]
\label{cor:geometric-uniform-law-unique}
For every real \(q\) with \(|q|<1\), if a probability measure \(\lambda\) on \(\RealNumbers\)
satisfies
\[
 \lambda
 =\bigl((u,x)\mapsto(1-q)u+qx\bigr)_*
   \bigl(\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]\otimes\lambda\bigr),
\]
then \(\lambda=\mu_q\).  Hence the refinement equation
\eqref{eq:geometric-law-refinement} characterizes the named law even at \(q=0\) and for
negative \(q\), without any support, density, or moment assumption on \(\lambda\).
\end{corollary}

The CDF and its positive-ratio density are now named as well.  Put
\begin{equation}
 F_q(x)=\operatorname{cdf}(\mu_q)(x),
 \qquad
 f_q(x)=\frac{F_q(x/q)-F_q((x-(1-q))/q)}{1-q}.
 \label{eq:geometric-uniform-cdf-density}
\end{equation}
Both definitions are total in the real parameter.  For every real \(q\), \(F_q\) is
measurable and monotone with values in \([0,1]\).  If \(|q|<1\), then
\begin{equation}
 F_q\in C(\RealNumbers),
 \qquad F_q(1-x)=1-F_q(x),
 \qquad F_q(1/2)=1/2.                                      \label{eq:geometric-cdf-reflection}
\end{equation}
If \(0\le q<1\), including \(q=0\), its exterior values are
\begin{equation}
 F_q(x)=0\quad(x\le0),
 \qquad F_q(x)=1\quad(x\ge1).                              \label{eq:geometric-cdf-tails}
\end{equation}

The totalized quotient in \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-cdf-density} has a sharp
parameter defect outside the positive-ratio range.  Lean proves
\[
 f_0(x)=0,\qquad q<0\Longrightarrow f_q(x)\le0.
\]
Consequently, for every \(q\le0\), clamping this selected formula by
\(\operatorname{ofReal}\) gives the zero \texttt{withDensity} measure.  When also
\(|q|<1\), that measure cannot equal the probability law \(\mu_q\).  This is a
formal nonidentity result for the displayed total formula, not a denial of the
already proved absolute continuity of \(\mu_q\); an exact Lean theorem for the
paper-level corrected signed-ratio density at negative \(q\) remains open.

For \(0<q<1\), conditioning on the head coordinate gives, for every real \(x\),
\begin{align}
 F_q(x)
 &=\int_0^1F_q\!\left(\frac{x-(1-q)u}{q}\right)\Differential u,
                                                                  \label{eq:geometric-cdf-integral}\\
 &=\frac{q}{1-q}\int_{(x-(1-q))/q}^{x/q}F_q(t)\Differential t.             \label{eq:geometric-cdf-interval-integral}
\end{align}
The second expression in \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-cdf-density} is then the everywhere
pointwise derivative of \(F_q\).  It is continuous, nonnegative, reflection-symmetric,
vanishes for \(x\le0\) and for \(x\ge1\), has ordinary support contained in \((0,1)\) and
topological support contained in \([0,1]\), and is compactly supported.  More precisely,
\begin{equation}
 \mu_q=(\operatorname{Leb}:\operatorname{Measure}(\RealNumbers))
   \mathbin{\mathrm{withDensity}}
   \bigl(x\mapsto\operatorname{ofReal}(f_q(x))\bigr),
 \qquad F_q,f_q\in C^\infty(\RealNumbers).                            \label{eq:geometric-density-rn}
\end{equation}
Thus the smoothness proof and the explicit Radon--Nikodym identification are formal in real
space for the full positive-ratio range, without passing through Fourier inversion.

At the half base the named objects recover the classical functions globally:
\begin{equation}
 F_{1/2}=\operatorname{weightedSumCDF}=F,
 \qquad f_{1/2}(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)\quad(x\in\RealNumbers).                 \label{eq:geometric-half-bridges}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Fixed half--quarter multisection]
\label{thm:geometric-half-quarter-multisection}
For a coordinate sequence \(\omega\), put
\(\omega^{(0)}_n=\omega_{2n}\) and \(\omega^{(1)}_n=\omega_{2n+1}\).
Then the normalized geometric series obeys the pointwise identity
\begin{equation}
 X_{1/2}(\omega)=\frac23X_{1/4}(\omega^{(0)})
                 +\frac13X_{1/4}(\omega^{(1)}).             \label{eq:geometric-half-quarter-pointwise}
\end{equation}
Under product Lebesgue measure, the two reindexed processes are independent copies.
Consequently, if \(\mu_q\) is the law of \(X_q\) and
\(\sigma_c(x)=cx\), then
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{1/2}
 =\bigl(\mu_{1/4}\otimes\mu_{1/4}\bigr)
    \mathbin{\mathrm{map}}\bigl((x,y)\mapsto\tfrac23x+\tfrac13y\bigr)
 =(\sigma_{2/3})_*\mu_{1/4}*(\sigma_{1/3})_*\mu_{1/4}.     \label{eq:geometric-half-quarter-law}
\end{equation}
No user hypothesis is needed at these fixed ratios.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal Lean counterpart]
The two public definitions \lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.evenCoordinates} and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.oddCoordinates}, followed by the three theorems
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.geometricUniformSeries_one_half_multisection},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.geometricUniformDistribution_one_half_multisection}, and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.geometricUniformDistribution_one_half_conv_one_quarter},
form the complete public surface of \path{GeometricUniformMultisection.lean}.
The first theorem is pointwise.  The second proves the full parity-process product law,
and the third rewrites its affine pushforward as measure convolution.  This fixed result
does not assert a general \(q\)- or \(m\)-multisection, an MGF or cumulant formula, a
centered-density convolution, or a spectral dissection.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Formal API crosswalk]
The twenty-four public declarations in \path{GeometricUniformLaw.lean} are

At compiler-validated checkpoint \texttt{d312c0603}, the generic affine interface in
\path{AffineIndependentCopy.lean} consists of the definition
\lean{ProbabilityTheory.affineIndependentCopyLaw} and the eight theorems
\lean{ProbabilityTheory.affineIndependentCopyLaw_isProbabilityMeasure},
\lean{ProbabilityTheory.affineIndependentCopyLaw_eq_map_prod},

The named specialization is
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.eq_geometricUniformDistribution_of_selfSimilar} in
\path{GeometricUniformUniqueness.lean}.  The generic theorem is formulated for a complete
second-countable real inner-product target and an arbitrary measurable digit map from an
arbitrary probability space; the named theorem specializes it to uniform digits.

The predecessor \path{GeometricUniformDictionary.lean} already supplies finite measure,
characteristic-function, MGF, and CGF tail factorizations.  The six public theorems in
\path{GeometricSincFactorization.lean} are
\lean{charFun_geometricUniformDigit},
\lean{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_prefix},
\lean{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_prefix_sinc},
\lean{tendsto_prefix_sinc_charFun},
\lean{charFun_weightedSumDistribution_prefix_sinc}, and
\lean{tendsto_prefix_sinc_charFun_weightedSumDistribution}.
The digit formula is unconditional in real \(q\).  For every \(|q|<1\), depth
\(m\), and real frequency \(t\), the phase-collapsed residual identity is
\[
 \phi_q(t)=e^{\ImaginaryUnit(1-q^m)t/2}
 \left(\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}
   \SincRad\!\left(\frac{(1-q)q^kt}{2}\right)\right)
 \phi_q(q^m t),
\]
and the phase-bearing prefix without the residual converges pointwise to \(\phi_q(t)\).
This includes \(q=0\) and negative \(q\); the final two declarations are exact
\(q=1/2\) wrappers for \lean{weightedSumDistribution}.  The Gamma-side product now
supplies a named locally uniform product, pointwise \lean{Multipliable} and
\lean{HasProd} forms, and entireness for the same rescaled sinc factors.  The new module
\path{GeometricSincCharacteristicFunction.lean} has no public definitions and exactly
the four public theorems
\lean{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricSincProduct} and
\lean{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricReciprocalGamma},
followed by \lean{tendstoLocallyUniformly_prefix_sinc_charFun} and
\lean{tendstoUniformlyOn_prefix_sinc_charFun}.
For
\[
 z=z_{q,t}:=\frac{(1-q)t}{2\pi},
\]
the first two give, under the same sharp hypothesis \(|q|<1\),
\[
 \phi_q(t)=e^{\ImaginaryUnit t/2}\Phi_q(z)
 =e^{\ImaginaryUnit t/2}G_q(z)G_q(-z).
\]
For the full prefix
\[
 P_{q,m}(t):=e^{\ImaginaryUnit(1-q^m)t/2}
 \prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{(1-q)q^kt}{2}\right),
\]
the last two prove \(P_{q,m}\to\phi_q\) locally uniformly on the real frequency line
and uniformly on every compact \(K\subseteq\RealNumbers\).  Thus all four results include
\(q=0\) and negative \(q\).  No complex-frequency analogue for the full phase-bearing
prefixes or uniform-convergence claim on all of \(\RealNumbers\) is made.  The separate
\path{RvachevPochhammerFactorization.lean} theorem below does supply the global
general-contracting-\(q\) spectral \(q^2\)-Pochhammer rearrangement of the pure sinc
product; it does not add a complex-frequency theorem for the full phase-bearing prefixes.

The generic CDF mechanisms are
\lean{continuous_cdf_of_nullSingleton},
\lean{cdf_reflection_sub}, and

The exact thirty-one-declaration interface in
