The subsequent zero-definition/ten-theorem
\path{GaussianBinomialFixedColumnRate.lean} surface is
\lean{norm_finiteQPochhammerIn_pow_sub_one_le_exp},
\lean{norm_finiteQPochhammerIn_pow_sub_one_le},
\lean{norm_finiteQPochhammerIn_self_mul_gaussianBinomial_sub_one_le},
\lean{norm_gaussianBinomial_sub_inv_finiteQPochhammerIn_le},
\lean{norm_gaussianBinomial_add_sub_inv_finiteQPochhammerIn_le},
\lean{tendsto_gaussianBinomial_add_atTop},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_fixedColumn_relativeError_isBigO},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_shifted_fixedColumn_relativeError_isBigO},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_fixedColumn_error_isBigO}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_shifted_fixedColumn_error_isBigO}.
In a normed commutative ring with normalized multiplicative norm,
\(\lVert q\rVert\le1\) gives
\[
 \bigl\lVert(q^m;q)_k-1\bigr\rVert
 \le \exp\!\bigl(k\lVert q\rVert^m\bigr)-1
 \le k e^k\lVert q\rVert^m,
\]
and, when \(k\le n\), the denominator-free relative estimate
\[
 \bigl\lVert(q;q)_k{n\brack k}_q-1\bigr\rVert
 \le k e^k\lVert q\rVert^{n-k+1}.
\]
This ring-level statement remains meaningful at roots of unity.  Over an
arbitrary normed field, \(\lVert q\rVert<1\) gives the fixed and shifted
nonasymptotic additive estimates, the shifted limit, and relative and additive
\texttt{IsBigO} bounds with rates \(q^{n-k+1}\) and \(q^{n+1}\).  No
completeness or \(q\ne0\) hypothesis is used, so \(q=0\) is included; the
constant is elementary rather than asserted sharp.  Together with
\lean{tendsto_gaussianBinomial_atTop}, the shifted limit and the two relative
error bounds prove all four clauses of the q-monograph's
\nolinkurl{thm:fixed-column-limit}; the additive pair are companion estimates.

The source-only \path{GaussianBinomialGreaterOneAsymptotics.lean} leaf has no
definitions and exactly two public theorems,
\lean{gaussianBinomial_gt_one_fixedColumn_relativeError_isBigO} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_gt_one_central_isEquivalent}.  For real \(q>1\) they
give, respectively,
\[
 (q^{-1};q^{-1})_k q^{-k(n-k)}{n\brack k}_q-1
   =O\bigl((q^{-1})^{n-k+1}\bigr),
 \qquad
 {2m\brack m}_q\sim q^{m^2}(q^{-1};q^{-1})_\infty^{-1}.
\]
Natural subtraction is total in the first theorem, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_inv} is invoked only eventually once \(k\le n\).
These three declarations make \nolinkurl{cor:qgreaterone} Exact with the
printed normalization.  No shifted-central, non-real, or wider-nome theorem
is asserted.

\path{JacobiTripleProduct.lean} has two definitions and twenty-five theorems.
Its exact public inventory is \lean{thetaExponent},
\lean{pentagonalExponent}, \lean{two_dvd_mul_sub_one},
\lean{mul_sub_one_nonneg}, \lean{two_mul_thetaExponent},
\lean{thetaExponent_natCast}, \lean{thetaExponent_neg_natCast},
\lean{choose_two_add_choose_two_succ_eq},
\lean{sum_mul_sum_eq_sum_antidiagonal}, \lean{thetaCoeff_pair_eq},
\lean{sum_antidiagonal_thetaCoeff}, \lean{finite_triple_product_poly},
\lean{pow_mul_finiteQPochhammerIn_div},
\lean{neg_one_zpow_natCast_sub}, \lean{finite_triple_product},
\lean{norm_gaussianBinomialInt_le},
\lean{tendsto_gaussianBinomialInt_central},
\lean{summable_pow_thetaExponent_mul_zpow},
\lean{hasSum_jacobi_triple_product},
\lean{hasSum_jacobi_triple_product'},
\lean{mul_three_mul_sub_one_nonneg},
\lean{two_dvd_mul_three_mul_sub_one},
\lean{two_mul_pentagonalExponent}, \lean{pentagonalExponent_eq},
\lean{pentagonalExponent_pos}, \lean{hasSum_pentagonal}, and
\lean{hasSum_pentagonal_pairs}.

Finally, \path{RogersSzegoPolynomial.lean} has one definition and nine
theorems: \lean{rogersSzego}, \lean{rogersSzego_zero},
\lean{rogersSzego_one}, \lean{rogersSzego_succ},
\lean{one_sub_pow_succ_mul_gaussianBinomial_succ_succ},
\lean{rogersSzego_dilation}, \lean{rogersSzego_three_term},
\lean{summable_norm_pow_div_finiteQPochhammerIn_self},
\lean{sum_antidiagonal_euler_mul_euler}, and
\lean{hasSum_rogersSzego_generating}.  Thus the six modules contribute four
definitions and sixty-five theorems, sixty-nine public declarations in total.

The exponent in the canonical product has a finite, base-generic double-counting
origin that is useful independently of sinc products.  For \(b,N,J\in\NaturalNumbers\), put
\[
 c_J(b,m):=\#\{j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}:b^j\mid m\}.
\]
If \(q>0\), multiplication by \(q\) identifies the positive multiples of \(q\)
not exceeding \(N\) with \(1,\ldots,\Floor{N/q}\).  Consequently, for
every function \(f\) into a commutative monoid,
\begin{align}
 \prod_{1\le n\le\Floor{N/q}}f(qn)
   &=\prod_{\substack{1\le m\le N\\q\mid m}}f(m),
                                                               \label{eq:base-layer-reindexing}\\
 \prod_{j=0}^{J}\prod_{1\le n\le\Floor{N/b^j}}f(b^jn)
   &=\prod_{m=1}^{N}f(m)^{c_J(b,m)}\qquad(b>0).             \label{eq:base-layer-regrouping}
\end{align}
For \(b>1\) and \(m\ne0\), if the displayed layer range reaches the
multiplicity, that is, if
\(\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)\le J\), then
\begin{equation}
 c_J(b,m)=\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)+1.               \label{eq:base-layer-count}
\end{equation}
Thus, under the explicit hypothesis
\(\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)\le J\) for every \(1\le m\le N\),
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{j=0}^{J}\prod_{1\le n\le\Floor{N/b^j}}f(b^jn)
 =\prod_{m=1}^{N}f(m)^{\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)+1}. \label{eq:base-layer-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
The same three identities hold in every additive commutative monoid, with
products and powers replaced by sums and natural scalar multiples.  In Lean,
the underlying finite-set equality is \lean{filter_dvd_eq_image};
\eqref{eq:base-layer-reindexing} and its additive companion are
\lean{prod_multiples_eq_prod_filter} and
\lean{sum_multiples_eq_sum_filter};
\eqref{eq:base-layer-regrouping} and its companion are
\lean{prod_layers_eq_prod_pow_card} and
\lean{sum_layers_eq_sum_nsmul_card}; and
\eqref{eq:base-layer-count}--\eqref{eq:base-layer-multiplicity} and the final
additive companion are \lean{card_filter_pow_dvd},
\lean{prod_layers_eq_prod_pow_multiplicity}, and
\lean{sum_layers_eq_sum_nsmul_multiplicity}, all in
\path{BaseLayerRegrouping.lean}.  These are finite algebraic identities.  In
particular they assert no infinite-product convergence, and the sufficient
layer-coverage condition must be supplied explicitly rather than inferred by
the API from a bound such as \(N<b^{J+1}\).

\subsection{Total local factorization and exact complex zero jets}
\label{sec:fourier-integer-zero-jets}

The complex sinc identities needed at an integer center are most useful after
their removable denominators have been cleared.  With the convention
\(\SincRad z=\sin z/z\), continuously extended at zero, one has for every
\(z\in\ComplexNumbers\), every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), and every \(a\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus\{0\}\),
\begin{align}
 z\SincRad(\pi z)
   &=\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi},                                      \label{eq:sinc-total-clear}\\
 (n+z)\SincRad\!\bigl(\pi(n+z)\bigr)
   &=(-1)^n z\SincRad(\pi z),                                        \label{eq:sinc-total-shift}\\
 (an+z)\SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi(an+z)}a\right)
   &=(-1)^n z\SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi z}a\right).                 \label{eq:sinc-total-scaled-shift}
\end{align}
Unlike identities obtained by dividing through by \(z\) or \(n+z\), these
equalities retain the points where either factor vanishes.  They are respectively
\lean{mul_complexSinc_pi}, \lean{mul_complexSinc_pi_add_nat}, and
\lean{mul_complexSinc_pi_div_add_nat} in
\path{IntegerZeroLocalFactorization.lean}; the last theorem allows an arbitrary
nonzero \emph{complex} scale, with no order or reality hypothesis.

Here is the reusable all-order differential principle.  Let \(\mathbb K\) be
any nontrivially normed field, let \(E\) be a normed \(\mathbb K\)-space, and
let \(U:\mathbb K\to E\) be \(C^k\) at zero.  For every \(n,k\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 D^k\!\left(z\mapsto z^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\right)(0)
 =\begin{cases}
   \bigl(\binom{k}{n}:\mathbb K\bigr)(n!:\mathbb K)\mathbin{\cdot}
      D^{k-n}U(0),&n\leq k,\\[1mm]
   0,&k<n,
  \end{cases}                                                     \label{eq:factored-all-index-jet}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbin{\cdot}\) denotes scalar action on \(E\).  This is the
vector-valued theorem \lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_smul_zero_eq_ite} in
\path{LeadingJet.lean}.  At the index \(k=n+r\), its conditional disappears:
\begin{equation}
 D^{n+r}\!\left(z\mapsto z^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\right)(0)
 =\bigl(\binom{n+r}{n}:\mathbb K\bigr)(n!:\mathbb K)\mathbin{\cdot}
   D^rU(0).                                                       \label{eq:factored-all-order-jet}
\end{equation}
The vector and scalar forms of \eqref{eq:factored-all-order-jet} are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_smul_zero_add} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_mul_zero_add}.  If a scalar-valued function \(f\)
agrees with \(z^nU(z)\) merely on a neighborhood of zero, the same formula is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_pow_mul}; no
separate regularity hypothesis on \(f\) is needed.  At an arbitrary center
\(z_0\), a vector-valued germ
\(f(z)=(z-z_0)^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\) has the all-index form
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_ite_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_smul} and the \(n+r\) form
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_smul_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_smul};
the scalar specialization is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_mul}.

The coefficient is deliberately the product
\(\binom{n+r}{n}n!\), with both natural numbers cast into \(\mathbb K\), not
the quotient \((n+r)!/r!\).  Thus none of these theorems requires factorial
division, characteristic zero, completeness, or analyticity.  In positive
characteristic the displayed scalar can vanish, so the generic statement
identifies the first \emph{potentially} nonzero jet rather than asserting
nonvanishing.  Setting \(r=0\) recovers the compatibility formula
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{\Differential^n}{\Differential z^n}\bigl(z^nU(z)\bigr)\right|_{z=0}
   =n!\,U(0),                                                       \label{eq:factored-leading-jet}
\end{equation}
exported as \lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_mul_zero}; its zero-germ companion is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_pow_mul}.

We now apply the total sinc identities to the standalone product
\(\Phi=\lean{rvachevFourierProduct}\).  Put
\begin{equation}
 P_d(w):=\prod_{h=0}^{d-1}
   \SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi w}{2^h}\right),
 \qquad P_0(w)=1.                                                  \label{eq:integer-zero-sinc-prefix}
\end{equation}
For arbitrary \(k,q\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(w\in\ComplexNumbers\), the global, denominator-cleared
factorization at the center \(2^kq\) is
\begin{equation}
 (2^kq+w)^{k+1}\Phi(2^kq+w)
 =(-1)^q w^{k+1}P_{k+1}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(\frac q2+\frac{w}{2^{k+1}}\right).                  \label{eq:dyadic-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul_add_factorization} in
\path{IntegerZeroLocalFactorization.lean}.  It includes even \(q\), when the
tail can retain further zero multiplicity, and also the degenerate center
\(q=0\).  If \(q\) is odd, its sign is exactly \(-1\); the corresponding
specialization is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul_odd_add_factorization}.

For a positive integer \(m\), let
\(d=\TwoAdicValuation(m)+1\) and let \(q=m/2^{\TwoAdicValuation(m)}\) be its canonical odd part.  Then
\begin{equation}
 (m+w)^d\Phi(m+w)
 =-w^dP_d(w)\Phi\!\left(\frac q2+\frac{w}{2^d}\right),             \label{eq:natural-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_nat_add_factorization}.  The fully signed
form is just as clean.  For \(m\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\}\), set
\begin{equation}
 d_m:=\TwoAdicValuation(|m|)+1,\qquad
 q_m:=\frac{|m|}{2^{\TwoAdicValuation(|m|)}},\qquad
 \sigma_m:=\Sign(m).
\end{equation}
Then \(q_m\) is odd and, for every complex \(w\),
\begin{equation}
 (m+w)^{d_m}\Phi(m+w)
 =-w^{d_m}P_{d_m}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(\frac{q_m}{2}+\sigma_m\frac{w}{2^{d_m}}\right).     \label{eq:integer-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_factorization}; the sign of the
integer center occurs only in the tail coordinate.  Equations
\eqref{eq:dyadic-center-total-factorization}--\eqref{eq:integer-center-total-factorization}
are polynomially cleared identities on all of \(\ComplexNumbers\), not punctured-neighborhood
statements.

The remaining analytic argument can therefore be packaged into a single local
unit.  The product \(\Phi\) is entire by
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_differentiable}, and, for every odd natural \(q\),
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(q/2)\ne0,                                                    \label{eq:odd-half-integer-nonzero}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_nat_div_two_ne_zero_of_odd}.  Define the total
cofactor
\begin{equation}
 U_m(w):=
 -\frac{P_{d_m}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(q_m/2+\sigma_m w/2^{d_m}\right)}{(m+w)^{d_m}}.      \label{eq:integer-zero-local-cofactor}
\end{equation}
The Lean definition \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor} is total for every integer
\(m\).  For \(m\ne0\) and \(m+w\ne0\),
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(m+w)=w^{d_m}U_m(w),                                         \label{eq:integer-zero-local-factorization}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_eq_pow_mul_cofactor}; the same equality
holds as a germ at \(w=0\) by
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_eventuallyEq_pow_mul_cofactor}.  In the
original coordinate this is the centered germ
\[
 \Phi(z)=(z-m)^{d_m}U_m(z-m)
 \quad\hbox{near }z=m,
\]
exported as
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_mul_cofactor}.

For every integer parameter \(m\), with no parity or nonzero hypothesis, the
total quotient \(U_m\) is analytic at every point \(w_0\) satisfying
\(m+w_0\ne0\), by
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt_of_add_ne}.  For nonzero \(m\), its
local-origin specialization is \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt}.
Its exact central value is
\begin{equation}
 U_m(0)=-\frac{\Phi(q_m/2)}{m^{d_m}}\ne0.                          \label{eq:integer-zero-unit-value}
\end{equation}
These three facts are \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt},
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero}, and
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero_ne}, respectively.  More precisely, the
middle theorem is the all-integer totalized identity
\[
 U_m(0)=
 -\frac{\Phi\!\left(\operatorname{divMaxPow}(|m|,2)/2\right)}
 {m^{\operatorname{padicValNat}(2,|m|)+1}};
\]
for \(m\ne0\) it reduces to \eqref{eq:integer-zero-unit-value}, and the last
theorem makes it an analytic unit.

Combining the germ factorization, the nonzero unit, and
\eqref{eq:factored-leading-jet} gives the exact complex multiplicity and its
entire leading jet:
\begin{align}
 \OrderOperator_{z=m}\Phi(z)&=d_m,                             \label{eq:integer-zero-complex-order}\\
 \Phi^{(j)}(m)&=0 &&(0\le j<d_m),                                  \label{eq:integer-zero-lower-jets}\\
 \Phi^{(d_m)}(m)
   &=-\frac{d_m!\,\Phi(q_m/2)}{m^{d_m}}\ne0.                       \label{eq:integer-zero-leading-jet}
\end{align}
In fact the division-free all-order identity is, for every \(r\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \Phi^{(d_m+r)}(m)
 =\binom{d_m+r}{d_m}d_m!\,U_m^{(r)}(0).
 \quad}                                                           \label{eq:integer-zero-all-higher-jets}
\end{equation}
The order statement is
\lean{analyticOrderAt_rvachevFourierProduct_int}; the lower derivatives are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_eq_zero_of_lt}; the displayed
all-order formula is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_order}; the displayed
leading coefficient combines its \(r=0\) case with
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero} and is exported as
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int}; and its nonvanishing is also
exported directly as
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_ne_zero}, all in
\path{IntegerZeroAnalyticOrder.lean}.  This strengthens the real
positive-integer limit in \Cref{thm:fourier-zeros}: it is a signed-integer,
complex-local theorem with the first surviving derivative evaluated exactly.

\subsection{Leading Laurent coefficient of the reciprocal centered MGF}
\label{sec:rvachev-centered-laurent-leading}

For a bounded Fabius solution (F), define the centered Rvachev moment
generating function in the manuscript normalization by
\begin{equation}
 M_F(t):=\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF}\,F\,t
 =\lean{centeredComplexGeneratingFunction}\,F\,(2t).       \label{eq:rvachev-centered-mgf-definition}
\end{equation}
The factor (2) corrects the half-scale coordinate of the repository's
pre-existing centered generating function.  If
(h_F:\lean{IsFabius}\,F), Fourier--Laplace rotation gives
\begin{equation}
 M_F(t)=\Phi\!\left(\frac{\mathrm i t}{2\pi}\right),       \label{eq:rvachev-centered-mgf-rotation}
\end{equation}
which is
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_eq_rvachevFourierProduct}.

The reusable product-coordinate statement is worth recording first.  For
every nonzero (m\in\IntegerNumbers), put
(d_m=\TwoAdicValuation(|m|)+1).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\substack{z\to m\\z\ne m}}
 (z-m)^{d_m}\Phi(z)^{-1}=U_m(0)^{-1}.                    \label{eq:rvachev-fourier-reciprocal-cofactor-limit}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{tendsto_sub_pow_mul_inv_rvachevFourierProduct_int}; it follows directly
from the local factorization in
\eqref{eq:integer-zero-local-factorization} and the fact that (U_m) is a
nonvanishing analytic unit.

Now let (n=2^v u\ne0), where (v\in\NaturalNumbers) and
(u\in\IntegerNumbers) is odd, and set (T_n=2\pi\mathrm i n).  Transporting
\eqref{eq:rvachev-fourier-reciprocal-cofactor-limit} through
\eqref{eq:rvachev-centered-mgf-rotation} gives the manuscript-normalized
leading Laurent law
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \lim_{\substack{t\to T_n\\t\ne T_n}}
 (t-T_n)^{v+1}M_F(t)^{-1}
 =-\frac{T_n^{v+1}}{M_F(\pi\mathrm i u)}.
 \quad}                                                          \label{eq:rvachev-centered-mgf-laurent-leading}
\end{equation}
The odd-core value in the denominator is nonzero.  The exact wrapper is
\lean{tendsto_rvachevCenteredMGF_laurent_two_pow_mul_odd}; the signed-integer
form before choosing (v,u) is
\lean{tendsto_rvachevCenteredMGF_laurent_int}.  Evenness converts the signed
odd core to Lean's positive \texttt{Nat.divMaxPow} representative through
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_pi_mul_I_int}, and
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_pi_mul_I_int_ne_zero_of_odd} proves nonvanishing.

Thus \path{RvachevLaurentLeading.lean} has the exhaustive public surface of
one definition, \lean{rvachevCenteredMGF}, and the six theorems
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_eq_rvachevFourierProduct},
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_pi_mul_I_int},
\lean{rvachevCenteredMGF_pi_mul_I_int_ne_zero_of_odd},
\lean{tendsto_sub_pow_mul_inv_rvachevFourierProduct_int},
\lean{tendsto_rvachevCenteredMGF_laurent_int}, and
\lean{tendsto_rvachevCenteredMGF_laurent_two_pow_mul_odd}.  This makes
\nolinkurl{is:p2:thm:Laurent-leading} Exact.  All three reciprocal limits use
the punctured neighborhood, essentially: Lean totalizes inversion by
(0^{-1}=0), so the displayed pole-cancelled expression does not have its
limiting value at the pole itself.  No lower Laurent coefficients,
pole-shell summation, or downstream Appell-coefficient asymptotic is claimed.

\subsection{Finite weighted scale arithmetic behind the zero orders}

The valuation in \eqref{eq:fourier-zero-order} belongs to a more general
finite additive calculus; neither primality of the scale base nor an analytic
infinite product is needed for this layer.  Let \((A,+,0)\) be an additive
commutative monoid, let \(w:\NaturalNumbers\to A\), and define the inclusive weighted
prefix
\begin{equation}
 P_w(n):=\sum_{h=0}^{n}w(h).                              \label{eq:weighted-inclusive-prefix}
\end{equation}
It satisfies
\begin{align}
 P_w(0)&=w(0),\qquad
 P_w(n+1)=P_w(n)+w(n+1),                                 \label{eq:weighted-prefix-boundaries}\\
 P_w(m+n)&=\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}w(h)+P_{w^{\langle m\rangle}}(n),
 \qquad w^{\langle m\rangle}(h):=w(m+h).                \label{eq:weighted-prefix-split}
\end{align}
Empty sums have their usual value.  More generally, if \(S\) is finite,
\(a:S\to\NaturalNumbers\), and \(a(x)<H\) for every \(x\in S\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{x\in S}P_w(a(x))
 =\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\#\{x\in S:h\le a(x)\}\odot w(h),     \label{eq:weighted-layer-cake}
\end{equation}
where \(q\odot y\) means the sum of \(q\) copies of \(y\).  This finite
layer-cake identity requires no subtraction, order, or multiplication on
\(A\).

For a natural base \(b\), put
\begin{equation}
 \nu_b(n):=\operatorname{padicValNat}(b,n),\qquad
 W_{b,w}(n):=P_w(\nu_b(n))
            =\sum_{h=0}^{\nu_b(n)}w(h).                  \label{eq:weighted-scale-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
Thus, for \(b>1\), \(n>0\), and \(k\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 W_{b,w}(b^kn)
   &=\sum_{h=0}^{k-1}w(h)+W_{b,w^{\langle k\rangle}}(n), \label{eq:weighted-scale-shift}\\
 W_{b,w}(bn)
   &=w(0)+W_{b,w^{\langle1\rangle}}(n).                 \label{eq:weighted-scale-one-shift}
\end{align}
For the divisibility form, the optimal algebraic hypothesis is only
\(b\ne1\).  If \(n>0\) and \(\nu_b(n)<H\), then
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,w}(n)=\sum_{\substack{0\le h<H\\b^h\mid n}}w(h).  \label{eq:weighted-scale-divisors-bounded}
\end{equation}
Since \(\nu_b(n)<n\), the canonical choice \(H=n\) gives
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,w}(n)=\sum_{\substack{0\le h<n\\b^h\mid n}}w(h).  \label{eq:weighted-scale-divisors}
\end{equation}
Summing over the positive integers through \(N\) and exchanging the two
finite sums yields
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{m=0}^{N-1}W_{b,w}(m+1)
 =\sum_{h=0}^{N-1}\bigl(N\mathbin{\mathrm{div}}b^h\bigr)\odot w(h)
 \qquad(b\ne1),                                         \label{eq:weighted-scale-cumulative}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathrm{div}\) denotes natural-number quotient.  For \(b>1\) this
is the familiar floor \(\Floor{N/b^h}\).

Pascal weights now give the hockey-stick identities
\begin{align}
 P_{h\mapsto\binom{h+r}{r}}(n)&=\binom{n+r+1}{r+1},      \label{eq:weighted-prefix-shifted-pascal}\\
 P_{h\mapsto\binom hr}(n)&=\binom{n+1}{r+1}.             \label{eq:weighted-prefix-pascal}
\end{align}
Consequently, with no restriction on \(b,n,r\),
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,h\mapsto\binom hr}(n)
 =\binom{\nu_b(n)+1}{r+1},                              \label{eq:weighted-scale-pascal}
\end{equation}
and, for \(b\ne1\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{m=0}^{N-1}\binom{\nu_b(m+1)+1}{r+1}
 =\sum_{h=0}^{N-1}\bigl(N\mathbin{\mathrm{div}}b^h\bigr)\binom hr.
                                                               \label{eq:weighted-scale-pascal-cumulative}
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The definition \eqref{eq:weighted-inclusive-prefix}, its zero and successor
laws, and its shifted splitting law are
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum}, \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_zero},
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum_succ}, and \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_add}.
The monoid-valued Fubini identity \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-cake} is
\lean{sum_inclusivePrefixSum_eq_sum_filter_card_nsmul}.

The scale definition \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-multiplicity} is
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity}; constant weights and unit natural weights
are evaluated by \lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_const} and
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_one_nat}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:weighted-scale-shift} and \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-one-shift} are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_base_pow_mul} and
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_base_mul}.  The bounded and canonical
divisibility sums \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-divisors-bounded} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-scale-divisors} are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_eq_sum_filter_pow_dvd_of_lt} and
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_eq_sum_filter_pow_dvd}; the cumulative
identity \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-cumulative} is
\lean{sum_range_weightedScaleMultiplicity}.

Finally, \eqref{eq:weighted-prefix-shifted-pascal} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-prefix-pascal} are
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum_add_choose} and \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_choose}.
Their valuation specialization and cumulative floor sum are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_choose} and
\lean{sum_range_padicValNat_choose}.  All declarations are in
\path{WeightedScaleMultiplicity.lean}.
\end{proof}

The finite arithmetic count has a sharp general-base form.  Write

\[
 s_b(N):=\sum_{d\in\operatorname{digits}_b(N)}d.
\]

For every integer \(b>1\), including a composite base, the logarithmic height
already contains all layers:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{m=0}^{N-1}W_{b,w}(m+1)
 =\sum_{h=0}^{\Floor{\log_bN}}
   \bigl(N\mathbin{\mathrm{div}}b^h\bigr)\odot w(h).
 \label{eq:weighted-scale-log-height}
\end{equation}
At unit natural weight this gives the exact digit recovery identities
\begin{align}
 (b-1)\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(1+\nu_b(n)\bigr)+s_b(N)&=bN,
 \label{eq:base-digit-additive}\\
 \sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigl(1+\nu_b(n)\bigr)
 &=\frac{bN-s_b(N)}{b-1}.
 \label{eq:base-digit-quotient}
\end{align}
They include \(N=0\); the quotient is natural-number division and the
preceding additive identity proves its exactness.

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The arbitrary-height and sharp logarithmic layer identities are
\lean{sum_range_weightedScaleMultiplicity_of_log_lt} and
\lean{sum_range_weightedScaleMultiplicity_log}.  Splitting off the zeroth
floor term is \lean{sum_range_div_pow_log_eq_self_add_tail};
\eqref{eq:base-digit-additive} and \eqref{eq:base-digit-quotient} are
\lean{sub_one_mul_sum_padicValNat_succ_add_digitSum} and
\lean{sum_range_padicValNat_succ_eq_sub_digitSum_div}.  These five theorems
are the exhaustive public surface of \path{BaseDigitMultiplicity.lean}.
\end{proof}

Lean totalizes the boundary cases:
\(\nu_b(0)=\nu_0(n)=\nu_1(n)=0\), and natural division by zero is zero.
Accordingly, the resulting \(W\)-values at those degenerate inputs are
algebraic conventions.  More importantly, this subsection proves finite
arithmetic identities only.  Formula \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-pascal}
supplies the binomial expression proposed for higher-rank Rvachev products;
the analytic bridge for admissible dyadic exponent sequences is now supplied
by the following separate layer.

\subsection{Recovering generalized Rvachev exponents from dyadic orders}

Let \(a:\NaturalNumbers\to\NaturalNumbers\) satisfy
\(\sum_{h\ge0}a(h)/2^h<\infty\), and set
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_a(z):=\prod_{h\ge0}
   \SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^h}\right)^{a(h)}.
 \label{eq:generalized-rvachev-product-identifiability}
\end{equation}
The complete generalized zero-divisor theorem and
\(\TwoAdicValuation(2^n)=n\) give the sparse but lossless sampling formula
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \OrderOperator_{z=2^n}\Phi_a(z)
   =W_{2,a}(2^n)=P_a(n)=\sum_{h=0}^{n}a(h).
 \quad}                                                        \label{eq:generalized-rvachev-dyadic-order}
\end{equation}
These orders are finite.  Hence Lean's \(\operatorname{ENat.toNat}\)
converts them back to natural numbers, and the exponent decoder is
\begin{align}
 a(0)
   &=\operatorname{ENat.toNat}\!\left(
       \OrderOperator_{z=1}\Phi_a(z)\right),                  \label{eq:generalized-rvachev-exponent-zero}\\
 a(n+1)
   &=\operatorname{ENat.toNat}\!\left(
       \OrderOperator_{z=2^{n+1}}\Phi_a(z)\right)
     -\operatorname{ENat.toNat}\!\left(
       \OrderOperator_{z=2^n}\Phi_a(z)\right).               \label{eq:generalized-rvachev-exponent-succ}
\end{align}
In particular, for two admissible sequences \(a,c\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\qquad \Phi_a=\Phi_c\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a=c.\qquad}
                                                               \label{eq:generalized-rvachev-product-injective}
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exact recovery argument]
Equation \eqref{eq:generalized-rvachev-dyadic-order} is
\lean{analyticOrderAt_generalizedRvachevProduct_two_pow}: the complete
integer zero-divisor formula identifies the order at a positive integer with
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity}, and
\lean{padicValNat_base_pow} reduces its value at \(2^n\) to the inclusive
prefix \(P_a(n)\).  At \(n=0\) the sampled point is \(2^0=1\), not the
origin, and \(P_a(0)=a(0)\); every product in
\eqref{eq:generalized-rvachev-product-identifiability} instead has value one
at the origin.  Next,
\(P_a(n+1)=P_a(n)+a(n+1)\).  Applying
\(\operatorname{ENat.toNat}\) to the finite natural-cast order formula and
using natural-number cancellation gives
\eqref{eq:generalized-rvachev-exponent-succ}.

The constructive formulas are
\lean{exponent_zero_eq_toNat_analyticOrderAt_generalizedRvachevProduct} and
\lean{exponent_succ_eq_toNat_analyticOrderAt_generalizedRvachevProduct}.
Equality of all dyadic orders implies equality of the exponent sequences by
\lean{exponentSequence_eq_of_analyticOrderAt_two_pow_eq}; its reusable
general-base cancellation lemma is
\lean{weightSequence_eq_of_weightedScaleMultiplicity_base_pow_eq}, valid for
every \(b>1\) in an additive cancellative commutative monoid.  Finally, full
function equality transports through \lean{analyticOrderAt} at every dyadic
point, so the decoder proves the forward implication in
\eqref{eq:generalized-rvachev-product-injective}; the reverse implication is
substitution.  This is \lean{generalizedRvachevProduct_eq_iff}.
All six theorems, and no definitions, form
\path{GeneralizedRvachevIdentifiability.lean}.
\end{proof}

The datum used here is the analytic divisor---the zero locations together
with their multiplicities.  The unweighted zero set alone is insufficient,
and sampling the product values at the dyadic zeros is also insufficient:
distinct positive exponent sequences can vanish at the same sampled points.
The two summability hypotheses in the comparison theorem are essential
because the underlying infinite product is totalized outside its admissible
range.  No spectral-zeta recovery, cumulant-sample recovery, or
identifiability of an underlying probability law is asserted; those remain
open interfaces.  With the later concurrent source union, the real-MGF bridge
formed the historical 905/11,458 checkpoint, the exhaustive complex
moment-product leaf formed the historical 906/11,461 checkpoint, and the
later exhaustive zero-definition/one-theorem
\path{HalfQBinomialRootSimplicity.lean} leaf raised the merged-main checkpoint
to 919/11,569.  The exhaustive exterior reciprocal-germ leaf raises that
checkpoint to 920/11,572, and the exhaustive sharp moment-polynomial degree
leaf raises it to the historical 921/11,575 checkpoint.  The exhaustive

\path{FinitePrefixAppellRecovery.lean} leaf gives the historical 923/11,610
checkpoint.  The exhaustive one-definition/four-theorem

The complex leaf's exhaustive public surface is the definition

\lean{differentiable_geometricUniformComplexMomentProduct}, and

For every complex \(q\) with \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), they give the actual
infinite product, its locally uniform convergence on the complex plane, and
its complex differentiability on the whole plane, together with the same
finite-q-Pochhammer normalization of its \(n\)-th Taylor coefficient.
This is the complex analytic analogue of the real moment identity, not a
complex probability-moment statement.

evaluation with the actual inner and exterior Taylor coefficients under

meaning of the manuscript normalization.  Thus

The reciprocity leaf has exactly the one definition
\lean{geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm} and the five theorems
\lean{geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm_of_norm_lt_one},
\lean{geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm_of_one_lt_norm},
\lean{analyticAt_geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm},
\lean{geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm_reciprocity}, and
\lean{geometricUniformComplexMomentGerm_moment_convolution}.  Off the unit
circle, the combined germ selects the inner entire product for
\(\lVert q\rVert<1\) and the exterior reciprocal germ for
\(1<\lVert q\rVert\).  If additionally \(q\ne0\), Lean proves, as an
\texttt{EventuallyEq} in the neighbourhood filter at zero,
\[
 M_q(z)M_{q^{-1}}(-z)=1.
\]
This is deliberately a local germ identity: the inner product equals one at
zero and hence is nonzero on some neighbourhood by continuity, but it has
zeros farther away, where Lean's total inverse would make a global pointwise
identity false.  In the inner regime the exterior definition gives
\(M_{q^{-1}}(-z)=M_q(z)^{-1}\) locally; in the outer regime the same argument
with \(q^{-1}\) reverses the two factors.  Differentiating the local identity
and using Leibniz together with the sign from \(z\mapsto-z\) gives, for every
\(n\),
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk(-1)^{n-k}
 M_q^{(k)}(0)M_{q^{-1}}^{(n-k)}(0)=\delta_{n0}.
\]
Thus the analytic-germ identity and its Taylor-moment convolution make the
canonical q-series theorem \nolinkurl{thm:qF-reciprocity} Exact.  The
hypotheses \(q\ne0\) and \(\lVert q\rVert\ne1\) are retained literally; no
unit-circle continuation, explicit common germ disc, global reciprocal
identity, Mahler-germ uniqueness theorem, or pole-divisor theorem is claimed.

The sharp-degree leaf's exhaustive public surface consists of
\lean{coeff_geometricUniformMomentPolynomial_choose_two},
\lean{coeff_geometricUniformMomentPolynomial_choose_two_sub_one}, and
\lean{geometricUniformMomentPolynomial_natDegree_eq}.  With
\(T_n=\binom n2\), they prove
\[
 [q^{T_n}]\mathcal P_n=\frac{B_n(1)}{n!},\qquad
 [q^{T_n-1}]\mathcal P_n=-\frac{B_n(1)}{n!}
   +\frac{B_{n-1}(1)}{2(n-1)!}\quad(n\geq2),
\]
and
\[
 \deg\mathcal P_n=
 \begin{cases}T_n,&n=1\text{ or }n\text{ is even},\\
 T_n-1,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Here Lean's \nolinkurl{bernoulli'} is the positive convention
\(B_n(1)=(-1)^nB_n\), with Mathlib's \(B_1=-1/2\).  Thus the canonical
\nolinkurl{prop:qF-P-degree-sharp} and the complete algebraic/analytic
frontier theorem \nolinkurl{p7:thm:Pn} are Exact.  This does not fill the

 \sum_{k<2^N}\varepsilon_k

 =2^{-\binom{N}{2}}n^{\underline{N}}x^{n-N}.

The first response has the manuscript's sign and

uses the literal printed grid \(x+s_{m+1}-k/2^m\).  The two \texttt{of\_lt}

\path{QLucas.lean} (0+7).  Their one definition and twenty-four theorems add
the cyclotomic carry criterion, complete primitive-root q-Pochhammer blocks,
q-Lucas over integral domains, and the integral q-Catalan polynomial with its
exact degree and Catalan specialization.

The newest interpolation and q-integral module counts are
\path{NewtonInterpolation.lean} (3+19) and
\path{QBetaIntegral.lean} (1+8).  Their four definitions and twenty-seven
theorems construct triangular Newton coefficients and interpolants, prove
evaluation, uniqueness, divided differences, and the geometric-grid formula,
and evaluate the Jackson q-beta integral in product and q-gamma forms for
\(0<q<1\) and positive arguments, with symmetry, positivity, and both
successor recurrences.  The Newton count includes the node-qualified family
and all seven definitionally identical \path{newtonInterpolant} compatibility
declarations.  No endpoint or \(q\to1\) limit is claimed.

The latest three q-series module counts are
\path{GaussianBinomialInteger.lean} (1+10),
\path{GaussianBinomialComplexOrder.lean} (1+5), and
\path{QPfaffSaalschutz.lean} (0+3).  Their two definitions and eighteen
theorems extend Gaussian coefficients to integer and principal-branch complex
upper indices, prove the integer q-Pascal and negative-index reflection laws,
derive the reciprocal finite and generalized complex-order q-binomial series,
and establish the terminating balanced \({}_3\phi_2\) q-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz
summation over a field.  Nonzero nomes and parameters, strict-contraction
bounds, and every finite-product nonvanishing premise remain explicit.

The zero-definition/five-theorem module
\path{QuantumMultinomial.lean} then decomposes natural tuple antidiagonals,
transports Gaussian symmetry to arbitrary semirings, proves q-multinomial
coefficient commutation, and establishes the ordered noncommutative
q-multinomial theorem.  Its hypotheses are exactly that the nome commute with
each variable and that \(x_jx_i=q(x_ix_j)\) for \(i<j\).  The statement is
finite and division-free, with no ambient commutativity or convergence
assumption.

The final bound module \path{GaussianBinomialBounds.lean} has no public
definitions and six theorems:
\lean{gaussianBinomial_inv}, \lean{one_le_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_pow_le_one},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_le_inv_qPochhammerInfIn},
\lean{pow_le_gaussianBinomial_of_one_lt}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_le_pow_div_of_one_lt}.  It evaluates Gaussian
reciprocity over fields, proves the constant-term lower bound and the uniform
infinite-q-Pochhammer upper bound for nonnegative real strict contractions,
and transfers them to reciprocal dimension-dominant two-sided bounds for
every real \(Q>1\).  The index condition \(k\le n\), nonzero nome for
reciprocity, and all order and strict-contraction hypotheses remain explicit.
Its proof
reuses the stronger ordered-field theorem
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_self_pos} from
\path{GeneralQConditionNumber.lean} rather than exporting a duplicate.

The terminating basic-hypergeometric closure is split exhaustively between
\path{TwoPhiOneReversal.lean} (two definitions and twelve theorems) and
\path{QChuVandermonde.lean} (ten theorems).  The former identifies a
terminating \({}_2\phi_1\) with the actual \nolinkurl{twoPhiOne} tsum, defines
the reflected parameter triple, and proves both involutivity and cancellation
after applying the reversal twice.  The latter proves both q-Chu--Vandermonde
sums, including a denominator-cleared second identity and the full-domain tsum
wrappers \lean{twoPhiOne_q_chu_vandermonde_first} and
\lean{twoPhiOne_q_chu_vandermonde_second}.  Consequently the two displayed
q-Chu formulas and the terminating reversal lemma are exact Lean crosswalks.
The separate claim that the second formula follows by reversal on its entire
displayed domain remains only partial: the compiled provenance theorem
\lean{q_chu_vandermonde_second_by_reversal} retains the auxiliary hypotheses
\(C\ne0\) and \((A;q)_n\ne0\), whereas the full-domain proof uses finite
q-Cauchy directly.  No rational-continuation or cleared commutative-ring
extension is asserted here.

On the real axis the origin is the unique global point of unit modulus:
\begin{equation}
 |\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)|=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x=0,
 \qquad x\ne0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)|<1.
                                                               \label{eq:fourier-global-peak}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{norm_rvachevFourier_eq_one_iff} and
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_lt_one_of_ne_zero}.  On each positive real lobe
the sign and peak are also exact.  If \(m\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(m<x<m+1\), then
\begin{equation}
 \FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)=\varepsilon_m|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)|, \label{eq:fourier-lobe-sign}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourier_eq_thueMorse_sign_mul_norm}.  If moreover \(m\ge1\),
there is exactly one point of \((m,m+1)\) maximizing the modulus over that
open lobe and exactly one point of the reflected lobe \((-(m+1),-m)\)
maximizing it there.  These are
\lean{existsUnique_isMaxOn_rvachevFourier_lobe} and
\lean{existsUnique_isMaxOn_rvachevFourier_neg_lobe}.  All five facade
declarations are in \path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.

Globally, the origin is the unique real frequency of unit modulus:
\begin{equation}
 \left|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)\right|\le1,
 \qquad
 \left|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(x)\right|=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x=0.
                                                               \label{eq:fourier-strict-global-peak}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the first inequality is strict whenever \(x\ne0\).  These statements are
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_le_one},
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_lt_one_of_ne_zero}, and
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_eq_one_iff} in \path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.

\subsection{Rapid decay and inversion}

For every \(N\in\NaturalNumbers\) there is a constant \(C_N>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
 |\xi|^N|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)|\le C_N\qquad(\xi\in\RealNumbers), \label{eq:schwartz-decay}
\end{equation}
and the companion formal theorem gives the corresponding polynomially weighted bound for
every iterated real-frequency derivative.  Fourier inversion holds in the exact form
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\int_\RealNumbers\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)\EulerE^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit x\xi}\Differential\xi. \label{eq:fourier-inversion}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{rvachevFourier_real_rapidDecay},
\lean{rvachevFourier_real_iteratedDeriv_rapidDecay}, and
\lean{rvachev_fourier_inversion_analytic} in
\path{PoissonSummation.lean} and \path{FourierAnalytic.lean}.

\subsection{Poisson summation and partitions of unity}

For \(u>0\) and \(t\in\RealNumbers\), the formal Poisson identity is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\RvachevUp(t+uk)
 =\frac1u\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(k/u)
   \EulerE^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit kt/u}.                                     \label{eq:poisson-up}
\end{equation}
It is \lean{rvachev_poisson_summation}.  Combining it with
\eqref{eq:fourier-integer-zeros} gives, for every positive integer \(m\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\RvachevUp\!\left(t+\frac{k}{m}\right)=m,          \label{eq:partition-unity}
\end{equation}
and in particular
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\RvachevUp(t+k)=1.                                \label{eq:integer-partition}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{rvachev_partition_one_over_nat} and
\lean{rvachev_partition_unity} in \path{PoissonSummation.lean}.

\subsection{Banach-free cyclic aliasing}
\label{sec:summable-cyclic-alias}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-range twists and cyclic aliasing]
\label{thm:summable-cyclic-alias}
The convergence mechanism behind a root-of-unity filter does not require a
norm.  Let \(R\) be any scalar type, let \(B\) be any index type, let \(I\)
be finite, and let \(E\) be a complete uniform additive commutative group
with a distributive \(R\)-action such that multiplication by each fixed
scalar is continuous.  If \(a:B\to E\) is summable, \(\ell:B\to I\), and
\(c:I\to R\), then
\begin{equation}
 b\longmapsto c(\ell(b))\,a(b)
 \quad\text{is summable}.                                \label{eq:finite-range-scalar-twist}
\end{equation}
Thus a scalar multiplier taking only finitely many values preserves
unconditional summability.

Separately, let \(E\) be a complete Hausdorff uniform additive commutative
group, write \(\bar n\) for the class of \(n\in\IntegerNumbers\) in
\(\operatorname{ZMod}(q)\), and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_r(a):=
 \sum_{\substack{n\in\IntegerNumbers\\\bar n=r}}a(n).                \label{eq:int-residue-tsum}
\end{equation}
For every \(q\in\NaturalNumbers\), including Lean's convention
\(\operatorname{ZMod}(0)=\IntegerNumbers\), the family
\(r\mapsto\mathcal R_r(a)\) has sum \(\sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}a(n)\).
In particular, for \(q>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r\in\IntegerNumbers/q\IntegerNumbers}\mathcal R_r(a)=\sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}a(n).
                                                               \label{eq:sum-int-residue-tsum}
\end{equation}

Suppose in addition that \(E\) is a complex module and multiplication by each
fixed complex scalar is continuous.  Let \(\chi_q\) be the standard additive
character of \(\IntegerNumbers/q\IntegerNumbers\).  For \(q>0\), put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_k(a):=
 \sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}\chi_q(-\bar n k)\,a(n).                  \label{eq:int-fourier-tsum}
\end{equation}
Every twisted series in \eqref{eq:int-fourier-tsum} is summable.  With the
unnormalized discrete Fourier transform
\[
 (\mathcal D_qf)(k):=
 \sum_{r\in\IntegerNumbers/q\IntegerNumbers}\chi_q(-rk)f(r),
\]
one has the exact forward and inverse identities
\begin{align}
 \mathcal D_q\bigl(\mathcal R_\bullet(a)\bigr)(k)
   &=\mathcal A_k(a),                                    \label{eq:dft-int-residue-tsum}\\
 \mathcal R_r(a)
   &=\frac1q\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers/q\IntegerNumbers}
       \chi_q(kr)\,\mathcal A_k(a).                      \label{eq:int-residue-dft-average}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The finite-range convergence principle \eqref{eq:finite-range-scalar-twist}
is \lean{summable_finite_range_smul}.  The two sums are
\lean{intResidueTsum} and \lean{intFourierTsum}; character twisting is
\lean{intFourierSummand_summable}.  The fiberwise summation statement and
its finite-modulus consequence are \lean{hasSum_intResidueTsum} and
\lean{sum_intResidueTsum}.  The forward transform and normalized inverse
are \lean{dft_intResidueTsum} and
\lean{intResidueTsum_eq_dft_average}.  All eight declarations are in
\path{SummableCyclicAlias.lean}.

The proof partitions the bilateral family into its residue fibers, observes
that each cyclic character is constant on a fiber, and applies the finite
DFT and its inverse.  Summability of \(a\) is the only convergence
hypothesis; norm-summability is not assumed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Finite odd-coset DFT traces}
\label{sec:finite-odd-dft}

There is also a finite root-of-unity calculation behind the half-integer spectral
frontier.  It is useful to isolate it from every analytic alias or limiting argument.

\begin{theorem}[Odd-coset character filter and all-order trace]
\label{thm:odd-coset-dft-trace}
Let $N>0$, let $R$ be a commutative domain, and let
$\zeta\in R$ be a primitive $2N$-th root of unity.  Then, for every
$S\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\zeta^{(2k+1)S}
 =\begin{cases}
   N(-1)^{S/N},&N\mid S,\\
   0,&N\nmid S.
  \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:odd-coset-root-filter}
\end{equation}
For samples $f_0,\ldots,f_{2N-1}\in R$, put
\begin{equation}
 A_k:=\sum_{j=0}^{2N-1}f_j\zeta^{(2k+1)j},
 \qquad S(J):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}J_\ell,                    \label{eq:odd-dft-definition}
\end{equation}
where $J=(J_1,\ldots,J_m)\in\{0,\ldots,2N-1\}^m$, and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal C_{N,m}(f):=
 \sum_J
  \begin{cases}
   (-1)^{S(J)/N}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{J_\ell},&N\mid S(J),\\
   0,&N\nmid S(J).
  \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:odd-dft-convolution}
\end{equation}
Then, for every $m\in\NaturalNumbers$, including $m=0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}A_k^m=N\,\mathcal C_{N,m}(f).             \label{eq:odd-dft-power-trace}
\end{equation}
Over a field, normalizing each coefficient by $N$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{A_k}{N}\right)^m
 =\frac{N}{N^m}\,\mathcal C_{N,m}(f).                     \label{eq:normalized-odd-dft-power-trace}
\end{equation}
The labels also satisfy the period-$N$ identity $A_{k+N}=A_k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
Equation \eqref{eq:odd-coset-root-filter} is
\lean{sum_odd_powers_eq_ramanujan}.  The unnormalized and normalized trace identities
are \lean{oddDFTPowerTrace_eq_ramanujanConvolution} and
\lean{normalizedOddDFTPowerTrace_eq_ramanujanConvolution}; the coefficient,
index-sum, and convolution definitions are \lean{oddDFT},
\lean{oddDFTIndexSum}, and \lean{oddRamanujanConvolution}.  The
label identity is \lean{oddDFT_add_period}.  The tuple expansion before applying the
root filter is separately exposed as
\lean{oddDFTPowerTrace_eq_indexSumExpansion}.  These declarations are in
\path{HalfIntegerOddDFT.lean}.

For arbitrary $N$, \eqref{eq:odd-coset-root-filter} sums the complete odd coset
modulo $2N$.  Passing from a full odd
orbit to one representative from each reflection pair is available as
\lean{normalizedOddDFTPowerTrace_eq_two_mul_half}, but only under the exact reflection
hypothesis supplied by its caller.  No theorem here identifies the $f_j$ with sampled
Fabius data, proves an inverse DCT or a bilateral alias formula, or supplies an analytic
error estimate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Moment expansion of the Fourier transform}

With
\begin{equation}
 c_n=\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2n}\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x.                    \label{eq:c-def}
\end{equation}
the exact entire series is
\begin{equation}
 \FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(-1)^n c_n}{(2n)!}(2\pi z)^{2n}.                  \label{eq:fourier-moment-series}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{rvachevFourier_eq_momentSeries} in
\path{AnalyticMoments.lean}.
For later finite filters the individual mode is named
\begin{equation}
 A_n(z):=\frac{(-1)^n c_n}{(2n)!}(2\pi z)^{2n}
 =\lean{rvachevFourierMomentTerm}\ z\ n.                  \label{eq:rvachev-fourier-moment-term}
\end{equation}
The declarations \lean{rvachevFourierMomentTerm_zero} and
\lean{rvachevFourierMomentTerm_scale} prove
\[
 A_0(z)=1,\qquad A_n(cz)=(c^2)^nA_n(z)
 \quad(c,z\in\ComplexNumbers,\ n\in\NaturalNumbers).
\]
More strongly than the equality of total sums in
\eqref{eq:fourier-moment-series},
\lean{hasSum_rvachevFourierMomentTerm} proves
\(\operatorname{HasSum}(A_\bullet(z),\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(z))\) for every bounded
Fabius solution.  These four declarations are in
\path{AnalyticMoments.lean}; the canonical product specialization used below
is in \path{RvachevQBinomialFilter.lean}.

\section{The global extension, derivatives, flatness, and inverse}
\label{sec:global}

\subsection{The signed extension and all iterated derivatives}

The translate series \eqref{eq:global-extension} is locally finite.  It agrees with the
bounded \(F\) on \([0,1]\), vanishes on \((-\infty,0]\), and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).             \label{eq:global-diffeq-again}
\end{equation}
These facts are \lean{extendedFabius_eq_fabiusReal},
\lean{extendedFabius_eq_zero_of_nonpos}, and
\lean{extendedFabius_hasDerivAt} in \path{GlobalExtension.lean}.

Iterating the dilation law gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
 \FabiusGlobal^{(m)}(x)
 &=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\FabiusGlobal(2^m x),                    \label{eq:global-derivatives}\\
 \RvachevUp^{(m)}(x)
 &=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\FabiusGlobal\!\left(2^m(x+1)\right)
 \qquad(-1\le x\le1).                                      \label{eq:up-global-derivatives}
\end{align}
They are \lean{iteratedDeriv_extendedFabius} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachev}.  The global extension obeys
\begin{equation}
 |\FabiusGlobal(x)|\le1,                                      \label{eq:global-bound}
\end{equation}
by \lean{abs_extendedFabius_le_one}.  Hence, for all \(m\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 |F^{(m)}(x)|\le2^{\binom{m+1}{2}},                        \label{eq:sharp-derivative-bound}
\end{equation}
and this bound is attained at \(x=2^{-m}\):
\begin{equation}
 F^{(m)}(2^{-m})=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}.                       \label{eq:sharp-derivative-attained}
\end{equation}
The exact bound, attainment, and supremum statements are
\lean{abs_iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_le},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_inv_two_pow_all}, and
\lean{isGreatest_abs_iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_all} in
\path{BoundedDerivatives.lean}.

In fact the single attaining point belongs to a complete matched-grid pattern.  For
$k,m\in\NaturalNumbers$ with $0\le m<2^k$,
\begin{equation}
 F^{(k)}\!\left(\frac{m}{2^k}\right)
 =\begin{cases}
   0,&m\text{ even},\\
   2^{\binom{k+1}{2}}\varepsilon_{\Floor{m/2}},&m\text{ odd}.
  \end{cases}                                             \label{eq:fabius-dyadic-jet-thue-morse}
\end{equation}
Thus the even numerators are exactly the zeros, while every odd numerator attains the
sharp absolute bound and the surviving signs form the Thue--Morse word.  The exact formula,
zero classification, and odd-node norm statement are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_dyadicGrid_eq_ite},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_dyadicGrid_eq_zero_iff}, and
\lean{abs_iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_dyadicGrid_of_odd} in
\path{BoundedDerivatives.lean}.

\subsection{Exact midpoint--endpoint transmutation}
\label{sec:midpoint-endpoint-transfer}

Subtracting the affine midpoint jet reveals an exact signed copy of the endpoint profile,
not merely a finite Taylor approximation.

\begin{theorem}[Midpoint--endpoint transfer and the complete midpoint jet]
\label{thm:midpoint-endpoint-transfer}
For every $0\le h\le\tfrac12$,
\begin{align}
 F\!\left(\tfrac12+h\right)
   &=\tfrac12+2h-F(h),&
 F\!\left(\tfrac12-h\right)
   &=\tfrac12-2h+F(h),                                    \label{eq:midpoint-value-transfer}\\
 F'\!\left(\tfrac12+h\right)
   &=2-F'(h),&
 F'\!\left(\tfrac12-h\right)
   &=2-F'(h).                                             \label{eq:midpoint-first-jet-transfer}
\end{align}
Moreover, every higher midpoint derivative vanishes:
\begin{equation}
 F^{(k)}\!\left(\tfrac12\right)=0
 \qquad(k\in\NaturalNumbers,\ k\ge2).                                  \label{eq:midpoint-higher-jets}
\end{equation}
Thus the complete Taylor jet at the midpoint is affine, even though $F$ is not analytic
there.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The value identities are \lean{fabiusReal_midpoint_add_eq} and
\lean{fabiusReal_midpoint_sub_eq}.  The first proof shows that
$h\mapsto F(\tfrac12+h)+F(h)$ has constant derivative $2$, then compares its value at
zero; reflection gives the companion identity.  The two endpoint-inclusive first-jet
identities are \lean{deriv_fabiusReal_midpoint_add_eq} and
\lean{deriv_fabiusReal_midpoint_sub_eq}; the latter reuses the global first-jet reflection
law \lean{deriv_fabiusReal_one_sub}.  Finally
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_half_eq_zero_of_two_le} proves
\eqref{eq:midpoint-higher-jets}: the below-one bounded derivative formula at $1/2$
samples the signed extension at the even integer $2^{k-1}$.  All midpoint-transfer
declarations are in \path{MidpointEndpointTransfer.lean}.

The value $F(1/2)=1/2$, slope $F'(1/2)=2$, and midpoint nonanalyticity are respectively
\lean{fabius_half}, \lean{deriv_fabiusReal_half}, and \lean{fabius_analyticAt_iff}.
\end{proof}

The transmutation transports exact integral data as well.  For every real oriented radius
$a$, global complementary reflection gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_{1/2-a}^{1/2+a}F(x)\,\Differential x=a.                       \label{eq:midpoint-centered-mass}
\end{equation}
This all-real strengthening of the frontier report's $0\le a\le1/2$ identity is
\lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusReal_centered}.  For arbitrary $a$ the display is
an oriented integral, not a nonnegative mass.  On the closed half-cell define
\[
 D_+(h):=F(\tfrac12+h)-\tfrac12-2h,
 \qquad
 D_-(h):=F(\tfrac12-h)-\tfrac12+2h.
\]
Then $D_+=-F$ and $D_-=F$ pointwise.  Consequently, for every weight
$w:\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers$ and $0\le a\le1/2$, oriented interval integration gives
\begin{align}
 \int_0^a w(h)D_+(h)\,\Differential h&=-\int_0^a w(h)F(h)\,\Differential h,
                                                               \label{eq:midpoint-weighted-right}\\
 \int_0^a w(h)D_-(h)\,\Differential h&= \int_0^a w(h)F(h)\,\Differential h.       \label{eq:midpoint-weighted-left}
\end{align}
These are \lean{intervalIntegral_mul_fabiusReal_midpoint_add_defect_eq_neg} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_fabiusReal_midpoint_sub_defect_eq}.  The constant-weight form
of \eqref{eq:midpoint-weighted-right}, named
\lean{intervalIntegral_fabiusReal_midpoint_add_defect_eq_neg}, is exactly the central
defect integral stated in the frontier report.  For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$, taking
$w(h)=(a-h)^n/n!$ gives every Cauchy kernel for an anchored repeated primitive at once;
this specialization is
\lean{intervalIntegral_repeatedPrimitiveKernel_fabiusReal_midpoint_add_defect_eq_neg}.

\begin{boxedremark}
\textbf{Exact current boundary.}
The value and derivative identities hold on the full closed half-cell, and the
centered-integral identity holds for every oriented real radius.  The arbitrary-weight theorem is
pointwise transport under an interval integral; the named Cauchy-kernel family records the
usual repeated-primitive kernels.  The midpoint-transfer module itself introduces no
recursive primitive operator; the next section's normalized Volterra calculus supplies
that general API, and the displayed kernel is compatible with it.  These results do not
by themselves assert local affineness, analytic
continuation, an inverse-defect asymptotic, or a finite spline quarter-cell formula.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Exact inverse midpoint defect}
\label{sec:inverse-midpoint-defect}

Let $I$ denote the totalized inverse of the bounded Fabius function and remove its
midpoint and affine midpoint jet in two stages:
\begin{equation}
 h(\delta):=I\!\left(\tfrac12+\delta\right)-\tfrac12,
 \qquad
 E(\delta):=h(\delta)-\tfrac{\delta}{2}.                   \label{eq:inverse-midpoint-definitions}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset} and
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect}.  The totalization matters only outside the unit
range.  Their endpoint normalizations are
\begin{equation}
 h(0)=E(0)=0,\qquad
 h\!\left(\tfrac12\right)=\tfrac12,\qquad
 E\!\left(\tfrac12\right)=\tfrac14.                        \label{eq:inverse-midpoint-endpoints}
\end{equation}
On the central half-cell the following identities are exact.

\begin{theorem}[Inverse midpoint fixed point]
\label{thm:inverse-midpoint-fixed-point}
For $0\le\delta\le\tfrac12$,
\begin{align}
 0\le h(\delta)&\le\tfrac12,                               \label{eq:inverse-offset-range}\\
 \delta&=2h(\delta)-F\bigl(h(\delta)\bigr),                 \label{eq:inverse-offset-equation}\\
 h(\delta)&=\tfrac{\delta}{2}
             +\tfrac12F\bigl(h(\delta)\bigr),              \label{eq:inverse-offset-fixed-point}\\
 E(\delta)&=\tfrac12F\bigl(h(\delta)\bigr)
            =\tfrac12F\!\left(\tfrac{\delta}{2}+E(\delta)\right),
                                                               \label{eq:inverse-defect-fixed-point}\\
 0\le E(\delta)&\le\tfrac14.                               \label{eq:inverse-defect-range}
\end{align}
In fact \eqref{eq:inverse-offset-range} holds for every $\delta\ge0$.  Globally,
including the clamped tails of the totalized inverse,
\begin{equation}
 h(-\delta)=-h(\delta),\qquad E(-\delta)=-E(\delta)
 \qquad(\delta\in\RealNumbers).                                      \label{eq:inverse-midpoint-odd}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The four identities in \eqref{eq:inverse-midpoint-endpoints} are
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_zero},
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_zero},
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_half}, and
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_half}.  For nonnegative $\delta$, monotonicity
of the totalized inverse at $1/2$, together with its global upper bound, gives
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_mem_Icc}.  When $\delta\le1/2$, applying $F$ to
$I(1/2+\delta)$ and then using the midpoint--endpoint identity
\eqref{eq:midpoint-value-transfer} gives
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_equation}.  Elementary rearrangement yields
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_fixedPoint},
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_eq_half_fabiusReal}, and the self-contained defect
equation \lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_fixedPoint}.  Positivity, monotonicity, and
$F(1/2)=1/2$ give the sharp uniform enclosure
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_mem_Icc}.  Finally the global inverse reflection law
gives \lean{fabiusInvMidpointOffset_neg} and
\lean{fabiusInvMidpointDefect_neg}.  All declarations are in
\path{InverseMidpointDefect.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{boxedremark}
\textbf{Exact current boundary.}
The fixed-point equation is a finite identity.  It is not, by itself, a proof that
$E(\delta)=O(\delta^N)$ for every $N$, that
$E(\delta)\sim\tfrac12F(\delta/2)$, or that logarithmic or Lambert--\(W\)
asymptotics transfer through the implicit argument.  Those require quantitative
flatness and stability estimates beyond the algebra proved here.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Normalized Volterra calculus and exact Fabius primitives}
\label{sec:normalized-volterra}

Repeated integration is most useful here in a form that remembers the base point, works for
either orientation of the endpoints, and does not introduce a new normalization at order
zero.  Let \(E\) be a real normed vector space, \(f:\RealNumbers\to E\), and \(a\in\RealNumbers\).  Write
\begin{align}
 (V_af)(x)&:=\int_a^x f(t)\Differential t,                                      \label{eq:volterra-first}\\
 L_a^0f&:=f, & L_a^{n+1}f&:=V_a(L_a^nf),                              \label{eq:volterra-literal}\\
 J_a^0f(x)&:=f(x), &
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)&:=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^n}{n!}\,f(t)\Differential t.               \label{eq:volterra-normalized}
\end{align}
Scalar multiplication in \(E\) is understood in the last display.  These are oriented
interval integrals: no hypothesis such as \(a\le x\) occurs.  In Lean they are the three
public definitions \lean{volterraPrimitive}, \lean{iteratedPrimitive}, and
\lean{normalizedVolterra} in \path{NormalizedVolterra.lean}.

\begin{theorem}[Literal primitive ladder]
\label{thm:literal-volterra-ladder}
For every \(m,n\in\NaturalNumbers\), literal iteration satisfies
\begin{align}
 L_a^0f&=f, &
 L_a^{n+1}f&=V_a(L_a^nf),                                      \label{eq:literal-volterra-rec}\\
 L_a^{m+n}f&=L_a^m(L_a^nf), &
 L_a^{n+1}f(a)&=0.                                             \label{eq:literal-volterra-semigroup}
\end{align}
If \(E\) is complete and \(f\) is continuous, then every \(L_a^nf\) is continuous and
\begin{align}
 D(L_a^{n+1}f)(x)&=L_a^nf(x),                                  \label{eq:literal-volterra-ftc}\\
 D^k(L_a^{k+m}f)&=L_a^mf,                                      \label{eq:literal-volterra-cancel}\\
 D^k(L_a^nf)&=L_a^{n-k}f\qquad(k\le n).                        \label{eq:literal-volterra-bounded-cancel}
\end{align}
The derivative in \eqref{eq:literal-volterra-ftc} exists in the strict sense, and
\(L_a^nf\) is \(C^n\).
\end{theorem}

The four algebraic identities are respectively
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_zero}, \lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ},
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_add}, and \lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_self}.  Continuity is
\lean{continuous_volterraPrimitive} and \lean{continuous_iteratedPrimitive}.  The strict
and ordinary pointwise FTC statements, and its equality-of-functions form, are
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_hasStrictDerivAt},
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_hasDerivAt}, and
\lean{deriv_iteratedPrimitive_succ}.  The two cancellation formulas and the smoothness
statement are \lean{iteratedDeriv_iteratedPrimitive_add},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_iteratedPrimitive}, and \lean{contDiff_iteratedPrimitive}.

\begin{theorem}[Canonical Cauchy kernel]
\label{thm:normalized-volterra-calculus}
The normalized kernel has the total order-zero and positive-order rules
\begin{align}
 J_a^0f(x)&=f(x), &
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)&=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^n}{n!}f(t)\Differential t,             \label{eq:J-unfold}\\
 J_a^1f(x)&=\int_a^x f(t)\Differential t, &
 J_a^{n+1}f(a)&=0.                                             \label{eq:J-one-base}
\end{align}
If \(f=g\) on the closed unoriented interval between \(a\) and \(x\), then
\(J_a^nf(x)=J_a^ng(x)\).  Real scalar multiplication commutes with \(J_a^n\), and any
finite sum of interval-integrable inputs may be passed through it.

If \(E\) is complete and \(f\) is continuous, then literal iteration and the kernel agree,
\begin{equation}
 L_a^nf=J_a^nf,                                               \label{eq:literal-equals-J}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{align}
 D(J_a^{n+1}f)(x)&=J_a^nf(x) &&\text{strictly},               \label{eq:J-strict-ftc}\\
 D^k(J_a^{k+m}f)&=J_a^mf,                                    \label{eq:J-cancel}\\
 J_a^{m+n}f&=J_a^m(J_a^nf).                                  \label{eq:J-semigroup}
\end{align}
Moreover \(J_a^nf\) is \(C^n\).
\end{theorem}

The four unfolding and base-point declarations are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_zero}, \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_one}, and \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_self}; locality,
scalar compatibility, and finite additivity are \lean{normalizedVolterra_congr},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_smul}, and \lean{normalizedVolterra_finsetSum}.
Equation \eqref{eq:literal-equals-J} is
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_eq_normalizedVolterra}.  The remaining conclusions are, in the
order displayed, \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_hasStrictDerivAt},
\lean{deriv_normalizedVolterra_succ},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_normalizedVolterra_add},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_add}, and \lean{contDiff_normalizedVolterra}.

The normalization is exactly covariant under every real affine map
\(A(t)=ct+d\):
\begin{align}
 J_{ca+d}^n f(cx+d)
 &=c^n J_a^n(f\circ A)(x),                                  \label{eq:volterra-affine}\\
 J_a^n(f\circ A)(x)
 &=(c^n)^{-1}J_{ca+d}^n f(cx+d)
 \qquad(c\ne0).                                             \label{eq:volterra-comp-affine}
\end{align}
The first, division-free form includes negative scales, whose sign is handled by the
oriented integral, and even \(c=0\); it also includes order zero.  Neither form assumes
integrability or completeness.  They are \lean{normalizedVolterra_affine} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_comp_affine}.

Changing the base point also has an exact finite form.  If \(f\) is interval integrable
on the two oriented pieces from \(a\) to \(b\) and from \(b\) to \(x\), then
\begin{equation}
 J_a^n f(x)
 =J_b^n f(x)
  +\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(x-b)^k}{k!}\,J_a^{n-k}f(b).
 \label{eq:volterra-basepoint-shift}
\end{equation}
No ordering of \(a,b,x\) is required.  At \(n=0\) the sum is empty and both Volterra
terms reduce to \(f(x)\).  This is \lean{normalizedVolterra_basepoint_shift}.  If, in
addition, \(f\) vanishes on the open unoriented interval between \(b\) and \(x\), then
the local positive-order tail is zero and
\begin{equation}
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(x-b)^k}{k!}\,J_a^{n+1-k}f(b).
 \label{eq:volterra-zero-tail-taylor}
\end{equation}
Only interval integrability from \(a\) to \(b\) is assumed in this corollary; no value
condition is imposed at either endpoint, and the vanishing hypothesis is vacuous when
\(b=x\).  The declaration is
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_eq_taylor_of_eq_zero}.

The hypothesis boundary is therefore sharp: affine covariance, the base-point identities,
and the direct kernel algebra take values in an arbitrary real normed vector space.
Completeness is used only for the strict-FTC and smooth literal-iteration layer, its
identification with the direct kernel, and the Taylor reconstruction below.  The four
affine and base-point declarations just named are post-mainline additions checked at
code checkpoint \texttt{e109088ed}.

The finite algebra of polynomial weights is especially clean when written with rising
factorials.  Put
\[
 n^{\overline r}:=n(n+1)\cdots(n+r-1),\qquad n^{\overline0}:=1,
\]
and write \(P^{[r]}\) for the \(r\)-th Hasse derivative of a real polynomial \(P\).

\begin{theorem}[Kernel raising, polynomial commutator, and Taylor reconstruction]
\label{thm:volterra-polynomial-taylor}
For every \(n,r\in\NaturalNumbers\) and fixed endpoint \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 J_a^n\bigl((x-\mathord\cdot)^r f\bigr)(x)
 =n^{\overline r}J_a^{n+r}f(x).                              \label{eq:J-kernel-raising}
\end{equation}
This includes \(n=0\): the rising factorial retains the identity when \(r=0\) and
annihilates the positive shifts when \(r>0\).  If \(f\) is interval integrable between
\(a\) and \(x\), then
\begin{align}
 J_a^n(Pf)(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{\operatorname{natdeg}P}
   (-1)^r n^{\overline r}P^{[r]}(x)J_a^{n+r}f(x),            \label{eq:J-polynomial}\\
 J_a^n(t^pf(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   J_a^{n+r}f(x).                                           \label{eq:J-monomial}
\end{align}
Here the second left side means \(J_a^n(t\mapsto t^pf(t))(x)\).  The formulas have
separate positive-order companions in which \(n\) is written \(n+1\); those companions
have the same hypotheses and retain the literal Cauchy-kernel order.

Finally, if \(E\) is complete and \(g:\RealNumbers\to E\) is \(C^{n+1}\), then
\begin{equation}
 J_a^{n+1}(D^{n+1}g)(x)
 =g(x)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(x-a)^k}{k!}D^kg(a).              \label{eq:J-taylor-remainder}
\end{equation}
In particular, if \(D^kg(a)=0\) for \(0\le k\le n\), then the left side is exactly
\(g(x)\).
\end{theorem}

The positive-order and all-order kernel identities are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_kernel_pow} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_kernel_pow}.  The Hasse-derivative formulas are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_polynomial} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_polynomial}; their monomial specializations are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_monomial} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_monomial}.  Taylor's formula and its zero-jet reconstruction
are \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_iteratedDeriv_eq_sub_taylor} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_iteratedDeriv_eq_of_zero_jet}.  Thus every declaration in
the generic module has a literal mathematical counterpart above, including the order-zero
and endpoint cases.

For a real order \(\alpha\), define the total oriented operator
\begin{equation}
 (I_{a+}^{\alpha}f)(x)
 :=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}f(t)\Differential t.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra}
\end{equation}
Here the real power is Lean's total real-power convention.  Its classical
Riemann--Liouville interpretation below is restricted to \(0<\alpha\) and \(a\le x\).

\begin{theorem}[Positive-real fractional Volterra foundation, covariance, and order raising]
\label{thm:fractional-volterra-foundation}
Let \(E\) be a real normed vector space and \(f,g:\RealNumbers\to E\).  For arbitrary real
\(\alpha,a,x,c,d\), the operator vanishes at its base point, depends only on the input on
the closed unoriented interval between \(a\) and \(x\), and satisfies
\begin{align}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}(cf)(x)&=cI_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x),
 & I_{a+}^{1}f(x)&=\int_a^x f(t)\Differential t.                       \label{eq:fractional-volterra-algebra}
\end{align}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), without endpoint-order or regularity assumptions,
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{n+1}f(x)=J_a^{n+1}f(x).                             \label{eq:fractional-volterra-natural}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(a\le x\), and \(f\) and \(g\) are continuous on \([a,x]\), then
the two fractional kernels are interval-integrable and
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}(f+g)(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x)+I_{a+}^{\alpha}g(x).                  \label{eq:fractional-volterra-add-input}
\end{equation}
More generally, let \(a\le b\), \(a\le x\), and let \(h\) be interval-integrable on
\([a,x]\).  If \(h=0\) on the open tail \((b,x)\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_a^x h(t)\Differential t=\int_a^{\min(x,b)}h(t)\Differential t.
 \label{eq:interval-integral-support-cutoff}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(f\) is continuous on \([a,x]\), and \(f=0\) on \((b,x)\), this
specializes to
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x)
 =\int_a^{\min(x,b)}\frac{(x-t)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}f(t)\Differential t.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-support-cutoff}
\end{equation}
For \(0<\alpha\), \(0<\beta\), and \(s\le x\), the shifted real beta kernel is
interval-integrable.  If \(s<x\), its raw value is
\begin{equation}
 \int_s^x(x-u)^{\alpha-1}(u-s)^{\beta-1}\Differential u
 =(x-s)^{\alpha+\beta-1}
   \frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-beta-kernel}
\end{equation}
Its Gamma-normalized form is
\begin{equation}
 \int_s^x
 \frac{(x-u)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}
 \frac{(u-s)^{\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\beta)}\Differential u
 =\frac{(x-s)^{\alpha+\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-normalized-beta-kernel}
\end{equation}
If, moreover, \(E\) is complete, \(a\le x\), and \(f\) is continuous on
\([a,x]\), then
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha+\beta}f(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[u\mapsto I_{a+}^{\beta}f(u)\right](x).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-semigroup}
\end{equation}
For \(v\in E\), completeness of \(E\), and a strict endpoint \(a<x\), the same
beta calculation gives the normalized shifted-power rule
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[t\mapsto
   \frac{(t-a)^{\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\beta)}v\right](x)
 =\frac{(x-a)^{\alpha+\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}v
 \qquad(\alpha,\beta>0),
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-normalized-rpow}
\end{equation}
and, for \(\rho>-1\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[t\mapsto(t-a)^\rho v\right](x)
 =\frac{\Gamma(\rho+1)(x-a)^{\alpha+\rho}}
        {\Gamma(\alpha+\rho+1)}v
 \qquad(\alpha>0).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-rpow}
\end{equation}
The constant specialization includes the degenerate endpoint:
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto v](x)
 =\frac{(x-a)^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}v
 \qquad(\alpha>0,\ a\le x).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-const}
\end{equation}
For arbitrary real \(\alpha\), \(0<c\), and \(a\le x\), increasing-affine covariance
requires no regularity, integrability, or completeness hypothesis:
\begin{align}
 I_{(ca+d)+}^{\alpha}f(cx+d)
 &=c^{\alpha}I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto f(ct+d)](x),
                                                               \label{eq:fractional-volterra-affine}\\
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto f(ct+d)](x)
 &=\left(c^\alpha\right)^{-1}I_{(ca+d)+}^{\alpha}f(cx+d).     \label{eq:fractional-volterra-comp-affine}
\end{align}
Finally, let \(E\) be complete, let \(0<\alpha\), \(a\le x\), and suppose that
\(g\) is continuous on \([a,x]\), has the right-sided interior derivative \(g'\),
and that \(g'\) is interval-integrable.  Gamma-normalized integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha+1}g'(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}g(x)
  -\frac{(x-a)^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}g(a).                 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-add-one-deriv}
\end{equation}
This includes \(a=x\); when \(g(a)=0\), the boundary term vanishes.  Only the right
derivative on the open interval is required; no two-sided derivative hypothesis is used.
\end{theorem}

The definition and ten exact theorems in \path{FractionalVolterra.lean} are
\lean{fractionalVolterra}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_self},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_congr}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_smul},
\lean{intervalIntegrable_fractionalVolterra_kernel},
\lean{intervalIntegral_eq_integral_min_of_eq_zero},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_eq_intervalIntegral_min_of_eq_zero},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_input}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_one}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_nat_succ}, together with
\lean{intervalIntegral_fractionalVolterra_betaKernel}.  The six theorems in
\path{FractionalVolterraSemigroup.lean} are
\lean{intervalIntegrable_fractionalVolterra_betaKernel},
\lean{intervalIntegral_fractionalVolterra_normalizedBetaKernel},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_normalized_rpow_smul},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_rpow_smul}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_const}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add}.  The four theorems in
\path{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean} are
\lean{fractionalVolterra_affine}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_comp_affine},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_deriv}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_deriv_of_eq_zero}.  Thus these three reusable operator
modules have one definition and twenty public theorems.  They prove no classical
order-zero law, reversed-endpoint fractional interpretation, semigroup theorem for merely
interval-integrable inputs or noncomplete targets, Caputo or Riemann--Liouville derivative
operator, complex-order theorem, nonpositive-scale affine covariance, or hierarchy-specific
formula by themselves.  The positive-real Fabius-CDF specialization is recorded in
\Cref{sec:probability-laplace-bridge}; the following total definition and six specialization
theorems bring the four-module total to two definitions and twenty-six public theorems.

The signed Fabius dilation law now enters only at the specialization step.

\begin{theorem}[Causal Rvachev fractional primitives and exact first shifts]
\label{thm:fabius-fractional-volterra-shift}
For every real \(\beta,x\), define the total causal primitive
\begin{equation}
 U_\beta(x):=I_{(-1)+}^{\beta}\RvachevUp(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\beta\) and \(-1\le x\), compact support gives the exact cutoff
\begin{equation}
 U_\beta(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\beta)}
   \int_{-1}^{\min(x,1)}(x-t)^{\beta-1}\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t.
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-min}
\end{equation}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\) and every real \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 U_{n+1}(x)=J_{-1}^{n+1}\RvachevUp(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-natural}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(0<\beta\), and \(-1\le x\), then
\begin{equation}
 U_{\alpha+\beta}(x)=I_{(-1)+}^{\alpha}U_\beta(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-semigroup}
\end{equation}
For \(0<\alpha\), the signed extension satisfies, for every \(x\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha+1}\FabiusGlobal(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}\FabiusGlobal\!\left(\frac{x}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-global-Fabius-shift}
\end{equation}
The bounded function satisfies, for every \(x\in[0,1]\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha+1}F(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}F\!\left(\frac{x}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-bounded-Fabius-shift}
\end{equation}
Finally the compactly supported fold, integrated from its left support endpoint, obeys
for every \(x\ge-1\)
\begin{equation}
 I_{(-1)+}^{\alpha+1}\RvachevUp(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}F\!\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-rvachev-Up-shift}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The total definition and six theorems in \path{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean} are
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_eq_intervalIntegral_min},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_nat_succ},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_add},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_extendedFabius_of_nonneg},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_fabiusReal}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_rvachevUp}.  Their domains are part of the statements:
the signed formula does not cover \(x<0\), the bounded formula is confined to
\([0,1]\), the cutoff and semigroup formulas start at \(-1\), and the support-truncated
formula is asserted only at positive order.

\begin{theorem}[Complete integer primitive ladder of the Fabius functions]
\label{thm:fabius-volterra-ladder}
For every \(n,p\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 J_0^n\FabiusGlobal(x)
 &=2^{\binom n2}\FabiusGlobal\!\left(\frac{x}{2^n}\right),     \label{eq:J-global-Fabius}\\
 J_0^n(t^p\FabiusGlobal(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   2^{\binom{n+r}{2}}
   \FabiusGlobal\!\left(\frac{x}{2^{n+r}}\right).              \label{eq:J-weighted-global-Fabius}
\end{align}
For the bounded function the same formulas hold whenever \(x\le1\):
\begin{align}
 J_0^nF(x)
 &=2^{\binom n2}F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^n}\right),              \label{eq:J-bounded-Fabius}\\
 J_0^n(t^pF(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   2^{\binom{n+r}{2}}
   F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^{n+r}}\right).                       \label{eq:J-weighted-bounded-Fabius}
\end{align}
All four identities include \(n=0\), arbitrary endpoint orientation, and the natural
power convention \(0^0=1\).
\end{theorem}

The four displayed statements are exactly
\lean{normalizedVolterra_extendedFabius},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_pow_mul_extendedFabius},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_fabiusReal_of_le_one}, and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_pow_mul_fabiusReal_of_le_one} in
\path{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean}.  Their proof architecture is deliberately triangular:
the global dilation equation identifies the \(n\)-th derivative of the scaled candidate;
its initial Taylor jet vanishes; \eqref{eq:J-taylor-remainder} reconstructs the candidate;
and \eqref{eq:J-monomial} supplies every natural polynomial weight.  The bounded formulas
use \lean{fabiusReal_eq_extendedFabius_of_le_one}: the oriented segment from \(0\) to any
\(x\le1\) remains inside the half-line on which the two functions agree.

\begin{boxedremark}
The generic positive-real operator is formalized in
\Cref{thm:fractional-volterra-foundation}, including its positive-real, ordered-endpoint,
continuous-Banach-input semigroup, shifted-power and constant evaluations,
positive-scale affine covariance, and right-derivative order-raising identity.  The
positive-real Fabius-CDF stopped-power and unit-endpoint moment formulas are formalized in
