\Cref{thm:fabius-fractional-stopped-power}, and the first signed, bounded, and
\(\RvachevUp\)-to-\(F\) fractional shifts in
\Cref{thm:fabius-fractional-volterra-shift}.  What remains includes the signed branch for
\(x<0\), iterated fractional shifts, the shifted dyadic lattice and its endpoint-\(\RvachevUp\)
wrapper, reflected or piecewise support-tail formulas, weighted hierarchy-specific
monomials, semigroups for shifted or reflected hierarchy variants, a full two-branch refinement law,
fractional inverse/quantile formulas, classical order-zero or reversed-endpoint
interpretations, Caputo and Riemann--Liouville derivative operators, complex powers and
complex order, and normal or Mellin continuation.  At integer order, separately named
packages for the complete \(\RvachevUp\) ladder based at \(-1\), the reflected \(1-F\) ladder,
arbitrary affine copies of \(F\), and the full piecewise polynomial tail after the support
of \(\RvachevUp\) also remain frontier targets.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Dyadic derivative cells and exact value distributions}
\label{sec:rvachev-derivative-distribution}

Write
\[
 T_n:=\binom{n+1}{2},
 \qquad
 \varepsilon_k:=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(k)}.
\]
For \(n,k\in\NaturalNumbers\), the formal cell coordinate is the total function
\begin{equation}
 c_{n,k}(y):=-1+\frac{2k+1+y}{2^n}.                       \label{eq:rvachev-derivative-cell}
\end{equation}
Its geometric use is \(0\le k<2^n\) and \(-1\le y\le1\).  In that range it maps
\([-1,1]\) into \([-1,1]\), and
\begin{equation}
 c_{n,0}(-1)=-1,\qquad
 c_{n,k}(1)=c_{n,k+1}(-1),\qquad
 c_{n,2^n}(-1)=1.                                        \label{eq:rvachev-cell-endpoints}
\end{equation}
Thus the images are exactly the \(2^n\) adjacent closed cells from \(-1\) to \(1\).

\begin{theorem}[Exact cellwise derivative tiling]
\label{thm:rvachev-derivative-cell}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), \(0\le k<2^n\), and \(-1\le y\le1\),
\begin{align}
 \RvachevUp^{(n)}\!\left(c_{n,k}(y)\right)
 &=2^{T_n}\varepsilon_k\RvachevUp(y),                            \label{eq:rvachev-cell-signed}\\
 \left|\RvachevUp^{(n)}\!\left(c_{n,k}(y)\right)\right|
 &=2^{T_n}\RvachevUp(y).                                         \label{eq:rvachev-cell-absolute}
\end{align}
In particular, the midpoint of the \(k\)-th cell has the exact signed value
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp^{(n)}\!\left(-1+\frac{2k+1}{2^n}\right)
 =2^{T_n}\varepsilon_k.                                   \label{eq:rvachev-cell-midpoint}
\end{equation}
All three formulas include \(n=0\), and the cell identities include both endpoints.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The coordinate \eqref{eq:rvachev-derivative-cell} is
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell}.  Its affine form, adjacent-endpoint identity, two outer
endpoint identities, and closed-interval range theorem are
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell_eq_div_add},
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell_one_eq_succ_neg_one},
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell_zero_neg_one},
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell_two_pow_neg_one}, and
\lean{rvachevDerivativeCell_mem_Icc}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:rvachev-cell-signed}--\eqref{eq:rvachev-cell-midpoint} are respectively
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachev_cell},
\lean{abs_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_cell}, and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachev_cell_zero}, all in
\path{RvachevDerivativeDistribution.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Continuous-test derivative distributions]
\label{thm:rvachev-derivative-continuous-tests}
Let \(E\) be a real Banach space and let \(H:\RealNumbers\to E\) be continuous.  For every
\(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), the Bochner interval integrals satisfy
\begin{align}
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)\Differential x
 &=
 2^{-n}\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(2^{T_n}\varepsilon_k\RvachevUp(y)\right)\Differential y,
                                                               \label{eq:rvachev-signed-mixture}\\
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(\left|\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right|\right)\Differential x
 &=
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(2^{T_n}\RvachevUp(y)\right)\Differential y,
                                                               \label{eq:rvachev-absolute-test}\\
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(2^{-T_n}\left|\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right|\right)\Differential x
 &=
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(\RvachevUp(y)\right)\Differential y.                    \label{eq:rvachev-normalized-test}
\end{align}
The signed formula is the exact \(2^n\)-term Thue--Morse mixture and includes order zero.
For every positive order \(0<n\), sharp signed normalization collapses that finite
mixture to the symmetric half-mixture
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(2^{-T_n}\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)\Differential x
 =\frac12\left(
   \int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(\RvachevUp(y)\right)\Differential y
   +\int_{-1}^{1}H\!\left(-\RvachevUp(y)\right)\Differential y
  \right).                                             \label{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-test}
\end{equation}
The restriction \(0<n\) is essential: at order zero the normalized signed profile is
the unsymmetrized function \(\RvachevUp\).

Let \(\lambda_{[-1,1]}\) denote Lebesgue measure restricted to the closed interval
\([-1,1]\), and let \(f_{\#}\mu\) denote the pushforward of \(\mu\) by \(f\).
The normalized absolute identity is proved as the exact equality of Borel measures
\begin{equation}
 \left(x\longmapsto
 2^{-T_n}\left|\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right|\right)_{\#}\lambda_{[-1,1]}
 =
 \RvachevUp_{\#}\lambda_{[-1,1]}.                                \label{eq:rvachev-absolute-pushforward}
\end{equation}
For \(0<n\), the normalized signed identity is likewise the exact Borel pushforward law
\begin{equation}
 \left(x\longmapsto
 2^{-T_n}\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)_{\#}\lambda_{[-1,1]}
 =\frac12\left(
   \RvachevUp_{\#}\lambda_{[-1,1]}
   +\left(y\longmapsto-\RvachevUp(y)\right)_{\#}\lambda_{[-1,1]}
  \right).                                             \label{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-pushforward}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
Equations \eqref{eq:rvachev-signed-mixture}--\eqref{eq:rvachev-normalized-test}
are \lean{intervalIntegral_comp_iteratedDeriv_rvachev},
\lean{intervalIntegral_comp_abs_iteratedDeriv_rvachev}, and
\lean{intervalIntegral_comp_normalized_abs_iteratedDeriv_rvachev}.
The positive-order half-mixture \eqref{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-test} is
\lean{intervalIntegral_comp_normalized_iteratedDeriv_rvachev}.
The pushforward equalities \eqref{eq:rvachev-absolute-pushforward} and
\eqref{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-pushforward} are respectively
\lean{map_normalized_abs_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_restrict_Icc} and
\lean{map_normalized_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_restrict_Icc}.  Both signed
half-mixture declarations assume exactly \(0<n\); the absolute declarations include
order zero.  All six declarations are in \path{RvachevDerivativeDistribution.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[All signed natural-power moments]
\label{cor:rvachev-derivative-signed-nat-moments}
For every \(n,m\in\NaturalNumbers\), ordinary natural powers satisfy the exact
Boolean-cube formula
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\left(\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)^m\Differential x
 =2^{-n}\left(1+(-1)^m\right)^n\left(2^{T_n}\right)^m
 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(y)^m\Differential y.              \label{eq:rvachev-signed-nat-moments}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if \(m\) is even, then for every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\)
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\left(\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)^m\Differential x
 =\left(2^{T_n}\right)^m
 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(y)^m\Differential y,              \label{eq:rvachev-even-nat-moments}
\end{equation}
whereas if \(m\) is odd and \(0<n\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\left(\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right)^m\Differential x=0.            \label{eq:rvachev-odd-nat-moments}
\end{equation}
The general and even formulas include derivative order zero and moment order zero.
The positive-order hypothesis in the odd formula is essential: when \(n=0\), the
factor \(\left(1+(-1)^m\right)^n\) is \(0^0=1\), and the formula returns the
generally nonzero base odd moment.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
Equations \eqref{eq:rvachev-signed-nat-moments},
\eqref{eq:rvachev-even-nat-moments}, and \eqref{eq:rvachev-odd-nat-moments} are respectively
\lean{intervalIntegral_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_pow},
\lean{intervalIntegral_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_pow_of_even}, and
\lean{intervalIntegral_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_pow_eq_zero_of_odd} in
\path{RvachevDerivativeDistribution.lean}.  The final theorem assumes exactly
\(0<n\) and odd \(m\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[All nonnegative real absolute moments]
\label{cor:rvachev-derivative-rpow-moments}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\) and every real \(p\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\left|\RvachevUp^{(n)}(x)\right|^p\Differential x
 =
 \left(2^{T_n}\right)^p
 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(y)^p\Differential y.                              \label{eq:rvachev-rpow-moments}
\end{equation}
Here the powers are Lean's real-power operation.  Thus \(p=0\) is included, with the
convention \(0^0=1\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is \lean{intervalIntegral_abs_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_rpow} in
\path{RvachevDerivativeDistribution.lean}; its hypothesis is exactly \(0\le p\).
\end{proof}

\begin{boxedremark}
\textbf{Exact current boundary.}
The signed API consists of the finite continuous-test-function mixture
\eqref{eq:rvachev-signed-mixture}, the positive-order normalized symmetric
half-mixture in continuous-test and Borel-pushforward form
\eqref{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-test} and
\eqref{eq:rvachev-normalized-signed-pushforward}, and the ordinary
\(\NaturalNumbers\)-power formulas
\eqref{eq:rvachev-signed-nat-moments}--\eqref{eq:rvachev-odd-nat-moments}.  The latter
are distinct from the nonnegative-real \lean{Real.rpow} absolute moments in
\eqref{eq:rvachev-rpow-moments}.  At every positive derivative order,
\lean{intervalIntegral_comp_normalized_iteratedDeriv_rvachev} identifies every
continuous Banach-valued test integral of the sharply normalized signed derivative
with the half-sum of the corresponding \(\RvachevUp\) and reflected
\(-\RvachevUp\) integrals.  The companion
\lean{map_normalized_iteratedDeriv_rvachev_restrict_Icc} is the exact equality of
their restricted-Lebesgue pushforward measures.  Both theorems require exactly
\(0<n\).  The symmetric signed Borel law is therefore closed; its sharp \(0<n\)
boundary preserves the unsymmetrized \(n=0\) profile.  An independent product-space
Rademacher realization is not yet packaged.  Nor does the current module state
the \lean{eLpNorm} or general rearrangement-invariant norm ladder, the exact inverse-Fabius
level sets, the beta-transform identities, or the later spectral layers.  Those claims
remain in the research-frontier report.  Equation
\eqref{eq:rvachev-absolute-pushforward} is the formal measure-theoretic base from which such
absolute-value corollaries can subsequently be proved.  The exhaustive public surface of
\path{RvachevDerivativeDistribution.lean} is one definition and eighteen theorems.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Effective flatness}

For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), \(x\ge0\), and \(2^n x\le1\), the formal development proves both
\begin{align}
 F(x)&\le2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}x^n,                            \label{eq:shape-flatness-power}\\
 F(x)&\le\frac{2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}\,x^n.              \label{eq:shape-flatness-ftc}
\end{align}
These are \lean{fabiusReal_le_two_pow_mul_pow} in
\path{EffectiveFlatness.lean} and
\lean{fabiusReal_le_two_pow_div_factorial_mul_pow} in
\path{SharpFlatness.lean}.  Their reflected forms at \(1\) and their folded forms for
\(\RvachevUp\) are also formalized in those modules.  In particular, for each natural power,
\[
 F(x)=o(x^n)\qquad(x\to0),
\]
as stated by \lean{fabiusReal_isLittleO_pow_at_zero} in
\path{FabiusFlatness.lean}.

\subsection{The precise analytic statements}

The exact real-analytic locus of the bounded function is
\begin{equation}
 F\text{ is analytic at }x
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x\notin[0,1].               \label{eq:analytic-locus-F}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabius_analyticAt_iff}, with canonical specialization
\lean{canonical_fabius_analyticAt_iff}.  For the other global forms the current proved
statements are deliberately one-sided:
\begin{align}
 &\RvachevUp\text{ is not analytic at any }x\in[-1,1],            \label{eq:nonanalytic-up-support}\\
 &\FabiusGlobal\text{ is not analytic at any }x\in[0,2).      \label{eq:nonanalytic-extension-block}
\end{align}
They are \lean{canonical_rvachev_not_analyticAt} and
\lean{extendedFabius_not_analyticAt} in \path{NowhereAnalytic.lean}.  No stronger global
locus is inferred here.

\subsection{The totalized inverse}

Let \(I:\RealNumbers\to[0,1]\) be the inverse order isomorphism on \([0,1]\), extended constantly by
\(0\) on the left and \(1\) on the right.  This is the formal \lean{fabiusInv} constructed
from \lean{fabiusOrderIso}.  It is continuous and nondecreasing; on \([0,1]\) it is a
strictly increasing bijection and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 F(I(y))=y,\qquad I(F(x))=x\qquad(x,y\in[0,1]).            \label{eq:fabius-inverse-identities}
\end{equation}
For \(0<y<1\), it is smooth and
\begin{equation}
 I'(y)
 =\frac{1}{F'(I(y))}
 =\frac{1}{2\,\RvachevUp(2I(y)-1)}
 >0.                                                       \label{eq:fabius-inverse-derivative}
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The range and inverse identities are
\lean{fabiusInv_mem_Icc}, \lean{fabiusReal_fabiusInv}, and
\lean{fabiusInv_fabiusReal}.  The order, continuity, and bijectivity statements are
\lean{strictMonoOn_fabiusInv}, \lean{monotone_fabiusInv},
\lean{continuous_fabiusInv}, and \lean{bijOn_fabiusInv}.
Interior smoothness and the derivative chain are
\lean{fabiusInv_contDiffOn_Ioo}, \lean{deriv_fabiusInv},
\lean{deriv_fabiusInv_eq_inv_two_mul_rvachevUp}, and
\lean{deriv_fabiusInv_pos}, all in \path{FabiusInverse.lean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact inverse modulus}

For a nonnegative length \(h\), set

\[
 M_h(x):=F(x+h)-F(x).
\]

Reflection gives \(M_h(1-h-x)=M_h(x)\).  The derivative shape proved above
shows that \(M_h\) is nondecreasing on
\((-\infty,(1-h)/2]\) and nonincreasing on
\([(1-h)/2,\infty)\), including intervals which meet the constant tails.
Consequently, whenever \(0\le a\le b\le1\),
\begin{equation}
 F(b-a)\le F(b)-F(a).                                      \label{eq:fabius-least-interval-mass}
\end{equation}
Writing \(G=I\) for the globally clamped inverse, transport through the order
isomorphism gives, for arbitrary \(u,v,x,y\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \lvert G(u)-G(v)\rvert\le G(\lvert u-v\rvert),
 \qquad G(x+y)\le G(x)+G(y).                              \label{eq:fabius-exact-inverse-modulus}
\end{equation}
The first bound is sharp.  For every \(\eta\ge0\), the greatest output gap
among pairs at input distance at most \(\eta\) is \(G(\eta)\), both over all
real inputs and after restricting both inputs to \([0,1]\).  Since \(G\) is
clamped, the same value is \(G(\min(\eta,1))\).

For \(0\le h\le1\), the corresponding exact strict uniform threshold is
\begin{equation}
 \bigl(\forall u,v\in\RealNumbers,
   \lvert u-v\rvert<\delta\Longrightarrow
   \lvert G(u)-G(v)\rvert<h\bigr)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \delta\le F(h).                                         \label{eq:fabius-effective-injectivity-threshold}
\end{equation}
No sign condition on \(\delta\) is needed; a nonpositive input radius makes
the implication vacuous.  Closed-threshold and contrapositive forms are also
formalized.

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The definition and fixed-length shape surface in \path{InverseModulus.lean}
are \lean{fabiusIntervalMass}, \lean{fabiusIntervalMass_reflect},
\lean{monotoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf}, and
\lean{antitoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf}.  The endpoint inequality and
its forward superadditive form are \lean{fabiusReal_sub_le_sub} and
\lean{fabiusReal_add_le}.  The ordered unit-interval, ordered global, and
order-free inverse-gap forms are
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_mem_Icc},
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_le}, and
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub}; inverse subadditivity is
\lean{fabiusInv_add_le}.  The absolute and metric forms in
\eqref{eq:fabius-exact-inverse-modulus} are
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le} and \lean{dist_fabiusInv_le}, and saturation is
\lean{fabiusInv_min_one}.  Attainment and the exact suprema are
\lean{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub},
\lean{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq},
\lean{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc}, and
\lean{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc_eq}.  Finally, the strict, closed,
contrapositive, and biconditional effective-injectivity declarations are
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_of_abs_sub_lt_fabiusReal},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_of_abs_sub_le_fabiusReal},
\lean{fabiusReal_le_abs_sub_of_le_abs_fabiusInv_sub}, and
\lean{forall_abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_iff}.
\end{proof}

This exact structural modulus does not by itself provide the closed elementary
dyadic lower bound, recursive-modulus packaging, tolerant-bisection realizer,
sequential-computability theorem, or input-bit asymptotics discussed in the
frontier computability report.

The same module controls the curvature of the inverse without asserting differentiability
at the clamped endpoints.  For \(0<y<1\),
\begin{equation}
 I''(y)=-\frac{F''(I(y))}{F'(I(y))^3}.                     \label{eq:fabius-inverse-second-derivative}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv}, with the corresponding derivative witness
\lean{deriv_fabiusInv_hasDerivAt}.  On the closed half intervals, continuity at the
endpoints and the interior derivative criterion give
\begin{equation}
 I\text{ is strictly concave on }[0,\tfrac12]
 \quad\hbox{and strictly convex on }[\tfrac12,1].           \label{eq:fabius-inverse-curvature}
\end{equation}
The exact declarations are \lean{strictConcaveOn_fabiusInv_firstHalf} and
\lean{strictConvexOn_fabiusInv_secondHalf}.

The pointwise statement is sharper.  The midpoint jet is
\begin{equation}
 I(1/2)=1/2,\qquad I'(1/2)=1/2,\qquad I''(1/2)=0,         \label{eq:fabius-inverse-midpoint-jet}
\end{equation}
and, for \(0<y<1\),
\begin{align}
 I''(y)<0&\iff 0<y<\tfrac12,\notag\\
 I''(y)>0&\iff \tfrac12<y<1,\notag\\
 I''(y)=0&\iff y=\tfrac12.                              \label{eq:fabius-inverse-curvature-loci}
\end{align}
The exact declarations are \lean{fabiusInv_half},
\lean{deriv_fabiusInv_half}, \lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_half}, and the
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_neg_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_pos_iff}, and
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_eq_zero_iff} family.  The hypothesis \(0<y<1\)
is essential in the last three statements: the totalized inverse is not
differentiable at the clamping endpoints.

The inverse inherits the exact symmetry and midpoint
\begin{equation}
 I(1-y)=1-I(y),\qquad I(1/2)=1/2,                          \label{eq:fabius-inverse-symmetry}
\end{equation}
and for \(0\le a\le2^n\),
\begin{equation}
 I\!\left(F\!\left(\frac{a}{2^n}\right)\right)=\frac{a}{2^n}. \label{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{fabiusInv_one_sub}, \lean{fabiusInv_half}, and
\lean{fabiusInv_fabiusDyadicUnit_of_le}.  Its exact analytic locus is likewise
\begin{equation}
 I\text{ is analytic at }y
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y\notin[0,1],               \label{eq:fabius-inverse-analytic-locus}
\end{equation}
by \lean{fabiusInv_analyticAt_iff}; in the open interval its derivative never vanishes by
\lean{deriv_fabiusInv_ne_zero}.

Flatness of \(F\) becomes infinite endpoint steepness of \(I\).  For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 y&=o\bigl(I(y)^n\bigr) &&(y\downarrow0),                  \label{eq:fabius-inverse-left-steepness}\\
 1-y&=o\bigl((1-I(y))^n\bigr) &&(y\uparrow1).             \label{eq:fabius-inverse-right-steepness}
\end{align}
These are \lean{id_isLittleO_fabiusInv_pow_at_zero_right} and
\lean{one_sub_isLittleO_one_sub_fabiusInv_pow_at_one_left}.  In particular, the two
one-sided difference quotients diverge:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{I(y)}{y}\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(y\downarrow0),
 \qquad
 \frac{1-I(y)}{1-y}\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(y\uparrow1). \label{eq:fabius-inverse-infinite-slopes}
\end{equation}
The formally proved counterparts are \lean{tendsto_fabiusInv_div_atTop} and
\lean{tendsto_one_sub_fabiusInv_div_one_sub_atTop}.  Thus the totalized inverse is not
differentiable at either clamping point, exactly as stated by
\lean{fabiusInv_not_differentiableAt_zero} and
\lean{fabiusInv_not_differentiableAt_one}; no finite endpoint derivative is being smuggled
into the curvature statement above.  The stronger limits of the positive interior derivative
itself are also
formalized:
\begin{equation}
 I'(y)\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(y\downarrow0),\qquad
 I'(y)\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(y\uparrow1).           \label{eq:fabius-inverse-derivative-blowup}
\end{equation}
They are \lean{tendsto_deriv_fabiusInv_atTop_at_zero_right} and
\lean{tendsto_deriv_fabiusInv_atTop_at_one_left}; these are limits through
the open interval, not finite derivatives at \(0\) or \(1\).

The exact uniform modulus of this clamped inverse is now separated from the
differential theory in \path{InverseModulus.lean}.  Its exhaustive public surface is one
definition, \lean{fabiusIntervalMass}, and thirty-one theorems:
\lean{fabiusIntervalMass_reflect},
\lean{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_add_nonpos},
\lean{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_one_le},
\lean{monotoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf},
\lean{antitoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf},
\lean{strictMonoOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf},
\lean{strictAntiOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf},
\lean{fabiusReal_sub_le_sub},
\lean{fabiusReal_lt_fabiusIntervalMass_of_mem_Ioo},
\lean{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_fabiusReal_iff},
\lean{fabiusReal_sub_lt_sub}, \lean{fabiusReal_sub_eq_sub_iff},
\lean{fabiusReal_add_le}, \lean{fabiusReal_add_eq_iff},
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_mem_Icc},
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_eq_sub_iff_of_mem_Icc},
\lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_le}, \lean{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub},
\lean{fabiusInv_add_le}, \lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq_iff_of_mem_Icc},
\lean{dist_fabiusInv_le}, \lean{fabiusInv_min_one},
\lean{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub}, \lean{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq},
\lean{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc},
\lean{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc_eq},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_of_abs_sub_lt_fabiusReal},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_of_abs_sub_le_fabiusReal},
\lean{fabiusReal_le_abs_sub_of_le_abs_fabiusInv_sub}, and
\lean{forall_abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_iff}.
For every nonnegative interval length, the mass increment is reflection-invariant,
vanishes on both constant tails, and has the global weak increase--decrease shape.  When
\(0<h\leq1\), the maximal support-meeting branches \([-h,(1-h)/2]\) and
\([(1-h)/2,1]\) are respectively strictly increasing and strictly decreasing.  On the
unit interval, the endpoint minimum has exact strict-interior and degenerate/endpoint
equality loci, and constrained superadditivity has its complete equality classification.
The resulting ordered and order-free inverse-gap bounds include complete ordered and
absolute equality loci on \([0,1]\), while clamping creates additional cases and therefore
precludes the corresponding global equality claims.  Subadditivity, absolute and metric
self-moduli, saturation at one, and attained exact \path{IsGreatest}/\path{sSup} moduli
hold over all real inputs and unit-interval inputs.  The final four theorems are the strict
and closed effective-injectivity bounds, their contrapositive separation form, and the
exact uniform strict-threshold biconditional.  This structural API does not claim the
computability report's closed \(\Delta_r\) lower bound, recursive-modulus packaging,
tolerant bisection realizer, sequential computability, combined computable-real-function
theorem, or bit-complexity estimate.

\subsection{Effective dyadic continuity of the inverse}
\label{sec:fabius-inverse-effective-continuity}

The exact inverse-modulus theory has an explicit primitive-recursive dyadic
specialization.  Define the natural denominators
\begin{equation}
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)!,
 \qquad
 D_{\Delta}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)^{r+1}.             \label{eq:inverse-effective-denominators}
\end{equation}
They are respectively \lean{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator}, whose displayed
closed form is \lean{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_eq}, and
\lean{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator}.  For every positive \(r\), retaining the
positive zeroth term in the exact dyadic recurrence gives the stronger estimate
\begin{equation}
 \frac{1}{2^{\binom r2}(r+1)!(2^r-1)}
 \le F(2^{-r}).                                           \label{eq:inverse-one-term-lower-bound}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusReal_inverse_two_pow_one_term_lower_bound}.  Replacing
\(2^r-1\) by \(2^r\), and then using \((r+1)!\le(r+1)^{r+1}\), yields for every
\(r\in\NaturalNumbers\)
\begin{align}
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)^{-1}&\le F(2^{-r}),                 \label{eq:inverse-factorial-lower-bound}\\
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)&\le D_{\Delta}(r),                 \label{eq:inverse-denominator-comparison}\\
 D_{\Delta}(r)^{-1}&\le F(2^{-r}).                       \label{eq:inverse-delta-lower-bound}
\end{align}
The three exact declarations are
\lean{inv_inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_fabiusReal},
\lean{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_deltaDenominator}, and
\lean{inv_inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_le_fabiusReal}.  Thus the final line is
the report's elementary \(\Delta_r\) bound, obtained here as a corollary of the
stronger factorial estimate rather than from an infinite-product cylinder event.

Both denominator functions are primitive recursive, by
\lean{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_primrec} and
\lean{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_primrec}.  For arbitrary real inputs \(u,v\),
the two corresponding strict and closed moduli are
\begin{align}
 |u-v|<D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)^{-1}
 &\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<2^{-r},                    \label{eq:inverse-factorial-modulus}\\
 |u-v|\le D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)^{-1}
 &\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|\le2^{-r},                  \label{eq:inverse-factorial-modulus-closed}\\
 |u-v|<D_{\Delta}(r)^{-1}
 &\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<2^{-r},                    \label{eq:inverse-delta-modulus}\\
 |u-v|\le D_{\Delta}(r)^{-1}
 &\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|\le2^{-r}.                  \label{eq:inverse-delta-modulus-closed}
\end{align}
These are
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_factorialDenominator},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_factorialDenominator},
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_deltaDenominator}, and
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_deltaDenominator}.  Taking
\(r=n\), the inequality \(n<2^n\) converts the first line to the reciprocal
precision convention: for every \(n>0\),
\begin{equation}
 |u-v|<D_{\mathrm{fac}}(n)^{-1}
 \Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n.                    \label{eq:inverse-effective-uniform-continuity}
\end{equation}
Consequently \lean{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous} proves
\lean{EffectivelyUniformContinuous} for the totalized inverse of every bounded
Fabius solution.

This is the exhaustive public surface of
\path{FabiusInverseEffectiveContinuity.lean}: two definitions and the twelve theorems
named above.  The last theorem establishes only the effective-uniform-continuity
clause.  The module supplies no tolerant-bisection realizer or sequential-computability
theorem, and therefore no combined \lean{IsComputableRealFunction} result.  It also
does not construct a logarithmic scale selector \(r(n)\), an exact least or ceiling
denominator, or an input-bit complexity estimate.

\subsection{Logarithmic reciprocal continuity of the inverse}
\label{sec:fabius-inverse-logarithmic-continuity}

The downstream module \path{FabiusInverseLogarithmicModulus.lean} replaces the
coarse choice \(r=n\) by the primitive-recursive selector

\begin{equation}
 r_{\log}(n)=\operatorname{Nat.log2}(n)+1.                \label{eq:inverse-logarithmic-order}
\end{equation}

This is \lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder}; its equation, primitive
recursiveness, least-order property for \(n>0\), and bound \(r_{\log}(n)\le n\)
are respectively
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_eq_succ_log2},
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_primrec},
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_isLeast}, and
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_le_self}.  In particular, for \(n>0\), it
is exactly the least natural \(r\) satisfying \(n<2^r\).

The definitions \lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator} and
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator} take value \(1\) at zero and,
for \(n>0\), are \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r_{\log}(n))\) and
\(D_{\Delta}(r_{\log}(n))\).  The positive-input equations are
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_of_pos} and
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator_of_pos}; primitive recursiveness
is proved by
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_primrec} and
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator_primrec}, and their pointwise
comparison is
\lean{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_le_deltaDenominator}.

For either logarithmic denominator \(d\) and every \(n>0\), both boundary forms
are available:
\begin{equation}
 |u-v|<d(n)^{-1}\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n,
 \qquad
 |u-v|\le d(n)^{-1}\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n.  \label{eq:inverse-logarithmic-moduli}
\end{equation}
The factorial declarations are
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_lt_logarithmicFactorialDenominator} and
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_le_logarithmicFactorialDenominator}; the
Delta declarations are
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_lt_logarithmicDeltaDenominator} and
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_le_logarithmicDeltaDenominator}.  The
closed input threshold still gives a strict final output because
\(2^{-r_{\log}(n)}<1/n\).  Finally,
\lean{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmic} and
\lean{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmicDelta} separately
package the factorial and report-exact Delta witnesses for
\lean{EffectivelyUniformContinuous}.

This is the exhaustive public surface of the module: three definitions and
fifteen theorems.  This module itself does not construct the exact endpoint-
mass ceiling denominator or a tolerant-bisection realizer.  The downstream
modules below supply the realizer, \lean{SequentiallyComputable}, and the
combined \lean{IsComputableRealFunction} theorem; input-bit asymptotics remain
open.

\subsection{Sharp exact endpoint-mass denominators}
\label{sec:fabius-inverse-exact-dyadic-modulus}

The next leaf, \path{FabiusInverseExactDyadicModulus.lean}, uses the exact
rational evaluator rather than a lower estimate.  At each dyadic order set
\begin{equation}
 D_*(r)=\Ceiling{\frac{1}{F(2^{-r})}} .
 \label{eq:inverse-exact-dyadic-denominator}
\end{equation}
The formal definition is \lean{inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator}; internally
it takes the natural ceiling of the reciprocal of
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow}.  The evaluator bridge
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_cast} identifies that rational with the displayed
real endpoint mass for every bounded Fabius solution.

For every \(r\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\lean{inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator_pos} proves \(D_*(r)>0\), and
\lean{inv_inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator_le_fabiusAtInverseTwoPow}
proves the exact rational inequality
\begin{equation}
 D_*(r)^{-1}\le F(2^{-r}).
 \label{eq:inverse-exact-dyadic-threshold}
\end{equation}
More precisely,
\lean{inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator_isLeast} says that \(D_*(r)\) is
the least positive natural \(d\) satisfying \(d^{-1}\le F(2^{-r})\).
Consequently, for arbitrary real \(u,v\),
\begin{equation}
 |u-v|<D_*(r)^{-1}
 \Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<2^{-r}.
 \label{eq:inverse-exact-dyadic-modulus}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_exactDyadicDenominator}.

The leastness is sharp for that fixed dyadic output target.  If
\(0<d<D_*(r)\), then
\lean{exists_fabiusInv_gap_of_lt_exactDyadicDenominator} supplies real inputs
with input gap below \(d^{-1}\) and inverse-output gap exactly \(2^{-r}\).
The witnesses in the proof are the endpoint pair
\(u=0\) and \(v=F(2^{-r})\).  Thus
\lean{inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator_isLeast_strictModulus} packages
\(D_*(r)\) as the least positive integer satisfying the universal strict
implication \eqref{eq:inverse-exact-dyadic-modulus}.

The proof is the ceiling argument with its equality case retained.  Positivity
of the exact dyadic recurrence makes its reciprocal positive, hence its natural
ceiling is positive.  The ceiling inequality gives
\(D_*(r)\ge F(2^{-r})^{-1}\); reciprocation gives
\eqref{eq:inverse-exact-dyadic-threshold}, and the converse ceiling
characterization proves the arithmetic leastness statement.  Casting the
rational bound to the reals and applying
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_of_abs_sub_lt_fabiusReal} proves the strict modulus.
For a smaller positive \(d\), strict failure of the ceiling inequality gives
\(F(2^{-r})<d^{-1}\); the endpoint pair above then has the required small input
gap but output gap \(2^{-r}\).  That counterexample turns membership plus
arithmetic leastness into leastness among all strict integer moduli.

For reciprocal output tolerances, the second definition is
\begin{equation}
 D_{*,\log}(0)=1,\qquad
 D_{*,\log}(n)=D_*(r_{\log}(n))\quad(n>0).
 \label{eq:inverse-exact-logarithmic-denominator}
\end{equation}
It is \lean{inverseFabiusExactLogarithmicDenominator}; the positive-input
equation is
\lean{inverseFabiusExactLogarithmicDenominator_of_pos}.  Combining the fixed
dyadic strict modulus with \(2^{-r_{\log}(n)}<1/n\) gives, for \(n>0\),
\begin{equation}
 |u-v|<D_{*,\log}(n)^{-1}
 \Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n,
 \label{eq:inverse-exact-logarithmic-witness}
\end{equation}
which is
\lean{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_lt_exactLogarithmicDenominator}.
Equation \eqref{eq:inverse-exact-logarithmic-witness} is a witness only:
leastness was proved for the stronger fixed target \(2^{-r_{\log}(n)}\), not
for the weaker target \(1/n\).  The value \(D_{*,\log}(0)=1\) is only a total
definition convention, and no modulus conclusion is asserted at zero.  The
two exact denominator maps are primitive recursive, by
\lean{inverseFabiusExactDyadicDenominator_primrec} and
\lean{inverseFabiusExactLogarithmicDenominator_primrec}.  Their natural-code
proof avoids assuming a separate normalized-rational computability theorem.
These certificates close the recursive-denominator clause, but do not by
themselves package an effective-uniform-continuity witness.

These two definitions and the ten theorems named above are the exhaustive
public surface of \path{FabiusInverseExactDyadicModulus.lean}.  In particular,
the module closes exact fixed-dyadic ceiling minimality but does not prove an
input-bit asymptotic or a least-denominator theorem for reciprocal target
\(1/n\).

\subsection{Fixed-depth tolerant inversion and computability of the total inverse}
\label{sec:fabius-inverse-computable}

The missing realizer is supplied by the generic unit-interval module
\path{EffectiveMonotoneInverse.lean}.  Its exhaustive public surface consists
of exactly two definitions,
\lean{SequentiallyComputableOn} and \lean{unitClamp}, and exactly six theorems,
\lean{unitClamp_sequentiallyComputable},
\lean{tolerantDifference_error},
\lean{tolerantDifference_safe_updates},
\lean{tolerantDifference_inconclusive},
\lean{tolerantBisection_correct}, and
\lean{effectiveInversionOn_Icc}.  Thus all helper state, midpoint-code, and
recursion declarations used by the implementation remain private.

The bisection is a fixed-depth natural recursion, not a search for a decidable
comparison of represented reals.  For requested output precision \(p\), a
state at depth \(s\) either carries a bracket index \(j\), representing
\([j2^{-s},(j+1)2^{-s}]\), or a certified midpoint numerator at denominator
\(2^s\).  With \(d\) the supplied positive computable denominator, the
comparison precision is

\begin{equation}
 q=p+d(p)+3+s,
 \qquad \delta=8\,2^{-q}.                              \label{eq:tolerant-bisection-grid}
\end{equation}

The midpoint \((2j+1)2^{-(s+1)}\) is fed to the forward oracle by an exactly
equal dyadic code at its required input grid.  The forward and target names
give a difference certificate of radius \(\delta/4\).  Comparing their signed
natural numerators with a margin of four output-grid units therefore has three
total branches: a certified positive difference keeps the left half, a
certified negative difference keeps the right half, and an inconclusive
comparison records the midpoint as already close enough by the inverse
modulus.  During the remaining recursion steps a recorded numerator is merely
doubled, preserving its real value while moving it to the next dyadic grid.
After exactly \(p\) steps it is consequently a numerator at denominator
\(2^p\).  If no comparison was inconclusive, the certified bracket has width
\(2^{-p}\).  This is the fixed-depth semantics proved uniformly by
\lean{tolerantBisection_correct}; equality at a midpoint can never cause
nontermination.

The abstract theorem \lean{effectiveInversionOn_Icc} assumes a computable
dyadic forward evaluator, strict monotonicity and both interval maps, an
\lean{InvOn} pair on \([0,1]\), a positive computable denominator \(d\), and
the implication
\[
 |u-v|<d(p)^{-1}\Longrightarrow |g(u)-g(v)|<2^{-p}.
\]
It concludes \lean{SequentiallyComputableOn} for \(g\) on the unit interval.
This theorem is deliberately an inverse-modulus consumer.  The adjacent
gap-to-modulus layer below derives such a \(d\) from a supplied computable
positive rational lower bound for every dyadic forward gap.  In
particular, the name \lean{effectiveInversionOn_Icc} does not claim an
arbitrary-endpoint interval algorithm without computable endpoint data.

The module \path{EffectiveGapInverse.lean} exposes exactly four public
definitions and four public theorems: \lean{EffectivelyUniformContinuousOn},
the structure
\lean{ComputablePositiveRationalSequence},
\lean{ComputablePositiveRationalSequence.value},
\lean{ComputablePositiveRationalSequence.reciprocalDenominator},
\lean{ComputablePositiveRationalSequence.reciprocalDenominator_spec},
\lean{inverseModulus_of_positiveRationalGap},
\lean{effectiveInversionOn_Icc_of_computablePositiveRationalGap}, and
\lean{clampedEffectiveInversion_of_computablePositiveRationalGap}.  Its
sequence consists of computable positive natural numerators and denominators.
The natural number
\[
 \frac{\lean{denominator}(p)}{\lean{numerator}(p)}+1
\]
is computable and positive, and its reciprocal is strictly below the encoded
rational value.  If \(f\) is strictly increasing on \([0,1]\), \(g\) maps that
interval to itself and is inverse to \(f\) there, and
\[
 \alpha(p)\le f(x+2^{-p})-f(x)
 \qquad (0\le x\le 1-2^{-p}),
\]
then \lean{inverseModulus_of_positiveRationalGap} gives the corresponding
\(2^{-p}\) inverse modulus.  Adding a computable dyadic evaluator for \(f\)
and the interval map for \(f\) proves both
\lean{SequentiallyComputableOn} and
\lean{EffectivelyUniformContinuousOn} for \(g\) on \([0,1]\).
The final theorem proves exactly
\[
 \lean{IsComputableRealFunction}\,(x\mapsto g(\lean{unitClamp}(x))).
\]
This clamped extension agrees with \(g\) on \([0,1]\); it does not assert
computability or continuity of the unclamped values of an arbitrary \(g\)
outside the unit interval.

The concrete module \path{FabiusInverseComputable.lean} has no public
definition and exactly one public theorem,
\lean{fabiusInv_isComputableRealFunction}.  It instantiates tolerant bisection
with \lean{fabiusHasComputableDyadicApproximation}, the unit-interval inverse
identities, and \lean{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator}; it then applies the
computable \(1\)-Lipschitz clamp to arbitrary input names.  This proves the
\lean{SequentiallyComputable} clause for the totalized inverse.  The theorem's
second clause is
\lean{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmicDelta}, so the final
result is the full statement
\[
 \lean{IsComputableRealFunction}\,(\lean{fabiusInv}\,F\,h_F).
\]
The fixed-depth module and Fabius specialization expose exactly nine public
declarations, while the rational-gap module adds the eight declarations above.
None adds a running-time or input-bit asymptotic theorem.  The generic
fixed-depth theorem still consumes its modulus; the gap theorem constructs one
only after the positive rational gap sequence is supplied.

\subsection{Precise non-elementarity}

The inductive predicate \lean{IsElementary} contains constants and the identity and is
closed under arithmetic operations, composition, exponential, logarithm, trigonometric
operations, and variable real powers whose elementary base is globally nonvanishing.  The
last closure statement is \lean{IsElementary.rpow_of_ne_zero}; the positive-base and
positive-region interfaces are \lean{IsElementary.rpow_of_pos} and
\lean{eqOn_rpow_of_pos}.  Every expression in this class has a dense
real-analytic locus:
\[
 \lean{IsElementary}\ g
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \overline{\{x:g\text{ is analytic at }x\}}=\RealNumbers.            \label{eq:elementary-dense-analytic}
\]
This is \lean{IsElementary.dense_analyticLocus}.  It yields the proved global obstructions
\begin{equation}
 F,\quad\RvachevUp,\quad\FabiusGlobal,\quad I
 \quad\hbox{are not elementary}.                           \label{eq:fabius-not-elementary}
\end{equation}
The exact declarations are
\lean{canonical_fabius_not_isElementary},
\lean{rvachev_not_isElementary},
\lean{extendedFabius_not_isElementary}, and
\lean{fabiusInv_not_isElementary}; local versions for \(F\) and \(I\) on \((0,1)\) are
\lean{canonical_fabius_not_isElementary_on_Ioo} and
\lean{canonical_fabiusInv_not_isElementary_on_Ioo}.

The larger formal class \lean{IsElementaryOrInverse} admits an inverse-branch constructor
only with an open branch domain, continuity and a right-inverse identity there, and
analyticity of the chosen total extension on the interior of the complement.  The canonical
global functions themselves are not members of that larger class:
\begin{equation}
 \neg\lean{IsElementaryOrInverse}\,F,
 \qquad \neg\lean{IsElementaryOrInverse}\,I.                \label{eq:not-elementary-or-inverse}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{fabius_not_isElementaryOrInverse} and
\lean{fabiusInv_not_isElementaryOrInverse}.  More strongly in a different direction, no
member of the class can represent \(F\) or \(I\) even merely on \((0,1)\), by
\lean{canonical_fabius_not_isElementaryOrInverse_on_Ioo} and
\lean{canonical_fabiusInv_not_isElementaryOrInverse_on_Ioo} in
\path{InverseNotElementary.lean}.

\section{Moments, generating functions, and reciprocal powers}
\label{sec:moments}

\subsection{Even and half moments}

Retain the even moments \(c_n\) from \eqref{eq:c-def}; in particular, \(c_0=1\).  Define
\begin{equation}
 d_0=1,\qquad
 d_n=n\int_0^1t^{n-1}\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t\quad(n\ge1).              \label{eq:d-integral}
\end{equation}
Both are positive rational sequences.  Their exact triangular recurrences, for \(n\ge1\),
are
\begin{align}
 (2n+1)(2^{2n}-1)c_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{2n+1}{2k}c_k,                   \label{eq:c-recurrence}\\
 (n+1)(2^n-1)d_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n+1}{k}d_k.                     \label{eq:d-recurrence}
\end{align}
These are the rational declarations \lean{moment_succ} and
\lean{halfMoment_succ} in \path{Arithmetic.lean}; positivity is
\lean{moment_pos} in \path{Paper06487Supplement.lean} and
\lean{halfMoment_pos} in \path{Arithmetic.lean}.  The displayed integral definitions are
\lean{integral_even_pow_mul_rvachev_eq_moment},
\lean{moment_eq_integral_formula}, and
\lean{halfMoment_eq_integral_formula_all} in \path{AnalyticMoments.lean}.

The entire generating function
\begin{equation}
 G(z)=1+z\int_0^1\RvachevUp(t)\EulerE^{zt}\Differential t
     =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_n\frac{z^n}{n!}                 \label{eq:G-def}
\end{equation}
satisfies
\begin{equation}
 G(2z)=\frac{\EulerE^z-1}{z}\,G(z),                             \label{eq:G-functional}
\end{equation}
with the removable value at \(z=0\).  The exact formal statements are
\lean{proposition_two_formula} and
\lean{complexGeneratingFunction_eq_series_formula} in
\path{AnalyticMoments.lean}; entire differentiability is
\lean{differentiable_complexGeneratingFunction} in
\path{NegativeLaplaceVerticalLog.lean}.

The two sequences are linked by
\begin{equation}
 d_n=2^{-n}\sum_{0\le k\le n/2}\binom n{2k}c_k,            \label{eq:d-from-c}
\end{equation}
and, at odd indices,
\begin{equation}
 d_{2k+1}=\frac{2k+1}{2}\,c_k.                             \label{eq:mom-d-odd-c}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{halfMoment_eq_evenMomentSum} and
\lean{halfMoment_odd_eq_moment} in \path{MomentPowerSeries.lean}.  Division-free natural
numerators for \(c_n\) and \(d_n\), together with their exact recurrences and odd-index
bridge, are formalized by \lean{momentNumerator},
\lean{halfMomentNumerator}, \lean{halfMomentNumerator_succ},
\lean{halfMomentNumerator_odd_index_eq}, and
\lean{halfMomentNumerator_odd_index_formula}.  No unproved numerical table is needed.

More explicitly, if \(C_n=\lean{momentNumerator}\,(n)\), then \(C_n\) is a positive
integer and
\begin{equation}
 c_n=
 \frac{C_n}
 {(2n+1)!!\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(2^{2j}-1)}.          \label{eq:moment-natural-normalization}
\end{equation}
The exact rational identity is \lean{moment_eq_momentNumerator_div}; the product
reindexing is \lean{evenMersenneProduct_eq_prod_Ico}, and positivity is
\lean{momentNumerator_pos}.  Thus \eqref{eq:moment-natural-normalization} records the
proved natural normalization, not a claim that the displayed denominator is already
reduced.

\subsection{Full centered moment and cumulant parity}
\label{sec:centered-cumulant-parity}

The even-indexed sequence $c_n$ admits a canonical full-order formal packaging.  Define
\begin{equation}
 M(t):=\sum_{n\ge0}\mu_n\frac{t^n}{n!}
      :=\sum_{n\ge0}c_n\frac{t^{2n}}{(2n)!},
 \qquad
 K(t):=\log M(t)=\sum_{n\ge0}\kappa_n\frac{t^n}{n!}.       \label{eq:centered-full-moment-cumulant-series}
\end{equation}
These are formal power series over $\RationalNumbers$; no analytic convergence is needed.

\begin{theorem}[Parity and Bernoulli--Mersenne cumulants]
\label{thm:centered-full-cumulants}
For every $n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{2n}=c_n,\qquad \mu_{2n+1}=0,\qquad \kappa_{2n+1}=0. \label{eq:centered-full-parity}
\end{equation}
Moreover $\kappa_0=0$, and for every $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \kappa_{2n}=\frac{2^{2n-1}B_{2n}}{n(4^n-1)}.}             \label{eq:centered-bernoulli-mersenne-cumulant}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $N\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 c_N=\frac1N\sum_{r=1}^{N}
   \binom{2N}{2r}\frac{2^{2r-1}B_{2r}}{4^r-1}\,c_{N-r}.   \label{eq:centered-bernoulli-mersenne-recurrence}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The full moment series, its coefficients, and the factorially denormalized families are
\lean{centeredRvachevFullMomentPowerSeries},
\lean{centeredRvachevFullMoment}, and
\lean{centeredRvachevFullCumulant}.  Even recovery and odd vanishing are
\lean{centeredRvachevFullMoment_even},
\lean{centeredRvachevFullMoment_odd},
\lean{centeredRvachevFullCumulant_even}, and
\lean{centeredRvachevFullCumulant_odd} in
\path{CenteredMomentParity.lean}.  The generic mechanism is the involution
$t\mapsto-t$: an even coefficient family is fixed by it, whereas an odd logarithmic
coefficient changes sign.  This is formalized without an integral-domain hypothesis by
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logCoeff_eq_zero_of_odd_coeff}.  More generally,
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_expand_of_hasSubst} shows that formal logarithms commute
with every nontrivial monomial substitution $X\mapsto X^p$ under exactly the
admissibility hypothesis for substitution by $\phi-1$; this includes coefficient rings
where its constant coefficient is nonzero but nilpotent.  The unit-constant specialization
is \lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_expand}.

The positive even formula \eqref{eq:centered-bernoulli-mersenne-cumulant} is
\lean{centeredRvachevEvenCumulant_eq_bernoulliMersenne_formula}, and
\eqref{eq:centered-bernoulli-mersenne-recurrence} is
\lean{moment_bernoulliMersenne_recurrence}, both in
\path{SinhDivBernoulliLog.lean}.  The construction is the formal full-order family
canonically attached to the already established even moments.  Interpreting every
$\mu_n$ as a separately integrated ordinary moment would additionally require the
corresponding all-order integral bridge; that analytic identification is not being
smuggled into the formal series statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Universal Bell and cumulant transforms}
\label{sec:moment-cumulant-algebra}

The algebra converting moments to cumulants is independent of the particular Fabius
law.  Over every commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra, vanishing of all odd moments forces
every odd coefficient of the formal logarithm, and hence every odd formal cumulant,
to vanish.  The generic declarations are
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logCoeff_eq_zero_of_odd_coeff} and
\lean{momentCumulant_eq_zero_of_odd_moments}.  Let \(R\) be any commutative
\(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra and let \(a:\NaturalNumbers\to R\).  Write
\begin{equation}
 (\mathcal Na)_n=\frac{a_n}{n!},
 \qquad
 (\mathcal Da)_n=n!a_n.                                  \label{eq:factorial-normalization}
\end{equation}
These changes of coefficient convention are mutually inverse.  For cumulants
\(\kappa:\NaturalNumbers\to R\) and moments \(\mu:\NaturalNumbers\to R\), define the complete Bell and
moment--cumulant transforms by the formal identities
\begin{align}
 \mathcal B(\kappa)_n
   &=n![X^n]\exp\!\left(\sum_{j\ge1}\kappa_j\frac{X^j}{j!}\right),
                                                               \label{eq:complete-bell-transform}\\
 \mathcal K(\mu)_n
   &=n![X^n]\log\!\left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}\mu_j\frac{X^j}{j!}\right).
                                                               \label{eq:moment-cumulant-transform}
\end{align}
Here the exponential and logarithm are formal power series implemented by finite
coefficient recurrences; no topology or field structure on \(R\) is being assumed.

\begin{proposition}[Bell recurrence and exact inversion]
\label{thm:moment-cumulant-algebra}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 \mathcal B(\kappa)_0&=1,
 &\mathcal K(\mu)_0&=0,                                  \label{eq:bell-cumulant-zero}\\
 \mathcal B(\kappa)_{n+1}
   &=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom nj\kappa_{j+1}
       \mathcal B(\kappa)_{n-j}.                         \label{eq:complete-bell-recurrence}
\end{align}
At every index, including zero, the two composites are exactly
\begin{align}
 \mathcal B(\mathcal K(\mu))_n
   &=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\ \mu_n,&n>0,\end{cases}
 &
 \mathcal K(\mathcal B(\kappa))_n
   &=\begin{cases}0,&n=0,\\ \kappa_n,&n>0.\end{cases}    \label{eq:bell-cumulant-inverses}
\end{align}
Consequently the transforms are inverse as functions when \(\mu_0=1\) and
\(\kappa_0=0\), respectively.  More precisely, if \(f:R\to S\) is a morphism of
commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebras, then
\begin{align}
 f\!\left(\mathcal B_R(\kappa)_n\right)
   &=\mathcal B_S(f\circ\kappa)_n,
 &
 f\!\left(\mathcal K_R(\mu)_n\right)
   &=\mathcal K_S(f\circ\mu)_n.                           \label{eq:bell-cumulant-naturality}
\end{align}

More generally, if \(E\) and \(A\) are ordinary coefficient sequences for the formal
exponent and mass series, then the coefficient-convention bridge is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal B(\mathcal D E)=\mathcal D(\lean{expCoeff}(E)),
 \qquad
 \mathcal K(\mathcal D A)=\mathcal D(\lean{logCoeff}(A)). \label{eq:bell-ordinary-bridge}
\end{equation}
Thus the same theorem applies without modification to polynomial rings, function rings,
and scalar extensions, not only to real- or rational-valued moment sequences.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The operations \(\mathcal N,\mathcal D,\mathcal B,\mathcal K\) are respectively
\lean{factorialNormalize}, \lean{factorialDenormalize},
\lean{completeBellPolynomial}, and \lean{momentCumulant}.  The two identities following
\eqref{eq:factorial-normalization} are
\lean{factorialNormalize_factorialDenormalize} and
\lean{factorialDenormalize_factorialNormalize}.  The zero values and
\eqref{eq:complete-bell-recurrence} are \lean{completeBellPolynomial_zero},
\lean{momentCumulant_zero}, and \lean{completeBellPolynomial_succ}.

Equation \eqref{eq:bell-cumulant-inverses} is
\lean{completeBellPolynomial_momentCumulant} together with
\lean{momentCumulant_completeBellPolynomial}; its normalized function-level forms are
\lean{completeBellPolynomial_momentCumulant_of_zero_eq_one} and
\lean{momentCumulant_completeBellPolynomial_of_zero_eq_zero}.  The bridge
\eqref{eq:bell-ordinary-bridge} is
\lean{completeBellPolynomial_factorialDenormalize} and
\lean{momentCumulant_factorialDenormalize}.  Finally, functoriality is
\eqref{eq:bell-cumulant-naturality}, formalized by
\lean{map_completeBellPolynomial} and \lean{map_momentCumulant}.  All declarations are in
\path{MomentCumulantAlgebra.lean} and assume only a commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Finite polynomial integrals from moments and formal cumulants]
\label{thm:polynomial-expectation-cumulant}
Let \(\Omega\) be a measurable space, let \(\mu\) be any measure on it, let
\(S\) be a semiring with a semiring homomorphism \(\varphi:S\to\RealNumbers\), and let
\(Z:\Omega\to\RealNumbers\).  For \(p\in S[X]\), write
\[
 p_n=[X^n]p,\qquad
 p_\varphi(x)=\sum_{n\in\SupportOperator(p)}\varphi(p_n)x^n,
 \qquad
 m_n=\operatorname{moment}_\mu(Z;n).
\]
Here \(\operatorname{moment}_\mu\) uses Mathlib's totalized Bochner-integral
convention; whenever \(Z^n\) is integrable it is the ordinary integral
\(\int_\Omega Z(\omega)^n\,\Differential\mu(\omega)\).
Assume only that \(Z^n\) is integrable for
\(n\in\SupportOperator(p)\), and put
\(\kappa=\mathcal K(m)\).  Then
\begin{align}
 \int_\Omega p_\varphi(Z(\omega))\,\Differential\mu(\omega)
   &=\sum_{n\in\SupportOperator(p)}\varphi(p_n)m_n,
                                                               \label{eq:polynomial-integral-moments}\\
   &=\sum_{n\in\SupportOperator(p)}
       \varphi(p_n)\mathcal B(\kappa)_n
       +\varphi(p_0)(m_0-1).                                \label{eq:polynomial-integral-cumulants}
\end{align}
If \(m_0=1\), the correction in
\eqref{eq:polynomial-integral-cumulants} vanishes.  This holds
automatically when \(\mu\) is a probability measure.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
All four public identifiers share the prefix
\texttt{integral\_eval}\textsubscript{2}\texttt{\_eq\_sum\_}.
In the order of the two displayed equations, the \(m_0=1\) corollary,
and the probability-measure corollary, their suffixes are
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2.8em,itemsep=0pt,topsep=2pt]
 \item \path{moment};
 \item \path{completeBell_momentCumulant_with_mass_correction};
 \item \path{completeBell_momentCumulant_of_moment_zero_eq_one};
 \item \path{completeBell_momentCumulant}.
\end{enumerate}
They are declared in \path{PolynomialExpectationCumulant.lean}.
They require neither boundedness nor independence, and no integrability
assumptions for powers outside the finite support of \(p\).
They formalize the finite moment-to-Bell rewrite used by endpoint transfer,
not the analytic transfer expansion or its remainder estimates.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Rvachev moment--Appell deconvolution and polynomial synthesis}
\label{sec:rvachev-moment-appell}

In the current compiled tree,
\path{RvachevMomentAppell.lean} specializes the formal Bell and Appell engines
to the Rvachev probability law.  Its six public definitions are
\lean{rvachevRawMomentRat}, \lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat},
\lean{rvachevAppellPolynomialRat}, \lean{rvachevAppellPolynomial}, and
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial}, together with
\lean{rvachevDeconvolutionLinearMap}.  Its thirty-three public theorems are
\lean{rvachevRawMomentRat_zero}, \lean{rvachevRawMomentRat_even},
\lean{rvachevRawMomentRat_odd}, \lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat_zero},
\lean{binomialConv_rvachevRawMomentRat_reciprocal},
\lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat_eq_completeBellPolynomial},
\lean{monic_rvachevAppellPolynomialRat},
\lean{natDegree_rvachevAppellPolynomialRat},
\lean{rvachevAppellPolynomial_eq_poly_cast},
\lean{monic_rvachevAppellPolynomial},
\lean{natDegree_rvachevAppellPolynomial},
\lean{eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_add},
\lean{integral_pow_mul_rvachev_eq_rvachevRawMomentRat_cast},
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_add_mul_rvachev},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolutionLinearMap_apply},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_zero},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_add},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_smul},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_finsetSum},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_C_mul},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_monomial},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_X_pow},
\lean{coeff_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_natDegree},
\lean{natDegree_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_le},
\lean{natDegree_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial},
\lean{leadingCoeff_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolutionLinearMap_injective},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_injective},
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_add_mul_rvachev},
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_sub_mul_rvachev},
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_sub_mul_rvachev}, and
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_mul_rvachev_eq_zero}.

Thus the full rational raw-moment sequence has the executable even moments at
even indices and zero at odd indices; its formal binomial-convolution
reciprocal is the corresponding complete-Bell family.  The rational and real
Rvachev reciprocal-moment Appell polynomials are monic of exact degree.  Write
\(A_n=\lean{rvachevAppellPolynomial}\ n\).  If
\(u_F=\lean{rvachevUp}\ F\) for any bounded Fabius solution, their analytic
bridges are
\begin{align}
 \int_{\RealNumbers} A_n(x+y)u_F(y)\Differential y&=x^n,
   \label{eq:rvachev-appell-smoothing}\\
 \int_{\RealNumbers} (\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P)(x+y)u_F(y)\Differential y&=P(x),
   \label{eq:rvachev-deconvolution-smoothing}
\end{align}
where \(\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P\) denotes
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial}\(P\).
Reflection invariance of the even Rvachev density gives the additional named
centered identities
\begin{align}
 \int_{\RealNumbers} A_n(x-y)u_F(y)\Differential y&=x^n,
   \label{eq:rvachev-appell-centered}\\
 \int_{\RealNumbers} (\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P)(x-y)u_F(y)\Differential y&=P(x)
   \label{eq:rvachev-deconvolution-centered}
\end{align}
These are respectively
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_sub_mul_rvachev} and
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_sub_mul_rvachev}.  For every
positive \(n\),
\lean{integral_eval_rvachevAppellPolynomial_mul_rvachev_eq_zero} further says
that \(A_n\) has Rvachev mean zero.  This coefficientwise operation sends
a monomial and \(X^n\) to the correspondingly scaled and unscaled
Rvachev--Appell polynomial.  Its triangular top term is exact: the coefficient
in the original natural degree is unchanged, so both natural degree and
leading coefficient are preserved.  Its kernel is trivial, and both the
linear-map package and the underlying operation are injective.  The linear-map
package proves preservation of zero, addition, real scalar multiplication,
finite sums, and multiplication by a constant polynomial; these are algebraic
identities and do not assert an analytic reciprocal MGF or Appell generating
series, a literal differential-operator expansion, reciprocal/deconvolution
parity, or the displayed low reciprocal coefficients.

In the current compiled tree,
\path{RvachevPolynomialSynthesis.lean} exports no public definitions and
exactly five public theorems:
\lean{tsum_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
\lean{normalized_tsum_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
\lean{sum_Ioo_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
\lean{normalized_sum_Ioo_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
and
\lean{normalized_tsum_shifted_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_rvachevUp}.
For \(M\in\NaturalNumbers\setminus\{0\}\) and
\(\deg P\le \TwoAdicValuation(M)\), they give
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}(\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P)(k/M)u_F(x-k/M)
   &=M P(x),                                                 \label{eq:rvachev-polynomial-synthesis-global}\\
 P(x)&=\frac1M\sum_{-2M<k<2M}
  (\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P)(k/M)u_F(x-k/M),\quad -1\le x\le1. \label{eq:rvachev-polynomial-synthesis-finite}
\end{align}
The first sum is Lean's global \texttt{tsum}; compact support makes it
pointwise finite.  The second is the exact uniform support truncation, including
the vanishing boundary atoms.

The fifth theorem has arbitrary real phase \(\theta\) and arbitrary real
evaluation point \(x\):
\begin{equation}
 P(x)=\frac1M\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}
  (\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P)\!\left(x-\frac{\theta+k}{M}\right)
  u_F\!\left(\frac{\theta+k}{M}\right).
 \label{eq:rvachev-polynomial-reproduction-arbitrary-phase}
\end{equation}
The displayed bilateral sum is Lean's \texttt{tsum}; since
\(\SupportOf{u_F}\subseteq[-1,1]\), a summand can be nonzero only when
\(\AbsoluteValue{\theta+k}\le M\), so only finitely many integers \(k\)
contribute.  The theorem assumes exactly \(M\ne0\) and
\(\deg P\le \TwoAdicValuation(M)\).  Thus the specialization \(M=2^N\)
formalizes arbitrary-phase reproduction of every polynomial
\(P\in\RealNumbers[X]\) with \(\deg P\le N\), equivalently every monomial
\(X^n\) for \(0\le n\le N\).

The compiled module \path{RvachevSuperconvergentSynthesis.lean} adds exactly
one public definition and eight public theorems.  Its definition
\lean{IsRvachevSuperconvergentPhase} selects \(0,1/2\) when
\(\TwoAdicValuation(M)+1\) is odd and \(1/4,3/4\) when it is even.
The theorem \lean{isRvachevSuperconvergentPhase_two_pow_iff} rewrites this for
\(M=2^N\): even \(N\) selects the endpoint phases and odd \(N\) the quarter
phases.  The remaining seven theorems are
\lean{tsum_quarter_monomial_eq_integral_of_even_deg},
\lean{tsum_three_quarters_monomial_eq_integral_of_even_deg},
\lean{tsum_shifted_monomial_eq_integral_superconvergent},
\lean{tsum_shifted_polynomial_eq_integral_superconvergent},
\lean{integral_polynomial_mul_rvachevUp_eq_normalized_tsum_superconvergent},
\lean{normalized_tsum_shifted_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_rvachevUp_superconvergent},
and
\lean{normalized_tsum_shifted_rvachevAppellPolynomial_mul_rvachevUp_superconvergent}.
For every nonzero natural \(M\), they prove selected-phase monomial and
polynomial identities through degree \(\TwoAdicValuation(M)+1\), physical
quadrature, deconvolved-polynomial reconstruction, and the Appell
specialization.  This arbitrary-\(M\) result is stronger than the
manuscript's dyadic-only claim.  The phase predicate uses exact real
representatives rather than congruence classes modulo one, and the module
asserts neither a complete phase classification nor maximality, positivity,
or rationality of the quadrature.

The current-tree module \path{LagrangeRvachevSynthesis.lean} has the exact
public inventory of two definitions,
\lean{lagrangeRvachevDecoder} and
\lean{lagrangeRvachevAtomCoefficient}, and seven theorems,
\lean{natDegree_lagrangeBasis_le_card_sub_one},
\lean{natDegree_lagrangeInterpolate_le_card_sub_one},
\lean{normalized_sum_Ioo_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
\lean{normalized_sum_Ioo_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eval_node},
\lean{lagrangeRvachevAtomCoefficient_eq_deconvolved_interpolate},
\lean{sum_Ioo_lagrangeRvachevAtomCoefficient_mul_shifted_rvachevUp}, and
\lean{sum_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eq_one}.  The decoder samples the Rvachev
deconvolution of a finite Lagrange basis, and the atom coefficient is its
\(1/M\)-normalized linear action on nodal data.  The first two theorems bound
the basis and interpolant degrees by one less than the node count without a
distinctness hypothesis.  If \(M\ne0\) and that degree bound is at most
\(\TwoAdicValuation(M)\), the next four identities give exact finite cardinal synthesis on
\([-1,1]\), its componentwise Kronecker-delta evaluation at distinct in-range
nodes, the coefficient/deconvolution identity, and reconstruction of the full
Lagrange interpolant.  The last theorem says that each fixed lattice-sample
decoder row has total mass one for a nonempty distinct node family.
Consequently generic finite-node synthesis, componentwise biorthogonality,
and the exact finite interpolation loop are proved.  This does not supply the
geometric-node Gaussian q-binomial/q-Pochhammer or elementary-symmetric
closed form for decoder entries, a bundled matrix/right-inverse or stochastic
matrix wrapper, or an optimal or minimum-variation decoder.  The geometric
closed form is supplied by the next module and the typed right-inverse wrapper
by \path{LagrangeRvachevMatrix.lean}; optimization remains open.

\path{RvachevAppellHasse.lean} has exactly one public definition,
\lean{Appell.polynomialTransform}, and fourteen public theorems:
\lean{Appell.polynomialTransform_apply},
\lean{Appell.polynomialTransform_monomial},
\lean{Appell.polynomialTransform_eq_sum_hasseDeriv_of_natDegree_lt},
\lean{Appell.polynomialTransform_eq_sum_hasseDeriv},
\lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat_odd},

\lean{geometric_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eq}.
Over a commutative semiring, the coefficientwise linear transform sends
monomials to an arbitrary Appell family and is exactly a finite sum of Hasse
derivatives.  The Rvachev specialization proves that the formal reciprocal
moment sequence vanishes in odd degree and reduces deconvolution to the even
Hasse derivatives.  Taylor's coefficient theorem and Vieta's formula identify
the Hasse derivatives of a finite root product with complementary elementary
symmetric functions.  In particular,
\lean{eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_qFallingPower} is the exact
q-falling-power formula of
\nolinkurl{gq:prop:q-Appell-falling}.

The field-valued Lagrange factorization and nodal-weight identity, followed by
the real geometric specialization
\lean{geometric_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eq}, give the complete Gaussian
q-Pochhammer prefactor times the finite even reciprocal-moment sum in
\nolinkurl{gq:thm:gaussian-Appell-decoder}.  These formulas are algebraically
total even when \(c=0\), \(q=0\), or nodes collide, because the Lagrange basis
uses totalized inversion; on that locus it is not a cardinal basis.  The
manuscript's cardinal interpretation retains \(c>0\), \(0<q<1\), a nonzero
dyadic mesh, and the degree and interval conditions of the separate synthesis
theorems.  Composing the q-falling formula with
\lean{normalized_sum_Ioo_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
and the decoder formula with
\lean{normalized_sum_Ioo_lagrangeRvachevDecoder_mul_shifted_rvachevUp},
makes both cited comb results Exact.  The coefficients
\lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat} are the formal reciprocal-moment sequence;
no convergence theorem for an analytic reciprocal MGF is asserted.  Atom
reconstruction remains owned by those synthesis declarations, and this leaf
adds no larger matrix right-inverse or decoder-optimality statement.

\subsubsection{Finite dyadic Appell prefixes and exact recovery}

The compiled leaf \path{FinitePrefixAppellRecovery.lean} constructs the
finite-prefix moment sequences rather than assuming the desired formulas.
Its eleven public definitions are
\lean{unitUniformRawMomentRat},
\lean{centeredUnitUniformRawMomentRat},
\lean{dyadicPrefixScaleRat}, \lean{dyadicPrefixMomentRat},
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixMomentRat},
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixMomentRat},
\lean{kabayaIriAppellPolynomialRat},
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat},
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat},
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixAppellScalePolynomialRat}, and
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixAppellScalePolynomialRat}.
The generic prefix recursively convolves the exact moments of the first (N)
uniform digits at scales (2^{-1},\ldots,2^{-N}); depth zero is the point
mass at zero.  The uncentered digit has moment (1/(r+1)), while the centered
digit has that value in even degree and zero in odd degree.

Write (P_n=\lean{kabayaIriAppellPolynomialRat}\,n),
(A_n=\lean{rvachevAppellPolynomialRat}\,n), and use superscript ([N])
for the corresponding prefix Appell polynomial.  The full one-step moment
recurrences yield exact prefix--tail binomial factorizations.  Reciprocal
uniqueness and the generic Appell transform then give
\begin{align}
 P_n^{[N]}(X)
  &=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom nr\,mu_r,2^{-Nr}P_{n-r}(X),
       &&\mu_r=\lean{halfMoment}\,r,                       \label{eq:finite-prefix-appell-uncentered}\
 A_n^{[N]}(X)
  &=\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}\binom n{2r},m_{2r},
       4^{-Nr}A_{n-2r}(X),
       &&m_{2r}=\lean{moment}\,r.                          \label{eq:finite-prefix-appell-centered}
\end{align}
These are
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat_eq_sum} and
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat_eq_sum_even}.  Their reusable
algebraic engine consists of
\lean{Appell.poly_binomialConv}, \lean{Appell.binomialConv_dilate}, and
\lean{Appell.dilate_dilate}; normalization and tail factorization are
\lean{dyadicPrefixMomentRat_zero},
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixMomentRat_zero},
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixMomentRat_zero},
\lean{dyadicPrefixMomentRat_binomialConv_tail},
\lean{binomialConv_uncenteredDyadicPrefixMomentRat_tail}, and
\lean{binomialConv_centeredDyadicPrefixMomentRat_tail}.

Promoting the scale to a formal outer variable (Q) produces elements of
\(RationalNumbers[X][Q]\), implemented as
\(lean{Polynomial}\,(\lean{Polynomial}\,\RationalNumbers)\).  Evaluation at
(Q=2^{-N}) and (Q=4^{-N}) recovers the two prefix polynomials by
\lean{uncenteredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat_eq_eval_scale} and
\lean{centeredDyadicPrefixAppellPolynomialRat_eq_eval_scale}.  Their exact
\emph{outer polynomial-valued} degrees are
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{natDegree}_Q P_n^{[\,\cdot\,]}=n,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{natDegree}_Q A_n^{[\,\cdot\,]}=\Floor{n/2},          \label{eq:finite-prefix-appell-outer-degrees}
\end{equation}
by \lean{natDegree_uncenteredDyadicPrefixAppellScalePolynomialRat} and
\lean{natDegree_centeredDyadicPrefixAppellScalePolynomialRat}.  The
polynomial-valued qualifier is essential.  After fixing the inner evaluation
point (X=x), the outer degree may fall; for centered odd (n), the top
inner Appell coefficient vanishes at (x=0).  Thus no universal exact
fixed-(x) degree statement is inferred from
\eqref{eq:finite-prefix-appell-outer-degrees}.

Finally let (w_{d,j}(q)=lean{geometricLagrangeWeight}\,q\,d\,j).  Evaluating
the two scale polynomials at zero gives, for every starting depth (N),
\begin{align}
 P_n(X)&=\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{n,j}(1/2)P_n^{[N+j]}(X),
                                                               \label{eq:finite-prefix-appell-recovery}\
 A_n(X)&=\sum_{j=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}
   w_{\Floor{n/2},j}(1/4)A_n^{[N+j]}(X).               \label{eq:finite-prefix-centered-recovery}
\end{align}
They are \lean{kabayaIriAppellPolynomialRat_eq_sum_prefix} and
\lean{rvachevAppellPolynomialRat_eq_sum_prefix}.  Hence the full uncentered
degree-(n) polynomial is recovered from any (n+1) consecutive prefixes,
and the full centered polynomial from any \(\Floor{n/2}+1\)
consecutive prefixes, with no limiting argument.  The seventeen theorems
listed in the preceding three paragraphs exhaust the theorem surface of the
module.  They make both
\nolinkurl{is:p2:thm:finite-prefix-expansion} and
\nolinkurl{is:p2:thm:exact-recovery} Exact.  This is exact rational
coefficient algebra; no analytic MGF convergence theorem is claimed.

The subsequent \path{RvachevLagrangeNodesOnly.lean} leaf has the one public
definition \lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomialRat} and exactly fourteen public
theorems:
\lean{map_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomialRat},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_eq_sum_appell},
\lean{eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_eq_sum_even_iterateDerivative},
\lean{rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_prod_X_sub_C_eq_sum_appell},
\lean{eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_lagrangeBasis_eq_sum_even_iterateDerivative},
\lean{eval_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomial_lagrangeBasis_eq_nodalWeight_mul_sum_appell},
\lean{lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eq_nodalWeight_mul_sum_appell},
\lean{map_lagrangeBasis_ratCast},
\lean{map_rvachevDeconvolvedPolynomialRat_lagrangeBasis},
\lean{lagrangeRvachevDecoder_eq_ratCast},
\lean{rvachevRawMomentRat_eq_centeredRvachevFullMoment},
\lean{momentCumulant_rvachevRawMomentRat_eq_centeredRvachevFullCumulant},
\lean{momentCumulant_rvachevRawMomentRat_even_eq_bernoulliMersenne}, and
\lean{rvachevReciprocalMomentRat_eq_completeBellPolynomial_neg_centeredCumulant}.
By composition, not by a single wrapper, these declarations make
\nolinkurl{cor:lag-nodes-only} Exact/Complete: they supply the distinct-node
ordinary-derivative form, the raw omitted-node Appell form, rational descent
and rational lattice samples, and the finite complete-Bell/Bernoulli--Mersenne
coefficient identification.  Rationality is asserted only at rational
evaluation points; the sampled identity is total at \(M=0\), while synthesis
retains \(M\ne0\) and its degree and interval hypotheses.  The Bell statement
is formal coefficient algebra, not analytic reciprocal-MGF convergence.  No
promotion of the larger cardinal/right-inverse rows or decoder optimality is
implied.

The downstream \path{LagrangeRvachevMatrix.lean} module packages precisely this scalar
loop as typed rectangular matrices.  Its exhaustive four-definition/abbreviation surface is
\lean{rvachevAtomIndexSet}, \lean{RvachevAtomIndex},
\lean{lagrangeRvachevEncoderMatrix}, and
\lean{lagrangeRvachevDecoderMatrix}.  The atom type is the subtype of the exact finite block

\[
 \{k\in\IntegerNumbers:-2M<k<2M\},
\]

with the two endpoints excluded.  If rows are indexed by the finite node family and columns
by this block, then the encoder and decoder entries are
\[
 A_{ik}=M^{-1}\RvachevUp(v_i-k/M),\qquad
 D_{kj}=\operatorname{lagrangeRvachevDecoder}(s,v,M,k,j).
\]
The six public theorems are
\lean{lagrangeRvachevEncoderMatrix_nonneg},
\lean{sum_lagrangeRvachevEncoderMatrix_row_eq_one},
\lean{sum_lagrangeRvachevDecoderMatrix_row_eq_one},
\lean{lagrangeRvachevEncoderMatrix_mul_decoderMatrix},
\lean{exists_neg_entry_of_rightInverse_of_row_overlap}, and
\lean{exists_lagrangeRvachevDecoderMatrix_entry_neg_of_row_overlap}.

Encoder nonnegativity requires neither distinct nodes nor a nonzero mesh.  Its row-sum-one
theorem requires \lean{IsFabius} for the chosen bounded solution, (M\ne0), and only that
the selected node lie in ([-1,1]).  Decoder row unitality instead requires a nonempty
distinct node family, but no Fabius or mesh-admissibility hypothesis.  For distinct nodes all
in ([-1,1]), (M\ne0), and
(lvert s\rvert-1\leq\TwoAdicValuation(M)), the scalar Kronecker identities give the typed
right-inverse equation (AD=I).

The generic overlap theorem is deliberately sharper about its algebraic assumptions.  For
finite matrices, entrywise (A\geq0), row-unital (D), (AD=I), and one column of (A)
strictly positive in two distinct rows force some entry of (D) to be negative; encoder row
normalization is not used.  The final theorem applies this implication to the Rvachev
matrices, conditional on an actual strictly positive overlap.  It does not prove that every
admissible node family has such an overlap.  This finite layer makes no claim about the
range, kernel, rank, trace, characteristic polynomial, or Cauchy--Binet data of (DA).
The preceding Hasse leaf supplies the scalar geometric closed form, but neither
module proves an optimal or minimum-variation decoder.

\clearpage
At the focused-build checkpoint of 30 August 2026,
\path{CompositeMeshSharpness.lean} exports one public definition,
\lean{rvachevCombExactThrough}, and seven public theorems:
\lean{exists_shift_tsum_shifted_monomial_ne_integral_nat_real},
\lean{rvachevCombExactThrough_iff_padicValNat},
\lean{rvachevCombExactThrough_iff_pow_two_dvd},
\lean{rvachevCombExactThrough_two_pow},
\lean{two_pow_le_of_rvachevCombExactThrough},
\lean{isLeast_rvachevCombExactThrough}, and
\lean{isLeast_rvachevCombExactThrough_even}.
The definition requires a nonzero natural mesh \(M\) and, for every real
polynomial \(P\) of natural degree at most \(d\) and every real shift
\(\theta\), the identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}P(\theta+k)u_F((\theta+k)/M)
 =\int_{\RealNumbers}P(x)u_F(x/M)\Differential x.                              \label{eq:rvachev-comb-exact-through}
\end{equation}
The two classification theorems prove
\begin{align}
 \text{\ttfamily rvachevCombExactThrough}(F,M,d)
  &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M\ne0\ \hbox{ and }\ d\le \TwoAdicValuation(M)\notag\\
  &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M\ne0\ \hbox{ and }\ 2^d\mid M.
 \label{eq:rvachev-comb-sharp-classification}
\end{align}
For every nonzero \(M\), the first theorem supplies a real shift at which the
monomial of degree \(\TwoAdicValuation(M)+1\) fails
\eqref{eq:rvachev-comb-exact-through}.  The remaining order theorems put
\(2^d\) in the exact class, bound every member below by \(2^d\), and prove
that \(2^d\) is its least member; at even cutoff \(2N\), this least universal
mesh is \(4^N\).

The word \emph{universal} is essential.  These declarations quantify over the
complete polynomial space through the cutoff.  They do not prove that \(2^d\)
is a least mesh for one individual \(P_d\), that \(4^N\) is least for one
particular Legendre partial sum \(S_N\), or that a target-adapted formula
cannot use a coarser mesh.

Together these modules prove formal coefficient reciprocity, polynomial
smoothing/synthesis, and the sharp universal comb threshold.  They do not
prove an analytic reciprocal-MGF or Appell generating series, a literal
reciprocal-MGF differential-operator identity, parity of the reciprocal or
deconvolved families, the displayed low reciprocal coefficients, or any
target-by-target minimality statement for the synthesis formulas.

\subsection{The probability--Laplace bridge}
\label{sec:probability-laplace-bridge}

Let \(\mu_X\) be the distribution of the weighted sum \(X\) from
\eqref{eq:random-X}.  Its atomlessness makes the open and closed survival
conventions agree on the nonnegative half-line, and both are exactly
\begin{equation}
 \mu_X([t,\infty))=\mu_X((t,\infty))=\RvachevUp(t)
 \qquad(t\ge0).                                                   \label{eq:survival-up}
\end{equation}
More generally, let \(E\) be a real Banach space and let
\(k:[0,1]\to E\) be Bochner integrable.  Fubini gives the
survival-kernel identity
\begin{equation}
 \int_{[0,1]}\left(\int_0^x k(t)\Differential t\right)\Differential\mu_X(x)
 =\int_0^1\RvachevUp(t)\,k(t)\Differential t.                              \label{eq:survival-kernel-banach}
\end{equation}
For every \(c\in[0,1]\), truncating the kernel gives the stronger endpoint
family
\begin{equation}
 \int_{[0,1]}\left(\int_0^{\min\{x,c\}} k(t)\Differential t\right)\Differential\mu_X(x)
 =\int_0^c\RvachevUp(t)\,k(t)\Differential t.                              \label{eq:survival-kernel-clipped}
\end{equation}
Thus the scalar formula
\(\int_0^1 k(t)\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t=\Expectation[K(X)]\), where
\(K(x)=\int_0^xk(t)\Differential t\), needs only \(k\in L^1(0,1)\).
In particular, for every natural \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\RealNumbers}x^n\Differential\mu_X(x)
 =n\int_0^1t^{n-1}\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t.                  \label{eq:survival-up-moments}
\end{equation}
Taking \(E=\ComplexNumbers\), regarded as a real Banach space, includes complex-valued
kernels with no separate argument.
