If \(g,g':\RealNumbers\to\RealNumbers\) are continuous and \(g\) has derivative \(g'(t)\) at every
\(t\in[0,1]\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_{[0,1]}g(x)\,\Differential\mu_X(x)
 =g(0)+\int_0^1g'(t)\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t.                           \label{eq:survival-fubini}
\end{equation}
More generally, let \(E\) be a real Banach space and let \(0\le c\le1\).  For a
map \(k:\RealNumbers\to E\), with interval integrability on \([0,c]\) in the first line
and on \([0,1]\) in the second, the partial and full survival layer-cake
identities are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^c \RvachevUp(t)k(t)\Differential t
 &=\int_{\RealNumbers}\left(\int_0^{\min\{z,c\}}k(t)\Differential t\right)\Differential\mu_X(z),
 \label{eq:survival-layer-cake-partial}\\
 \int_0^1 \RvachevUp(t)k(t)\Differential t
 &=\int_{\RealNumbers}\left(\int_0^z k(t)\Differential t\right)\Differential\mu_X(z).
 \label{eq:survival-layer-cake-full}
\end{align}
No positivity assumption is imposed on \(k\); taking \(E=\ComplexNumbers\) gives the
complex-kernel form used by transform arguments.

The atom-preserving closed-supergraph dual is the lower-CDF layer cake.  For any finite
measure \(\nu\) on \(\RealNumbers\), \(a\le b\), and Banach-valued \(k\) interval-integrable on
\([a,b]\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_a^b \nu(({-\infty},t])k(t)\Differential t
 =\int_{\RealNumbers}\left(
   \int_{\min\{\max\{z,a\},b\}}^b k(t)\Differential t\right)\Differential\nu(z).
 \label{eq:cdf-layer-cake-clamp}
\end{equation}
For the partial terminal form on \([c,b]\), only \(c\le b\) and the one-sided
almost-everywhere bound \(z\le b\) are needed.  Compact support on \([a,b]\) gives the
convenient partial corollary and the full form; the closed rays retain any atoms of \(\nu\).

\begin{theorem}[Fractional stopped-power formula for the Fabius CDF]
\label{thm:fabius-fractional-stopped-power}
For every positive real \(\alpha\) and every \(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha}F(x)
 =\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   \int_{\RealNumbers}(x-z)_+^\alpha\Differential\mu_X(z)
 =\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   \int_{\Omega}(x-X(\omega))_+^\alpha\Differential\mathbf P(\omega).
 \label{eq:fabius-fractional-stopped-power}
\end{equation}
This includes \(x\le0\), where both sides vanish.  At the unit endpoint, reflection of
the weighted-sum law gives
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha}F(1)
 =\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\int_{\RealNumbers}z^\alpha\Differential\mu_X(z)
 =\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   \int_{\Omega}X(\omega)^\alpha\Differential\mathbf P(\omega).
 \label{eq:fabius-fractional-endpoint-moment}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The generic closed-supergraph engine is
\lean{integral_smul_setIntegral_supergraph} in \path{SubgraphFubini.lean}.  Its finite-measure
clamped, one-sided-upper-supported partial, compactly supported partial, and full forms are
\lean{intervalIntegral_cdf_smul_eq_integral_clamp},
\lean{intervalIntegral_cdf_smul_eq_integral_max_of_ae_le},
\lean{intervalIntegral_cdf_smul_eq_integral_max_of_ae_mem_Icc}, and
\lean{intervalIntegral_cdf_smul_eq_integral_of_ae_mem_Icc} in
\path{CDFLayerCake.lean}.  The positive-real fractional forms---respectively without a
support hypothesis, with only an almost-everywhere lower support bound, directly for a
probability CDF under that same bound, and at the endpoint of an almost-everywhere compact
support interval---are
\lean{fractionalVolterra_measureReal_Iic_eq_integral_clamp},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_measureReal_Iic_eq_integral_posPart},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_cdf_eq_integral_posPart}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_measureReal_Iic_eq_integral_rpow_of_ae_mem_Icc} in
\path{FractionalCDFLayerCake.lean}.  Finally,
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.fractionalVolterra_fabiusReal_eq_integral_posPart},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.fractionalVolterra_fabiusReal_eq_uniformProduct_integral_posPart},
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.fractionalVolterra_fabiusReal_one_eq_integral_rpow}, and
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.fractionalVolterra_fabiusReal_one_eq_uniformProduct_integral_rpow}
in \path{FabiusFractionalIntegral.lean} are exactly
\eqref{eq:fabius-fractional-stopped-power}--
\eqref{eq:fabius-fractional-endpoint-moment}.  These declarations require neither a density
nor atomlessness.  They prove positive-real integral identities, not a fractional
derivative, a Caputo formula, a complex-order continuation, or a folded-\(\RvachevUp\), quantile,
or inverse-function specialization.

For \(s\in\RealNumbers\) and \(k\in\NaturalNumbers\), put
\begin{equation}
 L_k(s)=\int_{[0,1]}\EulerE^{-sx}x^k\,\Differential\mu_X(x).              \label{eq:raw-laplace-moment}
\end{equation}
These raw tilted moments agree with the formal analytic Fabius Laplace moments, and at zero
tilt
\begin{equation}
 L_n(0)=\int_{[0,1]}(1-x)^n\,\Differential\mu_X(x)=d_n.              \label{eq:probability-half-moment}
\end{equation}
On the real axis, every derivative of the generating function is a tilted moment:
\begin{equation}
 G^{(n)}(s)=L_n(-s),\qquad G^{(n)}(0)=d_n.                  \label{eq:G-derivative-laplace}
\end{equation}
Reflection of the law about \(1/2\) gives
\begin{equation}
 L_0(s)=\EulerE^{-s}L_0(-s),\qquad
 \EulerE^{s/2}L_0(s)=\EulerE^{-s/2}L_0(-s),                          \label{eq:laplace-reflection}
\end{equation}
so \(s\mapsto\EulerE^{s/2}L_0(s)\) is even.  Two useful quantitative forms, with all
variables real, are
\begin{align}
 L_0\!\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^2
   &\le L_0(a)L_0(b) &&(a,b\in\RealNumbers),                         \label{eq:laplace-midpoint}\\
 L_k(s)&\le (1/a)^k k!\,L_0(s-a) &&(a>0).                 \label{eq:laplace-shift}
\end{align}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The survival identity is
\lean{weightedSumDistribution_real_Ioi_eq_rvachevUp_of_nonneg}; its exact
closed-tail form is the new theorem
\lean{weightedSumDistribution_real_Ici_eq_rvachevUp_of_nonneg}, which uses
atomlessness to replace \((t,\infty)\) by \([t,\infty)\).
The general Banach-valued finite-measure Fubini theorem behind
\eqref{eq:survival-kernel-banach} is
\lean{integral_Icc_intervalIntegral_eq_intervalIntegral_smul_survival}.
Its finite-measure clipped form \eqref{eq:survival-kernel-clipped} is
\lean{integral_Icc_intervalIntegral_min_eq_intervalIntegral_smul_survival}.
Its Fabius and report-oriented scalar forms are
\lean{integral_Icc_intervalIntegral_eq_intervalIntegral_smul_rvachevUp} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_rvachevUp_eq_integral_Icc_intervalIntegral}.
The endpoint-parameterized Fabius versions are
\lean{integral_Icc_intervalIntegral_min_eq_intervalIntegral_smul_rvachevUp}
and
\lean{intervalIntegral_mul_rvachevUp_eq_integral_Icc_intervalIntegral_min}.
The continuously differentiable formula \eqref{eq:survival-fubini} is the
corollary
\lean{integral_unit_eq_zero_add_integral_deriv_mul_rvachevUp}.  The second
new theorem
\lean{integral_pow_weightedSumDistribution_eq_mul_intervalIntegral_rvachevUp}
is exactly \eqref{eq:survival-up-moments}; it quantifies over the full
weighted-sum law and every natural \(n\ge1\).  These declarations are in
\path{ProbabilityLaplaceMoments.lean}.  Together with the existing global
up/Fabius identities, the two additions make the frontier labels
\nolinkurl{prop:up-tail} and \nolinkurl{cor:up-moments} Exact; no density or
degree-zero moment claim is being added.
The Banach-valued partial and full formulas
\eqref{eq:survival-layer-cake-partial}--\eqref{eq:survival-layer-cake-full}
are additionally exposed under their local interval-integrability hypotheses as
\lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.intervalIntegral_rvachevUp_smul_eq_integral_min}
and \lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.intervalIntegral_rvachevUp_smul_eq_integral}.
Their generic engines are the support-free finite-measure theorem
\lean{intervalIntegral_survival_smul_eq_integral_clamp} and the two
interval-supported specializations
\lean{intervalIntegral_survival_smul_eq_integral_min_of_ae_mem_Icc} and
\lean{intervalIntegral_survival_smul_eq_integral_of_ae_mem_Icc} in
\path{SurvivalLayerCake.lean}.  The tilted and zero-tilt
identities are
\lean{unitLaplaceMoment_weightedSumDistribution_eq_fabiusLaplaceMoment},
\lean{unitLaplaceMoment_weightedSumDistribution_zero_eq_halfMoment}, and
\lean{unitEndpointMoment_weightedSumDistribution_eq_halfMoment} in
\path{ProbabilityLaplaceMoments.lean}.  The derivative identities are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_generatingFunction} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_generatingFunction_zero} in \path{LaplaceMoments.lean};
\eqref{eq:laplace-reflection} is
\lean{fabiusLaplaceMoment_zero_reflection} and
\lean{fabiusLaplaceMoment_zero_centered_even}.  Finally,
\eqref{eq:laplace-midpoint}--\eqref{eq:laplace-shift} are
\lean{fabiusLaplaceMoment_midpoint_sq_le_all} and
\lean{fabiusLaplaceMoment_le_shift} in \path{UnitLaplaceMomentBounds.lean}.
\end{proof}

The later exhaustive two-theorem extension of
\path{ProbabilityLaplaceMoments.lean} removes two remaining source-level
conventions.  The theorem
\lean{weightedSumDistribution_real_Ici_eq_rvachevUp_of_nonneg} proves
\begin{equation}
 \mu_X([t,\infty))=\RvachevUp(t)\qquad(t\geq0),              \label{eq:closed-rvachev-survival}
\end{equation}
using the atomlessness of the canonical weighted-sum law to replace the
strict survival ray by the closed one.  Together with
\lean{rvachevUp_eq_fabiusReal_one_sub_abs} and
\lean{rvachevUp_eq_one_sub_fabiusReal_of_nonneg}, this makes the q-monograph
label \nolinkurl{prop:up-tail} Exact, including its literal
\(\Probability(X\geq t)\) convention.  The second theorem,
\lean{integral_pow_weightedSumDistribution_eq_mul_intervalIntegral_rvachevUp},
proves for every natural \(n\geq1\)
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\RealNumbers}x^n\,\Differential\mu_X(x)
 =n\int_0^1t^{n-1}\RvachevUp(t)\,\Differential t,           \label{eq:closed-rvachev-moments}
\end{equation}
so \nolinkurl{cor:up-moments} is Exact with expectation interpreted as the
full-line integral against the law of \(X\).  Degree zero is deliberately
outside this formula: its left side is one whereas the displayed right side
would vanish.  These two declarations do not promote any broader MGF,
fractional-moment, or complex-moment claim.

\subsection{Exact values at reciprocal powers of two}
\label{sec:inverse-powers}

For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 d_n=n!\,2^{\binom n2}F(2^{-n}),
 \qquad
 F(2^{-n})=\frac{d_n}{n!\,2^{\binom n2}}.                  \label{eq:F-inverse-power}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{halfMoment_eq_fabius_formula} in
\path{AnalyticMoments.lean}.  The numerator-cleared version is
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_eq_halfMomentNumerator_formula} in
\path{ExactInversePower.lean}.

Writing \(V_n=F(2^{-n})\), one has \(V_0=1\), and for \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 V_n=
 \frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{2^n-1}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
 \frac{2^{\binom k2}}{(n-k+1)!}\,V_k.                      \label{eq:inverse-dyadic-recurrence}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_recurrence}.  The same sequence has exact finite path
and ordered-composition descriptions, proved by
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_eq_pathSum},
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_eq_composition_formula},
\lean{pathWeight_eq_boundary_product}, and
\lean{fabiusRecurrenceSequence_eq_sum_compositions} in
\path{FabiusInverseDyadicClosedForm.lean}.  Their positivity and recurrence uniqueness are
\lean{fabiusCompositionSum_pos} and \lean{triangularRecurrence_unique}.

The totalized inverse sends both exact rational presentations straight back to their
arguments.  In particular, for every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), including \(n=0\),
\begin{align}
 I\!\left(\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow}\,n\right)&=2^{-n},
                                                               \label{eq:inverse-power-inversion}\\
 I\!\left(2^{-\binom n2}\lean{fabiusCompositionSum}\,n\right)&=2^{-n}.
                                                               \label{eq:composition-inversion}
\end{align}
These are \lean{fabiusInv_fabiusAtInverseTwoPow} in \path{FabiusInverse.lean} and
\lean{fabiusInv_two_pow_choose_mul_fabiusCompositionSum} in
\path{FabiusInverseDyadicClosedForm.lean}.

\subsection{Parity and the exact two-adic valuation}

Lucas parity is packaged as
\lean{odd_binomial_coefficient_counts} and
\lean{card_odd_inner_binomial_coefficients}.  It implies the exact numerator theorem
\lean{momentNumerator_odd}.  For every \(n\ge1\), the reduced numerator and denominator of
\(2d_n\) are both odd, equivalently
\begin{equation}
 \TwoAdicValuation(d_n)=-1.                                            \label{eq:half-moment-valuation}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{two_mul_halfMoment_num_den_odd} together with
\lean{halfMoment_padicVal_two}.

Consequently, for \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 \TwoAdicValuation\!\left(F(2^{-n})\right)
 =-\binom n2-1-\TwoAdicValuation(n!),                                  \label{eq:valuation-inverse}
\end{equation}
by \lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_padicVal_two}.  The binary form uses the proved Legendre
identity
\begin{equation}
 \TwoAdicValuation(n!)=n-\BinaryDigitSum(n),                                       \label{eq:legendre-v2}
\end{equation}
namely \lean{factorial_padicVal_two}; the combined declaration is
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_padicVal_two_closed} in \path{TwoAdic.lean}.

The central-binomial specialization gives, for every \(n\ge0\),
\begin{align}
 \TwoAdicValuation\!\binom{2n}{n}&=\BinaryDigitSum(n),
                                                   \label{eq:central-binomial-valuation}\\
 \varepsilon_n&=(-1)^{\TwoAdicValuation\binom{2n}{n}}.
                                                   \label{eq:central-binomial-sign}
\end{align}
These are \lean{padicValNat_two_centralBinom} and
\lean{thueMorseSign_eq_neg_one_pow_centralBinom}.  The companion theorem
\lean{padicValNat_two_centralBinom_eq_zero_iff} says that the valuation
vanishes exactly when the binary weight is zero, hence only at \(n=0\);
a positive power of two has binary weight, and therefore valuation, one.
These three declarations exhaust the public surface of
\path{CentralBinomialValuation.lean} and give the direct formal crosswalk for
the central-binomial theorem in the Thue--Morse atlas.

The prime-power row identity is available directly at the valuation level.
For every prime \(p\), exponent \(m\), and \(0<j\le p^m\),
\begin{equation}
 \nu_p\!\binom{p^m}{j}+\nu_p(j)=m,
 \qquad
 \nu_p\!\binom{p^m}{j}=m-\nu_p(j).
 \label{eq:prime-power-binomial-valuation}
\end{equation}
The identities in \eqref{eq:prime-power-binomial-valuation} are
\lean{primePowerChoose_padicValNat_add} and
\lean{primePowerChoose_padicValNat}.  They include the right endpoint
\(j=p^m\) and the boundary \(m=0\).  The strict dyadic-comb specialization
\(0<j<2^m\) is \lean{twoPowChoose_padicValNat}.  The two preceding Pascal
rows satisfy the companion identities
\begin{equation}
 \nu_p\!\binom{p^m-1}{j}=0\quad(0\le j<p^m),
 \qquad
 \nu_p\!\binom{p^m-2}{j-1}=\nu_p(j)\quad(0<j<p^m).
 \label{eq:prime-power-companion-row-valuation}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{primePowerSubOneChoose_padicValNat} and
\lean{primePowerSubTwoChoose_padicValNat}; the latter's dyadic wrapper is
\lean{twoPowSubTwoChoose_padicValNat}.  Its strict upper bound is essential:
at \(j=p^m\) the selected column lies beyond row \(p^m-2\), so the binomial
coefficient is zero.  These six declarations, and no definitions, exhaust
\path{PrimePowerBinomialValuation.lean}.  No valuation-histogram theorem is
claimed.

\subsection{Bernoulli recurrences}

The formal development also proves Bernoulli convolution and recurrence forms for both
sequences.  For \(n\ge1\),
\begin{align}
 c_n&=\frac{1}{2^{2n}-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}
 2^{2n-2k}(2^{2k}-2)\binom{2n}{2k}B_{2k}c_{n-k},
                                                               \label{eq:mom-c-bernoulli}\\
 d_n&=\frac{n\,2^{n}}{4(2^n-1)}d_{n-1}
 -\frac{1}{2^n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{\Floor{n/2}}
 \binom{n}{2k}2^{n-2k}B_{2k}d_{n-2k}.                         \label{eq:mom-d-bernoulli}
\end{align}
Here \(B_1=-1/2\).  These are
\lean{moment_bernoulli_recurrence} and
\lean{halfMoment_bernoulli_recurrence}; their convolution forms and combined source-paper
statement are
\lean{moment_bernoulli_convolution},
\lean{halfMoment_bernoulli_convolution}, and
\lean{proposition_twenty_two_formula} in
\path{BernoulliRecurrences.lean}.

\section{Exact evaluation at arbitrary dyadic rationals}
\label{sec:dyadic}

The rationality theorem for dyadic arguments comes with two complementary exact
presentations:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a closed finite formula involving Thue--Morse signs and the rational moments $c_k$;
\item an executable bit-recursive Taylor evaluator.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{The closed Thue--Morse formula}

\begin{theorem}[Exact dyadic formula]
Let $n,a\in\NaturalNumbers$ with $0\le a\le2^n$.  Then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
 F\!\left(\frac{a}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}
 \sum_{h=0}^{a-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(h)}
 \sum_{k=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}
 \binom n{2k}c_k(2a-2h-1)^{n-2k}.}
                                                               \label{eq:dyadic-closed}
\end{equation}
An empty outer sum has value $0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The rational expression on the right is the definition \lean{fabiusDyadic}.  Its equality,
after coercion to \(\RealNumbers\), with \(F(a/2^n)\) under the displayed bound on \(a\) is
\lean{fabiusDyadic_cast} in \path{DyadicAnalytic.lean}.
\end{proof}

Every quantity on the right of \eqref{eq:dyadic-closed} is rational, so dyadic rationality is
immediate.  More importantly, the formula lives entirely in $\RationalNumbers$ and can be evaluated without
roundoff.


\begin{theorem}[Global dyadic formula]
\label{thm:dy-global-closed}
For all $n,a\in\NaturalNumbers$ the signed global extension satisfies
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
 \FabiusGlobal\!\left(\frac{a}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}
 \sum_{h=0}^{a-1}\varepsilon_h
 \sum_{k=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}
 \binom n{2k}c_k(2a-2h-1)^{n-2k}.}
                                                               \label{eq:dy-global-closed}
\end{equation}
No upper bound on $a$ is imposed.  An empty outer sum has value $0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The displayed rational identity is \lean{fabiusDyadic_cast_extended_nat} in
\path{GlobalDyadic.lean}, after unfolding the closed form \lean{fabiusDyadic}.
\end{proof}

This closed form depends only on the represented dyadic rational, not on the chosen
numerator--exponent pair.  Writing
\(\operatorname{Dyad}(n,m)=\lean{fabiusDyadic}\,n\,m\), Lean proves
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{Dyad}(n+k,2^km)&=\operatorname{Dyad}(n,m),
                                                               \label{eq:dyadic-refine-pow}\\
 \frac{m_1}{2^{n_1}}=\frac{m_2}{2^{n_2}}\ \text{in }\RationalNumbers
 &\Longrightarrow
 \operatorname{Dyad}(n_1,m_1)=\operatorname{Dyad}(n_2,m_2).   \label{eq:dyadic-rat-invariance}
\end{align}
The exact declarations are \lean{fabiusDyadic_refine_pow} and
\lean{fabiusDyadic_eq_of_rat_eq} in \path{GlobalDyadic.lean}.

Two special cases are the ones used in practice.

\begin{corollary}[Bounded case]
\label{cor:dy-bounded-case}
If $0\le a\le2^n$ then $\FabiusGlobal(a/2^n)=F(a/2^n)$, so \eqref{eq:dy-global-closed}
reduces to \eqref{eq:dyadic-closed}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The two functions agree on $[0,1]$ and $a/2^n\in[0,1]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bump case]
\label{cor:dy-bump-case}
Let $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ and $q\in\IntegerNumbers$.  If $|q|<2^n$, then
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp\!\left(\frac q{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}
 \sum_{h=0}^{q+2^n-1}\varepsilon_h
 \sum_{k=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}
 \binom n{2k}c_k\bigl(2q+2^{n+1}-2h-1\bigr)^{n-2k},
                                                               \label{eq:dy-bump-case}
\end{equation}
that is, \eqref{eq:dy-global-closed} evaluated at $a=q+2^n$.  If $|q|\ge2^n$, then
$\RvachevUp(q/2^n)=0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Taking $b=0$ in \eqref{eq:single-block} gives $\FabiusGlobal(x)=\RvachevUp(x-1)$ for $0\le x\le2$.
With $x=1+q/2^n=(q+2^n)/2^n$ and $|q|<2^n$ we have $0<x<2$, so
$\RvachevUp(q/2^n)=\FabiusGlobal\bigl((q+2^n)/2^n\bigr)$, and
\eqref{eq:dy-global-closed} with $a=q+2^n$ applies.  The last statement is the support
condition $\SupportOperator\RvachevUp=[-1,1]$.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:dy-bump-case} is the global form invoked in the proof of the uniform
denominator multiplier theorem below.  The point is that it genuinely requires
\Cref{thm:dy-global-closed} and not \eqref{eq:dyadic-closed}: for $0<q<2^n$ the numerator
$a=q+2^n$ exceeds $2^n$, so the bounded statement does not apply.  At the endpoints
$|q|=2^n$ the two clauses agree, both giving $0$; for $q=-2^n$ the outer sum is empty and
for $q=2^n$ the value is $\FabiusGlobal(2)=0$.

\begin{remark}[Why the sum is finite]
\label{rem:dy-finiteness}
Both index sets in \eqref{eq:dy-global-closed} are finite: the outer range is
\(0\le h<a\), and the inner range is \(0\le k\le\Floor{n/2}\).
The moments \(c_k\) are rational by the proved recurrence \eqref{eq:c-recurrence}.
Thus the formula is an exact computation in \(\RationalNumbers\), with no analytic truncation or
floating-point roundoff.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Exact evaluation without uncertified examples]
The finite sum is an exact rational algorithm, and its agreement with the analytic Fabius
function is \lean{fabiusDyadic_cast}.  Concrete numerical specializations that do not yet
have their own named Lean theorems are recorded only in the consolidated research-frontier
volume.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Thue--Morse cancellation}

The mechanism that makes the dyadic formulas simplify is the exact Prouhet cancellation
carried by the Thue--Morse signs.

\begin{lemma}[Affine power cancellation]
If \(d<b\), then for all \(X,Y\in\RationalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h=0}^{2^b-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(h)}(X+Yh)^d=0.               \label{eq:thue-affine-cancel}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is the strict-degree case of
\lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_of_le} in \path{ThueMorsePrefix.lean};
the monomial statement used internally is
\lean{thueMorsePowerSum_eq_zero_of_lt}.
\end{proof}

The same cancellation has an order-free centered formulation.  For half-steps
\(a_i\) indexed by a finite set \(s\), define
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s}f(x)
 :=\Delta_{(-2a_i)_{i\in s}}f\!\left(x+\sum_{i\in s}a_i\right),
                                                               \label{eq:symmetric-mixed-difference}
\end{equation}
where the right-hand side is the ordinary mixed-difference operator of
\path{ThueMorseMixedDifference.lean}.  This is
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference}.  It is evaluation at \(x\) for \(s=\varnothing\),
and for a singleton it is \(f(x+a_i)-f(x-a_i)\).  Peeling off \(i\notin s\)
gives
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s\cup\{i\}}f(x)
 =\Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s}f(x+a_i)
  -\Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s}f(x-a_i).                    \label{eq:symmetric-mixed-recurrence}
\end{equation}
The exact declarations are \lean{symmetricMixedDifference_empty},
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_singleton}, and
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_insert}.

Its Boolean-cube expansion is
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s}f(x)
 =\sum_{t\subseteq s}(-1)^{|t|}
   f\!\left(x+\sum_{i\in s}a_i-2\sum_{i\in t}a_i\right).
                                                               \label{eq:symmetric-mixed-cube}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{symmetricMixedDifference_eq_sum_powerset_smul}.  If \(p\) is a
polynomial over a commutative ring and \(\deg p\le |s|\), then
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,s}p(x)
 =[X^{|s|}]p\;|s|!\prod_{i\in s}(2a_i).                   \label{eq:symmetric-mixed-extraction}
\end{equation}
Thus every polynomial of degree strictly below \(|s|\) is annihilated, including
the zero-polynomial boundary when \(s=\varnothing\), and the first surviving
monomial \(X^{|s|}\) gives exactly the sign-free factor on the right.  These three
statements are \lean{symmetricMixedDifference_polynomial_eq_coeff_card},
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_polynomial_of_degree_lt}, and
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_pow_card}.

For the dyadic half-steps \(2^jh\), \(0\le j<m\), the definition
\lean{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference} has the zero-order value
\lean{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference_zero} and the centered block expansion
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{m,h}f(x)
 =\sum_{n<2^m}\varepsilon_n
   f\!\left(x+\sum_{j<m}2^jh-2nh\right).                  \label{eq:symmetric-dyadic-block}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference_eq_sum_thueMorseSign_smul}.
Over a characteristic-zero field, taking \(h=2^{-m}\) and reflecting the block
gives the increasing-grid form
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{m,2^{-m}}f(x)
 =\sum_{n<2^m}(-1)^m\varepsilon_n
   f\!\left(x-(1-2^{-m})+2n2^{-m}\right),                 \label{eq:symmetric-dyadic-grid}
\end{equation}
proved as
\lean{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference_inv_two_pow_eq_sum_thueMorseSign_smul}.
The positive-order spelling with \(m+1\) differences and mesh \(2^{-m}\) is
\lean{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference_inv_two_pow_succ_eq_sum_thueMorseSign_smul}.
These two definitions and eleven theorems are the exhaustive public API of
\path{ThueMorseSymmetricDifference.lean}; no limiting or differentiability
claim is made there.

The analytic corner formula is supplied by the companion
\path{ThueMorseCornerIntegral.lean}.  For a sequence of half-widths \(a_i\),
define the centered box integral recursively by
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal B_{a,0}(h;x)=h(x),\qquad
 \mathcal B_{a,N+1}(h;x)=
   \int_{-a_N}^{a_N}\mathcal B_{a,N}(h;x+u)\Differential u.
\end{equation*}
This is \lean{centeredBoxIntegral}; its two recursion equations are
\lean{centeredBoxIntegral_zero} and \lean{centeredBoxIntegral_succ}.  Suppose
\(a_i\geq0\) for \(i<N\), \(I\subseteq\RealNumbers\) is open and
order-connected, \(g\) is \(C^N\) on \(I\), and the entire segment
\[
 \left[x-\sum_{i<N}a_i,\ x+\sum_{i<N}a_i\right]
 \quad\hbox{is contained in }I.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{\mathrm{sym}}_{a,\{0,\ldots,N-1\}}g(x)
 =\mathcal B_{a,N}\!\left(g^{(N)};x\right).              \label{eq:symmetric-corner-box-integral}
\end{equation}
The range-indexed statement is
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_range_eq_centeredBoxIntegral};
\lean{symmetricMixedDifference_univ_eq_centeredBoxIntegral} is the identical
formula reindexed by \(\operatorname{Fin}N\).  These, the definition, and the
two recursion equations are the exhaustive one-definition/four-theorem
public surface of the module.  The assumptions are genuinely local: no
global \(C^N\) hypothesis is substituted.  They permit zero half-steps and
\(N=0\), hence strengthen the manuscript's positive-step case, but do not
assert a signed-half-step formula.

For the proof of \eqref{eq:symmetric-corner-box-integral}, peel the last
symmetric difference by \eqref{eq:symmetric-mixed-recurrence}.  At each
Boolean-cube corner, order-connectedness and containment of the full segment
place the translated interval inside \(I\), so the local fundamental theorem
of calculus replaces the endpoint difference by an integral of \(g'\).
Finite linearity moves the powerset sum through that integral.  The induction
hypothesis applied to \(g'\), whose local regularity is \(C^{N-1}\), gives the
remaining nested integral.  The zero-order case is evaluation by definition.
Together with the finite algebra above, this proves every clause of the
continuous-chaos report's \nolinkurl{thm:TM-corner}, now
\emph{Exact/Complete}.  It does
not provide the separate Walsh conditional-expectation construction or its
normalization.

The finite block polynomial packages the same signs without introducing an analytic
exponential identity:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h<2^b}\varepsilon_hz^h
 =\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(1-z^{2^j}).                            \label{eq:thue-block-product}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eq_product} in
\path{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.  At the level of the centered formal exponential series,
the corresponding zero has exact order \(b\), by
\lean{order_thueMorseCenteredPowerSeries} and
\lean{thueMorseCenteredPowerSeries_eq_product} in
\path{ThueMorseExponential.lean}.

Writing \(P_b(z)\) for either side of \eqref{eq:thue-block-product}, its zero at \(z=1\)
has exact multiplicity \(b\) over \(\IntegerNumbers[z]\):
\begin{equation}
 (1-z)^b\mid P_b(z),
 \qquad
 (1-z)^{b+1}\nmid P_b(z).                                  \label{eq:thue-block-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
The divisibility and matching nondivisibility statements are respectively
\lean{one_sub_X_pow_dvd_thueMorseBlockPolynomial} and
\lean{one_sub_X_pow_succ_not_dvd_thueMorseBlockPolynomial} in
\path{ThueMorseBlockAlgebra.lean}.

The ordinary derivatives of the finite block polynomial make that exact order
visible without passing to formal power series.

\begin{proposition}[Block-polynomial derivatives at one]
\label{prop:thue-block-derivatives}
For every \(b,r\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{\Differential^r}{\Differential z^r}
   \sum_{h<2^b}\varepsilon_hz^h\right|_{z=1}
 =r!\sum_{h<2^b}\varepsilon_h\binom hr.                   \label{eq:thue-block-derivative-moment}
\end{equation}
Consequently this derivative is zero when \(r<b\), while the first surviving
derivative is
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{\Differential^b}{\Differential z^b}
   \prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(1-z^{2^j})\right|_{z=1}
 =(-1)^b b!\,2^{\binom b2}.                               \label{eq:thue-block-derivative-sharp}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The derivative--binomial dictionary is
\lean{iterate_derivative_thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eval_one}; its strict
vanishing and first surviving cases are respectively
\lean{iterate_derivative_thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eval_one_of_lt} and
\lean{iterate_derivative_thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eval_one_self}, all in
\path{ThueMorseBlockAlgebra.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The first surviving Thue--Morse moment]
\label{prop:dy-tm-moment}
For every \(k\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\varepsilon_r\,r^k
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}k!.
 \;}                                                       \label{eq:dy-tm-moment}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is \lean{thueMorsePowerSum_self} in \path{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.
Together with \lean{thueMorsePowerSum_eq_zero_of_lt}, it is also the coefficient
calculation behind the exact-order theorem above.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Translation invariance]
\label{cor:dy-tm-moment-shift}
For every \(k\ge0\) and \(Q\in\RationalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\varepsilon_r\,(r+Q)^k
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}k!.                                  \label{eq:dy-tm-moment-shift}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
Apply \lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_self} with \(X=Q\) and \(Y=1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Centered reflection and the characteristic-two boundary]
\label{prop:thue-morse-centered-reflection}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring, let \(c\in R\) satisfy
\(c+c=2^m-1\), and put
\begin{equation}
 S_{m,r}(c)=\sum_{n=0}^{2^m-1}\varepsilon_n(n-c)^r\in R.
                                                                    \label{eq:thue-centered-moment}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 S_{m,r}(c)=(-1)^{m+r}S_{m,r}(c).                         \label{eq:thue-centered-reflect}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if \(m+r\) is odd, then \(2S_{m,r}(c)=0\).  This is the
characteristic-free conclusion: in a ring with \(2\)-torsion one cannot in general cancel
the factor \(2\).  Over \(\RationalNumbers\), taking \(c=(2^m-1)/2\) gives the familiar parity rule
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^m-1}\varepsilon_n
   \left(n-\frac{2^m-1}{2}\right)^r=0
 \qquad(m+r\ \text{odd}).                                \label{eq:thue-midpoint-parity}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The fixed-point identity is
\lean{sum_thueMorseSign_mul_centered_pow_reflect}, and its universal
\(2\)-torsion consequence is
\lean{two_mul_sum_thueMorseSign_mul_centered_pow_eq_zero}.  The rational
specialization, including the legitimate cancellation of \(2\ne0\), is
\lean{sum_thueMorseSign_mul_midpoint_pow_eq_zero}.  All three declarations are in
\path{ThueMorseMoments.lean}.
\end{proof}

The centered specialization \(Q=-2^k\) is therefore
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\varepsilon_r\,(r-2^k)^k
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}k!,                                  \label{eq:dy-tm-moment-centered}
\end{equation}
the exact declaration \lean{thueMorseCenteredPowerSum_self}.

\subsection{The exact leading-bit recurrence}

Let \(e,a\in\NaturalNumbers\) with \(0<a\le2^e\), and write
\begin{equation}
 b=\Floor{\log_2a},\qquad
 r=e-b,\qquad q=a-2^b,\qquad y=\frac{q}{2^e}.              \label{eq:bit-split}
\end{equation}
Thus \(0\le q<a\): the leading binary power has been removed.  Put
\(V_m=F(2^{-m})\), whose exact half-moment form is \eqref{eq:F-inverse-power}, and define
\begin{equation}
 T_r(y)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}
 \frac{2^{\binom{j+1}{2}}V_{r-j}}{j!}\,y^j.               \label{eq:Horner-polynomial}
\end{equation}
Then the exact rational recurrence is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 F\!\left(\frac a{2^e}\right)
 =T_r\!\left(\frac q{2^e}\right)
  -F\!\left(\frac q{2^e}\right).
 \;}                                                       \label{eq:taylor-block}
\end{equation}

The formal route stays entirely inside the rational dyadic model:
\lean{fabiusTaylorHorner_eq_sum} identifies the Horner routine with
\eqref{eq:Horner-polynomial};
\lean{fabiusInversePowTwoTable_hasBlockTaylor} proves the block identity;
\lean{blockTaylor_implies_bitRecurrence} turns it into the leading-bit recurrence; and
\lean{fabiusInversePowTwoTable_hasBitRecurrence} instantiates that recurrence for the
precomputed inverse-power table.  Finally,
\lean{fabiusDyadicUnit_eq_fabiusDyadic} and
\lean{fabiusDyadic_cast} identify the executable rational result with the analytic
function.  These declarations are in \path{DyadicClosedForm.lean} and
\path{DyadicAnalytic.lean}.

\subsection{The bit-recursive evaluator}

Use \(b,r,q,y\), and \(T_r\) from
\eqref{eq:bit-split}--\eqref{eq:Horner-polynomial}.  Equation
\eqref{eq:taylor-block} reduces the numerator from \(a\) to the strictly smaller
\(q\).

\begin{algorithm}[Exact dyadic evaluator]
\label{alg:dyadic-evaluator}
Precompute $V_j=F(2^{-j})$ for $0\le j\le e$ from
\eqref{eq:F-inverse-power}.  Define $\operatorname{Eval}(e,a)$ recursively by
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{Eval}(e,a)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&a\le0,\\
  1,&a\ge2^e,\\
  T_r(q/2^e)-\operatorname{Eval}(e,q),&0<a<2^e,
 \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
where \(b,r,q\) are given by \eqref{eq:bit-split}; evaluate \(T_r\) by Horner's rule.
\end{algorithm}

A literal pseudocode version is:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
Eval(e, a, V):
    if a <= 0:      return 0
    if a >= 2^e:    return 1

    b = floor_log2(a)
    r = e - b
    q = a - 2^b
    y = q / 2^e

    p = V[0]                       // highest Taylor coefficient, j = r
    for m = 1 .. r:
        j = r - m
        p = V[m] + (2^(j+1) * y / (j+1)) * p

    return p - Eval(e, q, V)
\end{lstlisting}
The displayed loop is one convenient Horner ordering; any exact evaluation of
\eqref{eq:Horner-polynomial} is equivalent.  In the formal source this evaluator is
\lean{fabiusDyadicValue}.

\begin{theorem}[Correctness and termination]
For every $e\in\NaturalNumbers$ and $a\in\IntegerNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Eval}(e,a)=F(a/2^e)                        \label{eq:evaluator-correct}
\end{equation}
under the bounded convention $F=0$ to the left and $F=1$ to the right.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The pseudocode is the rational routine \lean{fabiusDyadicValue}, whose analytic correctness
is \lean{fabiusDyadicValue_cast} in \path{PaperStatements.lean}.  The interior recursion is
\lean{fabiusInversePowTwoTable_hasBitRecurrence}; the corresponding total recursive
correctness theorem is \lean{fabiusDyadicUnitAux_eq_of_bitRecurrence}.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Reduction of an arbitrary dyadic argument}
\label{subsec:dy-triangular}

Algorithm~\ref{alg:dyadic-evaluator} evaluates the bounded function $F$ on $[0,1]$.  A total
evaluator for the signed global extension first folds the argument back into the unit interval
and records the associated sign.  The fold is a reduction modulo $2$ followed by a reflection.

\begin{theorem}[Triangular reduction]
\label{thm:dy-triangular}
Let $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ and put $s=2^n$.  Let $a\in\NaturalNumbers$ and write
\begin{equation}
 a=2sB+R,\qquad B=\Floor{\frac a{2s}},\qquad 0\le R<2s,
                                                               \label{eq:dy-triangular-split}
\end{equation}
and set
\begin{equation}
 C=\begin{cases}R,&R\le s,\\ 2s-R,&R>s.\end{cases}
                                                               \label{eq:dy-triangular-fold}
\end{equation}
Then $0\le C\le s$ and
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\ \FabiusGlobal\!\left(\frac a{2^n}\right)=\varepsilon_B\,F\!\left(\frac C{2^n}\right).\ }
                                                               \label{eq:dy-triangular}
\end{equation}
For $a\in\IntegerNumbers$ with $a<0$ one has $\FabiusGlobal(a/2^n)=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $x=a/2^n=2B+R/s$.  Since $0\le R<2s$ we have $2B\le x<2B+2$, so $x$ lies in the
$B$th block and \eqref{eq:single-block} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\varepsilon_B\RvachevUp(x-2B-1)=\varepsilon_B\RvachevUp\!\left(\frac Rs-1\right).
\end{equation*}
Put $t=R/s-1$, so that $-1\le t<1$.  The fold relation \eqref{eq:up-fold} says
$\RvachevUp(t)=F(t+1)$ for $t\le0$ and $\RvachevUp(t)=F(1-t)$ for $t>0$; on $[-1,1]$ both clauses read
$\RvachevUp(t)=F(1-|t|)$.  If $R\le s$ then $|t|=1-R/s$ and $F(1-|t|)=F(R/s)$.  If $R>s$ then
$|t|=R/s-1$ and $F(1-|t|)=F(2-R/s)=F\bigl((2s-R)/2^n\bigr)$.  In both cases the argument is
$C/2^n$ with $C$ as in \eqref{eq:dy-triangular-fold}, and $0\le C\le s$ is immediate from
$0\le R<2s$.  The final statement is the vanishing of $\FabiusGlobal$ on the negative half-line.
\end{proof}

Combining \eqref{eq:dy-triangular} with Algorithm~\ref{alg:dyadic-evaluator} gives a total exact
evaluator for $\FabiusGlobal$ at every dyadic rational: reduce with
\eqref{eq:dy-triangular-split}--\eqref{eq:dy-triangular-fold}, compute the block sign
$\varepsilon_B=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(B)}$ from the binary digits of $B=\Floor{a/2^{n+1}}$, and call
$\operatorname{Eval}(n,C)$.

The reduction is compatible with unreduced presentations.  Replacing $(n,a)$ by $(n+1,2a)$
doubles $s$ and $R$, leaves $B$ unchanged, and doubles $C$; the right side of
\eqref{eq:dy-triangular} is therefore unchanged, as it must be.

\begin{remark}[Recognizing dyadic arguments exactly]
\label{rem:dy-rational-interface}
A reduced rational $p/q$ with $q>0$ is dyadic exactly when $q$ is a power of two, and in
that case $n=\log_2q$ and $a=p$ are read off directly.  An exact rational interface to
$F$, $\RvachevUp$, and $\FabiusGlobal$ therefore tests a single integer condition on the reduced
denominator, returns ``not dyadic'' when it fails, and otherwise invokes the signed integer
evaluator above.  No floating-point recognition of powers of two is involved anywhere, so
the interface is exact on every input and cannot be defeated by rounding in the recognition
step.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Denominator bounds}

Let
\begin{equation}
 M_m=\prod_{j=1}^{m}(2^{2j}-1),
 \qquad (2m+1)!!=1\cdot3\cdots(2m+1).                     \label{eq:denominator-products}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform denominator multiplier]
Let $n\ge1$ and $a\in\IntegerNumbers$ with $|a|<2^n$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(a/2^n)\;n!\,2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}
 \bigl(2\Floor{n/2}+1\bigr)!!\,
 M_{\Floor{n/2}}                                          \label{eq:denominator-bound}
\end{equation}
is an integer.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is \lean{rvachevDyadic_mul_denominatorBound_isNatural_all} in
\path{DenominatorBound.lean}.  Its proof uses
\lean{moment_mul_common_denominator} for the moments and
\lean{fabiusDyadic_mul_denominatorBound_isInteger} for the bounded dyadic values.
\end{proof}

Let $\Delta_n$ be the least common multiple of the reduced denominators of the values
$\RvachevUp(a/2^n)$ with odd $a$ in the support.  The theorem gives the explicit divisibility bound
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_n\mid n!\,2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}
 \bigl(2\Floor{n/2}+1\bigr)!!M_{\Floor{n/2}}.            \label{eq:lcm-bound}
\end{equation}
A complementary lower statement is also formalized.

\begin{proposition}[The reciprocal power is one of the samples]
\label{prop:dy-delta-lower}
For every $n\ge1$ the reduced denominator of $F(2^{-n})$ divides $\Delta_n$.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \TwoAdicValuation(\Delta_n)\ge\binom n2+1+\TwoAdicValuation(n!).                  \label{eq:dy-delta-lower}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The denominator divisibility is \lean{proposition_fifteen} in
\path{PaperStatements.lean}, after the bridge
\lean{dyadicDenominator_eq_fabiusDyadicDenominator}.  The exact two-adic exponent is
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_padicVal_two_closed}.
\end{proof}

Together, \eqref{eq:lcm-bound} and \eqref{eq:dy-delta-lower} give the formally
proved denominator information used in this article.  Proposed exact factorizations of the
least common denominator, together with the associated numerical tables, are kept in the
consolidated research-frontier volume until their individual statements are formalized.

\subsection{The exact half-base \texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-binomial layer}
\label{subsec:q-binomial-dyadic}
\label{sec:qs-half-base-calculus}

The dyadic dilation forces the specialization \(q=1/2\).  For \(a,q\in\RationalNumbers\), define
\begin{equation}
 (a;q)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^j),                          \label{eq:qpochhammer}
\end{equation}
and write \(\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}\) for the Gaussian coefficient at that fixed base.  For
\(k\le n\), its exact Mersenne normalization is
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}
 =
 \frac{\mathfrak m_n}
 {\mathfrak m_k\mathfrak m_{n-k}\,2^{k(n-k)}},
 \qquad
 \mathfrak m_j=\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}(2^\ell-1).                 \label{eq:qs-qbinom-def}
\end{equation}
In particular the row is symmetric under \(k\mapsto n-k\).

\begin{theorem}[Finite half-base \(q\)-binomial theorem]
\label{thm:qs-finite-q-binomial}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(z\in\RationalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 (z;1/2)_n=
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}
 \GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}z^k.                                        \label{eq:half-q-binomial-theorem}
\end{equation}
At every dyadic node this gives the single closed evaluation
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}
 \GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}(2^m)^k
 =
 \begin{cases}
  0,&m<n,\\[2pt]
  (-1)^n\mathfrak m_m/\mathfrak m_{m-n},&n\le m.
 \end{cases}                                                \label{eq:qs-moment-annihilation}
\end{equation}
In particular, the sum vanishes if and only if \(m<n\).
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 (1/2;1/2)_n=2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}\mathfrak m_n.              \label{eq:half-q-mersenne}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
These are \lean{halfQBinomial_theorem},
\lean{qBinomial_half_two_pow_sum_eq_ite},
\lean{qBinomial_half_two_pow_sum_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{halfQPochhammer_eq_mersenne_div},
\lean{halfQBinomial_eq_mersenne}, and
\lean{halfQBinomial_symm} in \path{HalfQBinomial.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The denominator-free finite $q$-binomial theorem]
The half-base identity is a specialization of a ring-valued statement that does not
divide by a $q$-Pochhammer symbol.  For a commutative ring $R$ and $q\in R$, define
the polynomial Gaussian coefficients recursively by
\[
 (z;q)_0=1,\qquad (z;q)_{n+1}=(z;q)_n(1-zq^n),
\]
and
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k+1}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+1}{q}
   +q^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},\qquad
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{0}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{n}{q}=1,\qquad
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=0\quad(n<k).
\]
The recursive triangle is already defined over every semiring and is
functorial: for semirings \(R,S\) and every homomorphism \(\varphi:R\to S\),
\begin{equation}
 \varphi\!\left(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\right)=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{\varphi(q)}.
                                                               \label{eq:q-gaussian-map}
\end{equation}
Over a commutative semiring it also satisfies, for all natural \(n,k\), the
second Pascal orientation
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k+1}{q}
 =q^{k+1}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+1}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},                    \label{eq:q-pascal-alt}
\end{equation}
and, whenever \(k\le n\), the reflection law
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{n-k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.                            \label{eq:q-gaussian-symmetry}
\end{equation}
None of these structural laws uses division or cancellation; the zero extension above
the diagonal is what makes \eqref{eq:q-pascal-alt} valid without an index
side condition.

Then for every $z\in R$,
\begin{equation}
 (z;q)_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}z^k.
 \label{eq:finite-q-binomial-ring}
\end{equation}
The recursive coefficient already makes sense over an arbitrary semiring, while the
displayed subtraction requires a commutative ring.  No denominator, nonzero assumption,
or exclusion of $q=0$ and roots of unity occurs.  The recurrence, triangular boundaries,
and \eqref{eq:finite-q-binomial-ring} are the definition
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn}, its boundary and recurrence
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_zero} and \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_succ}, the
coefficient definition \lean{gaussianBinomial}, and the theorems
\lean{gaussianBinomial_zero_zero},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_zero_succ},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_succ_succ},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_zero_right},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_eq_zero_of_lt},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_self}, and
\lean{finite_qBinomial_theorem} in \path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean}.
The functoriality, alternate Pascal, and symmetry statements
\eqref{eq:q-gaussian-map}--\eqref{eq:q-gaussian-symmetry} are
\lean{map_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_succ_succ_alt}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_symm}, respectively, in the same module.

The companion \path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean} has no public definitions and exactly
four public theorems.  For a unit \(q\) in any commutative semiring,
\begin{equation}
 q^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
                                                               \label{eq:q-gaussian-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
is \lean{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity_units}; the statement is total in \(n,k\), since
the recursive Gaussian coefficients on both sides vanish when \(n<k\).  The theorem
\lean{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity} is exactly the nonzero-\(q\) wrapper over an arbitrary
semifield.  Over every commutative ring,
\lean{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_eq_zero_of_odd_degree} proves
\(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{-1}=0\) when \(k(n-k)\) is odd, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero} proves the total specialization
\(\GaussianBinomial{2a}{2b+1}{-1}=0\), including the above-diagonal cases.  These are finite
algebraic identities: they use no quotient formula, cancellation, integral-domain,
characteristic, topology, or convergence hypothesis.

The downstream \path{GaussianBinomialPalindromic.lean} has no public
definitions and exactly fourteen public theorems:
\lean{reflect_add_of_natDegree_le}, \lean{reflect_one'},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_natDegree_le},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_zero_left}, \lean{gaussianBinomial_diag'},
\lean{reflect_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_reflect},
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_zero},
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_top},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_natDegree}, \lean{gaussianBinomial_monic},
\lean{two_mul_derivative_gaussianBinomial_eval_one},
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_one_of_pos_of_lt}, and
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_one}.  Over every commutative
semiring the module supplies the two generic reflection helpers, the Gaussian
degree bound, zero and diagonal values, reflection in degree \(k(n-k)\),
constant and top coefficients one, bounded-index coefficient reversal, and
the division-free derivative-at-one mean identity
\[
 2\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{X}'\big|_{X=1}
 = k(n-k)\binom nk .
\]
The interior linear coefficient theorem assumes exactly \(0<k<n\) and gives
coefficient one.  The total theorem classifies it as
\[
 [X^1]\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{X}
 =\begin{cases}1,&0<k<n,\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
\]
thereby covering \(k=0\), \(k=n\), and \(n<k\).  Both results hold over every
commutative semiring, without nontriviality; exact degree and monicity alone
require the semiring to be nontrivial.

The companion \path{GaussianBinomialPolynomialStructure.lean} has no public
definitions and exactly five public theorems:
\lean{natDegree_gaussianBinomial_universal},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_universal_monic},
\lean{coeff_zero_gaussianBinomial_universal},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_universal_reflect}, and
\lean{coeff_gaussianBinomial_universal_symm}.  For \(k\le n\), these state that
the universal natural-coefficient polynomial
\(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{X}\in\NaturalNumbers[X]\) has exact natural degree
\(k(n-k)\), is monic, has constant coefficient one, and is fixed by reflection
in that degree.  Under the additional bound \(d\le k(n-k)\), the last theorem
identifies the coefficients in degrees \(d\) and \(k(n-k)-d\).  Together with
the row symmetry and reciprocal identities above, this supplies the universal
natural-coefficient-polynomial form of the same structure theorem.  The
adjacent fourteen-theorem module supplies the complete linear-coefficient
classifier over every commutative semiring.

Two further source-only modules have exhaustive finite-algebra inventories.
\path{CentralQBinomialReduction.lean} has no definitions and six theorems:
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_mul_neg}, \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_two_mul},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_map_ringHom},
\lean{central_gaussianBinomial_sq_mul_int},
\lean{central_gaussianBinomial_sq_mul}, and
\lean{central_gaussianBinomial_sq_div}.  They give sign pairing, even--odd
dissection, naturality, the integral certificate and division-free central
squared-base reduction over commutative rings, and its field quotient under
the two displayed nonvanishing hypotheses.
\path{CyclotomicFactorization.lean} has no definitions and seven theorems:
\lean{div_add_div_le_div}, \lean{div_le_div_add_div_add_one},
\lean{mem_range_and_mem_divisors_iff},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_X_eq_prod_cyclotomic},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_X_eq_gaussianBinomial_mul},
\lean{prod_cyclotomic_pow_div_extend}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_X_eq_prod_cyclotomic}.  These bound the cyclotomic
exponents by zero and one, reorganize divisor indices, factor the finite
q-product over commutative rings, and give the final Gaussian factorization
over an integral domain.

Four further modules supply the exhaustive root-of-unity and q-Catalan layer.
The zero-definition/three-theorem \path{PrimitiveRootBlock.lean} surface is
\lean{gaussianBinomial_isPrimitiveRoot_eq_zero},
\lean{neg_one_pow_mul_pow_choose_two}, and
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_isPrimitiveRoot}.  In a commutative integral domain,
a primitive \(d\)-th root \(\zeta\) makes
\(\GaussianBinomial{d}{k}{\zeta}=0\) for \(0<k<d\); for \(d>0\), its top phase is
\((-1)^d\zeta^{\binom d2}=-1\), and consequently
\((y;\zeta)_d=1-y^d\).

The zero-definition/seven-theorem \path{QLucas.lean} surface is
\lean{add_mul_add_sub_one},
\lean{choose_two_add}, \lean{coeff_finiteQPochhammerIn_neg_X},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_neg_X_block}, \lean{coeff_block_pow_mul},
\lean{pow_choose_two_add_mul_eq}, and \lean{gaussianBinomial_q_lucas}.
The first two are natural-number quadratic identities.  The polynomial
coefficient and block formulas compare the finite q-binomial expansion with
complete primitive-root blocks.  If \(d>0\), \(\zeta\) is a primitive
\(d\)-th root in a commutative integral domain, and \(b,s<d\), the final theorem is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{ad+b}{rd+s}{\zeta}
   =\binom ar\GaussianBinomial{b}{s}{\zeta}.
\]
Its same-shaped \path{two_mul_choose_two} helper is private; the unique public
\lean{two_mul_choose_two} belongs to \path{QChuVandermonde.lean}.

The terminating summation layer consists of
\path{TwoPhiOneReversal.lean} (2+12) and
\path{QChuVandermonde.lean} (0+10).  It proves the finite-to-tsum bridge,
reflected-parameter involution, one- and two-step reversals, both q-Chu
evaluations, full-domain actual-tsum wrappers, and a separately named
by-reversal route.  The displayed q-Chu formulas and reversal lemma are exact
under their displayed rational/nonvanishing hypotheses.  The claim that
reversal alone yields the second formula on the full displayed domain remains
partial: the named by-reversal theorem additionally assumes \(C\ne0\) and
\((A;q)_n\ne0\), whereas the full-domain theorem uses the direct
denominator-cleared q-Cauchy route.  Rational continuation and a cleared
commutative-ring extension remain unformalized.

The zero-definition/four-theorem \path{JacobiTwoSquareCount.lean} leaf proves
\lean{sumSqRep_two_eq_four_mul_twoSquareDivisorSum} for every nonzero natural
input and its prime-product form under the explicit even-valuation condition
at primes congruent to three modulo four.  Its
\lean{theta_sq_eq_chi4_lambert} and \lean{theta_sq_eq_odd_lambert} theorems
instantiate the reusable \path{TwoSquareTheorem.lean} kernels over every
complete normed field under the sole analytic hypothesis \(\lVert q\rVert<1\).

The zero-definition/three-theorem \path{CyclotomicDivisibility.lean} surface is
\lean{cyclotomic_exponent_eq_one_iff},
\lean{cyclotomic_dvd_gaussianBinomial_iff}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_mul_isPrimitiveRoot}.  For \(k\le n\) and \(d>0\), the
Gaussian cyclotomic exponent equals one exactly when
\(n\bmod d<k\bmod d\); over \(\RationalNumbers[X]\) this is exactly the
criterion for \(\Phi_d\) to divide \(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{X}\).  In a
commutative integral domain, a primitive \(n\)-th root with \(n>0\) also gives
\(\GaussianBinomial{an}{bn}{\zeta}=\binom ab\).

Finally, \path{QCatalan.lean} has the single noncomputable definition
\lean{qCatalan} and exactly eleven theorems:
\lean{map_qInt}, \lean{qInt_X_monic}, \lean{qInt_X_natDegree},
\lean{X_sub_one_mul_qInt}, \lean{qInt_X_eq_prod_cyclotomic},
\lean{qInt_X_dvd_gaussianBinomial_rat},
\lean{qInt_X_dvd_gaussianBinomial_int},
\lean{qInt_X_mul_qCatalan}, \lean{qCatalan_natDegree},
\lean{qCatalan_eval_one_mul}, and \lean{qCatalan_eval_one}.  The semiring-level
naturality theorem and commutative-ring q-integer formulas lead to
\([n+1]_X\mid\GaussianBinomial{2n}{n}{X}\) over both
\(\RationalNumbers[X]\) and \(\IntegerNumbers[X]\).  Thus the integral polynomial
\[
 C_n(X)=\frac{\GaussianBinomial{2n}{n}{X}}{[n+1]_X}
\]
has natural degree \(n(n-1)\), satisfies
\((n+1)C_n(1)=\binom{2n}{n}\), and evaluates to the ordinary Catalan number.

Four denominator-free product identities make this recursion especially useful downstream.
For natural \(m,n\), and for \(k\le n\),
\begin{align}
 (a;q)_{n+1}&=(1-a)(aq;q)_n,                              \label{eq:qpochhammer-shift}\\
 (a;q)_{m+n}&=(a;q)_m(aq^m;q)_n,                         \label{eq:qpochhammer-concat}\\
 (q;q)_k\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=(q^{n-k+1};q)_k,                 \label{eq:qfactorial-gaussian}\\
 (q;q)_n&=(q;q)_k(q;q)_{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.              \label{eq:qfactorial-full}
\end{align}
They are \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_succ_shift},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_add},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_self_mul_gaussianBinomial}, and
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_self_eq_mul_mul_gaussianBinomial}, respectively, again in
\path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean}.  All four hold over every commutative ring,
including empty products, zero divisors, positive characteristic, \(q=0\), and
roots of unity.  The final identity is the full denominator-free replacement
for the usual quotient definition of a Gaussian coefficient.

The companion \path{QPochhammerElementaryIdentities.lean} has exactly thirteen
public theorems.  Its commutative-ring layer consists of the two unit-valued
base reversals
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_base_reversal_units} and
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_base_reversal_units}, the root-safe terminating
numerator \lean{prod_pow_sub_pow_eq_finiteQPochhammerIn}, the first-column
clearer \lean{one_sub_mul_gaussianBinomial_one}, and the two adjacent cross
identities \lean{gaussianBinomial_adjacent_mul} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_row_adjacent_mul}.  In human notation these identities
are
\begin{align*}
 \prod_{j<k}(q^N-q^j)
   &=(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}(q^{N-k+1};q)_k \qquad(k\le N),\\
 (1-q^{k+1})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+1}{q}
   &=(1-q^{n-k})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},\\
 (1-q^{n-k})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
   &=(1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}.
\end{align*}
These formulas remain valid at roots of unity; the first also permits zero
divisors and \(q=0\), while the last two are total for all natural \(n,k\).
On and above the row boundary, zero extension makes both sides of each
adjacent identity vanish.

The seven field declarations are
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_base_reversal},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_base_reversal},
\lean{pow_mul_finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq_self_div},
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq_zero_of_lt},
\lean{gaussianBinomial_adjacent_div}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_row_adjacent_div}.  Both numerical base-reversal
orientations require exactly \(a\ne0\) and \(q\ne0\).  For \(k\le N\), the
cleared terminating identity needs only \(q\ne0\); the quotient
\[
 (q^{-N};q)_k
 =\frac{(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}(q;q)_N}
        {q^{Nk}(q;q)_{N-k}}
\]
additionally requires \((q;q)_{N-k}\ne0\), while the vanishing for \(k>N\)
again needs only \(q\ne0\).  For \(k<n\), the adjacent quotient corollaries
require, respectively, \(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\ne0\) and \(1-q^{k+1}\ne0\), or
\(\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}\ne0\) and \(1-q^{n-k}\ne0\); neither assumes \(q\ne0\).

The four-theorem module \path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean} supplies the exact
palindromic counterpart.  Its unit-valued theorem
\lean{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity_units} holds in every commutative semiring,
including semirings with zero divisors, and is total in both indices:
\[
 q^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.
\]
The wrapper \lean{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity} assumes a semifield and only
\(q\ne0\).  The remaining two theorems,
\lean{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_eq_zero_of_odd_degree} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero}, show over every commutative
ring that odd Gaussian degree forces vanishing at \(q=-1\), and in particular
\(\GaussianBinomial{2a}{2b+1}{-1}=0\).  The latter is total above the row as well.  These
proofs use neither a q-Pochhammer quotient nor domain cancellation.

Over the rationals, \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_rat_eq} identifies the generic product
with the established finite \(q\)-Pochhammer notation.  At $q=1/2$,
\lean{gaussianBinomial_half_eq_halfQBinomial} identifies the recursive coefficient
with the established rational quotient, and
\lean{halfQBinomial_theorem} is now the direct specialization of the ring theorem.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Denominator-free \(q\)-Vandermonde and shifted central forms]
\label{thm:q-vandermonde-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative semiring, let \(q\in R\), and let all displayed
unsigned indices be natural numbers.  Extend a Gaussian row to integer lower
indices by
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal G_q(n,t)=
 \begin{cases}\GaussianBinomial{n}{t}{q},&0\le t,\\0,&t<0,\end{cases}
 \qquad t\in\IntegerNumbers,                                      \label{eq:q-gaussian-int}
\end{equation}
where the natural coefficient already vanishes for \(n<t\).  This extension
has the total reflection law
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal G_q(n,n-t)=\mathcal G_q(n,t)\qquad(t\in\IntegerNumbers). \label{eq:q-gaussian-int-symm}
\end{equation}
The two zero-extended Vandermonde orientations are
\begin{align}
 \GaussianBinomial{m+n}{r}{q}
 &=\sum_{j=0}^{r}q^{(m-j)(r-j)}
   \GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r-j}{q},                         \label{eq:q-vandermonde-first}\\
 &=\sum_{j=0}^{r}q^{j\,(n-(r-j))}
   \GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r-j}{q}.                         \label{eq:q-vandermonde-second}
\end{align}
Here subtraction is natural subtraction; outside the admissible support one
of the zero-extended Gaussian coefficients vanishes.  Consequently no
condition \(r\le m+n\) is needed.  The central specialization is
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{m+n}{m}{q}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{m}q^{j^2}\GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}.       \label{eq:q-vandermonde-central}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, removing exactly the zero-extended terms gives the literal
Durfee support
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{m+n}{m}{q}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{\min(m,n)}q^{j^2}\GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}.
                                                               \label{eq:q-vandermonde-central-min}
\end{equation}
For every \(N,k\in\NaturalNumbers\), including \(N<k\), the positive shifted form is
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{2N}{N+k}{q}
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}q^{\ell(\ell+k)}
   \GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell}{q}\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell+k}{q}.                    \label{eq:q-vandermonde-shift-plus}
\end{equation}
If \(k\le N\), row reflection gives the same right side for
\(\GaussianBinomial{2N}{N-k}{q}\), and its literal-support form is
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{2N}{N-k}{q}
 =\sum_{\ell=k}^{N}q^{\ell(\ell-k)}
   \GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell}{q}\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell-k}{q}.                    \label{eq:q-vandermonde-shift-minus}
\end{equation}
Most strongly, for every \(k\in\IntegerNumbers\), put \(t^\natural=\max(t,0)\in\NaturalNumbers\).
Then the two natural cases combine into the literal finite-support integer sum
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal G_q(2N,N+k)
 =\sum_{\ell\in\IntegerNumbers}
   q^{\ell^\natural(\ell+k)^\natural}
   \mathcal G_q(N,\ell)\mathcal G_q(N,\ell+k).           \label{eq:q-vandermonde-shift-int}
\end{equation}
Only \(0\le\ell\le N\) can contribute; a nonzero term also has
\(0\le\ell+k\le N\), so its exponent is exactly
\(\ell(\ell+k)=\ell^2+\ell k\), as in the signed report formula.  In
particular, both sides vanish automatically when \(\lvert k\rvert>N\).
No identity in this theorem uses division, cancellation, a nonvanishing
hypothesis, or an exclusion of \(q=0\), roots of unity, positive
characteristic, or zero divisors.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
Equations \eqref{eq:q-vandermonde-first} and
\eqref{eq:q-vandermonde-second} are
\lean{gaussianBinomial_add_vandermonde} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_add_vandermonde'}.
The definition \eqref{eq:q-gaussian-int}, its cast, lower-zero, upper-zero,
and total-reflection laws are respectively
\lean{gaussianBinomialInt}, \lean{gaussianBinomialInt_ofNat},
\lean{gaussianBinomialInt_eq_zero_of_neg},
\lean{gaussianBinomialInt_eq_zero_of_lt}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomialInt_symm} in \path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean}.
The full-range and literal-\(\min(m,n)\) central identities are
\lean{gaussianBinomial_add_central} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_add_central_min}; the positive shift is
\lean{gaussianBinomial_two_mul_add_shifted_central}; and the two
negative-shift presentations are
\lean{gaussianBinomial_two_mul_sub_shifted_central} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_two_mul_sub_shifted_central_Icc}.  The total signed
finite-range and integer-sum forms are
\lean{gaussianBinomial_two_mul_int_shifted_central} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_two_mul_int_shifted_central_finsum}.  These nine
theorems exhaust the public surface of \path{QBinomialVandermonde.lean}.
The three natural-shift theorems retain their stated domains, while the last
two are total in \(k\in\IntegerNumbers\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted subsets and scaled $q$-binomial inversion]
\label{thm:q-binomial-inversion-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring and \(q,s\in R\); the first identity below
already holds when \(R\) is only a commutative semiring.  For
\(j\le k\le n\), the Gaussian chain identity is
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{k}{j}{q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n-j}{k-j}{q}.                     \label{eq:q-gaussian-chain}
\end{equation}
For every \(m,n\in\NaturalNumbers\), the alternating row specializes exactly to
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2+mk}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
   &=(q^m;q)_n,                                         \label{eq:q-alternating-general}\\
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
   &=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\0,&n>0.\end{cases}          \label{eq:q-alternating-delta}
\end{align}
The same finite product has the two exact weighted-subset faces
\begin{align}
 \sum_{\substack{T\subseteq\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\\|T|=r}}
   q^{\sum_{t\in T}t}
   &=q^{\binom r2}\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{q},                        \label{eq:q-subsets-zero-based}\\
 \sum_{\substack{T\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}\\|T|=r}}
   q^{\sum_{t\in T}t}
   &=q^{\binom{r+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{q}.                  \label{eq:q-subsets-one-based}
\end{align}
Both formulas are total in \(N,r\in\NaturalNumbers\); above the Gaussian diagonal both
sides vanish.

Define total lower-triangular kernels on natural indices by
\begin{align}
 K^{q,s}_{n,k}&=s^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},\label{eq:q-scaled-forward-kernel}\\
 L^{q,s}_{n,k}&=(-s)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.
                                                        \label{eq:q-scaled-inverse-kernel}
\end{align}
Natural subtraction causes no ambiguity above the diagonal because the
Gaussian coefficient is zero there.  With an empty sum when \(n<j\), both
finite convolution orders are the Kronecker delta:
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k\in [j,n]\cap\NaturalNumbers}L^{q,s}_{n,k}K^{q,s}_{k,j}
   &=\delta_{n,j},                                      \label{eq:q-scaled-left-delta}\\
 \sum_{k\in [j,n]\cap\NaturalNumbers}K^{q,s}_{n,k}L^{q,s}_{k,j}
   &=\delta_{n,j}.                                      \label{eq:q-scaled-right-delta}
\end{align}
In particular the scale \(s\) is independent of \(q\) and need not be
nonzero or invertible.

More generally, let \(M\) be an additive commutative monoid and an
\(R\)-module.  For sequences \(a,b:\NaturalNumbers\to M\), put
\begin{equation}
 (\mathcal T_{q,s}a)_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}K^{q,s}_{n,k}\,a_k,
 \qquad
 (\mathcal T^{-1}_{q,s}b)_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}L^{q,s}_{n,k}\,b_k,       \label{eq:q-scaled-module-transforms}
\end{equation}
where multiplication denotes the scalar action on \(M\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal T^{-1}_{q,s}\mathcal T_{q,s}=1,
 \qquad \mathcal T_{q,s}\mathcal T^{-1}_{q,s}=1,
 \qquad
 b=\mathcal T_{q,s}a\ \Longleftrightarrow\
 a=\mathcal T^{-1}_{q,s}b.                              \label{eq:q-scaled-module-inversion}
\end{equation}
At \(s=1\) this is classical \(q\)-binomial inversion, now for
module-valued rather than merely scalar-valued sequences.

There are two useful exact specializations.  The denominator-free geometric
Lagrange numerator in \eqref{eq:geometric-q-numerator} satisfies globally
\begin{equation}
 N_{n,k}(q)=L^{q,q}_{n,k},                               \label{eq:q-geometric-numerator-kernel}
\end{equation}
including \(n<k\), without an in-range hypothesis.  At Gaussian base
\(Q=q^2\) and scale \(s=-q\), put
\begin{equation}
 C'_{n,k}=K^{q^2,-q}_{n,k}
   =(-q)^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^2},
 \qquad
 B_{n,k}=L^{q^2,-q}_{n,k}
   =q^{(n-k)^2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^2}.                        \label{eq:q-gaussian-inverse-kernels}
\end{equation}
The two coefficient definitions and their displayed pointwise closed forms
already hold in an arbitrary, possibly noncommutative ring.  Commutativity is
needed only for the two convolution theorems, under which both total finite
products satisfy \(C'B=BC'=I\).  These identities use no analytic or
nonvanishing assumption.  By contrast, the normalized Lagrange quotient in
\eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-gaussian} retains its separate field,
finite-node injectivity, and displayed in-range hypotheses.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The eight public theorems of \path{BitPositionQBinomial.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{prod_one_add_mul_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{prod_one_add_pow_eq_sum_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{sum_powersetCard_two_pow},
\lean{sum_pow_bitPositionSum_filter_eq_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{sum_pow_bitPositionSum_filter_eq_gaussianBinomial'},
\lean{sum_pow_sum_powersetCard_eq_gaussianBinomial},
\lean{sum_pow_sum_powersetCard_Icc_eq_gaussianBinomial}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_one_eq_choose}.  The sixth and seventh declarations
are \eqref{eq:q-subsets-zero-based} and
\eqref{eq:q-subsets-one-based}.

The four definitions and seven theorems of
\path{QBinomialInversion.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{gaussianBinomial_mul},
\lean{sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating_mul_pow},
\lean{sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating},
\lean{gaussianBinomialKernel},
\lean{gaussianBinomialInverseKernel},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialKernel},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel},
\lean{gaussianBinomialKernel_left_orthogonality},
\lean{gaussianBinomialKernel_right_orthogonality},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialKernel_left_orthogonality}, and
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialKernel_right_orthogonality}.  They prove
\eqref{eq:q-gaussian-chain},
\eqref{eq:q-alternating-general}--\eqref{eq:q-alternating-delta}, and both
unscaled and scaled forms of
\eqref{eq:q-scaled-left-delta}--\eqref{eq:q-scaled-right-delta}.

The one definition and one theorem of
\path{FiniteTriangularTransform.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{lowerTriangularTransform} and \lean{lowerTriangularTransform_comp}.
For a possibly noncommutative semiring acting on an additive commutative
monoid, the theorem turns a total ordered convolution
\(\sum_{k\in[j,n]}K_{n,k}L_{k,j}=\delta_{n,j}\) into equality of the whole
composite sequence function with the input.  Its hypothesis also covers
\(n<j\), when the interval is empty.  The refactored Gaussian proof below
reuses this generic finite-sum theorem; the Gaussian kernels' zero extension
above the row supplies the same total convention.

The four definitions and four theorems of \path{QBinomialTransform.lean} are,
exhaustively,
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialTransform},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialInverseTransform},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialInverseTransform_transform},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomialTransform_inverseTransform},
\lean{scaledGaussianBinomial_inversion},
\lean{gaussianBinomialTransform},
\lean{gaussianBinomialInverseTransform}, and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_inversion}.  They prove
\eqref{eq:q-scaled-module-transforms}--\eqref{eq:q-scaled-module-inversion}
as whole-function equalities.  The two forward-transform definitions need
only a semiring acting on an additive commutative monoid; the two signed
inverse definitions need a ring with the same target assumptions.  The four
inversion theorems require a commutative ring, again acting on an additive
commutative monoid.  No additive inverses are required in the target.

The global bridge
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel}
belongs to \path{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean}.  It proves
\eqref{eq:q-geometric-numerator-kernel} for every pair of natural indices,
including above the diagonal, and makes the geometric numerator the
scale-\(q\) specialization of the characteristic polynomial in
\cref{thm:q-difference-annihilation-formal}.

Finally, the two definitions and four theorems of
\path{QBinomialInversionSpecializations.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{qGaussianResidualCoeff},
\lean{qGaussianReconstructionCoeff},
\lean{qGaussianResidualCoeff_eq},
\lean{qGaussianReconstructionCoeff_eq},
\lean{qGaussianReconstructionCoeff_residualCoeff_delta}, and
\lean{qGaussianResidualCoeff_reconstructionCoeff_delta}.  These are
the two closed forms in \eqref{eq:q-gaussian-inverse-kernels} and both
convolution orders.  The two definitions and the two pointwise equality
theorems require only \texttt{[Ring R]}; exactly the two convolution-delta
theorems require \texttt{[CommRing R]}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Scaled Gaussian characteristic and exact \(q\)-difference annihilation]
\label{thm:q-difference-annihilation-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring and let \(q,s,z\in R\).  With the total
inverse Gaussian row from \eqref{eq:q-scaled-inverse-kernel},
\[
 L^{q,s}_{n,k}=(-s)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},
\]
one has the homogeneous, denominator-free characteristic identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}L^{q,s}_{n,k}z^k
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(z-sq^j).                           \label{eq:q-scaled-characteristic}
\end{equation}
In particular, taking \(s=1\) and \(z=q^d\) gives every monomial moment:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}L^{q,1}_{n,k}(q^k)^d
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(q^d-q^j).                         \label{eq:q-difference-moments}
\end{equation}
The product contains the zero factor indexed by \(j=d\) whenever \(d<n\).

More generally, let \(A\) be a semiring, let
\(\varphi\colon A\to R\) be a semiring homomorphism, and let \(P\in A[X]\).
Write \(P^\varphi\in R[X]\) for coefficientwise scalar extension.  If
\(\deg P\le n\), then the row extracts the mapped top coefficient with its
exact surviving moment:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}L^{q,1}_{n,k}P^\varphi(q^k)
 =\varphi([X^n]P)\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(q^n-q^j).          \label{eq:q-difference-top-coeff}
\end{equation}
If instead \(\deg P<n\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}L^{q,1}_{n,k}P^\varphi(q^k)=0.          \label{eq:q-difference-annihilation}
\end{equation}
All four statements include \(n=0\) and the zero polynomial.  The geometric
nodes may collide and the surviving product in
\eqref{eq:q-difference-top-coeff} may itself vanish.  No division,
distinctness, nonzero or invertible base, domain, characteristic, topology,
or convergence assumption is present.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The public surface of \path{QDifferenceAnnihilation.lean} consists
exhaustively of four theorems:
\lean{sum_scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel_mul_pow},
\lean{sum_gaussianBinomialInverseKernel_mul_geometric_pow},
\texttt{qDifference\_sum\_eval}\textsubscript{2}\allowbreak
\texttt{\_eq\_map\_coeff\_mul}, and
\texttt{qDifference\_sum\_eval}\textsubscript{2}\allowbreak
\texttt{\_eq\_zero\_of\_degree\_lt}.
They prove, respectively,
\eqref{eq:q-scaled-characteristic}--\eqref{eq:q-difference-annihilation}.
The first two specialize the scaled inverse-kernel algebra over an arbitrary
commutative ring; the last two use the generic finite-polynomial-functional
API to retain semiring coefficients and arbitrary scalar extension into that
ring.  Thus the hypotheses and every boundary case in the statement are
literal formal interfaces, not consequences of an unrecorded
distinct-geometric-node assumption.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite \(q\)-Cauchy identities and the \(q\)-Bernstein partition]
\label{thm:q-cauchy-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring, let \(q,u,v,a,b,c,x\in R\), and let
\(n\in\NaturalNumbers\).  The primary and reflected finite \(q\)-Cauchy identities are
\begin{align}
 (uv;q)_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} (u;q)_k v^k(v;q)_{n-k},
                                                        \label{eq:q-cauchy-formal}\\
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} (v;q)_k v^{n-k}(u;q)_{n-k}.
                                                        \label{eq:q-cauchy-reflected-formal}
\end{align}
Define the denominator-free \(q\)-Bernstein term by
\begin{equation}
 B^{(q)}_{n,k}(x)=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} x^k(x;q)_{n-k}.          \label{eq:q-bernstein-basis}
\end{equation}
Then its full row is a partition of unity,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}B^{(q)}_{n,k}(x)=1.                       \label{eq:q-bernstein-unity}
\end{equation}
Finally, the second finite Cauchy convolution is
\begin{equation}
 (ac;q)_n(bc;q)_n
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 (a;q)_k(b;q)_k c^k(c;q)_{n-k}(abcq^k;q)_{n-k}.          \label{eq:q-cauchy-second-formal}
\end{equation}
All four identities are total in the displayed parameters and degree.  They
include \(n=0\), \(q=0\), roots of unity, positive characteristic, and zero
divisors, and use no quotient, cancellation, domain, or nonvanishing
hypothesis.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exhaustive API]
Equations \eqref{eq:q-cauchy-formal} and
\eqref{eq:q-cauchy-reflected-formal} are
\lean{finite_qCauchy_identity} and
\lean{finite_qCauchy_identity_reflected}.  The first also has the compatibility
spelling \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_mul_eq_sum_gaussianBinomial};
\eqref{eq:q-bernstein-basis} is the definition
\lean{qBernsteinBasis}; \eqref{eq:q-bernstein-unity} is
\lean{sum_qBernsteinBasis}; and \eqref{eq:q-cauchy-second-formal} is
\lean{finite_qCauchy_second_identity}.  These are exhaustively the one public
definition and five public theorems of \path{QBinomialCauchy.lean}.  The
partition of unity is the primary identity at \(u=0\); the second identity is
proved by expanding a finite triangle, reindexing it, and applying both
orientations of finite \(q\)-Cauchy.  Thus the proof never divides by a
\(q\)-Pochhammer factor.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted Pascal and elementary--complete orthogonality]
\label{thm:symmetric-orthogonality-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative semiring and let \(a:\iota\to R\) be a family on a
finite index type.  Write \(e_r(a)\) for the evaluation of the universal
elementary symmetric polynomial and \(h_r(a)\) for the existing complete
homogeneous evaluation.  Then \(e_0(a)=1\), and reindexing by any equivalence
\(e:\kappa\simeq\iota\) leaves the value unchanged:
\begin{equation}
 e_r(a\circ e)=e_r(a).                                   \label{eq:elementary-reindex}
\end{equation}
For a family indexed by a distinguished point and a finite tail, the two
weighted-Pascal laws are
\begin{align}
 e_{r+1}(x,a)&=x e_r(a)+e_{r+1}(a),                       \label{eq:elementary-option-pascal}\\
 h_{r+1}(x,a)&=x h_r(x,a)+h_{r+1}(a).                    \label{eq:complete-option-pascal}
\end{align}
The elementary identity is also exposed on a literal nonempty finite list as
\begin{equation}
 e_{r+1}(a_0,a_1,\ldots,a_m)
 =a_0e_r(a_1,\ldots,a_m)+e_{r+1}(a_1,\ldots,a_m).         \label{eq:elementary-fin-pascal}
\end{equation}
If \(R\) is a commutative ring, then for every degree \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k e_k(a)h_{n-k}(a)
 =\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\0,&n>0.
 \end{cases}                                             \label{eq:elementary-complete-delta}
\end{equation}
This includes the empty family and degree zero and uses no division,
characteristic, domain, or nonvanishing assumption.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exhaustive API]
The definition is \lean{elementarySymmetricEval}; its identification with
Mathlib's evaluated universal \texttt{MvPolynomial.esymm} is
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_eq_eval_esymm}.  The equation \(e_0=1\),
\eqref{eq:elementary-reindex}, \eqref{eq:elementary-option-pascal}, and
\eqref{eq:elementary-fin-pascal} are respectively
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_zero},
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_comp_equiv},
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_option_succ}, and
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_fin_succ}.  Equation
\eqref{eq:elementary-complete-delta} is
\lean{sum_elementarySymmetricEval_mul_completeHomogeneousEval}.  These are
exhaustively the one public definition and six public theorems of
\path{SymmetricFunctionOrthogonality.lean}.  The companion complete recurrence
\eqref{eq:complete-option-pascal} is
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_option_succ} in
\path{CompleteHomogeneous.lean}.  The structural elementary identities and
both Pascal recurrences hold already over commutative semirings; subtraction
