is needed only for the final alternating convolution.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Formal generating series for a finite alphabet]
\label{thm:symmetric-generating-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative semiring and let \(a:\iota\to R\) be indexed by a
finite type.  In the formal power-series ring \(R[[X]]\), set
\[
 E_a(X)=\sum_{r\geq0}e_r(a)X^r,
 \qquad H_a(X)=\sum_{r\geq0}h_r(a)X^r.
\]
Their coefficients are exactly the displayed symmetric-function
evaluations, and
\begin{equation}
 E_a(X)=\prod_{i\in\iota}(1+a_iX).                       \label{eq:elementary-generating-product-formal}
\end{equation}
If \(R\) is a commutative ring, then
\begin{equation}
 E_{-a}(X)H_a(X)=1,\qquad
 H_a(X)=\operatorname{invOfUnit}(E_{-a}(X),1).           \label{eq:symmetric-generating-reciprocal-formal}
\end{equation}
In particular, for every \(q\in R\) and \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n}(1-q^jX)\right)
 \left(\sum_{k\geq0}\GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}X^k\right)=1
 \quad\text{in }R[[X]].                                 \label{eq:q-reciprocal-finite-formal}
\end{equation}
These are formal identities, including the empty alphabet and all degenerate
values of \(q\).  They do not assert evaluation of the series at a scalar or
analytic convergence under \(\lvert a_i z\rvert<1\) or \(\lvert z\rvert<1\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exhaustive API]
The two definitions and six theorems of
\path{SymmetricFunctionGenerating.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries},
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries},
\lean{coeff_elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries},
\lean{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries},
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_eq_prod},
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_neg_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries},
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries_eq_invOfUnit_elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_neg},
and
\lean{prod_one_sub_qPow_X_mul_gaussianBinomialGeneratingSeries}.
The definitions, coefficient identities, and finite elementary product need
only a commutative semiring.  The reciprocity, canonical-inverse, and finite
Gaussian reciprocal identities require a commutative ring.  Thus the Lean
surface proves precisely the formal-power-series halves of the corresponding
analytic formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted symmetric-function inversion]
\label{thm:symmetric-transform-formal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring, let \(M\) be an additive commutative monoid
and an \(R\)-module, and let \(w:\iota\to R\) be indexed by a finite type.
For \(a,b:\NaturalNumbers\to M\), define
\begin{align}
 (\mathcal H_w a)_m&=\sum_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}(w)\,a_j,\\
 (\mathcal E_w b)_m&=\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-j}e_{m-j}(w)\,b_j,
                                                               \label{eq:weighted-symmetric-transforms-formal}
\end{align}
where the products with \(a_j,b_j\) denote scalar actions.  Then, as
equalities of whole sequence functions,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal E_w\mathcal H_w a=a,\qquad
 \mathcal H_w\mathcal E_w b=b,\qquad
 b=\mathcal H_w a\ \Longleftrightarrow\ a=\mathcal E_w b.       \label{eq:weighted-symmetric-inversion-formal}
\end{equation}
Both scalar kernels are extended by zero above the diagonal, so the kernel
orthogonalities are total even when the outer interval is empty.  There is
no division, topology, domain, characteristic, or nonvanishing assumption.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exhaustive API]
The four definitions and five theorems of
\path{SymmetricFunctionTransform.lean} are, exhaustively,
\lean{completeHomogeneousKernel}, \lean{signedElementaryKernel},
\lean{completeHomogeneousKernel_left_orthogonality},
\lean{completeHomogeneousKernel_right_orthogonality},
\lean{completeHomogeneousTransform}, \lean{signedElementaryTransform},
\lean{signedElementaryTransform_completeHomogeneousTransform},
\lean{completeHomogeneousTransform_signedElementaryTransform}, and
\lean{weightedSymmetricFunction_inversion}.  The complete-homogeneous kernel
and transform already need only a commutative semiring.  The signed kernel,
signed transform, both total orthogonalities, both inverse compositions, and
the inversion equivalence use a commutative ring; every module-valued
transform uses only an additive commutative monoid as its target.  The two
composition proofs reuse \lean{lowerTriangularTransform_comp} from the
one-definition, one-theorem generic surface of
\path{FiniteTriangularTransform.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Reversed Gaussian row, Lagrange weights, and all residual moments]
\label{thm:geometric-q-binomial-lagrange}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring and $q\in R$.  The zero-extended numerator row is
\begin{equation}
 N_{n,k}(q)=
 \begin{cases}
 (-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},&k\le n,\\
 0,&n<k.
 \end{cases}                                              \label{eq:geometric-q-numerator}
\end{equation}
The Lean definition is initially written with the reversed index \(n-k\),
but the division-free symmetry \eqref{eq:q-gaussian-symmetry} proves the
forward form displayed here.  In fact the zero-extended identity remains
valid for every pair \(n,k\) when natural subtraction is read with Lean's
truncation convention.  For every $z\in R$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}N_{n,k}(q)z^k
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(z-q^{j+1}),                           \label{eq:reversed-q-binomial}
\end{equation}
and, for every $0\le d\le n$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}N_{n,k}(q)(q^k)^d=(q;q)_n0^d.             \label{eq:geometric-q-numerator-moments}
\end{equation}

Now let $R$ be a field and suppose that the nodes $1,q,\ldots,q^n$ are distinct.
If $\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}$ is the evaluation-at-zero Lagrange weight belonging to $q^k$,
then $(q;q)_n\ne0$ and, for $k\le n$,
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}=\frac{N_{n,k}(q)}{(q;q)_n},
 \qquad
 (q;q)_n\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}=N_{n,k}(q).                  \label{eq:geometric-lagrange-gaussian}
\end{equation}
Finally every positive residual moment has the single closed form
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}(q^k)^d
 =(-1)^nq^{n(n+1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{d-1}{n}{q},
 \qquad d>0.                                             \label{eq:geometric-lagrange-residual}
\end{equation}
All statements include $n=0$.  The field-level formulas impose only distinctness of the
displayed finite node set; they do not add a redundant global ban on $q=0$ or on roots of
unity.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The numerator and its two branches are
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator},
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_of_le}, and
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_zero_of_lt}.
Its forward-index forms on the natural range and globally are
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_forward_of_le} and
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_forward}.
Its global inverse-kernel form is
\lean{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel};
this declaration is owned here by
\path{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean} and proves
\eqref{eq:q-geometric-numerator-kernel}.
Equations \eqref{eq:reversed-q-binomial} and
\eqref{eq:geometric-q-numerator-moments} are
\lean{reversed_finite_qBinomial_theorem} and
\lean{sum_geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_mul_pow}.  Node distinctness gives the
nonvanishing denominator by \lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_self_ne_zero_of_injOn}; the quotient
and cleared-denominator identities in \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-gaussian} are
\lean{geometricLagrangeWeight_eq_gaussianBinomial_div} and
\lean{finiteQPochhammerIn_mul_geometricLagrangeWeight_eq}.  The complete positive-degree
formula \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-residual} is
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial}.  These declarations are
all in \path{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean}; the finite-product identities in the
preceding remark are in \path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean}.
\end{proof}

Here $q$ is the Gaussian base and the ratio of the geometric nodes; it is unrelated to the
additive translation $Q$ in the translated Fabius formulas below.  Because
Theorem~\ref{thm:geometric-q-binomial-lagrange} is base-generic, the explicit
Gaussian--Lagrange identification and its residual moments are no longer research-frontier
obligations at Gaussian base $q=1/4$.  Over rational $0<q<1$,
\path{GeometricLagrangeQBinomial.lean} and
\path{GeometricLagrangeQMoments.lean} additionally express the weights and
all residual moments in the established quotient-defined $q$-Pochhammer and
$q$-binomial notation, prove the residual sign, and compute the exact finite
$\ell^1$ norm of the row.  Their dedicated quarter-base corollaries include
\lean{quarterGeometricLagrangeQMoment_eq_residual_qBinomial} and
\lean{sum_abs_quarterGeometricLagrangeWeight_eq_qPochhammer_ratio}.
The whole geometric Richardson triangle now has the exact formal factorization
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}\left(\sum_{j=0}^n\lambda_{n,j}^{(q)}y_j\right)X^n
  =(qX;q)_\infty\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{y_j}{(q;q)_j}X^j.
\]
The report-facing field-valued statement is
\lean{geometricLagrangeRichardson_generating} in
\path{GeometricRichardsonGenerating.lean}; it assumes only \(q\ne0\) and is an
identity of formal power series, so it makes no analytic convergence claim.
The same module first proves the stronger convolution identity
\lean{geometricRichardsonTransform_generating} over an arbitrary commutative
ring with totalized \path{Ring.inverse}, and its theorem
\lean{geometricRichardsonTransform_eq_sum_lagrange} identifies the coefficients
with the established Lagrange row over a field.  Its analytic companions
\lean{hasSum_geometricRichardsonTransform_mul_pow} and
\lean{hasSum_geometricLagrangeRichardson_mul_pow} require a complete normed
field, strict nome contraction, and absolute summability of the normalized
data series; the Lagrange form also assumes a nonzero nome.  The exhaustive
public surface is the three definitions
\lean{geometricRichardsonKernel},
\lean{qPochhammerNormalizedDataSeries}, and
\lean{geometricRichardsonTransform}, and the seven theorems
\lean{coeff_rescale_qPochhammerSeries_eq_geometricRichardsonKernel},
\lean{coeff_qPochhammerNormalizedDataSeries},
\lean{geometricRichardsonTransform_generating},
\lean{geometricRichardsonTransform_eq_sum_lagrange},
\lean{geometricLagrangeRichardson_generating},
\lean{hasSum_geometricRichardsonTransform_mul_pow}, and
\lean{hasSum_geometricLagrangeRichardson_mul_pow}.
The generic unconditionally summable geometric filter and its exact Gaussian
\(\mathsf{tsum}\) tail are proved in \Cref{prop:analytic-series-filter}.  The finite row
variation, with \(w_q(n,j)\) the $q$-Toeplitz weight
\lean{qToeplitzWeight} from \path{GeneralQConditionNumber.lean}, also has its exact
infinite limit: for every
\(0\le q<1\),
\[
 \sum_{j\le n}\lvert w_q(n,j)\rvert
 \longrightarrow \frac{(-q;q)_\infty}{(q;q)_\infty}.
\]
This is \lean{tendsto_sum_abs_qToeplitzWeight} in
\path{LimitConditionNumber.lean}; the same module proves positivity of the
denominator on the full range and the effective lower bound
\(1/(1-q)\le(-q;q)_\infty/(q;q)_\infty\), and promotes that bound to the
literal divergence theorem
\lean{tendsto_qConditionNumberLimit_atTop_at_one_left}.  The actual infinite
Rvachev sinc-product instantiation, at the quarter base and at a generic
complex squared base under finite-node injectivity, is now exactly
\cref{thm:rvachev-gaussian-filter}.  What remains in the research-frontier
volume is the different finite-prefix problem for \(P_{b,n}\): its quotient
and Bell-coefficient package, the analytic sign and remainder/bracketing
bounds, uniform accelerated and derivative convergence, and asymptotics.

The same finite row acts coefficientwise on every formal power series.  For
$q\in\RationalNumbers$, $p,n\in\NaturalNumbers$, and $D(X)\in\RationalNumbers[[X]]$, define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_{q,p,n}D
 :=\sum_{j=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,j}^{(q)}
      D\!\left(q^{n+j}X\right).                              \label{eq:generic-geometric-formal-filter}
\end{equation}
Then, for every $m\in\NaturalNumbers$, without a node-distinctness assumption,
\begin{equation}
 [X^m]\mathcal R_{q,p,n}D
 =[X^m]D\,q^{nm}
   \sum_{j=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,j}^{(q)}(q^j)^m.                 \label{eq:generic-geometric-filter-coeff}
\end{equation}
If $q\ne0$ and $(q;q)_p\ne0$, the constant coefficient is preserved and
degrees $1,\ldots,p$ vanish.  If moreover $0<q<1$ and $m>p$, then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 [X^m]\mathcal R_{q,p,n}D
 =[X^m]D\,(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}
   \GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q}\,q^{nm}.}                              \label{eq:generic-geometric-filter-qbinomial}
\end{equation}
The filter itself is \lean{geometricRichardsonPowerSeriesFilter} in
\path{QuarterCatalanRichardson.lean}.  Coefficient extraction from a finite
rescaling filter is
\lean{finiteRescaleFilter_coeff}; equations
\eqref{eq:generic-geometric-filter-coeff} and
\eqref{eq:generic-geometric-filter-qbinomial} are
\lean{geometricRichardsonPowerSeriesFilter_coeff} and
\lean{geometricRichardsonPowerSeriesFilter_coeff_eq_qBinomial}.  The constant and
cancelled-degree statements are
\lean{geometricRichardsonPowerSeriesFilter_coeff_zero} and
\lean{geometricRichardsonPowerSeriesFilter_coeff_eq_zero}.  Thus the quarter
Catalan calculation below is a specialization of a reusable filter theorem, not an
isolated coefficient computation.

There is nevertheless a second exact quarter-base result at the level of formal power
series.  Let \(D(Q)=\sum_{m\ge0}\delta_mQ^m\in\RationalNumbers[[Q]]\) be the unique series with
zero constant coefficient satisfying
\begin{equation}
 D(Q)+4D(Q)^2=\frac49Q.                                    \label{eq:quarter-catalan-formal-equation}
\end{equation}
Its positive-degree coefficients are
\begin{equation}
 \delta_{r+1}=(-1)^r C_r\frac{2^{4r+2}}{9^{r+1}},          \label{eq:quarter-catalan-formal-coefficients}
\end{equation}
where \(C_r\) is the \(r\)-th Catalan number.  For the geometric Lagrange weights
\(\lambda_{p,j}^{(1/4)}\) on \(1,4^{-1},\ldots,4^{-p}\), define the coefficientwise
filter
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_{p,n}(Q):=
 \sum_{j=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,j}^{(1/4)}D(4^{-(n+j)}Q).         \label{eq:quarter-catalan-richardson-filter}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Formal Catalan--Gaussian quarter filter]
\label{thm:quarter-catalan-richardson-filter}
For every \(p,n\in\NaturalNumbers\), all coefficients of
\(\mathcal R_{p,n}\) through degree \(p\) vanish.  For \(m>p\),
\begin{equation}
 [Q^m]\mathcal R_{p,n}
 =\delta_m(-1)^p4^{-\binom{p+1}{2}}
   \GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{1/4}\,4^{-nm}.                          \label{eq:quarter-catalan-filter-residual}
\end{equation}
The degree-(p+1) coefficient has the closed form
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 [Q^{p+1}]\mathcal R_{p,n}
 =\frac{C_p\,2^{4p+2}}
 {9^{p+1}4^{\binom{p+1}{2}+n(p+1)}}.}                      \label{eq:quarter-catalan-filter-leading}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The series \(D\), its coefficient formula, its quadratic equation, and its uniqueness are
\lean{quarterCatalanGermSeries},
\lean{quarterCatalanGermSeries_coeff_succ},
\lean{quarterCatalanGermSeries_equation}, and
\lean{existsUnique_quarterCatalanGermSeries} in
\path{QuarterCatalanGerm.lean}.  The filter is
\lean{quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter}.  Cancellation, the complete residual formula, and
the leading coefficient are
\lean{quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_eq_zero_of_le},
\lean{quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_eq_qBinomial}, and
\lean{quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_firstUncancelled_coeff} in
\path{QuarterCatalanRichardson.lean}.

The same formal-germ module also connects this Catalan normalization to the
distinguished dyadic quantile germ.  Its exact scaled functional equation is
\lean{dyadicGermTwo_functionalEquation}; after rescaling the parameter by \(9/4\),
\lean{rescale_dyadicGermTwo_eq_quadraticInverse} identifies it with
\(\lean{QuadraticInverse.inverse}\,4\).
The reverse rescaling and coefficient formula are
\lean{dyadicGermTwo_eq_rescale_quadraticInverse} and
\lean{coeff_dyadicGermTwo_succ}.  In particular the coefficient of degree
\(m+1\) is
\[
 \left(\frac49\right)^{m+1}(-4)^m C_m.
\]

Every assertion here is coefficientwise in \(\RationalNumbers[[Q]]\).  It neither asserts that the
formal germ converges to a real finite-prefix inverse nor supplies a remainder estimate,
sign theorem for a real extrapolation error, or analytic acceleration result.  Those
claims remain distinct from the proved Gaussian cancellation algebra.
\end{proof}

The full \path{QuarterCatalanGerm.lean} surface is two definitions and thirteen
theorems.  Besides the coefficient, equation, uniqueness, and injectivity declarations
used above, its four dyadic-germ bridges are
\lean{dyadicGermTwo_functionalEquation},
\lean{rescale_dyadicGermTwo_eq_quadraticInverse},
\lean{dyadicGermTwo_eq_rescale_quadraticInverse}, and
\lean{coeff_dyadicGermTwo_succ}.  They identify the distinguished rational dyadic
germ, after parameter rescaling by \(9/4\), with the unique Catalan inverse of
\(X+4X^2\), and give every positive coefficient as
\((4/9)^{m+1}(-4)^m C_m\).  This remains formal power-series algebra.

\begin{theorem}[Actual Catalan jet at the quarter inverse anchor]
\label{thm:fabius-inverse-quarter-jet}
Let \(F:\lean{BoundedFabius}\) satisfy \(h_F:\lean{IsFabius}\,F\), and write
\(G(y)=\lean{fabiusInv}\,F\,h_F\,y\).  For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{d^n}{dt^n}
   \left(G\!\left(\frac5{72}+t\right)-\frac14\right)\right|_{t=0}
 =n!\,[X^n]\bigl(\lean{QuadraticInverse.inverse}\,4\bigr).
 \label{eq:fabius-inverse-quarter-jet}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every \(m\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 G^{(m+1)}\!\left(\frac5{72}\right)
 =(m+1)!\,(-4)^m C_m.
 \label{eq:fabius-inverse-quarter-catalan}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Lean proof]
The separate \path{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean} has zero definitions and exactly
two theorems.  They are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_centeredFabiusInv_quarter_eq_quadraticInverse} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusInv_five_seventy_two_succ} in
\path{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean}.  The proof transfers the exact smooth quarter
jet of the forward Fabius function through Faà di Bruno and the local
inverse identity, then applies the scalar convolution recurrence for the
quadratic compositional inverse.  This proves equality of jets only.  It does
not assert equality of the inverse with a quadratic analytic germ on a
neighborhood, introduce a named nonzero flat-remainder theorem, or claim that
the known nonanalytic flat defect vanishes.
\end{proof}

The polynomial in \eqref{eq:half-q-binomial-theorem} has the complete rational root locus
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}z^k=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,j<n\text{ such that }z=2^j,
 \qquad z\in\RationalNumbers.                                             \label{eq:half-q-root-locus}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{qBinomial_half_sum_eq_zero_iff}.  The same statement is stable under scalar
extension at embedded rational arguments.  Let \(K\) be a field with a
\(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra structure, and write \(\iota:\RationalNumbers\to K\) for its canonical map.  Then, for
every \(z\in\RationalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \iota\!\left((-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}\right)(\iota z)^k=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,j<n\text{ such that }\iota z=2^j\text{ in }K.     \label{eq:half-q-scalar-root-locus}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{qBinomial_half_sum_algebraMap_eq_zero_iff} in
\path{HalfQBinomial.lean}.  The scalar statement classifies the embedded rational
arguments \(\iota z\); it does not assert a root classification for arbitrary elements of
\(K\).

The multiplicity layer is exact as well.  Over \(\RationalNumbers\), set
\[
 p_n(X):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}
   \lean{halfQBinomial}\,n\,k\,X^k.
\]
For every \(j<n\),
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{rootMultiplicity}_{p_n}(2^j)=1.
 \label{eq:half-q-root-simplicity}
\end{equation}
This is the sole public theorem
\lean{halfQBinomial_sum_rootMultiplicity_two_pow} in the zero-definition/
one-theorem module \path{HalfQBinomialRootSimplicity.lean}.  Its proof
differentiates the finite q-Pochhammer product: at \(2^j\), every product-rule
term vanishes except the one in which the \(j\)-th factor was deleted, and all
remaining factors are nonzero.  Together with
\lean{halfQBinomial_sum_eq_zero_iff} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_half_eq_halfQBinomial}, this completes both ``simple''
and ``no others'' in the canonical \nolinkurl{cor:halfbase-root-locus}, which
is therefore Exact.  The theorem is deliberately over \(\RationalNumbers\),
matching the coefficient and root-classification API; no arbitrary-base,
arbitrary-field, or general cyclotomic simplicity is claimed, and the
probabilistic \nolinkurl{cor:qbinom-inversion-law} remains Partial.

The same Gaussian coefficients form a geometric Prouhet functional.  Set
\[
 w_{n,k}=(-1)^k(1/2)^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2},
 \qquad \xi_k=-2^k.
\]
For every rational polynomial \(p\) with \(\deg p\le n\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}w_{n,k}p(\xi_k)
 =[X^n]p\;2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}(1/2;1/2)_n
 =[X^n]p\;\mathfrak m_n.                              \label{eq:half-q-gaussian-prouhet}
\end{equation}
Thus the functional annihilates every polynomial of degree below \(n\).
At \(n=0\), the degree-valued extractor covers all constant polynomials;
the strict-degree cancellation statement admits exactly the zero polynomial.
The two forms of \eqref{eq:half-q-gaussian-prouhet} are
\lean{halfQBinomial_negativeDyadic_polynomial_sum_eq_coeff} and
\lean{halfQBinomial_negativeDyadic_polynomial_sum_eq_mersenne}; strict-degree
cancellation is
\lean{halfQBinomial_negativeDyadic_polynomial_sum_eq_zero_of_degree_lt}, all in
\path{HalfQBinomial.lean}.

The inner cancellation in the dyadic formula is the exact Thue--Morse block identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}z^r
 =\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}(1-z^{2^j}),                              \label{eq:thue-generating-product}
\end{equation}
formalized as \lean{thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eq_product}.  Its affine power-sum form
annihilates every degree below \(k\), while its first surviving moment is
\((-1)^k2^{\binom k2}k!\); see
\lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_of_le} and
\lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_self}.

For comparison with the moment recurrence, put
\begin{equation}
 a_n=\frac{d_n}{n!},\qquad
 \mathscr A(X)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nX^n,\qquad
 E(X)=\frac{\EulerE^X-1}{X},                                     \label{eq:qs-a-and-series}
\end{equation}
with the removable value \(E(0)=1\).  The refinement is
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr A(2X)=E(X)\mathscr A(X),                            \label{eq:q-moment-refinement}
\end{equation}
and its coefficient recurrence is
\begin{equation}
 (2^n-1)a_n=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_j}{(n-j+1)!}
 \quad(n\ge1),\qquad a_0=1.                                 \label{eq:qs-a-recurrence}
\end{equation}
The functional identity follows from \lean{proposition_two_formula} and
\lean{complexGeneratingFunction_eq_series_formula} in \path{AnalyticMoments.lean};
the coefficient recurrence is \lean{halfMoment_original_recurrence} in
\path{BernoulliRecurrences.lean}.  These are the formal-series bridge used by the
specialized dyadic theorem.

For \(m,k,d\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(Q\in\ComplexNumbers\), write
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_{m;k,d}(Q)=
 \sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
       (r-m2^k+Q)^d.                                        \label{eq:qs-sigma-def}
\end{equation}
The exact result is the following finite identity.

\begin{theorem}[Translated global dyadic formula]
\label{thm:qs-translation}
For all \(m,n\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(Q\in\ComplexNumbers\),
\begin{align}
 \FabiusGlobal(m/2^n)
 &=\frac{1}{2^{n^2}(1/2;1/2)_n}
   \sum_{k=0}^{n}
   \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}
        {4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!} \notag\\
 &\qquad\qquad\times
   \sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
      (r-m2^k+Q)^{n+k}.                                    \label{eq:qbinomial-dyadic}
\end{align}
The real number on the left is embedded in \(\ComplexNumbers\).  The entire right-hand side is
independent of the common translation \(Q\).  When \(m\le2^n\), the left side is the bounded
value \(F(m/2^n)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The complete normalized finite expression is packaged as a polynomial in the translation
and proved equal to the constant centered formula by
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseDyadicTranslatedFormulaPolynomial_eq_const}.
Its identification with the signed dyadic value over \(\ComplexNumbers\) is
\lean{globalFabius_dyadic_eq_qBinomialThueMorseDyadic_translated_sum_complex};
the rational core and real, complex, and Gaussian-rational specializations are in
\path{FabiusQBinomialFormula.lean} and
\path{FabiusDyadicQBinomialScalar.lean}.
\end{proof}

The rational specialization is also independent of how the dyadic argument is written.
If
\[
 \frac{m_1}{2^{n_1}}=\frac{m_2}{2^{n_2}},
\]
then the two translated rational formulas agree even when their common translations
\(Q_1,Q_2\in\RationalNumbers\) differ.  This is
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseDyadicTranslatedFormula_eq_of_rat_eq}; the one-step refinement
\((m,n)\mapsto(2m,n+1)\) is
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseDyadicTranslatedFormula_refine}.  Over every field over \(\RationalNumbers\),
both conclusions persist even when the scalar translations and the dyadic presentations
are changed simultaneously; these stronger wrappers are
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseDyadicTranslatedFormulaIn_eq_of_rat_eq} and
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseDyadicTranslatedFormulaIn_refine} in
\path{FabiusDyadicQBinomialScalar.lean}.

Binary complementation gives the raw inverse-dyadic form
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \FabiusGlobal(2^{-n})=
 \frac{1}{(-2)^{n^2}(1/2;1/2)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}(r+Q)^{n+k}.}               \label{eq:qbinomial-raw}
\end{equation}
Its translated numerator is constant by
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseRawTranslatedNumeratorPolynomial_eq_const}.  The
algebraic statement is in fact valid over every field \(K\) equipped with a
\(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra structure and every scalar translation \(Q\in K\): the
fully expanded right-hand side equals the canonical inverse-dyadic rational
value after its scalar embedding by
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_cast_eq_qBinomialThueMorse_rawTranslated_sum}.
Translation independence and the intermediate value identification are
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseRawTranslatedFormulaIn_eq_centered} and
\lean{qBinomialThueMorseRawTranslatedFormulaIn_eq_fabiusAtInverseTwoPow}.
For every scalar type \(K\) carrying Mathlib's \texttt{RCLike} structure, the
corresponding theorem for an arbitrary bounded Fabius solution is
\lean{fabiusFunction_inverse_two_pow_eq_qBinomialThueMorseRawTranslatedFormulaIn}.
All five declarations are in \path{FabiusRawQBinomialScalar.lean}; the final
one uses the \texttt{RCLike} hypothesis only to embed the real Fabius value.
Variable-base \(q\)-interpolation formulas, barycentric identities, numerical
cancellation studies, and complexity comparisons are maintained in the
consolidated research-frontier volume rather than used as theorems here.

% BEGIN GL(n,F_2) SUBSECTION (added 2026-09-02)
\subsection{The prefactor is the order of \texorpdfstring{\(\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb F_2)\)}{GL(n,F2)}}
\label{sec:qs-general-linear}

The constant \(2^{n^2}(1/2;1/2)_n\) standing in front of every form of the
dyadic identity above is not merely a normalization: it is the order of the
general linear group over the two-element field.  Both halves of the identity
can in fact be freed of the base \(1/2\) altogether, and what is left counts
subspaces.

The first step is a falling-product identity with no hypotheses at all.

\begin{lemma}[Falling product of powers]
\label{lem:qs-falling-product}
In every commutative ring and for every \(q\),
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{i<n}\bigl(q^{n}-q^{i}\bigr)
 =q^{\binom n2}\prod_{j<n}\bigl(q^{j+1}-1\bigr).
 \label{eq:qs-falling-product}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

This is \lean{prod_pow_sub_pow_eq_pow_choose_two_mul} in
\path{GLOrderProduct.lean}.  No invertibility or nonvanishing assumption
occurs, so \eqref{eq:qs-falling-product} holds at roots of unity, in positive
characteristic and in the presence of zero divisors.

Applied at \(q=2\), and combined with the Mersenne normalization
\lean{two_pow_nat_sq_mul_halfQPochhammer}, it identifies the prefactor:
\begin{equation}
 2^{n^{2}}(1/2;1/2)_n=\prod_{i<n}\bigl(2^{n}-2^{i}\bigr),
 \label{eq:qs-prefactor-product}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{two_pow_nat_sq_mul_qPochhammer_half_eq_prod} in
\path{FabiusDyadicGaussianForm.lean}.  The right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:qs-prefactor-product} is the number of ordered bases of
\(\mathbb F_2^{\,n}\).

\begin{theorem}[The dyadic prefactor is a group order]
\label{thm:qs-prefactor-gl}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 2^{n^{2}}(1/2;1/2)_n=\bigl|\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb F_2)\bigr|.
 \label{eq:qs-prefactor-gl}
\end{equation}
More generally, over a finite field \(K\) with \(Q=|K|\) elements,
\begin{equation}
 \bigl|\mathrm{GL}_n(K)\bigr|
 =Q^{\binom n2}\prod_{j<n}\bigl(Q^{j+1}-1\bigr)
 =Q^{n^{2}}\,(1/Q;1/Q)_n .
 \label{eq:qs-gl-qpochhammer}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The three statements are
\lean{two_pow_nat_sq_mul_qPochhammer_half_eq_card_GL},
\lean{cast_card_generalLinearGroup_eq_pow_choose_mul_prod} and
\lean{cast_card_generalLinearGroup_eq_pow_sq_mul_qPochhammer} in
\path{FabiusGeneralLinearDenominator.lean}.  The exponent bookkeeping in
\eqref{eq:qs-gl-qpochhammer} is the square split
\(\binom n2+\binom{n+1}2=n^{2}\), which is \lean{choose_square_split} in
\path{Arithmetic.lean}.  So the denominators of the exact dyadic Fabius values
are the group orders
\[
 1,\;1,\;6,\;168,\;20160,\;9999360,\;20158709760,\;\dots
\]

\begin{remark}[What is and is not proved here]
\label{rem:qs-gl-provenance}
The order of \(\mathrm{GL}_n\) is Mathlib's \lean{Matrix.card_GL_field} and is
not reproved anywhere in this development;
\lean{card_generalLinearGroup_eq_prod_range} of \path{RankMatrixCount.lean}
only reindexes it from \(\mathrm{Fin}\,r\) to \(\mathrm{Finset.range}\,r\), so
it is a bridge and not a source.  What is established here is the cast to
\(\RationalNumbers\) --- the ambient statement lives in \(\NaturalNumbers\)
with truncated subtraction --- together with the identification of the
resulting product with the \(q\)-Pochhammer prefactor.
\end{remark}

The second step removes the base \(1/2\) from the summand.  Gaussian
reciprocity, \lean{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity}, converts the quotient
\(q\)-binomial at \(1/2\) into the polynomial Gaussian binomial at \(2\) at
the cost of a monomial \(2^{k(n-k)}\), and \lean{four_pow_choose_two} rewrites
\(4^{\binom k2}\) as \(2^{k(k-1)}\).  The two exponents merge, because for
\(k\le n\)
\begin{equation}
 k(n-k)+k(k-1)+k=kn
 \label{eq:qs-exponent-collapse}
\end{equation}
in \(\NaturalNumbers\) with truncated subtraction
(\lean{exponent_collapse}).  Consequently
\[
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}}
 =\frac{\GaussianBinomial nk2\cdot 2^{k}}{2^{kn}},
\]
in which \(\GaussianBinomial nk2\) is an \emph{integer}: the number of
\(k\)-dimensional subspaces of \(\mathbb F_2^{\,n}\).

\begin{theorem}[Subspace form of the dyadic identity]
\label{thm:qs-subspace-form}
For every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 F\!\left(2^{-n}\right)
 =\frac{1}{\bigl|\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb F_2)\bigr|}
  \sum_{k=0}^{n}
   \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk2\cdot 2^{k}}{2^{kn}\,(n+k)!}
   \sum_{r<2^{k}}\varepsilon_r\bigl(r-2^{k}\bigr)^{n+k}.
 \label{eq:qs-subspace-form}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

This is \lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_eq_card_GL_sum} in
\path{FabiusGeneralLinearDenominator.lean}, with the product form
\lean{fabiusAtInverseTwoPow_eq_gaussian_sum} of
\path{FabiusDyadicGaussianForm.lean} and the real-valued transfers
\lean{fabius_inverse_two_pow_eq_card_GL_inv_mul} and
\lean{fabius_inverse_two_pow_eq_gaussian_div_prod} alongside.

Neither side of \eqref{eq:qs-subspace-form} mentions the base \(1/2\), and no
\(q\)-Pochhammer quotient, no \(4^{\binom k2}\) and no \(2^{n^{2}}\) survives.
Every constant on the right counts something over \(\mathbb F_2\): the weight
\(\GaussianBinomial nk2\) enumerates the \(k\)-dimensional subspaces of
\(\mathbb F_2^{\,n}\), and the normalizing constant is the order of the
automorphism group of that space.  The exact dyadic values of the Fabius
function are therefore a signed, factorially weighted average over the
subspace lattice of \(\mathbb F_2^{\,n}\).

\begin{remark}[The Thue--Morse sign inside the \(q\)-Pochhammer]
\label{rem:qs-tm-pochhammer}
The appearance of a \(q\)-Pochhammer symbol beside a Thue--Morse block sum in
\eqref{eq:qs-subspace-form} is not a coincidence of normalizations.  Over any
commutative ring and with no hypothesis on \(z\) or \(q\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n<2^{N}}\varepsilon_n\,z^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}q^{\sigma(n)}
 =(z;q)_N ,
 \label{eq:qs-tm-pochhammer}
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma(n)\) is the sum of the positions of the one bits of \(n\):
a finite \(q\)-Pochhammer symbol \emph{is} a Thue--Morse alternating sum,
bigraded by the two binary statistics.  This is
\lean{sum_thueMorseSign_mul_pow_bigraded_eq_finiteQPochhammerIn} in
\path{ThueMorseQPochhammer.lean}.  Combining it with
\eqref{eq:qs-gl-qpochhammer} expresses the group order itself as an
alternating digit sum,
\(\bigl|\mathrm{GL}_n(K)\bigr|
 =\sum_{m<2^{n}}\varepsilon_m\,Q^{\,n^{2}-\BinaryDigitSum(m)-\sigma(m)}\)
(\lean{cast_card_generalLinearGroup_eq_thueMorse_sum} in
\path{GeneralLinearThueMorseSum.lean}), the exponent being non-negative
because \(\BinaryDigitSum(m)+\sigma(m)\le\binom{n+1}2\le n^{2}\).
\end{remark}

% END GL(n,F_2) SUBSECTION

\section{The global binary-reduction telescope}
\label{sec:global-binary-reduction}

The Taylor-block recursion also applies to the signed global extension $\FabiusGlobal$.  It can
therefore be iterated along the binary expansion of an arbitrary nonnegative real number,
rather than only along the finite expansion of a dyadic rational.  The reductions need not
terminate, but their residuals tend geometrically to zero.

Define the finite reduction polynomial
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal T_m(y)=
 \sum_{j=0}^{m}
 2^{\binom{j+1}{2}-\binom{m-j}{2}}
 \frac{d_{m-j}}{(m-j)!}\frac{y^j}{j!}.                   \label{eq:global-reduction-polynomial}
\end{equation}
All powers of $2$ here are integer powers.  In particular, negative exponents are allowed,
and $\mathcal T_0(y)=1$.  The signed Taylor-block identity is
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal(2^{-m}+y)=-\FabiusGlobal(y)+\mathcal T_m(y),
 \qquad 0\le y<2^{-m}.                                   \label{eq:global-taylor-reduction}
\end{equation}

Fix $x\ge0$.  At scale $m$, put
\begin{equation}
 p_m=\Floor{2^mx},\qquad
 y_m=x-\frac{p_m}{2^m},\qquad 0\le y_m<2^{-m}.            \label{eq:global-prefix-tail}
\end{equation}
The previous prefix requires a genuine half-scale value at $m=0$:
\begin{equation}
 p^*_{m-1}=\begin{cases}
  \Floor{x/2},&m=0,\\
  p_{m-1},&m\ge1.
 \end{cases}                                             \label{eq:global-previous-prefix}
\end{equation}
Uniformly, $p^*_{m-1}=\Floor{p_m/2}$.  Thus
$p_m=2p^*_{m-1}+\delta_m$ with $\delta_m\in\{0,1\}$.  Define
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_m(x)=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(p_m)}(2p^*_{m-1}-p_m),\qquad
 R_m(x)=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(p_m)}\FabiusGlobal(y_m).                  \label{eq:global-reduction-coefficient}
\end{equation}
The coefficient $\alpha_m$ is zero or a sign, and it is nonzero precisely when the newly
exposed binary digit is $1$.

\begin{lemma}[One-step residual identity]
For $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_m(x)\mathcal T_m(y_m)=R_{m-1}(x)-R_m(x).          \label{eq:global-one-step-residual}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $p_m=2p_{m-1}$, then $y_m=y_{m-1}$, the Thue--Morse signs agree, and
$\alpha_m=0$, so both sides vanish.  If $p_m=2p_{m-1}+1$, then
\begin{equation*}
 y_{m-1}=2^{-m}+y_m,\qquad
 (-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(p_m)}=-(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(p_{m-1})},\qquad
 \alpha_m=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(p_{m-1})}.
\end{equation*}
Apply \eqref{eq:global-taylor-reduction} to $y_{m-1}$ and multiply by the old prefix sign;
the two copies of $\FabiusGlobal(y_m)$ combine exactly into $R_{m-1}-R_m$.
\end{proof}

The scale-zero identity is separate and retains the parity of the integer block:
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\alpha_0(x)\mathcal T_0(y_0)+R_0(x).       \label{eq:global-scale-zero-step}
\end{equation}
Indeed, write $p_0=2p^*_{-1}+\delta_0$ and use the two-unit translation law
\eqref{eq:single-block}.  When $\delta_0=0$, the polynomial term vanishes.  When
$\delta_0=1$, the identity $\FabiusGlobal(1+y_0)=-\FabiusGlobal(y_0)+1$ supplies it.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite binary telescope]
For $x\ge0$ and $N\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=
 \sum_{m=0}^{N}\alpha_m(x)\mathcal T_m(y_m(x))+R_N(x).}
                                                               \label{eq:global-finite-binary-telescope}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Start with \eqref{eq:global-scale-zero-step}.  Summing
\eqref{eq:global-one-step-residual} for $m=1,\ldots,N$ telescopes all intermediate residuals.
The case $N=0$ is exactly the scale-zero identity.
\end{proof}

For $N\ge1$, $0\le y_N<2^{-N}\le1/2$.  On this interval
$0\le\FabiusGlobal(y)=F(y)\le2y$, so
\begin{equation}
 |R_N(x)|\le2^{1-N}.                                     \label{eq:global-residual-bound}
\end{equation}
The estimate is independent of $x$.  Therefore, if $B_N(x)$ denotes the finite sum in
\eqref{eq:global-finite-binary-telescope}, then for every $x\in\RealNumbers$ and $N\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 |B_N(x)-\FabiusGlobal(x)|\le2^{1-N}.                       \label{eq:global-binary-uniform-bound}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{norm_globalBinaryReductionSum_sub_extendedFabius_le}.  Lean's natural-floor
convention extends every $B_N$ by zero on $x\le0$, where $\FabiusGlobal$ also vanishes, and
\lean{globalBinaryReductionSum_tendstoUniformly_extendedFabius} proves that the finite
telescopes converge uniformly on the whole line.
The one-step identity then gives, for $m\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 |\alpha_m(x)\mathcal T_m(y_m)|
 \le |R_{m-1}|+|R_m|\le4\,2^{-(m-1)}.                   \label{eq:global-summand-majorant}
\end{equation}
Thus the limiting series is absolutely, not merely conditionally, convergent.

\begin{theorem}[Absolutely convergent global binary series]
For every $x\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}
 \alpha_m(x)\mathcal T_m(y_m(x)).}                       \label{eq:global-binary-reduction-series}
\end{equation}
On $0\le x\le1$, the sum is equivalently $F(x)$.
\end{theorem}

Lean's natural-floor definitions make the telescope genuinely all-real: when \(x\le0\),
every binary prefix, summand, residual, and \(\FabiusGlobal(x)\) vanishes termwise.  Thus the
finite identity and the \(m=0\) infinite series hold for every \(x\in\RealNumbers\), as
\lean{extendedFabius_eq_globalBinaryReductionSum_add_remainder_all} and
\lean{extendedFabius_eq_tsum_globalBinaryReductionSummand_all} in
\path{FabiusBinaryReductionSeries.lean}.  The fully expanded parity-power version has the
same all-real scope by \lean{globalFabius_eq_tsum_fabiusParityPower_literal_all} in
\path{FabiusParityPowerSeries.lean}.  The displayed floor formulas above remain ordinary
nonnegative-floor formulas on their stated domain.

The index $m=0$ is indispensable globally.  For $0\le x<1$ it vanishes, so a series
starting at $m=1$ happens to be valid there.  At $x=1$, however, the zeroth term is $1$ and
all later coefficients vanish.  More generally it is the zeroth term that distinguishes
even and odd integer blocks of the signed extension.

\subsection{The corrected parity-power presentation}
\label{subsec:corrected-parity-power}

The same summands have a more literal presentation in terms of prefix parity and inverse
dyadic values.  Define, using integer exponents,
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_j=\frac{((j:\IntegerNumbers)+2)((j:\IntegerNumbers)-1)}2
 =\binom{j+1}{2}-1.                                      \label{eq:parity-power-integer-exponent}
\end{equation}
The integer interpretation matters: $\kappa_0=-1$, hence $2^{\kappa_0}=1/2$; natural
subtraction would silently give the wrong term.  Put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal I_m(y)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}
 2^{\kappa_j}\FabiusGlobal\bigl(2^{(j:\IntegerNumbers)-(m:\IntegerNumbers)}\bigr)
 \frac{y^j}{j!}.                                         \label{eq:parity-power-inner}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}
For every $m$ and $y$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal I_m(y)=\frac12\mathcal T_m(y).                 \label{eq:parity-power-half-reduction}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $j\le m$, set $r=m-j$.  The inverse-dyadic identity
\eqref{eq:F-inverse-power} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \FabiusGlobal(2^{j-m})=
 \frac{d_{m-j}}{(m-j)!\,2^{\binom{m-j}{2}}}.
\end{equation*}
Together with $\kappa_j=\binom{j+1}{2}-1$, this identifies each summand with one half of
the corresponding summand in \eqref{eq:global-reduction-polynomial}.
\end{proof}

Let $\tau(a)=\BinaryDigitSum(a)\bmod2$.  Since
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{(-1)^a-1}{2}=2\Floor{\frac a2}-a,
\end{equation*}
the literal summand
\begin{align}
 \Lambda_m(x)
 &:=
 \bigl((-1)^{\Floor{2^mx}}-1\bigr)
 (-1)^{\tau(\Floor{2^mx})}                               \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\quad\times
 \sum_{j=0}^{m}2^{((j+2)(j-1))/2}
 \FabiusGlobal(2^{j-m})
 \frac{\left(x-\Floor{2^mx}/2^m\right)^j}{j!}           \label{eq:parity-power-literal-summand}
\end{align}
is exactly $\alpha_m(x)\mathcal T_m(y_m)$.  Both exponents
$((j+2)(j-1))/2$ and $j-m$ in this display are integer exponents, not truncated natural
subtractions.

\begin{warning}
\label{warn:blu-natural-subtraction}
In \eqref{eq:qs-sigma-def} and \eqref{eq:qbinomial-dyadic}, the summation indices are cast
into the ambient characteristic-zero field before the subtraction in $r-m2^k+Q$ is
performed.  This is the typing used by the formal theorem; the displayed subtraction is
therefore field subtraction, never truncated subtraction on natural numbers.
\end{warning}

\begin{theorem}[Corrected parity-power series]
For every $x\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\FabiusGlobal(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Lambda_m(x),}  \label{eq:corrected-parity-power-series}
\end{equation}
and the series converges absolutely.  On the half-open unit interval $0\le x<1$, the
zeroth summand vanishes and one may start at $m=1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The elementary parity identity and \eqref{eq:parity-power-half-reduction} prove
$\Lambda_m=\alpha_m\mathcal T_m(y_m)$ term by term.  The claim follows from
\eqref{eq:global-binary-reduction-series}.  The half-open corollary follows from
$\Floor{x}=0$; the closed endpoint $x=1$ requires the restored zeroth term.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The global complex \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial series}
\label{subsec:global-q-series}

The finite constant-polynomial identity from \cref{subsec:q-binomial-dyadic} can be inserted
at every scale of the binary telescope.  For $Q\in\ComplexNumbers$, define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal N_n^{(Q)}=
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
 (r-2^k+Q)^{n+k}.                                       \label{eq:q-global-numerator}
\end{equation}
Translation invariance and the inverse-dyadic normalization give
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\mathcal N_n^{(Q)}}
 {2^{\binom n2}(1/2;1/2)_n}
 =\frac{2^n d_n}{n!}.                                    \label{eq:q-taylor-normalization}
\end{equation}
The normalization differs from the $2^{n^2}$ in
\eqref{eq:qbinomial-dyadic}: here it produces a Taylor coefficient rather than a function
value.

For $m\in\NaturalNumbers$, put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_m^{(Q)}(y)=
 2^{-\binom{m+1}{2}}
 \sum_{n=0}^{m}
 \frac{(2^{m+1}y)^{m-n}}{(m-n)!}
 \frac{\mathcal N_n^{(Q)}}
 {2^{\binom n2}(1/2;1/2)_n}.                             \label{eq:q-reduction-polynomial}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-binomial Taylor identity]
For every $m$, every $y\in\RealNumbers$, and every $Q\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_m^{(Q)}(y)=\mathcal T_m(y),                  \label{eq:q-reduction-equals-taylor}
\end{equation}
where the real polynomial on the right is embedded in $\ComplexNumbers$.  In particular, each finite
summand is pointwise independent of $Q$ before any infinite sum is taken.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert \eqref{eq:q-taylor-normalization} into
\eqref{eq:q-reduction-polynomial} and set $j=m-n$.  Collecting the powers of $2$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 2^{-\binom{m+1}{2}}\,2^{(m+1)j}\,2^{m-j}
 =2^{\binom{j+1}{2}-\binom{m-j}{2}}.
\end{equation*}
The remaining factorials and moments are exactly those in
\eqref{eq:global-reduction-polynomial}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Global complex $q$-binomial series]
For fixed $Q\in\ComplexNumbers$ and every $x\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \FabiusGlobal(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}
 \alpha_m(x)\mathcal Q_m^{(Q)}(y_m(x)),}                 \label{eq:global-q-binomial-series}
\end{equation}
with the left side embedded in $\ComplexNumbers$.  The series converges absolutely.
\end{theorem}

The displayed floor presentation is stated on the nonnegative half-line.  The literal Lean
summand has a stronger all-real scope: for every \(x\in\RealNumbers\), its series is absolutely
summable and sums to the signed global extension, while for \(x\le0\) every term and the
target vanish.  These are
\lean{summable_norm_qBinomialFabiusGlobalSummand_all},
\lean{hasSum_qBinomialFabiusGlobalSummand_all},
\lean{extendedFabius_eq_tsum_qBinomialFabiusGlobalSummand_all},
\lean{globalFabius_eq_tsum_qBinomialFabiusGlobalSummand_all}, and
\lean{qBinomialFabiusGlobalSummand_eq_zero_of_nonpos} in
\path{FabiusGlobalQBinomialSeries.lean}.  The older nonnegative signatures are retained as
compatibility wrappers.

This is not a new infinite basic-hypergeometric identity.  The outer sum is the analytic
binary telescope, while at each scale a finite $q$-binomial identity presents the same
Taylor polynomial.  Expanding one summand makes the architecture explicit:
\begin{align}
 &\alpha_m(x)\,2^{-\binom{m+1}{2}}
 \sum_{n=0}^{m}
 \frac{(2^{m+1}x-2p_m(x))^{m-n}}{(m-n)!}
 \frac1{2^{\binom n2}(1/2;1/2)_n}                       \notag\\
 &\qquad\times
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
 (r-2^k+Q)^{n+k}.                                       \label{eq:global-q-literal-summand}
\end{align}
Convergence comes entirely from the binary-tail majorant
\eqref{eq:global-summand-majorant}; the special-function manipulations are finite and
algebraic.

\section{A complex \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial discrete limit}
\label{sec:q-discrete-limit}

There is a second way in which the same Gaussian row produces $\FabiusGlobal$.  It is a genuine
limit of finite shifted splines rather than a sequence of partial sums of
\eqref{eq:global-q-binomial-series}.  Reuse the safe midpoint cutoff $N_p(x)$ from
\eqref{eq:centered-cutoff}.  For $x\ge0$ it is equivalent to the inclusive source range
\begin{equation}
 0\le r\le\Floor{2^px-\frac12},                           \label{eq:discrete-cutoff-inclusive}
\end{equation}
including the empty case where the upper endpoint is $-1$.

For fixed $Q\in\ComplexNumbers$ and $x\in\RealNumbers$, define
\begin{align}
 \mathcal D_n^{(Q)}(x)
 &=
 \frac1{2^{n^2}(1/2;1/2)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial nk{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}             \notag\\
 &\quad\times
 \sum_{r=0}^{N_{n+k}(x)-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
 \bigl(r-2^{n+k}x+Q\bigr)^{n+k}.                         \label{eq:discrete-q-approximant}
\end{align}

\begin{theorem}[Complex discrete-limit formula]
For every fixed $Q\in\ComplexNumbers$ and every $x\in\RealNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\mathcal D_n^{(Q)}(x)\longrightarrow\FabiusGlobal(x)}
                                                               \label{eq:discrete-q-limit}
\end{equation}
in $\ComplexNumbers$.  If $0\le x\le1$, the limit is equivalently $F(x)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $x\le0$, every safe cutoff $N_p(x)$ is zero.  Hence every shifted spline and every
approximant \eqref{eq:discrete-q-approximant} is zero, as is $\FabiusGlobal(x)$.  It remains
to treat $x\ge0$.

For a degree $p$ and complex shift $Q$, introduce the finite shifted spline
\begin{equation}
 S_p^{(Q)}(x)=
 \frac{(-1)^p}{2^{\binom p2}p!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{N_p(x)-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}
 (r-2^px+Q)^p.                                           \label{eq:complex-shift-spline}
\end{equation}
At $Q=1/2$ this is exactly the centered spline
\eqref{eq:centered-spline}, so by \eqref{eq:centered-spline-global-limit},
\begin{equation*}
 S_p^{(1/2)}(x)\longrightarrow\FabiusGlobal(x).
\end{equation*}
\begin{lemma}[Fixed-cutoff Taylor expansion of a complex shift]
\label{lem:res-spline-taylor}
Read \eqref{eq:centered-spline} at $p=0$ as
\begin{equation}
 S_0(x)=\sum_{r=0}^{N_0(x)-1}\varepsilon_r,               \label{eq:res-degree-zero-spline}
\end{equation}
so that a centered branch $S_d$ is defined for every integer $d\ge0$.  Fix $p\ge1$ and
$x\ge0$, and abbreviate $M=N_p(x)$.  For every $d$ with $0\le d\le p$, the degree-$d$ branch
carried by that one fixed cutoff satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{2^{\binom d2}d!}\sum_{r=0}^{M-1}\varepsilon_r
 \left(2^px-\frac12-r\right)^{d}
 =S_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right),                           \label{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch}
\end{equation}
and in particular $\left|S_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right)\right|\le1$.  Moreover, for every
$Q\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 S_p^{(Q)}(x)-S_p^{(1/2)}(x)
 =\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}
 \frac{2^{\binom d2}}{2^{\binom p2}(p-d)!}
 \left(\frac12-Q\right)^{p-d}
 S_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right).                            \label{eq:res-spline-taylor}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the shifted spline in its descending orientation.  Since
$(-1)^p(r-2^px+Q)^p=(2^px-Q-r)^p$, definition \eqref{eq:complex-shift-spline} reads
\begin{equation}
 S_p^{(Q)}(x)=\frac1{2^{\binom p2}p!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{M-1}\varepsilon_r\left(2^px-Q-r\right)^{p},
 \qquad M=N_p(x),                                         \label{eq:res-descending-spline}
\end{equation}
and at $Q=1/2$ this is \eqref{eq:centered-spline}.  The cutoff $M$ depends on $p$ and $x$
only.  It is unaffected by $Q$, and it is held fixed in what follows while the degree of the
summand varies; that is the whole point of \eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch}.

We prove \eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch}.  The dilation $x\mapsto2^{\,p-d}x$ compensates
exactly for the drop in degree, in the sense that
\begin{equation}
 N_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right)
 =\max\left(0,\Floor{2^{p}x+\frac12}\right)
 =N_p(x)=M                                                \label{eq:res-cutoff-invariance}
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:centered-cutoff}.  If $M=0$, both sides of
\eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch} are empty sums and there is nothing to prove.  Assume
$M\ge1$.  Every index in the range satisfies
\[
 r\le M-1=\Floor{2^px+\frac12}-1\le2^px-\frac12,
\]
so every base $2^px-\frac12-r$ occurring in \eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch} is
nonnegative and the truncation in \eqref{eq:centered-spline-positive} is inactive.  For
$d\ge1$, formula \eqref{eq:centered-spline-positive} evaluated at the point $2^{\,p-d}x$,
whose own cutoff is $M$ by \eqref{eq:res-cutoff-invariance}, is therefore literally the left
side of \eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch}.  For $d=0$ both sides equal
$\sum_{r=0}^{M-1}\varepsilon_r$, by \eqref{eq:res-degree-zero-spline} and
\eqref{eq:res-cutoff-invariance}.

For the uniform bound, the rescaled argument \(2^{p-d}x\) is nonnegative.
The all-degree declaration \lean{abs_fabiusUniformSpline_le_one} therefore gives the
claim directly, including \(d=0\).

Now expand.  Put $w=2^px-\frac12$ for the descending argument at $Q=1/2$ and
$t=\frac12-Q$ for its increment, so that $2^px-Q=w+t$.  The binomial theorem gives
\[
 (w+t-r)^{p}=\sum_{d=0}^{p}\binom pd\,t^{\,p-d}(w-r)^{d}.
\]
Multiply by $\varepsilon_r\bigl/\bigl(2^{\binom p2}p!\bigr)$, sum over $0\le r\le M-1$, and
interchange the two finite sums.  Using \eqref{eq:res-descending-spline} on the left and
\eqref{eq:res-fixed-cutoff-branch} on each inner sum,
\begin{align}
 S_p^{(Q)}(x)
 &=\sum_{d=0}^{p}\frac{t^{\,p-d}}{2^{\binom p2}p!}\binom pd
   \sum_{r=0}^{M-1}\varepsilon_r(w-r)^{d}                 \notag\\
 &=\sum_{d=0}^{p}\frac{2^{\binom d2}}{2^{\binom p2}(p-d)!}\,t^{\,p-d}\,
   S_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right),                          \label{eq:res-full-expansion}
\end{align}
because $\binom pd\bigl/p!=1\bigl/\bigl(d!\,(p-d)!\bigr)$ and the inner sum is
$2^{\binom d2}d!\,S_d(2^{\,p-d}x)$.  The term $d=p$ is $S_p(x)=S_p^{(1/2)}(x)$; subtracting
it leaves \eqref{eq:res-spline-taylor}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Which convention makes the sign disappear]
\label{rem:res-sign-convention}
The expansion \eqref{eq:res-spline-taylor} is free of alternating signs only because the
increment is measured in the \emph{descending} variable $2^px-Q$ of
\eqref{eq:res-descending-spline}, that is, because the coefficient carries
$\left(\tfrac12-Q\right)^{p-d}$ and not $\left(Q-\tfrac12\right)^{p-d}$.  If one instead
measures the shift by the ascending increment $q=Q-\tfrac12$, which is the natural reading
when the branch is written as a function of the ascending argument $r-2^px+Q$ of
\eqref{eq:complex-shift-spline}, then the identity acquires exactly the factor
$(-1)^{p+d}$:
\begin{equation}
 S_p^{(Q)}(x)-S_p^{(1/2)}(x)
 =\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{p+d}
 \frac{2^{\binom d2}}{2^{\binom p2}(p-d)!}\,
 q^{\,p-d}\,S_d\!\left(2^{\,p-d}x\right),
 \qquad q=Q-\frac12.                                      \label{eq:res-ascending-form}
\end{equation}
The two displays are the same statement, since $(-1)^{p+d}=(-1)^{p-d}$ and therefore
$(-1)^{p+d}q^{\,p-d}=\left(\tfrac12-Q\right)^{p-d}$.  It is worth being explicit about where
the sign does \emph{not} live: it is not a matter of renormalizing the lower branches.  The
alternating prefactor $(-1)^d$ of \eqref{eq:centered-spline} is cancelled exactly by the
$(-1)^d$ produced when the ascending argument is reversed, so the branch values
$S_d(2^{\,p-d}x)$ appearing in \eqref{eq:res-spline-taylor} and in
\eqref{eq:res-ascending-form} are the same numbers.  The sign belongs to the increment
alone, and the convention that removes it is the descending one.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Formal bound]
Only the exponential bound \eqref{eq:complex-shift-spline-bound} is asserted here; it is
\lean{norm_fabiusComplexShiftSpline_sub_center_le_half_pow_mul_exp} in
\path{FabiusComplexShiftSpline.lean}.  A proposed sharper constant is recorded in the
research-frontier gap register pending a corrected named theorem.
\end{remark}

Expanding a fixed cutoff branch about $Q=1/2$ gives an exact finite Taylor combination of
lower-degree centered branches.  The normalized Thue--Morse bounds then give, for $p\ge1$
and $x\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \left|S_p^{(Q)}(x)-S_p^{(1/2)}(x)\right|
 \le2^{-(p-1)}\exp\!\left(|Q-\tfrac12|\right).            \label{eq:complex-shift-spline-bound}
\end{equation}
The nonpositive half-line is handled separately by exact vanishing, and
\lean{fabiusComplexShiftSpline_tendsto_globalFabius_all} proves that every fixed complex
shift has the same pointwise spline limit for every real $x$.

Now reindex the outer sum in \eqref{eq:discrete-q-approximant} by $j=n-k$.  Direct
collection of signs, powers, and factorials gives the exact finite identity
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal D_n^{(Q)}(x)=
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}S_{2n-j}^{(Q)}(x),             \label{eq:discrete-toeplitz-reindex}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \omega_{n,j}=
 \frac{(-1)^j\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}\,2^{-\binom{j+1}{2}}}
 {(1/2;1/2)_n}.                                          \label{eq:discrete-toeplitz-weight}
\end{equation}
The finite $q$-binomial theorem gives the row normalization
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}=1.                            \label{eq:discrete-row-sum}
\end{equation}
The same theorem identifies the entire row rather than only its value at one.

\begin{proposition}[Toeplitz row polynomial and module-valued $q$-Richardson cancellation]
\label{prop:discrete-row-polynomial}
For $z\in\RationalNumbers$, put $W_n(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}z^j$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 W_n(z)=\frac{(z/2;1/2)_n}{(1/2;1/2)_n}.                 \label{eq:discrete-row-polynomial}
\end{equation}
Consequently its rational zeros are exactly
\begin{equation}
 W_n(z)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 z\in\{2,2^2,\ldots,2^n\}.                              \label{eq:discrete-row-roots}
\end{equation}
Moreover, let $M$ be any additive commutative monoid carrying a $\RationalNumbers$-module
structure.  If an $M$-valued sequence has the finite geometric-error model
$u_p=L+\sum_{r=1}^{n}2^{-rp}\mathbin{\cdot}a_r$, where
$\cdot$ denotes scalar multiplication, then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}\mathbin{\cdot}u_{2n-j}=L.
                                                               \label{eq:discrete-row-richardson}
\end{equation}
Thus the cancellation needs no multiplication, topology, order, or norm on
the target.  Taking $M=\RationalNumbers$ recovers the rational formula
$u_p=L+\sum_{r=1}^{n}a_r2^{-rp}$.  This module-valued extension concerns
cancellation only; the root classification above remains a statement for
rational $z$.  All statements include $n=0$: the row polynomial is then $1$,
the root set is empty, and the error sum is empty.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The full generating identity and its root classification are
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_mul_pow} and
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_mul_pow_eq_zero_iff}.  The reusable
single-mode identity is
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_mul_geometricMode_smul_eq_zero},
and the module-valued cancellation theorem is
\lean{discreteLimitWeight_qRichardson_exact_module}; its $M=\RationalNumbers$
specialization is \lean{discreteLimitWeight_qRichardson_exact}.  All are in
\path{FabiusDiscreteLimitToeplitz.lean}.  The former proof of
\eqref{eq:discrete-row-sum} is now its specialization at $z=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Finite moments, Lagrange weights, and local exactness]
The row calculation is an instance of two reusable algebraic layers.  First, let $I$ be
finite, let $\varphi:R\to S$ be a homomorphism from a semiring to a commutative semiring,
let $w_i,x_i\in S$ for $i\in I$, and let $p\in R[X]$ have degree at most $N$.  If
$p^\varphi$ denotes
coefficientwise mapping, then the finite weighted functional expands as
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{i\in I} w_i p^\varphi(x_i)
 =\sum_{d=0}^{N}\varphi([X^d]p)\sum_{i\in I} w_i x_i^d.
\end{equation*}
Thus prescribed moments select a prescribed coefficient; repeated nodes and a zero
surviving moment are allowed, and no field or subtraction is needed.  The same-ring
coefficient and strict-cancellation forms are
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_coeff_mul_of_moments} and
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_zero_of_degree_lt} in
\path{FinitePolynomialFunctional.lean}; that module also contains the scalar-extension
forms used by the later Prouhet arguments.

Its exhaustive public surface has no definitions and exactly sixteen theorems:
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_sum_coeff_mul_moment},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_eval₂_of_moments},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_coeff_mul_moment},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_topCoeff_mul_moment},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_constantCoeff_mul_sum},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_constantCoeff},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_map_coeff_mul_of_moments},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_map_coeff_mul_top_moment},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_eq_zero_of_degree_lt},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval₂_congr_of_map_coeff_eq},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_eval_of_moments},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_coeff_mul_of_moments},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_coeff_mul_top_moment},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_eq_zero_of_degree_lt},
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_congr_of_coeff_eq}, and
\lean{sum_weight_mul_eval_affine_of_topCoeff_extractor}.
The first ten allow scalar extension from an arbitrary semiring into a
commutative semiring; the remaining same-ring forms require only a commutative
semiring.  Repeated nodes and a zero selected moment are allowed, and the
degree-valued statements include \(n=0\) and the zero polynomial.

The last theorem is the general affine transport principle.  If a weighted
functional extracts \([X^n]q\,c\) from every \(q\) of degree at most \(n\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_i w_i p(a+b x_i)=b^n c[X^n]p                         \label{eq:finite-functional-affine}
\end{equation}
for every \(p\) of degree at most \(n\).  Put
\(q(X)=p(a+bX)\), apply the supplied extractor to \(q\), and compute
\([X^n]q=b^n[X^n]p\).  The implementation treats \(b=0\) separately when
proving this coefficient formula, so \eqref{eq:finite-functional-affine}
needs neither \(b\ne0\) nor distinct transformed nodes and includes \(n=0\).
Composing it with
\lean{halfQBinomial_negativeDyadic_polynomial_sum_eq_mersenne} proves the
q-monograph's \nolinkurl{cor:geometric-prouhet-affine} exactly under its
established \(\RationalNumbers[X]\) convention.

Second, over a field, distinct nodes $v_i$ have evaluation weights
$\lambda_i(x)$ given by the Lagrange basis at $x$.  They reproduce every polynomial of
degree less than the number of nodes, are characterized uniquely by their monomial
moments, and at the first omitted degree satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_i\lambda_i(x)v_i^{|I|}=x^{|I|}-\prod_i(x-v_i).
\end{equation*}
These are respectively \lean{sum_lagrangeEvalWeight_mul_eval},
\lean{eq_lagrangeEvalWeight_of_moments}, and
\lean{sum_lagrangeEvalWeight_mul_pow_card}.  On the distinct geometric nodes
$1,q,\ldots,q^n$ at the target $0$, the moments through degree $n$ are $0^d$;
in particular degree zero has mass one, while positive degrees vanish.  The declarations
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow} and
\lean{geometricLagrange_richardson_exact_of_eq} in
\path{GeometricLagrange.lean} include both $n=0$ and an arbitrary block start, including
start zero.

The first omitted degree is only the first member of an exact all-order
family.  Let \(h_r(x,(v_i)_{i\in I})\) be the complete homogeneous symmetric
polynomial of degree \(r\) in the target \(x\) and the node family.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_i\lambda_i(x)v_i^{|I|+r}
 =x^{|I|+r}-\prod_i(x-v_i)\,
   h_r(x,(v_i)_{i\in I})\qquad(r\ge0).                    \label{eq:lagrange-all-residual-moments}
\end{equation}
More generally, the same formula holds over any commutative ring for
arbitrary weights and possibly repeated nodes whenever the moments below
\(|I|\) reproduce evaluation at \(x\).  No division or distinctness is used
in this representation-independent form.  The universal symmetric-polynomial
evaluation and its adjoining-variable recurrence are
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_eq_eval_hsymm} and
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_option_succ}; the quotient evaluation is
\lean{eval_completeHomogeneousQuotient} in
\path{CompleteHomogeneous.lean}.  The companion
\path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean} packages these evaluations as the
formal series \lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn}.  Its coefficient
and adjoining-variable laws are
\lean{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} and
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert}; over every commutative
ring, \lean{prod_one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} clears the
finite linear-denominator product and
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_eq_invOfUnit_prod} identifies the
series with its canonical \texttt{PowerSeries.invOfUnit} reciprocal.  This is
a formal coefficient identity and asserts no analytic evaluation,
convergence, or Bell/generalized-harmonic conversion.

The separate finite-algebra module \path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean}
supplies precisely that conversion.  For a finite family \(a_i\), put
\begin{equation*}
 p_k=\sum_i a_i^k,\qquad \kappa_0=0,\qquad
 \kappa_{k+1}=k!\,p_{k+1}.
\end{equation*}
The definitions are \lean{completeHomogeneousPowerSum} and
\lean{completeHomogeneousBellInput}.  Over every commutative semiring, the
division-free identity
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Bell.complete}(\kappa)_n=n!\,h_n(a)          \label{eq:complete-homogeneous-bell}
\end{equation}
is \lean{bellComplete_completeHomogeneousBellInput}.  Over a commutative
\(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra, the normalized pointwise report form
\begin{equation*}
 h_n(a)=\operatorname{factorialNormalize}
   \bigl(\operatorname{completeBellPolynomial}(\kappa)\bigr)_n
\end{equation*}
is
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}.
These statements are finite algebra: the first uses no division, and neither
asserts convergence of a formal or analytic series.

The generic residual theorem
\lean{sum_weight_mul_pow_card_add} and its distinct-node Lagrange
specialization \lean{sum_lagrangeEvalWeight_mul_pow_card_add} are in
\path{LagrangeResidualMoments.lean}.  Thus
\eqref{eq:lagrange-all-residual-moments} extends the preceding first-error
formula rather than replacing its low-degree exactness foundation.

There is also a finite-family analytic companion to this formal algebra.
The theorem \lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_isBigO_pow} in
\path{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean} says that coordinatewise
\(\BigO(g)\) bounds on a fixed finite alphabet give an
\(\BigO(g^n)\) bound for its degree-\(n\) complete homogeneous
evaluation.  This evaluated asymptotic statement does not assert convergence
of the preceding formal power series or its Bell/generalized-harmonic
conversion.

On the shifted reciprocal alphabet \(b_j=(\lambda+j)^{-1}\), \(0\le j\le r\),
\path{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean} names the generalized harmonic sums and
their Bell input as \lean{shiftedReciprocalPowerSum} and
\lean{shiftedReciprocalBellInput}.  The exact specialization
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_shiftedReciprocal_eq_bell} rewrites
\(h_n(b_0,\ldots,b_r)\) by the right-hand side of the normalized form above.
The declarations
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_card_add_eq_bell} and
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_bell} apply the same
rewrite to the all-offset and \(s\ge1\) reciprocal-grid moments.  These
normalized identities are stated over characteristic-zero fields; the direct
complete-homogeneous specialization uses the rational-algebra structure.
The shifted-reciprocal definitions require no
positivity or nonvanishing hypothesis on \(\lambda\), because field inversion
in Lean is total.

For the actual Fabius endpoint problem, put
\(x_{\lambda,j}=(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)}\).  The module
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseLockedPullback.lean} pulls the arbitrary-order
logarithmic expansion back to each fixed \(x_{\lambda,j}\), freezing
the one-periodic correction and all periodic saddle coefficients at the base
phase \(\lambda\).  The estimates in
\path{LambertReciprocalAsymptotics.lean} show that the fixed order-\(r\)
reciprocal Lagrange row has total variation \(\BigO(\lambda^r)\) and
balance this growth against sufficiently high shifted inverse powers.  Writing
\(\Psi\) for \lean{negativeLaplacePsi}, the definitions
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedReducedSample},
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator},
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm}, and
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum} in
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtraction.lean} give, for every fixed
\(r,S\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 E_r(\lambda)-\Psi(\lambda)-\sum_{n=0}^{S-1}R_{r,n}(\lambda)
 =\BigO\bigl(\lambda^{-(r+1+S)}\bigr),
 \qquad \lambda\to+\infty .                         \label{eq:phase-locked-finite-extraction}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_residual_isBigO};
its proof uses the node expansion through order \(2r+S+1\).  The cases
\(S=0\) and \(S=1\) are the named leading and first-omitted-moment
bounds, and
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_tendsto_periodicAlong} gives
convergence to \(\Psi(a)\) along \(\lambda=a+n\).  These are
fixed-index Poincaré estimates; no uniformity for \(r\) or \(S\)
growing with \(\lambda\) is claimed.

Finally, \path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtractionBell.lean} changes neither the
residual definitions nor the preceding estimate.  It gives the exact rewrite
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 R_{r,n}(\lambda)
   &=(-1)^r\left(\prod_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^{-1}\right) \\
   &\quad{}\times \operatorname{factorialNormalize}
     \bigl(\operatorname{completeBellPolynomial}
       (\kappa^{(r,\lambda)})\bigr)_n
     \,c_{r+1+n}(\lambda),
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
where \(\kappa^{(r,\lambda)}\) is
\lean{shiftedReciprocalBellInput} and \(c_m\) denotes
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}.  The single-term and finite-sum
declarations are \lean{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell} and
\lean{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell}.  This three-module
Bell tranche closes only the finite complete-homogeneous/Bell/generalized-
harmonic conversion and the exact finite residual rewrites.  It proves no
convergence of the infinite residual series, multiplicative or exponentiated
Bell relative-error hierarchy, higher extractor, Lambert sign or bracketing
claim, or growing-order uniformity.

\begin{proposition}[Principal specialization and denominator-free geometric residuals]
\label{prop:geometric-principal-residuals}
Let $R$ first be a commutative semiring.  For arbitrary $c,q\in R$ and
$p,r\in\NaturalNumbers$, complete homogeneous evaluation on a geometric alphabet is
\begin{align}
 h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^p)
   &=\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{r}{q},              \label{eq:complete-homogeneous-geometric}\\
 h_r(c,cq,\ldots,cq^p)
   &=c^r\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}=c^r\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{r}{q}.        \label{eq:complete-homogeneous-scaled-geometric}
\end{align}
These identities are recursive and denominator-free: they remain valid at
$q=0$, at roots of unity, in positive characteristic, and in the zero
semiring.

Now let $R$ be a commutative ring and let $w_0,\ldots,w_p\in R$.  If
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(q^k)^d=0^d\qquad(0\le d\le p),        \label{eq:geometric-exact-row-hypothesis}
\end{equation}
then every positive moment is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(q^k)^m
 =(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q},
 \qquad m>0.                                             \label{eq:geometric-exact-row-all-moments}
\end{equation}
There is a strictly more general scaled-node form.  If exactness is assumed
directly on the displayed nodes,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(cq^k)^d=0^d\qquad(0\le d\le p),       \label{eq:scaled-geometric-exact-row-hypothesis}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(cq^k)^m
 =c^m(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q},
 \qquad m>0.                                             \label{eq:scaled-geometric-exact-row-all-moments}
\end{equation}
No nonzeroness or unit hypothesis is imposed on $c$; this direct scaled
hypothesis is therefore meaningful even for zero divisors.

Over a field, distinctness of $1,q,\ldots,q^p$ supplies
\eqref{eq:geometric-exact-row-hypothesis} for the Lagrange weights
$\lambda_{p,k}^{(q)}$.  More precisely, injectivity of \(k\mapsto q^k\) on
\(0\leq k\leq p\) gives, for every \(P\in F[X]\) with
\(\operatorname{natDegree}P\leq p\),
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,k}^{(q)}P(cq^k)=P(0).
\]
Here \(c\) is arbitrary, including zero; this strictly subsumes the
nonzero-scale premise of the q-monograph's
\nolinkurl{cor:scaled-geometric-moments}.  Together with
\eqref{eq:scaled-geometric-exact-row-all-moments}, it makes that corollary
Exact by composition.  Consequently, for every block start $s\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,k}^{(q)}(q^{s+k})^m
 =(-1)^p q^{sm+\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q},
 \qquad m>0.                                             \label{eq:shifted-geometric-lagrange-all-moments}
\end{equation}
In particular, at block start zero and degree \(m=p+1+r\), complementary-index
symmetry gives the report-facing orientation
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,k}^{(q)}(q^k)^{p+1+r}
 =(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{r}{q}.           \label{eq:geometric-complete-homogeneous-residual}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The semiring-level infrastructure is
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_smul},
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_option_zero},
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_fin_succ},
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range}, and
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalAt_zero} in
\path{CompleteHomogeneous.lean}.  Splitting off the leading variable $1$
and scaling the remaining alphabet by $q$ turns the head--tail recurrence
into q-Pascal's recurrence.  This proves
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_geometric}; its common-scale and range-indexed
forms are \lean{completeHomogeneousEval_scaled_geometric} and
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial}.  A second
direct principal-specialization induction gives the degree-indexed orientation
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial_degree}.
Comparing the two orientations proves the denominator-free identities
\lean{gaussianBinomial_add_symm} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_symm_via_completeHomogeneous}; the latter is a second,
principal-specialization proof of the core reflection theorem.  The
report-facing range alias is \lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_geometric_range};
all of these declarations are in \path{GeometricCompleteHomogeneous.lean} or
\path{GeometricLagrangeCompleteHomogeneous.lean}.

The offset-degree engines for $m=p+1+r$ are
\lean{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_succ_add} and
\lean{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_succ_add}.  Above-diagonal
vanishing of the recursive Gaussian coefficient folds the cancelled degrees
and every higher residual into
\lean{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_of_pos} and
\lean{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos}.  Their field-valued
Lagrange specializations are
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_succ_add},
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_of_pos},
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos}, and
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_shifted_pow_of_pos}, together with the
polynomial-exactness theorem
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_eval_scaled_geometric}, in the
zero-definition/nine-theorem module
\path{GeometricResidualMoments.lean}.  The pre-existing pointwise closed
weight and cleared-denominator formulas remain in
\path{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean}; its positive-moment theorem is now a
thin corollary of this denominator-free engine.  Finally,
\eqref{eq:geometric-complete-homogeneous-residual} is
\lean{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_succ_add_eq_gaussianBinomial} in
\path{GeometricLagrangeCompleteHomogeneous.lean}; it changes only the
complementary Gaussian index and introduces no new interpolation argument.
\end{proof}

The same moment calculation acts diagonally on every formal power series, not
only on a polynomial of bounded degree.  This produces a reusable operator in
which low-degree cancellation and every surviving geometric residual are
visible coefficient by coefficient.

\begin{proposition}[Finite formal-series filters and the Gaussian residual]
\label{prop:finite-power-series-filter}
Let \(R\) be a commutative semiring, let \(\iota\) be an arbitrary index type,
let \(S\subseteq\iota\) be finite, and choose nodes \(x_i\in R\), weights
\(w_i\in R\), and a formal power series \(f\in R[[X]]\).  Repeated nodes are
allowed.  Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal T_{S,x,w}f(X)=\sum_{i\in S}w_i f(x_iX).           \label{eq:finite-series-filter}
\end{equation}
Then, for every \(m\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 [X^m]\mathcal T_{S,x,w}f
 =\left(\sum_{i\in S}w_ix_i^m\right)[X^m]f.               \label{eq:finite-series-filter-coeff}
\end{equation}
The operator commutes with a further rescaling of \(X\), and with every
commutative-semiring homomorphism when the nodes, weights, and coefficients
are transported together.

More generally, suppose a target \(t\in R\) and an order \(p\in\NaturalNumbers\) satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{i\in S}w_ix_i^d=t^d\qquad(0\le d\le p).            \label{eq:finite-series-filter-exact}
\end{equation}
Degree zero in this hypothesis gives \(\sum_iw_i=1\).  Hence the filter
preserves the constant coefficient, and for every \(m\le p\),
\begin{equation}
 [X^m]\mathcal T_{S,x,w}f=t^m[X^m]f.                      \label{eq:finite-series-filter-low}
\end{equation}
At \(t=0\), this preserves degree zero and cancels every degree
\(1\le m\le p\), including the boundary case \(p=0\) without pretending that
\(0^0\) vanishes.

Now let \(R\) be a commutative ring, take the nodes
\(1,q,\ldots,q^p\), and assume the exactness condition
\eqref{eq:geometric-exact-row-hypothesis}.  For
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal G_{q,p,w}f(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{p}w_jf(q^jX),
\end{equation*}
all coefficients, including the mass-one boundary, are
\begin{equation}
 [X^m]\mathcal G_{q,p,w}f=
 \begin{cases}
  [X^0]f,&m=0,\\[2pt]
  (-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q}[X^m]f,&m>0.
 \end{cases}                                               \label{eq:geometric-series-filter-residual}
\end{equation}
Thus the above-diagonal zero of the recursive Gaussian coefficient gives all
cancelled degrees, and the same formula gives every later residual.  No
division, convergence, distinctness, nonzeroness, or field structure is used.
Over a field, distinctness of the geometric nodes makes the Lagrange row a
canonical specialization of the same identity.
\end{proposition}

The generic operator and its diagonal action are
\lean{finitePowerSeriesFilter} and
\lean{coeff_finitePowerSeriesFilter} in
\path{FinitePowerSeriesFilter.lean}.  Its rescaling and scalar-transport laws
are \lean{finitePowerSeriesFilter_rescale} and
\lean{map_finitePowerSeriesFilter}; exactness, mass one, constant preservation,
and low-degree cancellation are
\lean{sum_weight_eq_one_of_finite_exact},
\lean{constantCoeff_finitePowerSeriesFilter_of_exact},
\lean{coeff_finitePowerSeriesFilter_of_exact_of_le}, and
\lean{coeff_finitePowerSeriesFilter_eq_zero_of_pos_of_le}.
The geometric operator is \lean{geometricSeriesFilter}; its positive-degree
coefficient theorem and the all-index identity
\eqref{eq:geometric-series-filter-residual} are
\lean{coeff_geometricSeriesFilter_of_exact} and
\lean{geometricSeriesFilter_eq_residual_mk} in
\path{GeometricPowerSeriesFilter.lean}.  The distinct-node Lagrange
specializations are
\lean{coeff_geometricLagrangeSeriesFilter_of_pos} and
\lean{geometricLagrangeSeriesFilter_eq_residual_mk}.  Every series in this
paragraph is formal: none of these declarations asserts analytic convergence,
evaluation at a nonzero argument, or an infinite acceleration formula.

There is now a separate convergent-series companion.  It deliberately uses
Mathlib's unconditional summability rather than silently identifying a natural-order
boundary sum with a \(\mathsf{tsum}\).

\begin{proposition}[Unconditionally summable analytic filters and their Gaussian tail]
\label{prop:analytic-series-filter}
Let \(K\) be a commutative semiring, let \(E\) be a normed additive commutative group
with a \(K\)-module structure, let \(S\) be finite, and choose nodes \(x_i\in K\),
weights \(w_i\in K\), coefficients \(a_m\in E\), and a scale \(z\in K\).  Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_{S,x,w}(a;z)
 :=\sum_{i\in S}\sum_{m\ge0}w_i(x_i z)^m a_m.              \label{eq:finite-analytic-series-filter}
\end{equation}
Assume for every \(i\in S\) that the displayed weighted inner series is
