unconditionally summable.  Then the finite and infinite sums interchange and
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_{S,x,w}(a;z)
 =\sum_{m\ge0}\left(\sum_{i\in S}w_i x_i^m\right)z^m a_m. \label{eq:finite-analytic-series-filter-diagonal}
\end{equation}
If, moreover,
\(\sum_{i\in S}w_i x_i^d=t^d\) for \(0\le d\le p\), then
\begin{align}
 \mathcal A_{S,x,w}(a;z)
 &=\sum_{d=0}^{p}(tz)^d a_d                                      \notag\\
 &\quad+\sum_{r\ge0}
 \left(\sum_{i\in S}w_i x_i^{p+1+r}\right)
 z^{p+1+r}a_{p+1+r}.                                      \label{eq:finite-analytic-series-filter-head-tail}
\end{align}
At target \(t=0\), the finite head is exactly \(a_0\), including \(p=0\); the
positive degrees never pass through the \(0^0\) boundary.

Now let \(K\) be a commutative ring with continuous constant scalar multiplication on
\(E\) (each fixed scalar acts continuously), take
\(x_j=q^j\) for \(0\le j\le p\), and suppose the row is exact at zero through
degree \(p\); write \(\mathcal A_{q,p,w}\) for this geometric specialization.  It is
enough to assume unconditional summability of
\(\sum_m(q^jz)^m a_m\) only when \(w_j\ne0\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_{q,p,w}(a;z)
 =a_0+\sum_{r\ge0}
 (-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}
 z^{p+1+r}a_{p+1+r}.                                     \label{eq:geometric-analytic-series-filter-tail}
\end{equation}
Over a field, distinct geometric nodes supply the Lagrange specialization and its
shifted-scale form \(z=q^{\mathrm{start}}\).  A zero-weight node imposes no convergence
condition: its weighted series is identically zero.
\end{proposition}

The definitions are \lean{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter} and
\lean{geometricAnalyticSeriesFilter} in \path{AnalyticSeriesFilter.lean}.  The generic
diagonal, exact-head, and zero-target declarations are
\lean{summable_finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_diagonal},
\lean{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_tsum},
\lean{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_head_add_tail_of_exact}, and
\lean{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_constant_add_tail_of_exact_zero}.  The Gaussian,
Lagrange, and shifted-scale tail declarations are
\lean{geometricAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_constant_add_gaussian_tsum},
\lean{geometricLagrangeAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_constant_add_gaussian_tsum}, and
\lean{geometricLagrangeAnalyticSeriesFilter_shifted}.  Thus this module has exactly two
definitions and seven public theorems.  These results exclude series converging only in
their natural order at a boundary point.  They also assert no formal-to-analytic
power-series evaluation bridge, radius or uniform-convergence theorem,
norm/sign/error bound, asymptotic estimate, or positivity statement.  The
actual Rvachev sinc-product instantiation is a separate theorem, so that the
generic module retains precisely this abstract scope.

\begin{theorem}[Exact Gaussian filter of the Rvachev sinc product]
\label{thm:rvachev-gaussian-filter}
Let \(c,z\in\ComplexNumbers\), let \(p\in\NaturalNumbers\), put \(q=c^2\), and assume only that
\[
 j\longmapsto q^j
 \quad\hbox{is injective on }\{0,\ldots,p\}.
\]
Writing \(\Phi=\lean{rvachevFourierProduct}=\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}\), one has the
exact entire identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{p}\lambda_{p,j}^{(q)}\Phi(c^jz)
 =1+\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}
   (-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}
   A_{p+1+r}(z).                                         \label{eq:rvachev-gaussian-filter}
\end{equation}
There is no contraction, realness, positivity, or global nonvanishing
hypothesis on \(c\).  At \(c=1/2\), hence \(q=1/4\), the finite node
condition is proved internally, so \eqref{eq:rvachev-gaussian-filter} holds
for every complex \(z\) and every natural \(p\) with no hypothesis.
The same generic formula is exported for the Fourier transform of every
bounded \(F\) satisfying \(\lean{IsFabius}\ F\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The named moment series sums to the standalone product by
\lean{hasSum_rvachevFourierMomentTerm_product}, and its iterated scaling law
is \lean{rvachevFourierMomentTerm_pow_scale}.  Passing the finite Lagrange
row through that unconditional sum and inserting the Gaussian residual gives
\lean{geometricLagrange_rvachevFourierProduct_eq_gaussian_tsum}.  Its
assumption-free \(c=1/2\), \(q=1/4\) specialization is
\lean{quarterLagrange_rvachevFourierProduct_eq_gaussian_tsum}; the
model-generic Fourier-transform form is
\lean{geometricLagrange_rvachevFourier_eq_gaussian_tsum}.  These five
declarations exhaust \path{RvachevQBinomialFilter.lean}.
\end{proof}

The theorem closes the coefficient bridge for the \emph{infinite} Rvachev
product.  It does not identify the frontier report's finite products
\(P_{b,n}\), their quotient \(P_{b,n}/P_b\), or their Bell coefficients with
this series.  Nor does it prove a conditionally convergent boundary theorem,
an analytic sign or bracketing/error bound, uniform or derivative
convergence, or an asymptotic acceleration estimate.  The algebraic
\((-1)^p\) in \eqref{eq:rvachev-gaussian-filter} should not be promoted to
one of those analytic sign claims.

For the present half-geometric grid, write $\lambda_{n,k}^{(1/2)}$ for the
evaluation-at-zero weight belonging to the node $2^{-k}$.  The Toeplitz row is exactly the
same row in reverse order:
\begin{equation*}
 \boxed{\omega_{n,j}=\lambda_{n,n-j}^{(1/2)}}\qquad(0\le j\le n).
\end{equation*}
Indeed, for every $d\ge0$ its complete reversed moment is
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\omega_{n,n-k}2^{-kd}
 =2^{-nd}\frac{(2^d/2;1/2)_n}{(1/2;1/2)_n},
\end{equation*}
and for $0\le d\le n$ this is $0^d$, with $0^0=1$.  The pointwise bridge and
these two moment forms are
\lean{discreteLimitWeight_eq_geometricLagrangeWeight},
\lean{geometricLagrangeWeight_half_eq_discreteLimitWeight},
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_reverse_mul_half_pow}, and
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_reverse_moment}.  Their finite-sum form is
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_smul_eq_geometricLagrangeWeight}.

For every $d>0$, the same reversed row has the sharper residual form
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\omega_{n,n-k}\bigl((1/2)^k\bigr)^d
 =(-1)^n(1/2)^{n(n+1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{d-1}{n}{1/2}.             \label{eq:toeplitz-complete-residual}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{sum_range_discreteLimitWeight_reverse_moment_eq_halfQBinomial} in
\path{FabiusDiscreteLimitToeplitz.lean}.  Thus one Gaussian coefficient records every
positive residual degree: its above-diagonal boundary gives the cancelled range, while
its diagonal boundary gives the first surviving moment.  The corresponding boundary
declarations are \lean{gaussianBinomial_eq_zero_of_lt} and
\lean{gaussianBinomial_self} in \path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean}.

Consequently the global model in the proposition can be weakened to one finite block.
For any $s\in\NaturalNumbers$, if only the samples $0\le k\le n$ satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 u_{s+k}=L+\sum_{r=1}^{n}2^{-r(s+k)}\mathbin{\cdot}a_r,
\end{equation*}
then
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}\mathbin{\cdot}u_{s+n-j}=L.
\end{equation*}
This is \lean{discreteLimitWeight_qRichardson_exact_module_at_of_eq} in
\path{FabiusDiscreteLimitToeplitz.lean}.  At $n=0$ its hypothesis is simply $u_s=L$,
and $s=0$ needs no separate convention.  The grid ratio is $1/2$.  A mode with ratio
$1/4$ and index $r$ has half-grid degree $2r$, so this row cancels it only when
$2r\le n$; this is not an $n$-mode theorem on a $1/4$ grid.
\end{remark}

Applying it with the opposite sign also gives the uniform variation bound
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}|\omega_{n,j}|
 =\frac{(-1/2;1/2)_n}{(1/2;1/2)_n}\le16.                 \label{eq:discrete-row-variation}
\end{equation}
Finally, every sampled degree satisfies $2n-j\ge n$.  A finite-row Toeplitz lemma therefore
carries the convergence of $S_p^{(Q)}(x)$ to the weighted rows
\eqref{eq:discrete-toeplitz-reindex}.  Explicitly, given $\epsilon>0$, choose $P$ so that
$|S_p^{(Q)}(x)-\FabiusGlobal(x)|<\epsilon/16$ for $p\ge P$.  If $n\ge P$, then every
$2n-j\ge n\ge P$, and the row-sum identity together with
\eqref{eq:discrete-row-variation} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}S_{2n-j}^{(Q)}(x)-\FabiusGlobal(x)\right|
 \le\sum_{j=0}^{n}|\omega_{n,j}|\,
 |S_{2n-j}^{(Q)}(x)-\FabiusGlobal(x)|<\epsilon.
\end{equation*}
This proves \eqref{eq:discrete-q-limit}.
\end{proof}

Two distinctions prevent common misreadings of this theorem.  First, unlike the complete
finite dyadic numerator and each summand of the global $q$-series, a finite value
$\mathcal D_n^{(Q)}(x)$ can depend on $Q$; it is the limit that is independent of a fixed
shift.  Second, the Toeplitz row \eqref{eq:discrete-toeplitz-reindex} is not a finite binary
telescope or a partial sum of \eqref{eq:global-q-binomial-series}.  These finite expressions
share a limit but are not equal term by term.

The two properties of the reindexed row \eqref{eq:discrete-toeplitz-weight} used above are
both evaluations of the finite $q$-binomial theorem \eqref{eq:half-q-binomial-theorem}, at
$z=1/2$ and at $z=-1/2$ respectively.  Both use the exponent identity
$\binom j2+j=\binom{j+1}2$.

For the row sum, evaluate \eqref{eq:half-q-binomial-theorem} at $z=1/2$.  The left side is
$(1/2;1/2)_n$ by definition, and the right side is
\begin{equation}
 (1/2;1/2)_n
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j2^{-\binom j2}\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}\left(\frac12\right)^{j}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}\,2^{-\binom{j+1}2}.  \label{eq:qs-row-sum-derivation}
\end{equation}
Dividing by $(1/2;1/2)_n$ gives exactly $\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}=1$, which is
\eqref{eq:discrete-row-sum}.

For the total variation, evaluate \eqref{eq:half-q-binomial-theorem} at $z=-1/2$ instead.
Each factor $(-1)^j(-1/2)^j=2^{-j}$ is now positive, so the alternating signs disappear:
\begin{equation}
 (-1/2;1/2)_n
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j2^{-\binom j2}\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}\left(-\frac12\right)^{j}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}\,2^{-\binom{j+1}2}.        \label{eq:qs-row-variation-derivation}
\end{equation}
Since $\GaussianBinomial nj{1/2}>0$, the summands of \eqref{eq:qs-row-variation-derivation} are the
absolute values of those of \eqref{eq:qs-row-sum-derivation}.  Dividing by $(1/2;1/2)_n$
gives the exact evaluation
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}|\omega_{n,j}|=\frac{(-1/2;1/2)_n}{(1/2;1/2)_n}, \label{eq:qs-row-variation-exact}
\end{equation}
which is the first equality in \eqref{eq:discrete-row-variation}.  The formally proved
finite-product estimate supplies the bound by $16$ used in the Toeplitz argument above.

\section{Iterated prefix sums of the signed Thue--Morse sequence}
\label{tm:sec-prefix}

The signed Thue--Morse sequence $\varepsilon_n=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}$ has already appeared twice in
this article: once as the coefficient sequence of the locally finite global expansion
\eqref{eq:global-extension}, and once as the sign pattern that makes the finite dyadic sums
of \cref{sec:dyadic} collapse.  There is a third, entirely discrete, way in which
the same signs produce the Fabius function.  Sum the sequence, sum the result, and iterate.
After $k$ summations the sequence has been convolved with an explicit binomial kernel, and
after a dyadic normalization the resulting integer grid reproduces, in the
limit, the Rvachev bump and hence $F$.

This section develops that construction: the convolution law and its explicit binomial
kernel, the signed dyadic reflection preceding each terminal zero run, the zero runs
themselves and their exact boundary values, the two generating-series identities, their
analytic realization and natural-boundary theorem, the discrete functional equation
satisfied by the normalized grid, and the
precise sense in which the normalized grid converges.  The convergence statement requires
one shift of the prefix order and the centered reading of the grid cells; the literal
normalization does not converge, and that failure is recorded separately in
\cref{tm:sub-literal-endpoint}.  Two further pieces of foundational material are attached at
the end: the lower branch of Lambert's $W$-function, which the asymptotic sections of this
article use but never construct, and an exact binomial--logarithm formula for the
Thue--Morse bit.

The two binary recurrences used throughout this section are
\begin{align}
 \varepsilon_{2m}&=\varepsilon_m,&
 \varepsilon_{2m+1}&=-\varepsilon_m,                       \label{eq:tm-even-odd}\\
 \BinaryDigitSum(2m)&=\BinaryDigitSum(m),&
 \BinaryDigitSum(2m+1)&=\BinaryDigitSum(m)+1.                                     \label{eq:weight-even-odd}
\end{align}
They are exactly
\lean{thueMorseSign_two_mul},
\lean{thueMorseSign_two_mul_add_one},
\lean{binaryWeight_two_mul}, and
\lean{binaryWeight_two_mul_add_one} in \path{DyadicClosedForm.lean}.

\subsection{Iterated prefix sums and the binomial convolution law}
\label{tm:sub-convolution}

\begin{definition}[Iterated prefix sums]
\label{thm:tm-prefix-def}
Set $\sigma_0(n)=\varepsilon_n$ and, for $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_{k+1}(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sigma_k(j).                \label{eq:tm-prefix-def}
\end{equation}
All sums are \emph{inclusive}: the index $n$ itself is summed.
\end{definition}

Every $\sigma_k(n)$ is an integer, and $\sigma_k(0)=1$ for all $k$.  The inclusive
convention has one consequence that must be kept in view throughout, because it is the
source of an index shift later:
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_{k+1}(n+1)-\sigma_{k+1}(n)=\sigma_k(n+1).          \label{eq:tm-forward-diff}
\end{equation}
The increment of a prefix sum is the lower-order value at the \emph{new} index, not at the
old one.

The first prefix sum nevertheless vanishes at every odd index:
\[
 \sigma_1(2m+1)=0\qquad(m\in\NaturalNumbers).
\]
This is exactly \lean{iteratedPrefix_one_two_mul_add_one} in
\path{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.

The general structure is a discrete convolution.  Write
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal B_k(i)=\binom{i+k-1}{k-1}\qquad(k\ge1,\ i\ge0)   \label{eq:tm-kernel}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{iteratedPrefixKernel}.  Because the argument \(i=n-m\) is
nonnegative in the sums below, no separate indicator is needed.

\begin{theorem}[Binomial convolution law]
\label{thm:tm-convolution}
For every $k\ge1$ and every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_k(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\mathcal B_k(n-m)\,\varepsilon_m
 =\sum_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n-m+k-1}{k-1}\varepsilon_m.         \label{eq:tm-kernel-sum}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, in a form valid for every $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_{k+1}(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n-m+k}{k}\varepsilon_m. \label{eq:tm-kernel-sum-shifted}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $k$ in the form \eqref{eq:tm-kernel-sum-shifted}.  For $k=0$ the binomial
coefficient is $1$ and the assertion is the definition of $\sigma_1$.  Assume
\eqref{eq:tm-kernel-sum-shifted} for $k$.  Then
\begin{align*}
 \sigma_{k+2}(n)
 &=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sigma_{k+1}(j)
  =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{m=0}^{j}\binom{j-m+k}{k}\varepsilon_m\\
 &=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\varepsilon_m\sum_{i=0}^{n-m}\binom{i+k}{k}
  =\sum_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n-m+k+1}{k+1}\varepsilon_m,
\end{align*}
where the interchange is the triangular reindexing $i=j-m$ and the inner evaluation is the
hockey-stick identity $\sum_{i=0}^{N}\binom{i+k}{k}=\binom{N+k+1}{k+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The kernel in \eqref{eq:tm-kernel-sum} is exactly the coefficient sequence of
$(1-z)^{-k}$.  \Cref{thm:tm-convolution} is therefore the coefficient form of the series
identity proved in \cref{thm:tm-generating} below, and either statement may be taken as the
definition of the other.  Both are recorded here because the coefficient form is what one
actually evaluates, while the series form is what one manipulates.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Prouhet annihilation, signed dyadic reciprocity, and exact zero runs}
\label{tm:sub-zero-runs}

\Cref{eq:thue-affine-cancel} already records that a complete dyadic block of Thue--Morse
signs annihilates every polynomial of degree below the block exponent.  For the prefix sums
we also need the sharp boundary case, which identifies the first power that survives.

\begin{lemma}[Sharp Prouhet extraction]
\label{thm:tm-prouhet-extract}
Write $S_{r,d}=\sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_h h^d$.  Then $S_{r,d}=0$ for $0\le d<r$, and
\begin{equation}
 S_{r,r}=(-1)^r2^{\binom r2}r!.                            \label{eq:tm-power-sum-sharp}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for all rational numbers $a_0,\ldots,a_r$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_h\sum_{d=0}^{r}a_dh^d
 =a_r\,(-1)^r2^{\binom r2}r!,                              \label{eq:tm-polynomial-extract}
\end{equation}
so summation against a complete dyadic block of Thue--Morse signs annihilates every
polynomial of degree below $r$ and, on polynomials of degree at most $r$, extracts the
leading coefficient up to the explicit nonzero factor $(-1)^r2^{\binom r2}r!$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The vanishing statement is \eqref{eq:thue-affine-cancel} with $X=0$, $Y=1$.  For the
boundary case, split the block by parity rather than by halves.  Since
$\varepsilon_{2j}=\varepsilon_j$ and $\varepsilon_{2j+1}=-\varepsilon_j$ by
\eqref{eq:tm-even-odd},
\begin{equation*}
 S_{r+1,d}
 =\sum_{j<2^r}\varepsilon_j\bigl[(2j)^d-(2j+1)^d\bigr]
 =-\sum_{c=0}^{d-1}\binom dc2^{c}S_{r,c},
\end{equation*}
because $(2j+1)^d-(2j)^d=\sum_{c<d}\binom dc(2j)^c$.  Take $d=r+1$.  All terms with $c<r$
vanish by the first assertion, leaving only $c=r$:
\begin{equation*}
 S_{r+1,r+1}=-\binom{r+1}{r}2^rS_{r,r}=-(r+1)2^rS_{r,r}.
\end{equation*}
Since $S_{0,0}=\varepsilon_0=1$, induction gives
$S_{r,r}=(-1)^rr!\,2^{0+1+\cdots+(r-1)}=(-1)^rr!\,2^{\binom r2}$, which is
\eqref{eq:tm-power-sum-sharp}.  Formula \eqref{eq:tm-polynomial-extract} follows by
exchanging the two sums: every index $d<r$ contributes $a_dS_{r,d}=0$, and the index $d=r$
contributes $a_rS_{r,r}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Affine sharp case]
\label{thm:tm-affine-sharp}
For all $X,Y\in\RationalNumbers$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_h(X+Yh)^r=(-1)^rY^r2^{\binom r2}r!.
\end{equation*}
In particular the sum does not depend on the translation $X$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Expand $(X+Yh)^r$ by the binomial theorem and apply \eqref{eq:tm-polynomial-extract}: the
coefficient of $h^r$ is $Y^r$.
\end{proof}

The sharp extractor also has a direct commutative-ring form.  If $R$ is any commutative
ring and $p\in R[X]$ satisfies $\deg p\le r$, then, with the signs and natural numbers
mapped into $R$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_h p(h)
 =[X^r]p\;(-1)^r2^{\binom r2}r!.                            \label{eq:tm-polynomial-extract-ring}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{thueMorse_polynomial_sum_eq_coeff_ring_of_degree_le}; its strict-degree
cancellation form is \lean{thueMorse_polynomial_sum_eq_zero_ring_of_degree_lt}, both in
\path{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.  The word ``extractor'' here describes the functional
identity, not division by its sharp factor: in positive characteristic the image of
$2^{\binom r2}r!$ can vanish, so the displayed equality need not recover $[X^r]p$ from
the sum.  The degree-valued hypothesis also treats the zero polynomial correctly; at
$r=0$ the extraction formula includes constant polynomials and the zero polynomial,
whereas the strict-degree condition admits only the latter.

Likewise the affine sharp identity holds directly in $R$:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_h(x+yh)^r
 =(-1)^r y^r2^{\binom r2}r!,                               \label{eq:tm-affine-sharp-ring}
\end{equation}
formalized as \lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_self_ring}, with the unified range
$d\le r$ given by \lean{thueMorse_affine_power_sum_of_le_ring}.  For $r>0$ the sharp
sum vanishes at $y=0$.  At the joint boundary $r=0$, $y=0$, Lean's convention
$0^0=1$ instead gives the correct one-term value $1$ on both sides; no positive-degree
reading should be imposed on that case.

The usual equal-sums formulation is now available without leaving the same
commutative-ring setting.

\begin{proposition}[Even--odd Prouhet partition]
\label{thm:tm-even-odd-prouhet}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring, let $s\subseteq\NaturalNumbers$ be finite, and let
$g\colon\NaturalNumbers\to R$.  With the integer sign $\varepsilon_h$ and each
natural index mapped into $R$, one has
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h\in s}\varepsilon_hg(h)
 =\sum_{\substack{h\in s\\ \BinaryDigitSum(h)\ \mathrm{even}}}g(h)
  -\sum_{\substack{h\in s\\ \BinaryDigitSum(h)\ \mathrm{odd}}}g(h).
                                                               \label{eq:tm-even-odd-split}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $d,r\in\NaturalNumbers$ and $d<r$, then for every $x,y\in R$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\substack{0\le h<2^r\\ \BinaryDigitSum(h)\ \mathrm{even}}}(x+yh)^d
 =\sum_{\substack{0\le h<2^r\\ \BinaryDigitSum(h)\ \mathrm{odd}}}(x+yh)^d.       \label{eq:tm-even-odd-affine-prouhet}
\end{equation}
In particular, setting $x=0$ and $y=1$ gives equality of the $d$th power sums
of the even- and odd-binary-weight parts of $\{0,\ldots,2^r-1\}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every binary weight is either even or odd, and the two cases are disjoint.  By
the definition $\varepsilon_h=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(h)}$, the sign is $1$ in
the first case and $-1$ in the second, which proves
\eqref{eq:tm-even-odd-split}.  For the full dyadic block, expand the signed
affine sum as
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{d}\binom dk x^{d-k}y^k
       \sum_{h<2^r}\varepsilon_hh^k.
\]
Every inner moment vanishes because $k\le d<r$.  Thus the left side of
\eqref{eq:tm-even-odd-split}, with $g(h)=(x+yh)^d$, is zero, and the two
filtered sums agree.  The raw-power statement is the indicated specialization.
\end{proof}

The three formal counterparts are respectively
\lean{sum_thueMorseSign_mul_eq_sum_even_binaryWeight_sub_sum_odd_binaryWeight},
\lean{sum_even_binaryWeight_affine_pow_eq_sum_odd_binaryWeight_affine_pow}, and
\lean{sum_even_binaryWeight_pow_eq_sum_odd_binaryWeight_pow}, all in
\path{ThueMorseSparseProuhet.lean}.  The first is valid on an arbitrary finite
set; the latter two use the whole dyadic block and the literal strict range
$d<r$.  No positive-characteristic nonvanishing assumption is needed, because
these are equalities rather than coefficient-recovery statements.

The binary-support parametrization used next has a canonical global foundation.  Write
\(\operatorname{bits}(n)=\operatorname{bitSupport}(n)\) and
\(E(T)=\sum_{j\in T}2^j\) for a finite set \(T\subseteq\NaturalNumbers\).  This support is exactly
Mathlib's finite set of binary indices, and
\begin{equation}
 j\in\operatorname{bits}(n)\Longleftrightarrow
 n.\operatorname{testBit}(j)=\mathrm{true},
 \qquad E(\operatorname{bits}(n))=n,
 \qquad \operatorname{bits}(E(T))=T.                     \label{eq:tm-bit-support-dictionary}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \BinaryDigitSum(E(T))=|T|,
 \qquad \varepsilon_{E(T)}=(-1)^{|T|}.                   \label{eq:tm-bit-support-weight-sign}
\end{equation}
The exact declarations are \lean{bitSupport_eq_toFinset_bitIndices},
\lean{mem_bitSupport}, \lean{sum_two_pow_bitSupport},
\lean{bitSupport_sum_two_pow}, \lean{binaryWeight_sum_two_pow_eq_card}, and
\lean{thueMorseSign_sum_two_pow} in \path{ThueMorseBitSupport.lean}.

Restricting the same inverse pair gives the canonical equivalence
\begin{equation*}
 \{T:T\subseteq\{j\in\NaturalNumbers:j<m\}\}\ \simeq\ \{h\in\NaturalNumbers:h<2^m\},
 \qquad T\longmapsto E(T),
\end{equation*}
and hence, for every function into an additive commutative monoid,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{T\subseteq\{j\in\NaturalNumbers:j<m\}} f(E(T))
 =\sum_{h=0}^{2^m-1}f(h).                                \label{eq:tm-powerset-dyadic-reindex}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{powersetRangeEquivFin} and \lean{sum_powerset_two_pow}; the exact image
statement is \lean{image_sum_two_pow_powerset_range}.  The singleton boundary at $m=0$
is included explicitly by \lean{powersetRangeEquivFin_zero_apply}, all in
\path{ThueMorseBitSupport.lean}.

The public Boolean-cube theorem uses the genuine degree
\(\deg p\in\NaturalNumbers\cup\{-\infty\}\), rather than the natural degree that assigns the zero
polynomial degree zero.  Over any commutative ring, for a finite set \(S\), weights
\(w_j\), and a polynomial satisfying \(\deg p<|S|\), it states
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|T|}
 p\!\left(x+\sum_{j\in T}w_j\right)=0.                    \label{eq:tm-powerset-degree-cancel}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{sum_powerset_neg_one_pow_eval_of_degree_lt} in
\path{ThueMorseSparseProuhet.lean}.  The degree convention matters at the boundary
\(S=\varnothing\): the hypothesis then forces \(p=0\), so the theorem correctly includes
the empty-support case instead of declaring it vacuous.  With \(S\) the bit support of
\(n\) and \(w_j=2^jh\), the corresponding sparse submask parametrization is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{T\subseteq\operatorname{bits}(n)}(-1)^{|T|}
 p\!\left(x+h\sum_{j\in T}2^j\right)=0
 \quad\text{when }\deg p<\BinaryDigitSum(n),                           \label{eq:tm-submask-degree-cancel}
\end{equation}
formalized as \lean{sum_submask_neg_one_pow_eval_of_degree_lt}.  It is valid also at
\(n=0\), where again the only polynomial satisfying the strict degree bound is zero.

At the level of the centered formal exponential series, the corresponding zero has exact
order \(r\); this is \lean{order_thueMorseCenteredPowerSeries}.  For the binomial kernel
\eqref{eq:tm-kernel}, the directly applicable formally proved form is instead the
coefficient-extraction theorem \lean{thueMorse_polynomial_sum_eq_coeff}, displayed in
\eqref{eq:tm-polynomial-extract}.

\begin{theorem}[Signed dyadic reciprocity]
\label{thm:tm-dyadic-reciprocity}
Let $k,r,d\in\NaturalNumbers$ satisfy $k\le r$ and $k+d<2^r$.  Then the part of the
order-$k$ prefix row immediately preceding its final $k$ positions is the
initial part of that row, read backwards and multiplied by one fixed sign:
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_k\bigl(2^r-k-1-d\bigr)
   =(-1)^{r-k}\sigma_k(d).                                \label{eq:tm-dyadic-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
Consequently reflection preserves, rather than excludes, its zero locus:
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_k\bigl(2^r-k-1-d\bigr)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sigma_k(d)=0.                                           \label{eq:tm-dyadic-zero-iff}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The identity \eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-reciprocity} and equivalence
\eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-zero-iff} are formalized respectively as
\lean{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window} and
\lean{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window_eq_zero_iff}.  At $k=0$ the first
identity is precisely the dyadic bit-complement law
\lean{thueMorseSign_dyadic_complement}.  Its proof below uses the independently
established left boundary in \cref{thm:tm-zero-run} as the seed for the prefix
recurrence.

\begin{theorem}[Exact dyadic zero runs]
\label{thm:tm-zero-run}
Let $1\le k\le r$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \sigma_k(n)&=0 &&\text{for } 2^r-k\le n\le2^r-1,          \label{eq:tm-zero-run}\\
 \sigma_k\bigl(2^r-k-1\bigr)&=(-1)^{r-k},                  \label{eq:tm-run-left}\\
 \sigma_k\bigl(2^r\bigr)&=-1.                              \label{eq:tm-run-right}
\end{align}
Formula \eqref{eq:tm-run-left} also holds for $k=0$, where it reduces to
$\varepsilon_{2^r-1}=(-1)^r$, and \eqref{eq:tm-run-right} also holds for $k=0$.  Hence the
zero run terminating at $2^r-1$ has length exactly $k$: it is bounded on the left by a value
of modulus one and on the right by the value $-1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and Lean correspondence]
The terminal block is formalized by
\lean{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_run}; its endpoint and reverse-indexed forms
are \lean{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_endpoint} and
\lean{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_reverse}.  Its left and right boundaries are
\lean{iteratedPrefix_before_dyadic_run} and
\lean{iteratedPrefix_at_dyadic}.  All five declarations are in
\path{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.  The mathematical argument behind them is as
follows.

\emph{Endpoint.}  Put $q=k-1$, so $0\le q<r$.  By \eqref{eq:tm-kernel-sum},
\begin{equation*}
 \sigma_k(2^r-1)
 =\sum_{m=0}^{2^r-1}\binom{2^r-1-m+q}{q}\varepsilon_m
 =\frac1{q!}\sum_{m<2^r}\varepsilon_m\prod_{i=1}^{q}\bigl(2^r-1-m+i\bigr).
\end{equation*}
The product is a polynomial in $m$ of degree $q<r$, so the sum vanishes by
\eqref{eq:tm-polynomial-extract}.

\emph{Rest of the run.}  We prove, by induction on $d$, that $\sigma_k(2^r-1-d)=0$ whenever
$1\le k\le r$ and $0\le d<k$.  The case $d=0$ is the endpoint.  Suppose the claim holds for
$d$ and that $d+1<k$, so $k\ge2$ and $1\le k-1\le r$.  The induction hypothesis applied at
order $k$ gives $\sigma_k(2^r-1-d)=0$, and applied at order $k-1$, which is legitimate
because $d<k-1$, gives $\sigma_{k-1}(2^r-1-d)=0$.  Now use \eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff} with
$n=2^r-1-(d+1)$, so that $n+1=2^r-1-d$:
\begin{equation*}
 0-\sigma_k(n)=\sigma_k(n+1)-\sigma_k(n)=\sigma_{k-1}(n+1)=0 .
\end{equation*}
This proves \eqref{eq:tm-zero-run}, since the indices $2^r-k,\ldots,2^r-1$ are exactly the
$2^r-1-d$ with $0\le d<k$.

\emph{Left boundary.}  Induct on $k$.  For $k=0$ the number $2^r-1$ has $r$ binary digits,
all equal to one, so $\varepsilon_{2^r-1}=(-1)^r$.  Assume \eqref{eq:tm-run-left} at order
$k$ and let $k+1\le r$.  Put $n=2^r-(k+1)-1$, so $n+1=2^r-k-1$.  By the induction hypothesis
$\sigma_k(n+1)=(-1)^{r-k}$, and by \eqref{eq:tm-zero-run} at order $k+1$ we have
$\sigma_{k+1}(n+1)=\sigma_{k+1}(2^r-(k+1))=0$.  Then \eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff} gives
$-\sigma_{k+1}(n)=(-1)^{r-k}$, that is, $\sigma_{k+1}(n)=(-1)^{r-(k+1)}$.

\emph{Right boundary.}  Induct on $k$.  For $k=0$, $\BinaryDigitSum(2^r)=1$, so
$\varepsilon_{2^r}=-1$.  If \eqref{eq:tm-run-right} holds at order $k$ and $k+1\le r$, then
by \eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff} and \eqref{eq:tm-zero-run},
$\sigma_{k+1}(2^r)=\sigma_{k+1}(2^r-1)+\sigma_k(2^r)=0-1=-1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:tm-dyadic-reciprocity}]
Induct first on the prefix order $k$.  At order zero, $\sigma_0=\varepsilon$, and
reflecting $d$ in the block $0,\ldots,2^r-1$ complements its first $r$ binary
digits.  Thus
$\varepsilon_{2^r-1-d}=(-1)^r\varepsilon_d$, which is
\eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-reciprocity} for $k=0$.

Now suppose the identity is known at order $k$ and prove it at order $k+1$ by
induction on $d$.  For $d=0$, equation \eqref{eq:tm-run-left} and
$\sigma_{k+1}(0)=1$ give exactly the required identity.  For the induction
step, put
\[
 n=2^r-(k+1)-1-(d+1),
 \qquad s=(-1)^{r-(k+1)}.
\]
The same-order induction hypothesis at offset $d$ and the lower-order
hypothesis at offset $d+1$ have the common reflected index $n+1$ and give
\[
 \sigma_{k+1}(n+1)=s\sigma_{k+1}(d),
 \qquad
 \sigma_k(n+1)=(-1)^{r-k}\sigma_k(d+1)=-s\sigma_k(d+1).
\]
Applying the forward-difference law \eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff} first at $n$
and then at $d$ therefore yields
\begin{align*}
 \sigma_{k+1}(n)
 &=\sigma_{k+1}(n+1)-\sigma_k(n+1)\\
 &=s\bigl(\sigma_{k+1}(d)+\sigma_k(d+1)\bigr)
  =s\sigma_{k+1}(d+1),
\end{align*}
which closes both inductions.  Finally, the multiplier $(-1)^{r-k}$ is
nonzero, so \eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-reciprocity} is equivalent to
\eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-zero-iff}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
At the level of statements, setting $d=0$ in
\eqref{eq:tm-dyadic-reciprocity} and using
\lean{iteratedPrefix_at_zero}, namely $\sigma_k(0)=1$, recovers the left
boundary \eqref{eq:tm-run-left}.  Reciprocity pairs any zeros that occur in
the pre-run window; it does not claim that window is zero-free.  Nor does it
imply the terminal zero run \eqref{eq:tm-zero-run} or the separate right
boundary \eqref{eq:tm-run-right}, both of which were proved independently
above.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
The right boundary value is not $+1$.  Every iterated prefix sum, at every order
$k\le r$, takes the value $-1$ at the index $2^r$.  This single sign is what makes the
literal normalization of \cref{tm:sub-grid-equation} fail at the right endpoint; see
\cref{tm:sub-literal-endpoint}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Two generating-series identities}
\label{tm:sub-generating}

All series in this subsection are formal power series over $\IntegerNumbers$; no convergence hypothesis
on a complex variable is used or needed.

Let
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal K_r(z)=\sum_{n<2^r}\varepsilon_nz^n,
 \qquad
 \mathcal K(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}\varepsilon_nz^n,
 \qquad
 \mathcal S_k(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}\sigma_k(n)z^n.               \label{eq:tm-series-def}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Block product and coefficient stabilization]
\label{thm:tm-block-product}
For every \(r\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal K_r(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\bigl(1-z^{2^j}\bigr),   \label{eq:tm-block-product}
\end{equation}
and, whenever \(n<2^r\), the coefficient of \(z^n\) in this finite product is
\(\varepsilon_n\).  Hence each coefficient stabilizes once the finite product reaches a
large enough dyadic block.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The finite product is \lean{thueMorseBlockPolynomial_eq_product}; the stable coefficient
identity is \lean{coeff_finite_thueMorse_product}, both in
\path{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Prefix generating series]
\label{thm:tm-generating}
For every \(k\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal S_k(z)=\frac{\mathcal K(z)}{(1-z)^k},            \label{eq:tm-prefix-series}
\end{equation}
or equivalently
\begin{equation}
 (1-z)^k\,\mathcal S_k(z)=\mathcal K(z).                  \label{eq:tm-prefix-series-cleared}
\end{equation}
For each fixed coefficient index, either side can be computed from a sufficiently long
finite product \eqref{eq:tm-block-product}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The two identities are \lean{iteratedPrefixSeries_eq} and
\lean{one_sub_X_pow_mul_iteratedPrefixSeries}; their finite coefficient bridge is
\lean{coeff_eq6_finite} in \path{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.
\end{proof}

We use \(\prod_{j\ge0}(1-z^{2^j})\) only as shorthand for the coefficientwise stabilization
of the finite products \eqref{eq:tm-block-product}; no separate infinite-product object is
asserted.

The two identities of \cref{thm:tm-generating} connect the prefix sums to the polynomial
approximants used in \cref{tm:sub-corrected}.  These are the combinatorial step polynomials
already introduced in \eqref{eq:four-P-product}; their coefficient interpretation and exact
degree were proved there.  In the index convention of this section,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal P_n(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(1+z+\cdots+z^{2^i-1}\bigr),
 \qquad
 g_n=\deg \mathcal P_n=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(2^i-1\bigr)=2^{n+1}-n-2.
                                                          \label{eq:tm-approx-poly}
\end{equation}
Since $1-z^{2^j}=(1-z)(1+z+\cdots+z^{2^j-1})$ and the factor with $j=0$ is trivial,
\eqref{eq:tm-block-product} factors as
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal K_{n+1}(z)=(1-z)^{n+1}\mathcal P_n(z).                    \label{eq:tm-block-vs-approx}
\end{equation}
Comparing \eqref{eq:tm-block-vs-approx} with \eqref{eq:tm-prefix-series-cleared} at order
$k=n+1$ and using stabilization below $2^{n+1}$ gives the exact coefficient identification
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_{n+1}(m)=\bigl[z^m\bigr]\mathcal P_n(z)\qquad(0\le m<2^{n+1}).
                                                          \label{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff}
\end{equation}
Note that \eqref{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff} must fail at $m=2^{n+1}$, because
$g_n<2^{n+1}$ makes the right side zero while the left side is $-1$ by
\eqref{eq:tm-run-right}.  That single index is the exceptional right endpoint of
\cref{thm:tm-corrected-convergence}.

\subsection{The analytic Thue--Morse series and its natural boundary}
\label{tm:sub-analytic-series}

The series \(\mathcal K\) in \eqref{eq:tm-series-def} was interpreted there as a formal
power series.  On the open unit disc the same coefficients define an analytic function,
and we retain the notation \(\mathcal K\):
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal K(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_nz^n,
 \qquad |z|<1.                                                \label{eq:tm-disc-series}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Analytic product and Mahler equation]
\label{thm:tm-disc-series}
For every \(z\in\ComplexNumbers\) with \(|z|<1\), the series \eqref{eq:tm-disc-series} converges and
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal K(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigl(1-z^{2^j}\bigr).     \label{eq:tm-analytic-product}
\end{equation}
The function \(\mathcal K\) is holomorphic on \(|z|<1\) and satisfies
\begin{align}
 \mathcal K(z)&=(1-z)\mathcal K(z^2),                         \label{eq:tm-mahler}\\
 \mathcal K(z)&=\left(\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}(1-z^{2^j})\right)
                 \mathcal K(z^{2^k}) \qquad(k\in\NaturalNumbers).        \label{eq:tm-mahler-iterate}
\end{align}
On the real radius,
\begin{equation}
 |\mathcal K(x)|\le 1-x\qquad(0\le x<1),                    \label{eq:tm-real-bound}
\end{equation}
and \(\mathcal K(0)=1\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The analytic function is \lean{thueMorseDiscSeries}.  Convergence, the product identity,
and holomorphy are respectively \lean{summable_thueMorseDiscSeries},
\lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_eq_tprod}, and
\lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_differentiableOn}.  Convergence of the product itself is
\lean{multipliable_one_sub_pow_two_pow_complex} in
\path{ThueMorseInfiniteProduct.lean}.  Equations \eqref{eq:tm-mahler} and
\eqref{eq:tm-mahler-iterate} are \lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_mahler} and
\lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_iterate}; \eqref{eq:tm-real-bound} and the value at zero are
\lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_real_le} and \lean{thueMorseDiscSeries_zero}.  Except for the
product-convergence declaration just cited, all these declarations are in
\path{ThueMorseDiscSeries.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dyadic radial zeros and the natural boundary]
\label{thm:natural-boundary}
If \(k\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(\zeta\in\ComplexNumbers\) satisfy \(\zeta^{2^k}=1\), then
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\rho\to1^-}\mathcal K(\rho\zeta)=0.                  \label{eq:tm-radial-zero}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every \(z_0\in\ComplexNumbers\) with \(|z_0|=1\) and every \(R>0\), there is no function
\(g:\ComplexNumbers\to\ComplexNumbers\) which is holomorphic on \(\{z:|z-z_0|<R\}\) and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 g(z)=\mathcal K(z)
 \quad\text{whenever}\quad |z-z_0|<R\ \text{ and }\ |z|<1. \label{eq:tm-no-continuation}
\end{equation}
Thus, in precisely this local-disc sense, every point of the unit circle is singular and
the unit circle is a natural boundary for \(\mathcal K\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The radial limit is \lean{tendsto_thueMorseDiscSeries_radial_zero}.  More generally, if
\(n\in\NaturalNumbers\) is nonzero and \(\zeta^n=1\), then \(|\zeta|=1\); this is
\lean{norm_eq_one_of_pow_eq_one}.  The exact quantified nonextension statement
\eqref{eq:tm-no-continuation}, and hence the
natural-boundary conclusion in the stated local-disc sense, is
\lean{thueMorse_natural_boundary}.  All three declarations are in
\path{ThueMorseNaturalBoundary.lean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The functional equation of the normalized prefix grid}
\label{tm:sub-grid-equation}

The natural dyadic normalization of the prefix sums is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal V_k(j)=\frac{\sigma_k(j)}{2^{\binom k2}},
 \qquad
 x_{k,j}=\frac j{2^k},                                     \label{eq:tm-grid-def}
\end{equation}
so that $\mathcal V_k$ is a rational-valued function on the level-$k$ dyadic grid.  The
exponent $\binom k2$ is forced: it is the exponent that appears in the sharp Prouhet value
\eqref{eq:tm-power-sum-sharp}, in the centered spline \eqref{eq:centered-spline}, and in the
derivative scaling \eqref{eq:global-derivatives}.

\begin{theorem}[Discrete functional equation]
\label{thm:tm-grid-equation}
Let $q\ge0$ and $0\le j<2^q$, and put $h=2^{-(q+1)}$ and $x=x_{q+1,j}$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\mathcal V_{q+1}(j+1)-\mathcal V_{q+1}(j)}{h}
 =2\,\mathcal V_q(j+1),                                    \label{eq:tm-grid-equation}
\end{equation}
and the abscissa attached to the right-hand value is
\begin{equation}
 x_{q,j+1}=2(x+h)=2x_{q+1,j+1}\in[0,1].                   \label{eq:tm-grid-argument}
\end{equation}
The same identity holds verbatim for the shifted grid
$\widetilde{\mathcal V}_k(j)=\sigma_{k+1}(j)/2^{\binom k2}$ of
\cref{tm:sub-corrected}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Because $\binom{q+1}2=\binom q2+q$, the forward difference \eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal V_{q+1}(j+1)-\mathcal V_{q+1}(j)
 =\frac{\sigma_{q+1}(j+1)-\sigma_{q+1}(j)}{2^{\binom q2+q}}
 =\frac{\sigma_q(j+1)}{2^{\binom q2}}\cdot\frac1{2^q}
 =\frac{\mathcal V_q(j+1)}{2^q}.
\end{equation*}
Dividing by $h=2^{-(q+1)}$ multiplies by $2^{q+1}$ and gives \eqref{eq:tm-grid-equation}.
For \eqref{eq:tm-grid-argument}, $2(x+h)=2(j+1)/2^{q+1}=(j+1)/2^q=x_{q,j+1}$, and the
hypothesis $j<2^q$ is exactly the condition $j+1\le2^q$ that keeps this abscissa in the unit
interval.  The shifted grid satisfies the same computation with $\sigma$ replaced by
$\sigma_{\bullet+1}$ throughout.
\end{proof}

\Cref{eq:tm-grid-equation} is the discrete counterpart of the global differential equation
\eqref{eq:global-diffeq-again}: a difference quotient at level $q+1$ equals twice a
level-$q$ value at the doubled abscissa.  Two features of the discrete statement have no
continuous analogue and are easy to lose.  First, the right-hand side is evaluated at the
\emph{forward} node $2(x+h)$ rather than at $2x$; this is the inclusive-prefix convention
\eqref{eq:tm-forward-diff}.  Second, the identity is an assertion about a level-$q$ value,
so it is meaningful as a statement about $F$ or $\RvachevUp$ only when the doubled abscissa lies in
the relevant interval, that is, only when $j<2^q$.  Both features are used in
\cref{tm:sub-corrected}.

\subsection{The literal right endpoint does not converge}
\label{tm:sub-literal-endpoint}

The unshifted normalization printed in the source formula cannot converge to a function
whose right-endpoint value is \(1\): the sequence of literal values at the index \(2^k\)
does not tend to \(1\).  This exact negative statement is
\lean{paperPrefixGridValue_endpoint_not_tendsto_one} in
\path{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.  The corrected normalization below is therefore a
mathematical requirement, not a cosmetic reindexing.

\subsection{Corrected normalization and pointwise convergence}
\label{tm:sub-corrected}

We now identify the normalized prefix grid with the histogram approximants of the
probabilistic construction, and deduce convergence.  Two adjustments to the naive reading
are required, and both are forced by identities already proved: the prefix order must be
shifted by one, because \eqref{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff} matches $\sigma_{n+1}$ with $\mathcal P_n$; and
the grid index $m$ must be read as labelling the \emph{center} of a cell of width $2^{-n}$,
not the left endpoint of a cell of width $2^{-(n+1)}$.

For the polynomial $\mathcal P_n$ of \eqref{eq:tm-approx-poly}, place the coefficient of
the monomial $z^m$ at
\begin{equation}
 \xi_{n,m}=\frac{2m-g_n}{2^{n+1}},                    \label{eq:tm-center}
\end{equation}
and spread it uniformly over the cell $I_{n,m}$ of width $2^{-n}$ centered at $\xi_{n,m}$.
The consecutive centers are spaced $2^{-n}$ apart, so these finitely many cells tile their
support interval
\[
 \left[-1+\frac{n+1}{2^{n+1}},\ 1-\frac{n+1}{2^{n+1}}\right].
\]
The resulting step approximant is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal W_n(x)=\frac{2^n}{2^{\binom{n+1}2}}
 \sum_{m=0}^{g_n}\bigl[z^m\bigr]\mathcal P_n(z)\cdot
 \IndicatorOf{I_{n,m}}(x),                                 \label{eq:tm-histogram}
\end{equation}
where a point shared by two adjacent cells is given the weight $\tfrac12$ in each, so that
the two contributions add to the single value the formula is intended to have.  This
endpoint convention is not cosmetic: with ordinary closed-interval indicators the value at
$x=0$ would be counted twice already for $n=1$.

\begin{theorem}[Histogram approximants]
\label{thm:tm-histogram-limit}
For every $n\ge0$ the function $\mathcal W_n$ satisfies $0\le\mathcal W_n\le1$, is nondecreasing on
$(-\infty,0)$, is nonincreasing on $(0,\infty)$, and satisfies $\mathcal W_n(0)=1$.  Moreover
$\mathcal W_n(x)\to\RvachevUp(x)$ for every real $x$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $n\ge1$, this is exactly \cref{thm:four-step-approx}, since
\eqref{eq:tm-approx-poly} and \eqref{eq:four-P-product} define the same polynomial, while
\eqref{eq:tm-center} and \eqref{eq:tm-histogram} give the same cells and normalization as
\eqref{eq:four-cell} and \eqref{eq:four-step-approximant}.  At $n=0$ the empty product is
$\mathcal P_0=1$, $g_0=0$, and $\mathcal W_0$ is the half-weighted indicator of
$[-1/2,1/2]$, so the stated order and normalization properties are immediate.  The
pointwise convergence of the sequence is already the final assertion of
\cref{thm:four-step-approx}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact identification of the corrected grid]
\label{thm:tm-histogram-identity}
Put $\widetilde{\mathcal V}_n(m)=\sigma_{n+1}(m)/2^{\binom n2}$.  For every $n\ge0$ and
every $m$ with $0\le m<2^{n+1}$,
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde{\mathcal V}_n(m)=\mathcal W_n(\xi_{n,m}).          \label{eq:tm-grid-eq-histogram}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $m\le g_n$, the point $\xi_{n,m}$ is the center of $I_{n,m}$ and lies in no other cell,
so \eqref{eq:tm-histogram} evaluates there to
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{2^{n}}{2^{\binom{n+1}2}}\bigl[z^m\bigr]\mathcal P_n(z).
\end{equation*}
Since $\binom{n+1}2=\binom n2+n$, this is
$\bigl[z^m\bigr]\mathcal P_n/2^{\binom n2}=\widetilde{\mathcal V}_n(m)$ by
\eqref{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff}.  If $g_n<m<2^{n+1}$, then $\xi_{n,m}$ lies strictly to the
right of the finite support interval above, so $\mathcal W_n(\xi_{n,m})=0$; meanwhile
$[z^m]\mathcal P_n=\sigma_{n+1}(m)=0$ because $\deg\mathcal P_n=g_n$.  Thus both sides
again agree.
\end{proof}

Sampling at the dyadic floor now gives an approximation scheme.  For $x\in\RealNumbers$ set
\begin{equation}
 j_n(x)=\max\!\left(0,\Floor{2^{n+1}x}\right),
 \qquad
 \mathcal J_n(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal V}_n\bigl(j_n(x)\bigr)
 =\frac{\sigma_{n+1}\bigl(j_n(x)\bigr)}{2^{\binom n2}}.    \label{eq:tm-sample}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Moving cell centers]
\label{thm:tm-moving-center}
For every $x\in[0,1)$, $\xi_{n,j_n(x)}\to2x-1$ as $n\to\infty$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:tm-center},
$\xi_{n,j_n(x)}=2\cdot j_n(x)2^{-(n+1)}-g_n2^{-(n+1)}$.  The first term tends to
$2x$ because $0\le2^{n+1}x-j_n(x)<1$, and
$g_n2^{-(n+1)}=1-(n+2)2^{-(n+1)}\to1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Stability under moving arguments]
\label{thm:tm-moving}
If $u_n\to y$ in $\RealNumbers$, then $\mathcal W_n(u_n)\to\RvachevUp(y)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\varepsilon>0$.  Suppose first $y>0$.  By continuity of $\RvachevUp$ choose
$d\in(0,y/2]$ with $|\RvachevUp(y\pm d)-\RvachevUp(y)|<\varepsilon/2$.  For all large $n$ we have
$u_n\in[y-d,y+d]\subset(0,\infty)$, so the monotonicity of \cref{thm:tm-histogram-limit} on
$(0,\infty)$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal W_n(y+d)\le\mathcal W_n(u_n)\le\mathcal W_n(y-d).
\end{equation*}
By pointwise convergence at the two fixed points $y\pm d$, for all large $n$ both outer
terms lie within $\varepsilon/2$ of $\RvachevUp(y\pm d)$, hence within $\varepsilon$ of $\RvachevUp(y)$.
The case $y<0$ is identical using monotonicity on $(-\infty,0)$.  For $y=0$ the upper bound
is $\mathcal W_n(u_n)\le1=\RvachevUp(0)$, and the lower bound is $\mathcal W_n(-d)$, $\mathcal W_n(0)=1$, or
$\mathcal W_n(d)$ according to the sign of $u_n$; each of the three converges to a value within
$\varepsilon/2$ of $\RvachevUp(0)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Corrected prefix approximation]
\label{thm:tm-corrected-convergence}
For every \(x\le1\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J_n(x)\longrightarrow\RvachevUp(2x-1),                      \label{eq:tm-corrected-limit}
\end{equation}
and on the whole nonpositive half-line the finite-stage values are exactly
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J_n(x)=\mathcal J_n(0)=2^{-\binom n2}\qquad(x\le0).  \label{eq:tm-left-tail-exact}
\end{equation}
and consequently, for every $x\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J_n\!\left(\frac x2\right)\longrightarrow F(x).      \label{eq:tm-corrected-fabius}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(x\le0\), the natural-floor clamp is exact:
\lean{correctedPrefixGridSample_eq_at_zero_of_nonpos} and
\lean{correctedPrefixGridSample_zero} give \eqref{eq:tm-left-tail-exact}, while
\lean{correctedPrefixGridSample_tendsto_of_nonpos} gives the asserted limit.  Now let
$0<x<1$.  Then $2^{n+1}x<2^{n+1}$, so $j_n(x)<2^{n+1}$ and
\cref{thm:tm-histogram-identity} applies:
$\mathcal J_n(x)=\mathcal W_n(\xi_{n,j_n(x)})$.  Combining
\cref{thm:tm-moving-center} with \cref{thm:tm-moving} gives
$\mathcal J_n(x)\to\RvachevUp(2x-1)$.

At $x=1$ the identification fails, because $j_n(1)=2^{n+1}$ is exactly the first index not
covered by \eqref{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff}.  Here the value is computed instead from
\eqref{eq:tm-run-right} with $k=r=n+1$:
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal J_n(1)=\frac{\sigma_{n+1}(2^{n+1})}{2^{\binom n2}}=-\frac1{2^{\binom n2}}
 \longrightarrow0=\RvachevUp(1),
\end{equation*}
since $\binom n2\to\infty$ and $\RvachevUp(1)=F(0)=0$.  Altogether this is the public theorem
\lean{correctedPrefixGridSample_tendsto_rvachevUp_of_le_one} in
\path{ThueMorseApproximation.lean}.  For \eqref{eq:tm-corrected-fabius}, apply
it at $x/2\in[0,1]$ and use $2(x/2)-1=x-1\le0$, so that $\RvachevUp(x-1)=F(x)$ by
\eqref{eq:up-fold}.
\end{proof}

\begin{warning}
\Cref{thm:tm-corrected-convergence} is a pointwise statement and nothing more.  No uniform
rate is asserted, and none is proved.  The proof passes through
\cref{thm:tm-moving}, whose only quantitative inputs are the shared monotonicity of the
$\mathcal W_n$ and the modulus of continuity of the limit $\RvachevUp$ at the single point $2x-1$; the
sandwich it uses degrades as that modulus degrades.  A uniform bound of the kind supplied
for the centered splines by \eqref{eq:centered-error} is available from
\cref{sec:centered-splines}, but it is a different approximation scheme, and the two should
not be conflated.
\end{warning}

\begin{remark}
The two corrections are independent of one another and both are necessary.  Shifting the
prefix order from $k$ to $k+1$ is what makes \eqref{eq:tm-prefix-eq-coeff} available;
reading the index $m$ as the center \eqref{eq:tm-center} of a cell of width $2^{-n}$ rather
than as an abscissa $m2^{-k}$ is what supplies the affine change of variable $x\mapsto2x-1$
between the unit interval and the support of $\RvachevUp$.  In particular the corrected grid is a
sample of the compactly supported bump, not of the distribution function; this is why its
right-endpoint limit is $0$ and not $1$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The lower Lambert branch}
\label{tm:sub-lambert}

The asymptotic analysis of \cref{sec:sharp-asymptotic} is organized around the coordinate
\eqref{eq:W-def}, where $W_{-1}$ denotes the lower real branch of Lambert's
$W$-function \cite{corless1996}.  That section, and the elementary expansions derived from
it in \cref{sec:elementary-asymptotic}, use three facts about $W_{-1}$ without proving any
of them: that the branch exists on $(-\EulerE^{-1},0)$, that it is characterized by being the
solution below $-1$, and that it has the two-term expansion recorded below.  We supply them
here, since they are the foundational input for \eqref{eq:lambda-equation} and for every
subsequent estimate in the Lambert variable.

\begin{proposition}[Existence, defining equation, and uniqueness]
\label{thm:tm-lambert-branch}
For \(z\in(-\EulerE^{-1},0)\), let \(W_{-1}(z)\) denote the totalized lower branch
\lean{lowerLambertW}.  Then
\begin{equation}
 W_{-1}(z)<-1,
 \qquad
 W_{-1}(z)\,\EulerE^{W_{-1}(z)}=z,                              \label{eq:tm-lambert-eq}
\end{equation}
and $W_{-1}(z)$ is the unique real $w<-1$ with $w\EulerE^w=z$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
These are respectively \lean{lowerLambertW_lt_neg_one},
\lean{lowerLambertW_mul_exp}, and \lean{lowerLambertW_unique} in
\path{LowerLambertW.lean}.
\end{proof}

On the smooth interior, \(W_{-1}\) is continuous and strictly decreasing, and its image is
exactly \((-\infty,-1)\).  At every \(z\in(-\EulerE^{-1},0)\), it is differentiable with
\begin{equation}
 \frac{d}{dz}W_{-1}(z)
 =\frac{W_{-1}(z)}{z\bigl(1+W_{-1}(z)\bigr)}<0.              \label{eq:lower-lambert-derivative}
\end{equation}
These assertions are \lean{lowerLambertW_continuousOn},
\lean{lowerLambertW_strictAntiOn}, \lean{lowerLambertW_image},
\lean{deriv_lowerLambertW}, and \lean{deriv_lowerLambertW_neg}; the underlying derivative
witness is \lean{lowerLambertW_hasDerivAt}, all in \path{LowerLambertW.lean}.

The continuity statement now also includes the finite branch point: the raw lower branch is
continuous on \([-\EulerE^{-1},0)\), by
\lean{lowerLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\lean{lowerLambertW_continuousOn_Ico}.  Its principal companion has the corresponding
right-continuity and full-domain continuity on \([-\EulerE^{-1},\infty)\), namely
\lean{principalLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\lean{principalLambertW_continuousOn_Ici} in \path{PrincipalLambertW.lean}.  At the regular
point \(0\), that module also proves
\(\operatorname{deriv}W_0(0)=1\) and \(W_0(z)\sim z\), through
\lean{deriv_principalLambertW_zero} and \lean{principalLambertW_isEquivalent_zero}.
These endpoint-continuity declarations do not assert a derivative at the branch point.
On the principal open domain, the nonsingular first-derivative identity is
\begin{equation}
 W_0'(z)=
 \left[\EulerE^{W_0(z)}\bigl(W_0(z)+1\bigr)\right]^{-1},
 \qquad z>-\EulerE^{-1}.                                      \label{eq:principal-lambert-derivative}
\end{equation}
The exact curvature formula is proved for both real branches.  If \(j=0\) and
\(z>-\EulerE^{-1}\), or if \(j=-1\) and
\(-\EulerE^{-1}<z<0\), then
\begin{equation}
 W_j''(z)=
 -\frac{\EulerE^{-2W_j(z)}\bigl(W_j(z)+2\bigr)}
        {\bigl(W_j(z)+1\bigr)^3}.                          \label{eq:raw-lambert-curvature}
\end{equation}
For the principal branch this is strictly negative everywhere on the open domain,
\(W_0''(0)=-2\), and \(W_0\) is strictly concave on the full closed natural domain
\([-\EulerE^{-1},\infty)\).  The nonsingular first-derivative identity, second-derivative
witness and formula, zero value, strict negativity, and strict concavity are respectively
\lean{deriv_principalLambertW},
\lean{deriv_principalLambertW_hasDerivAt},
\lean{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW},
\lean{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_zero},
\lean{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_neg}, and
\lean{strictConcaveOn_principalLambertW} in
\path{LambertWCurvature.lean}.

For the lower branch, put \(z_*=-2\EulerE^{-2}\), so that
\(W_{-1}(z_*)=-2\).  Within \((-\EulerE^{-1},0)\),
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 W_{-1}''(z)>0&\iff z<z_*,\\
 W_{-1}''(z)=0&\iff z=z_*,\\
 W_{-1}''(z)<0&\iff z_*<z.
 \end{aligned}                                             \label{eq:lower-lambert-curvature-sign}
\end{equation}
Consequently \(W_{-1}\) is strictly convex on
\([-\EulerE^{-1},z_*]\) and strictly concave on \([z_*,0)\).
The derivative witness and pointwise formula are
\lean{deriv_lowerLambertW_hasDerivAt} and
\lean{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW}; the three equivalences are
\lean{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_pos_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_eq_zero_iff}, and
\lean{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_neg_iff}; and the strict-shape statements are
\lean{strictConvexOn_lowerLambertW_left} and
\lean{strictConcaveOn_lowerLambertW_right}, all in
\path{LambertWCurvature.lean}.  The special value is
\lean{lowerLambertW_neg_two_mul_exp} in
\path{LambertBranchDichotomy.lean}.

The strict-shape theorems include the finite branch point by continuity; they do not give
a derivative or a second derivative there.  The later branch-point leaves close the
one-sided vertical geometry and the first square-root term, as follows.

\subsubsection{The exact branch-gap coordinate and its symmetric laws}
On the common open branch domain, put
\begin{equation}
 -\EulerE^{-1}<x<0,
 \qquad a=W_0(x),\qquad b=W_{-1}(x),\qquad
 \delta=a-b>0.                                             \label{eq:lambert-branch-gap}
\end{equation}
Then the two branches and their common argument are determined by the single
positive coordinate \(\delta\):
\begin{align}
 a&=-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1},
 &b&=-\frac{\delta\exp(\delta)}{\exp(\delta)-1}
     =-\frac{\delta}{1-\exp(-\delta)},                    \label{eq:lambert-gap-pair}\\
 x&=-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1}
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1}\right). \label{eq:lambert-gap-arg}
\end{align}
Conversely, every \(\delta>0\) inserted into these formulas produces exactly
one \(x\in(-\EulerE^{-1},0)\), and the displayed \(a,b\) are respectively
\(W_0(x),W_{-1}(x)\).  Thus
\(x\mapsto W_0(x)-W_{-1}(x)\) is a bijection from
\((-\EulerE^{-1},0)\) onto \((0,\infty)\), with inverse given by
\eqref{eq:lambert-gap-arg}.  In the equivalent coordinate
\(t=\exp(\delta)>1\),
\begin{equation}
 W_0(x)=-\frac{\log t}{t-1},\qquad
 W_{-1}(x)=-\frac{t\log t}{t-1},\qquad
 x=-\frac{\log t}{t-1}\,t^{-1/(t-1)}.                    \label{eq:lambert-gap-t}
\end{equation}

Here is the elementary proof mirrored by the Lean decomposition.  The two
defining equations give \(a\exp(a)=b\exp(b)\).  Since neither branch value is
zero, division yields \(b/a=\exp(a-b)=\exp(\delta)\).  Hence
\(b=a\exp(\delta)\), while
\(\delta=a-b=a(1-\exp(\delta))\); solving gives
\eqref{eq:lambert-gap-pair}, and substitution into \(x=a\exp(a)\) gives
\eqref{eq:lambert-gap-arg}.  For the converse define \(a,b\) by the same
formulas.  The strict tangent inequality
\(1+\delta<\exp(\delta)\) gives \(-1<a<0\), its negative-argument form gives
\(b<-1\), and direct algebra gives \(b=a-\delta\) and
\(b\exp(b)=a\exp(a)\).  The two real-branch uniqueness theorems therefore
identify these values with \(W_0,W_{-1}\).  They also prove both inverse laws,
and hence the claimed bijection.

The same coordinate makes every symmetric identity immediate:
\begin{align}
 \frac{W_{-1}(x)}{W_0(x)}&=\exp(\delta),                  \label{eq:lambert-gap-ratio}\\
 W_0(x)+W_{-1}(x)&=-\delta\coth(\delta/2),               \label{eq:lambert-gap-sum}\\
 W_0(x)W_{-1}(x)&=\frac{\delta^2}{4\sinh^2(\delta/2)}.  \label{eq:lambert-gap-product}
\end{align}
Indeed, adding and multiplying \eqref{eq:lambert-gap-pair} gives the
exponential-rational forms; the definitions of \(\cosh\) and \(\sinh\)
give \eqref{eq:lambert-gap-sum}--\eqref{eq:lambert-gap-product}.  For
\(y>0\), \(\sinh y>y\) and
\((y\cosh y-\sinh y)'=y\sinh y>0\).  With \(y=\delta/2\), these facts give
the strict interior bounds
\begin{equation}
 W_0(x)+W_{-1}(x)<-2,
 \qquad 0<W_0(x)W_{-1}(x)<1.                              \label{eq:lambert-gap-strict}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli exponential generating function in the gap coordinate]
\label{thm:lambert-gap-bernoulli-series}
For every \(w\in\ComplexNumbers\),
\[
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_nw^n}{n!}\text{ converges}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \lvert w\rvert<2\pi.
\]
In particular, the series diverges at every point on or outside
\(\lvert w\rvert=2\pi\).
Writing
\[
 E_{\exp}(w)=
 \begin{cases}
  (\exp(w)-1)/w,&w\ne0,\\
  1,&w=0,
 \end{cases}
\]
the series has the canonical removable value
\[
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_nw^n}{n!}=E_{\exp}(w)^{-1}
 \qquad (\lvert w\rvert<2\pi).
\]
For every real \(z\) with \(\lvert z\rvert<2\pi\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \left\lvert\frac{B_nz^n}{n!}\right\rvert<\infty.
 \label{eq:bernoulli-egf-absolute}
\end{equation}
If also \(z\ne0\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_nz^n}{n!}
   =\frac{z}{\exp(z)-1}.                                  \label{eq:bernoulli-egf}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for \(x\in(-\EulerE^{-1},0)\), with
\(\delta=W_0(x)-W_{-1}(x)<2\pi\),
\begin{align}
 W_0(x)&=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_n\delta^n}{n!},
                                                               \label{eq:lambert-principal-bernoulli}\\
 W_{-1}(x)&=-\delta-
       \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_n\delta^n}{n!}.
                                                               \label{eq:lambert-lower-bernoulli}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal coefficient bound and Cauchy-product proof]
Put \(q=\lvert z\rvert/(2\pi)\), so \(0\le q<1\), and set
\[
 c_r=\frac{B_{2(r+1)}}{2(r+1)(2(r+1))!}.
\]
The even-zeta estimate already proved for the Bernoulli logarithm gives
\[
 \left\lvert c_rz^{2(r+1)}\right\rvert
 \le \frac{\pi^2}{6}q^{2(r+1)}.
\]
All odd Bernoulli numbers beyond \(B_1\) vanish, while for the positive even
indices
\[
 \left\lvert\frac{B_{2(r+1)}z^{2(r+1)}}{(2(r+1))!}\right\rvert
 =2(r+1)\left\lvert c_rz^{2(r+1)}\right\rvert
 \le 2(r+1)\frac{\pi^2}{6}q^{2(r+1)}.
\]
Thus every term of index \(n\ge2\) is bounded by
\(n(\pi^2/6)q^n\).  The series \(\sum n q^n\) converges, and adding the two
initial terms proves \eqref{eq:bernoulli-egf-absolute}.

The same norm comparison at \(\lvert w\rvert\) proves convergence for complex
\(w\) whenever \(\lvert w\rvert<2\pi\).  Conversely, let
\[
 Z_{r+1}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m^{2(r+1)}}\ge 1.
\]
The exact even-Bernoulli identity used in Lean gives
\[
 \left\lvert
   \frac{B_{2(r+1)}w^{2(r+1)}}{(2(r+1))!}
 \right\rvert
 =2Z_{r+1}
   \left(\frac{\lvert w\rvert}{2\pi}\right)^{2(r+1)}.
\]
If \(\lvert w\rvert\ge2\pi\), these even-index term norms are at least \(2\),
so the terms do not tend to zero and the series cannot converge.  This proves
the exact complex convergence locus, including divergence on the boundary.

For the value of the sum away from the origin, the same argument works over
\(\ComplexNumbers\).  Write
\[
 f_n=\frac{B_nz^n}{n!},\qquad
 g_n=\frac{z^n}{n!}-\IndicatorOf{\{n=0\}}.
\]
The first sequence is absolutely summable by the preceding bound.  The second
is absolutely summable because the exponential series is absolutely summable
and the subtracted sequence has finite support; its sum is \(\exp(z)-1\).
Mathlib's formal power-series identity
\[
 \left(\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{B_n}{n!}X^n\right)(\exp X-1)=X
\]
says coefficientwise that
\[
 \sum_{i+j=n}f_i g_j=
 \begin{cases}z,&n=1,\\0,&n\ne1.\end{cases}
\]
Absolute summability therefore justifies the analytic Cauchy product and gives
\[
 \left(\sum_{n\ge0}f_n\right)(\exp(z)-1)
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\sum_{i+j=n}f_i g_j=z.
\]
When \(z\ne0\), the product identity itself makes
\(\exp(z)-1\ne0\): a zero denominator would force \(z=0\).  Division gives
the complex quotient value.  At \(z=0\), the Bernoulli series has only its
constant term and sums to \(1=E_{\exp}(0)^{-1}\).  Lean combines these cases
with the exact convergence criterion in
\lean{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_complex_iff}; restricting to real
nonzero \(z\) proves \eqref{eq:bernoulli-egf}.  On the branch
domain, \(\delta>0\), hence the extra hypothesis gives
\(\lvert\delta\rvert<2\pi\).  Substituting \eqref{eq:bernoulli-egf} into
\(W_0=-\delta/(\exp\delta-1)\), and then using
\(W_{-1}=W_0-\delta\), proves
\eqref{eq:lambert-principal-bernoulli}--\eqref{eq:lambert-lower-bernoulli}.
The five public declarations supporting these claims are respectively
\lean{summable_norm_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial},
\lean{summable_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_iff},
\lean{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial},
\lean{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_complex_iff}, and
\lean{principalLambertW_lowerLambertW_eq_bernoulliSeries}.
\end{proof}

The forward pairing and symmetric identities have the strict input hypotheses
in \eqref{eq:lambert-branch-gap}.  The four converse-coordinate definitions
are total, \lean{gapLower_eq_mul_exp} is unconditional, the reconstruction
theorems use \(\delta>0\), and the logarithmic-coordinate theorems use
\(t>1\); the inverse and bijection packages connect exactly the two open
domains.  At the branch point \(x=-\EulerE^{-1}\), the
gap is zero, the displayed quotients have zero denominators, and the limiting
sum and product attain \(-2\) and \(1\), so neither the parametrization as
stated nor the strict inequalities include that endpoint.  At \(x=0\), the
classical lower branch has a singular extended endpoint, so it too is
excluded; no claim is made about the all-real totalization there.  The
absolute-summability statement includes \(z=0\), but the quotient statement
does not: its series has value \(B_0=1\) there, whereas Lean's totalized
\(0/(\exp 0-1)\) is zero.  The complex \lean{HasSum} equivalence gives the
exact radius, proves divergence at every point on and outside
\(\lvert z\rvert=2\pi\), and evaluates the series throughout the open disk as
\(\lean{complexExpm1Div}(z)^{-1}\).  Thus the complex Bernoulli EGF is Exact
under this canonical removable-origin convention, whose value at zero is
one and which agrees with \(z/(\exp z-1)\) away from zero; it is not a claim
about Lean's literal totalized quotient at zero, nor a holomorphy theorem for
a named sum function.  Its Lambert specialization still
excludes both branch endpoints and additionally requires \(\delta<2\pi\).
It is a convergent series in the gap coordinate, not a higher-order or full
Puiseux expansion in \(x+\EulerE^{-1}\), and it adds no logarithmic expansion
at \(x=0\).

\subsubsection{Branch-point vertical geometry and leading square-root law}
Put
\begin{equation}
 b=-\exp(-1),\qquad \Delta(z)=z-b=z+\exp(-1),\qquad
 \mathcal S(z)=\sqrt{2\exp(1)\bigl(z+\exp(-1)\bigr)}.
 \label{eq:lambert-branch-point-scale}
\end{equation}
Every limit in this paragraph is one-sided, with \(z\downarrow b\), equivalently along
the filter of points strictly to the right of \(b\).  The two interior derivatives and
the two endpoint secant slopes satisfy
\begin{align}
 W_0'(z)&\longrightarrow+\infty,
 &W_{-1}'(z)&\longrightarrow-\infty,
                                                        \label{eq:lambert-branch-derivative-limits}\\
 \frac{W_0(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}&\longrightarrow+\infty,
 &\frac{W_{-1}(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}&\longrightarrow-\infty.
                                                        \label{eq:lambert-branch-secant-limits}
\end{align}
Thus neither branch has a finite right derivative at \(b\): formally, each fails
\(\operatorname{DifferentiableWithinAt}\) on the right-hand set \((b,\infty)\).
Moreover, each totalized real branch fails to be differentiable at \(b\) as an
all-real function.

The proof of \eqref{eq:lambert-branch-derivative-limits} keeps the inverse derivative in
the nonsingular form
\[
 W_j'(z)=\frac{1}{\exp(W_j(z))(W_j(z)+1)}.
\]
Endpoint continuity gives \(W_j(z)\to-1\).  On the principal branch the denominator
therefore tends to zero through positive values, while on the lower branch it tends to
zero through negative values; inversion gives the two signed infinite limits.  Principal
concavity gives \(W_0'(z)\le (W_0(z)-W_0(b))/(z-b)\), and lower convexity on the first
shape interval gives
\((W_{-1}(z)-W_{-1}(b))/(z-b)\le W_{-1}'(z)\).  These inequalities transfer the
derivative limits to \eqref{eq:lambert-branch-secant-limits}.  Mathlib's one-sided
derivative-divergence obstruction then rules out a finite right derivative; ordinary
differentiability would imply that forbidden within-set differentiability.

\lean{tendsto_deriv_lowerLambertW_branchPoint_atBot} & Lower derivative tends to \(-\infty\) from the right.\\

For the first-order asymptotics, the forward map
\(\phi(w)=w\exp w\) has a double contact at \(w=-1\).  A
punctured-neighborhood L'H\^opital calculation gives the limit
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\phi(w)+\exp(-1)}{(w+1)^2}
 \longrightarrow\frac{\exp(-1)}2
 \qquad(w\to-1,\ w\ne-1).                                \label{eq:lambert-forward-quadratic-ratio}
\end{equation}
Indeed, after differentiating numerator and denominator, the quotient reduces away
from \(-1\) to \(\exp(w)/2\).  Composing with either branch, using
\(W_j(z)\exp(W_j(z))=z\), and then inverting the nonzero limit proves
\begin{equation}
 \frac{(W_j(z)+1)^2}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow2\exp(1),
 \qquad j\in\{0,-1\}.                                    \label{eq:lambert-branch-squared-ratio}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, each squared displacement is asymptotic to
\(2\exp(1)(z+\exp(-1))\).  Finally, the strict signs
\(W_0(z)+1>0\) and \(W_{-1}(z)+1<0\) select the two square roots:
\begin{equation}
 W_0(z)+1\sim +\mathcal S(z),
 \qquad
 W_{-1}(z)+1\sim -\mathcal S(z)
 \qquad(z\downarrow-\exp(-1)).                            \label{eq:lambert-branch-signed-sqrt}
\end{equation}
