\begin{corollary}[Solution of the saddle equation]
\label{thm:tm-lambert-lambda}
Let $x>0$ with $Lx<\EulerE^{-1}$, where $L=\log2$ as in \eqref{eq:L-def}, and put
$\lambda=-W_{-1}(-Lx)/L$ as in \eqref{eq:lambda-def}.  Then
$\lambda2^{-\lambda}=x$, which is \eqref{eq:lambda-equation}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The saddle equation is \lean{paperLambertN_eq9} in \path{LowerLambertW.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Two-term expansion]
\label{thm:tm-lambert-expansion}
As $\epsilon\downarrow0$,
\begin{equation}
 W_{-1}(-\epsilon)-\bigl(\log\epsilon-\log|\log\epsilon|\bigr)\longrightarrow0.
                                                          \label{eq:tm-lambert-expansion}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is \lean{tendsto_lowerLambertW_expansion} in \path{LowerLambertW.lean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A binomial--logarithm formula for the Thue--Morse bit}
\label{tm:sub-binomial-log}

The zero-one Thue--Morse bit is \(\tau(n)=\BinaryDigitSum(n)\bmod2\), so that
\(\varepsilon_n=1-2\tau(n)\).  Its primitive-recursive evaluator is
\lean{tmBitPR}; the theorems \lean{tmBitPR_of_pos} and
\lean{tmBitPR_eq_thueMorseBit} prove its agreement with the binary-weight definition.
The bit also admits a closed formula in which the binary structure is hidden inside a
single row of Pascal's triangle.  Set
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal X(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom nk}\in\IntegerNumbers.        \label{eq:tm-parity-sum}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Glaisher count]
\label{thm:tm-glaisher}
The number of odd entries in the $n$\/th row of Pascal's triangle is $2^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\nu(n)$ denote that number, so that $\nu(n)$ is the number of nonzero coefficients of
$(1+z)^n$ in $\mathbb F_2[z]$.  There $(1+z)^2=1+z^2$, hence
\begin{equation*}
 (1+z)^{2n}=\bigl(1+z^2\bigr)^n,
 \qquad
 (1+z)^{2n+1}=\bigl(1+z^2\bigr)^n(1+z).
\end{equation*}
The substitution $z\mapsto z^2$ does not change the number of nonzero coefficients, so
$\nu(2n)=\nu(n)$; and multiplying a polynomial in $z^2$ by $1+z$ produces two summands with
disjoint supports, one even and one odd, so $\nu(2n+1)=2\nu(n)$.  Since $\BinaryDigitSum(2n)=\BinaryDigitSum(n)$
and $\BinaryDigitSum(2n+1)=\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1$ by \eqref{eq:weight-even-odd}, and $\nu(0)=1=2^{\BinaryDigitSum(0)}$,
induction on the binary representation gives $\nu(n)=2^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The logarithm is exact]
\label{thm:tm-log-exact}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 n+1-\mathcal X(n)=2^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1}.                           \label{eq:tm-log-exact}
\end{equation}
In particular $n+1-\mathcal X(n)$ is a positive power of two, and
$\log_2\bigl(n+1-\mathcal X(n)\bigr)=\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1$ is an integer.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each summand of \eqref{eq:tm-parity-sum} is $-1$ at an odd entry and $+1$ at an even entry,
and the row has $n+1$ entries.  Hence $\mathcal X(n)=(n+1)-2\nu(n)$ with $\nu$ as in
\cref{thm:tm-glaisher}, so $n+1-\mathcal X(n)=2\nu(n)=2^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Binomial--logarithm formula]
\label{thm:tm-bit-formula}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \tau(n)=\frac{1+(-1)^{\,\log_2\!\left(n+1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom nk}\right)}}{2},
 \qquad
 \varepsilon_n=1-2\tau(n).                                 \label{eq:tm-bit-formula}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{thm:tm-log-exact} the exponent equals $\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1$.  If $\BinaryDigitSum(n)$ is even then
$\tau(n)=0$ and $(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1}=-1$, so the right side is $0$; if $\BinaryDigitSum(n)$ is odd then
$\tau(n)=1$ and $(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1}=1$, so the right side is $1$.  The second identity is
$\varepsilon_n=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}=1-2\bigl(\BinaryDigitSum(n)\bmod2\bigr)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The order of the two steps matters.  As printed, \eqref{eq:tm-bit-formula} raises $-1$ to a
logarithm, an operation that is meaningful only because the argument of that logarithm has
first been proved to be exactly a power of two.  The formal development therefore proves
\eqref{eq:tm-log-exact} before it interprets the outer expression at all, evaluates the
logarithm on the natural numbers, and reads the final division in $\RationalNumbers$.  A floating-point
evaluation of the same display, or an interpretation of the logarithm as a real-valued
function whose output is then rounded, is an approximation to a formula rather than the
formula.
\end{remark}

\section{Endpoint asymptotics: the exact formal hierarchy}
\label{sec:asymptotic-decomposition}
\sectionmark{Endpoint asymptotics}

The behavior of \(F(x)\) as \(x\downarrow0\) is governed by three nested structures: an
exact dyadic product on the negative Laplace axis, a genuinely nonconstant periodic
correction on logarithmic scale, and a lower-Lambert saddle coordinate.  This section
states the hierarchy in the form proved in Lean.  Longer heuristic derivations, numerical
experiments, and proposed refinements without exact theorem counterparts are collected in
\path{docs/semi-formalized-research-frontiers/semi-formalized-research-frontiers.tex}.

Throughout, put
\begin{equation}
 L=\log 2.                                                   \label{eq:L-def}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The negative-Laplace product and its exact decomposition}

For \(s>0\), the entire generating function \(G\) from \eqref{eq:G-def} satisfies
\begin{equation}
 G(-s)=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{1-\exp(-s/2^j)}{s/2^j}.                              \label{eq:negative-Laplace-product}
\end{equation}
This is the real specialization of
\lean{complexGeneratingFunction_neg_eq_tprod} in
\path{NegativeLaplaceVertical.lean}.  If
\[
 \kappa(u)=\log\!\left(\frac{1-\exp(-u)}{u}\right),
 \qquad
 \Lambda(s)=\log G(-s)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\kappa(s/2^j),
\]
then the series is absolutely summable for every \(s>0\); the precise declarations are
\lean{summable_negativeLaplaceTerm},
\lean{exp_negativeLaplaceLog_eq_generatingFunction_neg}, and
\lean{negativeLaplaceLog_eq_log_laplaceMoment}.

Let \(P\) denote \lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicCorrection} and let \(E\) denote
\lean{negativeLaplaceTailError}.  The exact decomposition is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \Lambda(s)=
 -\frac{(\log s)^2}{2L}+\frac{\log s}{2}
 +P(\log_2 s)+E(s)
 \;}\qquad(s>0).                                            \label{eq:Lambda-exact-periodic}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 0\le E(s)\le
 \frac{\exp(-s)}{(1-\exp(-s))^2},\qquad
 E(s)\le4\exp(-s)\quad(s\ge L).                             \label{eq:negative-Laplace-tail-bound}
\end{equation}
These are exactly
\lean{negativeLaplaceLog_exact_periodic_decomposition},
\lean{negativeLaplaceTailError_nonneg},
\lean{abs_negativeLaplaceTailError_le}, and
\lean{abs_negativeLaplaceTailError_le_four_exp} in
\path{NegativeLaplace.lean}.  The correction \(P\) is \(C^\infty\) and one-periodic by
\lean{contDiff_infty_negativeLaplacePeriodicCorrection} and
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicCorrection_add_one}.

Write
\begin{equation}
 \overline P=\int_0^1P(u)\,\Differential u,\qquad
 \Psi(t)=P(t)-\overline P.                                  \label{eq:Psi-def}
\end{equation}
Then \(\Psi\) is \(C^\infty\), one-periodic, and has mean zero:
\[
 \Psi(t+1)=\Psi(t),\qquad \int_0^1\Psi(t)\,\Differential t=0.
\]
The formal counterparts are
\lean{contDiff_infty_negativeLaplacePsi},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_add_one}, and
\lean{integral_negativeLaplacePsi_zero}.

The first logarithmic derivative has a correspondingly sharp full-ray expansion.  As
\(s\to+\infty\),
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda'(s)=
 \frac{-\log s/L+1/2+\Psi'(\log_2s)/L}{s}
 +\BigO(s^{-2}).                                            \label{eq:negative-Laplace-first-derivative}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Lambda'\) is the formal \lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst}, identified with the
derivative of \(\Lambda\) by \lean{negativeLaplaceLog_hasDerivAt}.  The exact
\(\BigO(s^{-2})\) statement is
\lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst_sub_periodic_main_isBigO_inv_sq_real} in
\path{LaplacePeriodicSecondOrder.lean}.  This promotes the proved derivative estimate
without importing the longer unformalized contour and Mellin derivations from the frontier
volume.

\subsection{The Gamma--zeta Fourier series}

For \(k\in\IntegerNumbers\), use the Fourier convention
\begin{equation}
 \FourierSeriesCoefficient{\Psi}{k}=\int_0^1\Psi(t)\exp(-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit kt)\,\Differential t,
 \qquad
 \chi_k=\frac{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit k}{L}.                                \label{eq:Psi-Fourier-def}
\end{equation}
The zero mode vanishes, and every nonzero mode has the closed form
\begin{equation}
 \FourierSeriesCoefficient{\Psi}{0}=0,\qquad
 \FourierSeriesCoefficient{\Psi}{k}=
 -\frac{\Gamma(-\chi_k)\zeta(1-\chi_k)}{L}
 \quad(k\ne0).                                              \label{eq:Psi-Fourier}
\end{equation}
Every coefficient with \(k\ne0\) is nonzero, so \(\Psi\) is genuinely nonconstant.
The coefficient sequence is absolutely summable and reconstructs \(\Psi\) pointwise:
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(t)=\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\FourierSeriesCoefficient{\Psi}{k}\exp(2\pi\ImaginaryUnit kt).
                                                                  \label{eq:Psi-Fourier-series}
\end{equation}
These assertions are
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_zero},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_eq_neg_gamma_zeta},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_ne_zero},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_not_constant},
\lean{summable_negativeLaplacePsiGammaZetaFourierCoeff}, and
\lean{hasSum_negativeLaplacePsi_gammaZeta_fourierSeries} in
\path{PeriodicFourier.lean}.  No numerical truncation or stronger coefficient-decay
claim is needed here.

\subsection{The evaluated constant}

Let \(\gamma\) be Euler's constant and let \(\gamma_1\) denote the formally defined first
Stieltjes constant \lean{firstStieltjesConstant}.  The regularized Gamma--zeta
combination
\[
 A_0=\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\gamma_1-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
\]
is \lean{gammaZetaConstant}; its equivalent common-denominator form is proved by
\lean{gammaZetaConstant_eq_div_twelve}.  The split, convergent finite-part Bose integral
is evaluated as this same constant by
\lean{boseFinitePartIntegral_eq_gammaZetaConstant}.

The formal finite part is independent of where the convergent integral is split.  More
precisely, put
\[
 \kappa_0(x)=\frac1x\log\!\left(\frac{1-\exp(-x)}x\right),
 \qquad
 \kappa_\infty(x)=\frac1x\log(1-\exp(-x)).
\]
For every \(c>0\), both integrals below are convergent and
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_{(0,c]}\kappa_0(x)\,dx+
 \int_{(c,\infty)}\kappa_\infty(x)\,dx+
 \frac{(\log c)^2}{2}=A_0.}                                  \label{eq:bose-finite-part-cutoff}
\end{equation}
If \(0<a\le b\), changing the splitting point is governed by the exact transition identity
\begin{equation}
 \int_{(a,b]}\bigl(\kappa_\infty(x)-\kappa_0(x)\bigr)\,dx
 =\frac{(\log b)^2-(\log a)^2}{2}.                           \label{eq:bose-cutoff-transition}
\end{equation}
Here the two kernels are \lean{boseFinitePartSmallKernel} and
\lean{boseFinitePartLargeKernel}; their integrability is
\lean{integrableOn_boseFinitePartSmallKernel_Ioc} and
\lean{integrableOn_boseFinitePartLargeKernel_Ioi_of_pos}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:bose-finite-part-cutoff} and \eqref{eq:bose-cutoff-transition} are respectively
\lean{boseFinitePartIntegral_eq_gammaZetaConstant_of_pos} and
\lean{integral_boseFinitePartLargeKernel_sub_small_Ioc} in
\path{BoseFinitePartIntegral.lean}.  Thus the constant is represented by ordinary convergent
real integrals, without assigning a value to a divergent integral.

In turn,
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicMean_eq} gives
\[
 \overline P=\frac{A_0}{L}-\frac{L}{12}.
\]
After the Gaussian normalization, the sharp Fabius constant is
\begin{equation}
 C_F=
 \frac{6\gamma^2+12\gamma_1-\pi^2}{12L}
 -\frac{7L}{12}-\frac12\log\pi.                             \label{eq:sharp-constant}
\end{equation}
This is the exact identity
\lean{fabiusSharpAsymptoticConstant_eq} in
\path{FabiusSharpConstant.lean}.

\subsection{A fully effective dyadic bound}

The dyadic specialization admits a proved numerical threshold and error constant.  For
\(n\ge1\), define
\begin{align}
 D_n={}&-\frac L2n^2-n\log n+\left(1+\frac L2\right)n
       -\frac{(\log n)^2}{2L}+C_F+\Psi(\log_2n) \notag\\
     &-\frac n2\bigl(\Lambda''(n)+\Lambda'(n)^2\bigr).       \label{eq:dyadic-effective-main}
\end{align}
Here the final term is exactly \lean{dyadicEndpointSecondOrder}; the derivative
identifications are \lean{negativeLaplaceLog_hasDerivAt} and
\lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst_hasDerivAt}.  Then, for every \(n\ge224043\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \left|\log F(2^{-n})-D_n\right|
 \le\frac{2512945}{12n}.}
                                                               \label{eq:dyadic-effective-bound}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{abs_log_fabius_dyadic_sub_explicitCumulantMain_le} in
\path{FabiusDyadicSharpCumulant.lean}.  The threshold is a sufficient certified threshold,
not an optimality claim.  Its proof uses the pointwise bounds
\lean{dyadicEndpointSecondOrder_sq_le},
\lean{dyadicHigherLaplaceMoments_le}, and
\lean{abs_dyadicEndpointLaplaceLogError_add_secondOrder_le_unconditional} in
\path{LaplaceMomentBounds.lean}, with respective constants \(256/n\), \(104448/n\), and
\(209408/n\) on their stated ranges.

\subsection{The lower-Lambert saddle and exact reduction}
\label{sec:sharp-asymptotic}

On the endpoint-inclusive lower-branch domain
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal D_L=\left(0,\frac{\EulerE^{-1}}L\right],                \label{eq:lambert-phase-domain}
\end{equation}
let
\begin{equation}
 W=W_{-1}(-Lx),                                             \label{eq:W-def}
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=\lambda(x)=-\frac{W}{L},
 \qquad r=r(x)=2^\lambda.                                  \label{eq:lambda-def}
\end{equation}
The global Lean definitions are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius}; here we use them on \(\mathcal D_L\).  For every
\(x\in\mathcal D_L\), their exact identities are
\begin{equation}
 \lambda2^{-\lambda}=x,\qquad
 \frac{\lambda}{r}=x,\qquad
 rx=\lambda,\qquad
 \log r=L\lambda.                                          \label{eq:lambda-equation}
\end{equation}
The endpoint-inclusive quotient and product identities are
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_div_radius_of_le} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius_mul_argument_of_le}; the first equality in
\eqref{eq:lambda-equation} is the quotient identity rewritten using \(r=2^\lambda\).
The logarithmic identity in fact holds for every real \(x\), not only on
\(\mathcal D_L\); this is \lean{log_fabiusLambertRadius}.  The corresponding
strict-interior wrappers are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_div_radius} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius_mul_argument}.  All five declarations are in
\path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}.

This lower phase is also the exact specialization at
\((m,A,\beta)=(1,1,L)\) of the generic scaled power--exponential inverse;
this is \lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_one_one_log_two} in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertFabius}\texttt{.lean}.  More generally, for
\(m\in\PositiveIntegers\), \(A>0\), and \(\beta>0\), put
\[
 S_{m,A,\beta}(t)=At^m\EulerE^{-\beta t},
 \qquad P_{m,A,\beta}=A\left(\frac m\beta\EulerE^{-1}\right)^m.
\]
Writing \(\lambda_0\) and \(\lambda_{-1}\) for the principal and lower
Lambert phases, their exact images are
\[
 \lambda_0\bigl([0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\bigr)
   =\left[0,\frac m\beta\right],
 \qquad
 \lambda_{-1}\bigl((0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\bigr)
   =\left[\frac m\beta,\infty\right].
\]
These are \lean{principalPowerExponentialPhase_image_Icc} and
\lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_image_Ioc}; the companion declarations
\lean{principalPowerExponentialPhase_invOn} and
\lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_invOn} make each phase a two-sided
\(\operatorname{InvOn}\) inverse of \(S_{m,A,\beta}\) on the corresponding
displayed interval pair.  They are proved in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}.  On the principal branch, the
closed endpoint step uses the exact restriction
\(W_0([-\EulerE^{-1},0])=[-1,0]\), namely
\lean{principalLambertW_image_Icc} in \path{PrincipalLambertW.lean}.

The same hypotheses give continuity on the full natural profile-value domains,
including the common finite branch point:
\[
 \lambda_0\in C\bigl([0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\bigr),
 \qquad
 \lambda_{-1}\in C\bigl((0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\bigr).
\]
These are \lean{principalPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Icc} and
\lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Ioc} in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean}; their proof composes the raw
endpoint APIs above with the globally continuous normalized argument
\lean{powerExponentialLambertArgument_continuous}.  Moreover, for
\(x\in(0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\) and \(\mu\ge0\), Lean proves the exact dichotomy
\begin{equation}
 S_{m,A,\beta}(\mu)=x
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mu=\lambda_0(x)\ \text{or}\ \mu=\lambda_{-1}(x).           \label{eq:power-exponential-root-dichotomy}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{powerExponentialSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower}; on the strict
interior \(0<x<P_{m,A,\beta}\), the two roots are unequal by
\lean{principalPowerExponentialPhase_ne_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}, both in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}.  The nonnegativity assumption on \(\mu\) is
essential: for even \(m\), negative inputs can create additional positive profile values.

On that same strict profile-value interval, both inverse branches have the exact second
derivative
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_j''(x)=
 \frac{\lambda_j(x)
   \left[m-\bigl(m-\beta\lambda_j(x)\bigr)^2\right]}
 {x^2\bigl(m-\beta\lambda_j(x)\bigr)^3},
 \qquad j\in\{0,-1\}.                                    \label{eq:power-exponential-phase-curvature}
\end{equation}
The corresponding derivative witnesses are
\lean{deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt} and
\lean{deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt}; the pointwise identities are
\lean{deriv_deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase} and
\lean{deriv_deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}, all in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean}.
That generic module stops at the exact formula: it does not yet package the
thresholds \(m\mathbin{\pm}\sqrt m\), their zero/sign classifications, or
generic strict-convexity and strict-concavity corollaries.

The compiled small-input package uses the intrinsic positive scale
\begin{equation}
 \epsilon_{m,A,\beta}(x)
 =\frac{\beta}{m}\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m}.             \label{eq:power-exponential-epsilon}
\end{equation}
With the same parameter hypotheses, as \(x\downarrow0\),
\begin{align}
 \lambda_0(x)&\sim\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m},
                                                               \label{eq:power-exponential-principal-small}\\
 \lambda_{-1}(x)&\longrightarrow+\infty,                     \label{eq:power-exponential-lower-divergence}\\
 \lambda_{-1}(x)-M_{m,A,\beta}(x)&\longrightarrow0,          \label{eq:power-exponential-lower-intrinsic}
\end{align}
where
\[
 M_{m,A,\beta}(x)
 =-\frac m\beta\left(
   \log\epsilon_{m,A,\beta}(x)
   -\log\left|\log\epsilon_{m,A,\beta}(x)\right|\right).
\]
The exact Lean crosswalk is \lean{powerExponentialLambertEpsilon},
\lean{principalPowerExponentialPhase_isEquivalent_rpow},
\lean{tendsto_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_nhdsGT_zero_atTop},
\lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhaseIntrinsicMain}, and
\lean{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_sub_intrinsicMain_tendsto_zero} in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean}.  This generic theorem stops at the intrinsic
\(\epsilon\)-form with an \(o(1)\) remainder.  A cleaned formula in
\(\log(A/x)\), further logarithmic terms, and a full series are not part of this package.
The separate interior curvature formula \eqref{eq:power-exponential-phase-curvature} does
not itself supply named generic-phase branch-point vertical-tangent, secant-slope, or
leading square-root wrappers; the raw-branch results above have not yet been transported
through the scaled coordinate.

For the classical parameters \((m,A,\beta)=(1,1,L)\), the principal root is
\lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} and the lower root is \lean{fabiusLambertPhase}.
The specialized wrappers
\lean{fabiusSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower},
\lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_ne_fabiusLambertPhase},
\lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Icc}, and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousOn_Ioc} give respectively the nonnegative-root
dichotomy, strict-interior separation, and the two full-domain continuity statements.
Finally \lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_isEquivalent_id} gives
\(\lambda_0(x)\sim x\) as \(x\downarrow0\).  These declarations are in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertFabius}\texttt{.lean}; the generic lower divergence and
intrinsic expansion above specialize to the same lower Fabius phase.

On the same closed-upper domain, the phase has the sharp lower bound and endpoint
characterization
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)\ge\frac1L,
 \qquad
 \lambda(x)=\frac1L
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 x=\frac{\EulerE^{-1}}L.                                        \label{eq:lambert-phase-closed-bound}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \lambda\!\left(\frac{\EulerE^{-1}}L\right)=\frac1L.             \label{eq:lambert-phase-branch-point}
\end{equation}
Moreover, \(\lambda\) is strictly decreasing on \(\mathcal D_L\), and its exact image is
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(\mathcal D_L)=\left[\frac1L,\infty\right).         \label{eq:lambert-phase-closed-image}
\end{equation}
The bound, equality characterization, endpoint value, strict antitonicity, and image are
respectively \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_ge_inv_log_two},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_eq_inv_log_two_iff},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_branchPoint},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_strictAntiOn_Ioc}, and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_image_Ioc} in \path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}.

Equivalently, under the hypotheses \(x>0\) and \(Lx\le\EulerE^{-1}\), the same
lower bound and the strict positivity \(0<\lambda(x)\) are
\lean{one_div_log_two_le_fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_pos_of_le}.  Thus the algebraic saddle identities
and continuity extend to the finite branch point even though the differentiable formulas
below do not.

The differentiable API is deliberately restricted to the open interior
\(0<x<\EulerE^{-1}/L\).  There \(\lambda\) is strictly decreasing, its image is
\((1/L,\infty)\), and it is differentiable with
\begin{equation}
 \lambda'(x)=
 \frac{\lambda(x)}{x\bigl(1-L\lambda(x)\bigr)}<0.            \label{eq:lambert-phase-derivative}
\end{equation}
The interior continuity wrapper is \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousOn}; the remaining
claims are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_strictAntiOn}, \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_image},
\lean{deriv_fabiusLambertPhase}, and \lean{deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_neg} in
\path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}; the derivative witness is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_hasDerivAt}, and pointwise continuity is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousAt}.

The full curvature profile is also exact.  Define
\begin{equation}
 x_*:=\frac{2\EulerE^{-2}}L.                                    \label{eq:fabius-lambert-inflection-input}
\end{equation}
Then \(0<x_*<\EulerE^{-1}/L\), \(\lambda(x_*)=2/L\), and for
\(0<x<\EulerE^{-1}/L\),
\begin{equation}
 \lambda''(x)=
 \frac{L\lambda(x)^2\bigl(2-L\lambda(x)\bigr)}
 {x^2\bigl(1-L\lambda(x)\bigr)^3}.                        \label{eq:fabius-lambert-curvature}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lambda''(x)>0&\iff x<x_*,\\
 \lambda''(x)=0&\iff x=x_*,\\
 \lambda''(x)<0&\iff x_*<x.
 \end{aligned}                                             \label{eq:fabius-lambert-curvature-sign}
\end{equation}
Thus the lower phase is strictly convex on \((0,x_*]\) and strictly concave on
\([x_*,\EulerE^{-1}/L]\).  In Lean, \(x_*\) is
\lean{fabiusLambertInflectionInput}; its domain membership and phase value are
\lean{fabiusLambertInflectionInput_mem_Ioo} and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput}.  The second derivative, its three sign loci,
its exact zero, and the two strict-shape statements are respectively
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_pos_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_neg_iff},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput},
\lean{strictConvexOn_fabiusLambertPhase_left}, and
\lean{strictConcaveOn_fabiusLambertPhase_right} in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean}.

The principal Fabius phase has a complementary global result.  For every real \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_0(x)=-\frac{W_0(-Lx)}L.                           \label{eq:fabius-principal-lambert-affine}
\end{equation}
It is continuous on \((-\infty,\EulerE^{-1}/L]\); for every \(x<\EulerE^{-1}/L\),
\begin{align}
 \lambda_0'(x)
 &=\left[\EulerE^{W_0(-Lx)}\bigl(W_0(-Lx)+1\bigr)\right]^{-1},
                                                               \label{eq:fabius-principal-lambert-derivative}\\
 \lambda_0''(x)
 &=\frac{L\EulerE^{-2W_0(-Lx)}\bigl(W_0(-Lx)+2\bigr)}
 {\bigl(W_0(-Lx)+1\bigr)^3}>0.                              \label{eq:fabius-principal-lambert-curvature}
\end{align}
In particular \(\lambda_0''(0)=2L\), and \(\lambda_0\) is strictly convex on the whole
closed half-line \((-\infty,\EulerE^{-1}/L]\).  The exact affine formula and continuity theorem
are \lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_eq_principalLambertW} and
\lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Iic}.  The first-derivative witness and
identity are \lean{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt} and
\lean{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase}; the second-derivative witness, identity,
positivity, zero value, and whole-half-line strict convexity are
\lean{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_pos},
\lean{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_zero}, and
\lean{strictConvexOn_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase}, all in
\path{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean}.

Returning to the lower phase, at the small-argument endpoint \(x=0\), the phase and its
reciprocal have the exact limits
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad \lambda(x)^{-1}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                    \label{eq:lambert-phase-endpoint-limits}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{tendsto_fabiusLambertPhase_nhdsGT_zero_atTop} and
\lean{tendsto_inv_fabiusLambertPhase_nhdsGT_zero} in
\path{FabiusLambertRates.lean}.

\subsubsection{Phase-locked reciprocal Richardson algebra}

The exact inverse relation also supplies a finite lattice on which every periodic
coefficient is frozen, not merely asymptotically stable.  Fix
$\lambda_0>1/L$ and set
\begin{equation}
 x_j=(\lambda_0+j)2^{-(\lambda_0+j)},
 \qquad h_j=(\lambda_0+j)^{-1}.                             \label{eq:lambert-phase-locked-nodes}
\end{equation}
Then $x_j\in\mathcal D_L^\circ$ and
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x_j)=\lambda_0+j.                                \label{eq:lambert-phase-locked-inversion}
\end{equation}
Consequently every one-periodic function $G$ satisfies
$G(\lambda(x_j))=G(\lambda_0)$.

For $0\le j\le r$, let $\omega_{r,j}$ be the evaluation-at-zero Lagrange
weight on $h_0,\ldots,h_r$.  Its explicit real form is
\begin{equation}
 \omega_{r,j}
 =\frac{(-1)^{r-j}}{r!}\binom rj(\lambda_0+j)^r.           \label{eq:lambert-reciprocal-weight}
\end{equation}
These weights obey
\begin{align}
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}&=1,                            \label{eq:lambert-reciprocal-mass}\\
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda_0+j)^{-m}&=0
   \qquad(1\le m\le r),                                   \label{eq:lambert-reciprocal-cancellation}\\
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}G(\lambda(x_j))&=G(\lambda_0). \label{eq:lambert-periodic-preservation}
\end{align}
Every residual moment is explicit as well.  For $t\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda_0+j)^{-(r+1+t)}
 =(-1)^r\left(\prod_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda_0+j)^{-1}\right)
 h_t(h_0,\ldots,h_r).                                      \label{eq:lambert-reciprocal-residual}
\end{equation}

The node and reciprocal-row definitions are \lean{lambertPhaseLockedNode},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalNode}, and
\lean{shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight}.  Node-domain membership, phase
locking, and periodic preservation are
\lean{lambertPhaseLockedNode_mem_lowerBranch},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_phaseLockedNode},
\lean{Periodic.apply_fabiusLambertPhase_phaseLockedNode}, and
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_periodicPhaseLocked}.  The weight formula,
mass-one identity, cancellation, and all-residual identity are
\lean{shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_eq_choose_of_pos},
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_eq_one},
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_zero}, and
\lean{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_residual} in
\path{LambertPhaseLockedRichardson.lean}.

This is exact finite interpolation algebra.  It does not by itself extract the endpoint
fluctuation $\Psi$ from sampled values of $F$, justify applying an asymptotic expansion
uniformly across the row, or prove a Richardson remainder estimate.  Such analytic steps
must be supplied separately.

The corrected main term is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M(x)=
 -\frac L2\lambda(x)^2+
 \left(1+\frac L2\right)\lambda(x)
 -\frac12\log\lambda(x)+C_F+\Psi(\lambda(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:sharp-main-lambda}
\end{equation}
This is definition \lean{fabiusSharpLambertMain}.  The exact saddle action
itself is endpoint-inclusive: whenever \(x>0\) and \(Lx\le\EulerE^{-1}\),
\begin{equation}
 r(x)x+\Lambda(r(x))-\frac12\log\bigl(2\pi\lambda(x)\bigr)
 =\mathcal M(x)+E(r(x)).                                  \label{eq:lambert-saddle-action}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusLambertSaddleAction_eq_of_le} in
\path{FabiusSharpLambertMain.lean}.  If
\(\mathcal R(x)\) denotes the normalized saddle ratio
\lean{fabiusSaddleRatio} at \(r(x)\) and \(\lambda(x)\), then Lean proves the exact
error separation
\begin{equation}
 \log F(x)-\mathcal M(x)=\log\mathcal R(x)+E(r(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:sharp-exact-reduction}
\end{equation}
The declaration is
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertMain_eq_log_ratio_add_tail} in
\path{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean}.

The forward product tail in this separation is negligible at every algebraic order of the
exact phase.  Along \(x=2^{-t}\), for each \(N\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 E\!\left(r(2^{-t})\right)
 =o\!\left(\lambda(2^{-t})^{-N}\right)
 \qquad(t\to+\infty).                                     \label{eq:lambert-tail-all-power-little-o}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{negativeLaplaceTailError_dyadicLambert_isLittleO_inv_pow} in
\path{FabiusSharpLambertTransfer.lean}.  At \(N=0\) it says directly that the tail tends
to zero; larger \(N\) show that it contributes to none of the inverse-phase coefficients.

The analytic input to that reduction is an exact Bromwich formula.  For every \(r>0\)
and \(x\in\RealNumbers\), as an identity in \(\ComplexNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\exp(rx)\int_{\RealNumbers}
 \exp(2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi x)\,
 \frac{G(-(r+2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi))}{r+2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\,\Differential\xi.
                                                               \label{eq:bromwich}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabius_bromwich}; the dimensionless, Gaussian-normalized version used by
the saddle machinery is \lean{fabius_bromwich_scaled}.  The supporting Laplace-transform
identity is \lean{generatingFunction_neg_div_eq_laplace}.  All three occur in
\path{LaplaceTransform.lean} and \path{FabiusBromwichInput.lean} with the positivity and
integrability hypotheses shown there.

\subsection{The compact online formula and its missing periodic factor}
\label{sec:compact-online-lambert}

A January 2022 comment in a Mathematics Stack Exchange discussion asked whether the
preceding calculation implied a particularly compact lower-Lambert formula for the
endpoint behavior \cite{reshetnikovMSEasymptotic}.  With the notation of
\eqref{eq:W-def}, its logarithmic main term is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=
 -\frac{7L}{12}-\frac12\log(\pi x)
 +\frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
 \right).
                                                               \label{eq:mse-lambert-log-main}
\end{equation}
This is the human-readable form of definition \lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertMain} in
\path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.  Written in ordinary mathematical notation, the
exponential of this logarithmic encoding is the expression printed in the discussion:
\begin{equation}
 A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=
 \frac{1}{2^{7/12}\sqrt{\pi x}}
 \exp\!\left[
  \frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
  \right)
 \right].                                                     \label{eq:mse-lambert-main}
\end{equation}
For \(x>0\), this factor is strictly positive.  Lean names it
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertFactor};
\lean{exp_fabiusWikipediaLambertMain_eq_WikipediaLambertFactor} identifies it
with \(\exp(\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x))\), and
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertFactor_pos} proves its positivity.  All three
declarations occur in \path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.
The proposed symbol \(F(x)\sim A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)\) means that the ratio should tend
to \(1\) as \(x\downarrow0\).

That ratio-one claim is false.  The answer on the cited page first derives a Gamma--zeta
periodic term, then explicitly omits it from the subsequent approximation while noting
that it must be retained for more precise asymptotics.  The exact formal decomposition
shows what is lost: on the positive lower-Lambert domain,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M(x)=\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)+\Psi(\lambda(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:mse-corrected-log-main}
\end{equation}
Here \lean{fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_WikipediaLambertMain_add} is the all-real
identity between the two named compact definitions, while
\lean{fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_sharpLambertMain} identifies the corrected one
with \(\mathcal M\) on the stated domain.  Consequently the proved ratio-one asymptotic
is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 F(x)\sim{}&
 \frac{1}{2^{7/12}\sqrt{\pi x}}
 \exp\!\left[
  \frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
  \right)
 \right]\\
 &{}\times\exp\!\left(\Psi(-W/L)\right)
 \end{aligned}}
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                      \label{eq:mse-lambert-corrected}
\end{equation}
This is the direct theorem
\lean{fabius_isEquivalent_WikipediaLambertFactor_mul_periodic} in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}; the underlying exact positive-side factorization is
\lean{exp_fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_WikipediaLambertFactor_mul_periodic}
in \path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.  The plus sign in
\eqref{eq:mse-corrected-log-main} is essential: the missing multiplier is
\(\exp(\Psi(\lambda(x)))\), with \(\lambda(x)=-W/L\).

The logical bridge from logarithmic error to ratio-one equivalence is itself formalized.
Along any filter, if \(f\) is eventually positive, then
\begin{equation}
 f\sim\exp(g)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \log f-g\longrightarrow0.
                                                               \label{eq:exp-equivalent-log-error}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{isEquivalent_exp_iff_tendsto_log_sub}.  In the present case Lean proves
both
\begin{equation}
 \log F(x)-\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)\not\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0)                                       \label{eq:mse-log-error-nonzero}
\end{equation}
and that the same residual is not
\(\BigO(({-\log x})^{-1})\).  These are respectively
\lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaLambertMain_not_tendsto_zero} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaLambertMain_not_isBigO}.  Applying
\eqref{eq:exp-equivalent-log-error} gives the direct theorem
\begin{equation}
 F\not\sim A_{\mathrm{MSE}},                                 \label{eq:mse-not-equivalent}
\end{equation}
namely \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_exp_WikipediaLambertMain}; its direct
literal-factor form is \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_WikipediaLambertFactor}.
The criterion and all four obstruction declarations in this paragraph occur in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.  Thus the nonconvergence of the sampled periodic
correction, rather than its numerical size, is decisive for ratio-one equivalence.

\begin{theorem}[Exact cluster interval for the online quotient]
\label{thm:mse-lambert-ratio-cluster}
For \(x>0\), put
\[
 R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x):=\frac{F(x)}{A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)},\qquad
 m_\Psi:=\min_{0\le t\le1}\Psi(t),\qquad
 M_\Psi:=\max_{0\le t\le1}\Psi(t).
\]
Then \(m_\Psi<M_\Psi\), and the quotient has the sharper additive asymptotic model
\begin{equation}
 R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)-\exp\!\bigl(\Psi(\lambda(x))\bigr)\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                      \label{eq:mse-lambert-ratio-model}
\end{equation}
Write \(\operatorname{Cl}_{0^+}(R_{\mathrm{MSE}})\) for the set of all \(z\in\RealNumbers\)
for which there is a sequence \(x_n\to0\), eventually positive, such that
\(R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x_n)\to z\).  Its complete cluster set is
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Cl}_{0^+}(R_{\mathrm{MSE}})
   =[\exp(m_\Psi),\exp(M_\Psi)].                            \label{eq:mse-lambert-cluster-interval}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{x\downarrow0}R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=\exp(m_\Psi)
 <\exp(M_\Psi)=\limsup_{x\downarrow0}R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x).  \label{eq:mse-lambert-liminf-limsup}
\end{equation}
Consequently no real constant repairs the omitted factor: in Mathlib's
filter-theoretic asymptotic-equivalence relation,
\begin{equation}
 \forall c\in\RealNumbers,\qquad
 F\not\sim cA_{\mathrm{MSE}}\quad(x\downarrow0).             \label{eq:mse-lambert-no-constant}
\end{equation}
For \(c\ne0\), the last assertion is the usual failure of
\(F(x)/(cA_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x))\to1\).  The formal declaration also includes
\(c=0\), without interpreting a quotient by zero.
\end{theorem}

The online discussion supplies \(A_{\mathrm{MSE}}\); the exact cluster interval is a
consequence of the corrected formal asymptotic.  The endpoint descriptions
\(\exp(m_\Psi)\) and \(\exp(M_\Psi)\) are exact variational formulas in terms of the
explicit periodic correction.  No finite elementary evaluation, numerical certification,
sign claim for the extrema, or uniqueness claim for their phases is asserted here.

The phase-sampling mechanism is reusable.  Every value of a one-periodic map \(p\) is a
cluster value of \(p(\lambda(x))\) as \(x\downarrow0\); if \(p\) is continuous and its
codomain is Hausdorff, the complete cluster set is exactly \(p(\RealNumbers)\).  These statements
are \lean{mapClusterPt_periodic_comp_fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{mapClusterPt_periodic_comp_fabiusLambertPhase_iff} in
\path{FabiusWikipediaObstruction.lean}.

For the specialization above, \(R_{\mathrm{MSE}}\) is
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio}, while \(m_\Psi\) and \(M_\Psi\) are
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin} and \lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMax}.  The exact
global range, pointwise extremal bounds, and endpoint order are respectively
\lean{range_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_Icc_periodMin_periodMax},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_le_value},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_value_le_periodMax}, and
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_le_periodMax}; attainment and strict separation are
\lean{exists_mem_Icc_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_periodMin},
\lean{exists_mem_Icc_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_periodMax}, and
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_lt_periodMax}.  Exponentiation of the exact range is
\lean{range_exp_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_Icc_periodExtrema}.

The ratio-one comparison with the sampled periodic model is
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_isEquivalent_periodic}, and the stronger additive
statement \eqref{eq:mse-lambert-ratio-model} is
\lean{tendsto_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_sub_periodic}.  The interval identity
\eqref{eq:mse-lambert-cluster-interval} is
\lean{mapClusterPt_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_iff}; the two endpoint limits
are \lean{liminf_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_eq} and
\lean{limsup_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_eq}; and the universal obstruction
is \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_const_mul_WikipediaLambertFactor}.  These declarations
all occur in \path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.

\subsection{The first two periodic saddle corrections}

Define
\begin{equation}
 A_1(u)=
 \frac1{24}+\frac1{2L}
 -\frac{\Psi''(u)+\Psi'(u)^2}{2L^2}.                       \label{eq:A1}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection}.  It is continuous, one-periodic, and bounded
by
\lean{continuous_fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection},
\lean{fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection}.  The first corrected expansion is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
 +\BigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-2}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:first-corrected-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
It is the \(N=2\) specialization of
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO}, together with
\lean{fabiusSharpLambertExpansion_two}.

The second coefficient has a compact closed form in the bounded exponent jets.  For each
\(m\), write \(J_m(u)\) for the jet
\lean{negativeLaplaceBoundedExponentJet}\((m,u)\), and define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 A_2(u)=\frac1{24}\bigl(&8J_0^3+12J_0^2J_1+12J_0^2+48J_0J_1+12J_0J_2\\
                       &+6J_1^2+24J_1+20J_2+3J_3\bigr)(u).
 \end{split}                                                \label{eq:A2-jets}
\end{equation}
This is exactly \lean{fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection}; it is continuous, one-periodic,
and bounded by
\lean{continuous_fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection},
\lean{fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection}.  The formally proved
second-correction theorem is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
 +\frac{A_2(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^2}
 +\BigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-3}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:second-corrected-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
Its exact declarations are
\lean{fabiusSharpLambertExpansion_three} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_twoSaddleCorrections_isBigO} in
\path{FabiusSecondSaddleExpansion.lean}; the companion theorem
\lean{log_fabius_sub_twoSaddleCorrections_isBigO_negLog} gives the literal remainder
\(\BigO((-\log x)^{-3})\).

\subsection{The recursive all-orders theorem}

For \(j\in\NaturalNumbers\), let \(a_j(u)\) denote
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}\((j,u)\).
Every \(a_j\) is continuous, one-periodic, and bounded, by
\lean{continuous_fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient},
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}.  The initial identifications are
\[
 a_0=0,\qquad a_1=A_1,\qquad a_2=A_2,
\]
proved by
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_zero},
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_one_eq_firstSaddleCorrection}, and
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_two_eq_secondSaddleCorrection}.

For every \(N\in\NaturalNumbers\), the complete exact-phase expansion is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{a_j(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^j}
 +\BigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-N}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:all-orders-log}
\end{equation}
The precise formal statement is
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertMain_hasAsymptoticExpansion}; its finite-remainder
forms are
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO_negLog} in
\path{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean}.

The coefficients are constructive rather than existential.  The formal development
identifies the jet weights as coefficients of
\[
 \prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\bigl(X-(k+1)\bigr),
\]
proves closed forms for the periodic and bounded exponent jets, and gives recursive
exponent-polynomial identities.  The exact declarations are
\lean{negativeLaplaceJetPolynomial_eq_prod},
\lean{negativeLaplaceJetStirling_eq_coeff},
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicJet_eq_closedForm},
\lean{negativeLaplaceBoundedExponentJet_eq_closedForm},
\lean{negativeLaplaceExponentCoefficient_succ_eq}, and
\lean{negativeLaplaceExponentPolynomial_succ_eq}.  This coefficient-of-a-product
description is the proved statement; no identification with an external Stirling-number
API is asserted here.

The algebraic conversion between relative-mass and logarithmic coefficients is normalized
at every index rather than only asymptotically.  If \(\operatorname{ExpCoeff}\) and
\(\operatorname{LogCoeff}\) denote the formal recurrences \lean{expCoeff} and
\lean{logCoeff}, then
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{ExpCoeff}(\operatorname{LogCoeff}(a))_n
  &=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\a_n,&n>0,\end{cases} \notag\\
 \operatorname{LogCoeff}(\operatorname{ExpCoeff}(E))_n
  &=\begin{cases}0,&n=0,\\Expectation_n,&n>0.\end{cases}              \label{eq:saddle-coefficient-normalization}
\end{align}
These are \lean{expCoeff_logCoeff_eq_ite} and
\lean{logCoeff_expCoeff_eq_ite}; the corresponding identities of formal power series are
\lean{expSeries_logCoeff_eq_massSeries_normalize_zero} and
\lean{logSeries_expCoeff_eq_exponentSeries_normalize_zero} in
\path{SaddleLogExpansionAlgebra.lean} and
\path{SaddleLogExpansionPowerSeries.lean}.

The recurrence-defined logarithm is also multiplicative at the level of
formal power series.  Let \(R\) be a commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra and define
\[
 \mathfrak L(C):=\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf}(C),
 \qquad C\in R[[X]],
\]
by applying \lean{logSeries} to the coefficient family of \(C\).  Rebuilding
a series from that family gives \(C\) itself by
\lean{SaddleExpansion.massSeries_coeff}; this reconstruction lemma needs only
that \(R\) be a commutative ring.  For every \(C\), even before a unit-constant
hypothesis is imposed,
\begin{equation}
 [X^0]\mathfrak L(C)=0,                                    \label{eq:formal-log-zero-constant}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{SaddleExpansion.constantCoeff_logOf}.  If
\([X^0]C=1\), then
\begin{equation}
 C\,\mathfrak L(C)'=C',                                   \label{eq:formal-log-differential-equation}
\end{equation}
by \lean{SaddleExpansion.mul_derivative_logOf}; conversely,
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_eq_of} says that
\eqref{eq:formal-log-zero-constant} and
\eqref{eq:formal-log-differential-equation} characterize \(\mathfrak L(C)\)
uniquely.

It follows that unit-constant series satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak L(AB)=\mathfrak L(A)+\mathfrak L(B),
 \qquad \mathfrak L(1)=0,                                 \label{eq:formal-log-multiplicative}
\end{equation}
and, for every finite set \(S\) and family with \([X^0]F_i=1\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak L\!\left(\prod_{i\in S}F_i\right)
 =\sum_{i\in S}\mathfrak L(F_i).                         \label{eq:formal-log-finite-product}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_mul},
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_one}, and
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_prod} in
\path{LogSeriesMultiplicative.lean}.  They are identities in \(R[[X]]\): no
convergence, analytic logarithm, branch choice, or passage to an infinite
product is asserted.

\subsection{Universal finite endpoint-transfer algebra}
\label{sec:endpoint-transfer-polynomials}

The formal coefficient stage of endpoint transfer is independent of every
probability and asymptotic estimate.  To distinguish the universal endpoint
polynomials from the periodic correction \(P\), write
\(P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)=\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial}(r)\).  In
\(\RationalNumbers[X]\), define
\begin{equation}
 e_0(X)=0,\qquad e_{n+1}(X)=-\frac{X^{n+2}}{n+2},\qquad
 P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)=\operatorname{ExpCoeff}(e)_r.        \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Then the recursive, formal-series, and unordered-partition descriptions agree:
\begin{align}
 P_{n+1}^{\mathrm{end}}(X)
   &=-\frac1{n+1}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{j+1}{j+2}
       X^{j+2}P_{n-j}^{\mathrm{end}}(X),                    \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-recurrence}\\
 \sum_{r\ge0}P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)t^r
   &=\exp\!\left(\sum_{r\ge0}e_r(X)t^r\right)
    =\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j\ge2}\frac{X^jt^{j-1}}j\right), \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-formal-series}\\
 P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)
   &=\sum_{f\in\mathcal P_r}\prod_{j=1}^{r}
       \frac{e_j(X)^{f(j)}}{f(j)!},                        \notag\\
 \mathcal P_r
   &=\left\{f:\NaturalNumbers\to\NaturalNumbers\;\middle|\;
       \substack{\SupportOperator(f)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\},\\
                 \sum_{j=1}^{r}j f(j)=r}\right\}.         \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-partitions}
\end{align}
The first coefficients are
\begin{equation}
 P_0^{\mathrm{end}}=1,\qquad P_1^{\mathrm{end}}=-\frac{X^2}{2},\qquad
 P_2^{\mathrm{end}}=\frac{X^4}{8}-\frac{X^3}{3},\qquad
 P_3^{\mathrm{end}}=-\frac{X^6}{48}+\frac{X^5}{6}-\frac{X^4}{4}. \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-first-four}
\end{equation}
Because \(\RationalNumbers[X]\) is the free rational algebra on one generator, evaluating
\(X\) at any element \(z\) of a commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra commutes both with
each \(P_r^{\mathrm{end}}\) and with the complete formal power series.

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The definitions are \lean{endpointTransferExponentCoefficient},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial}, and \lean{endpointTransferSeries} in
\path{EndpointTransferPolynomials.lean}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-recurrence}--\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-partitions}
are respectively \lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_succ},
\lean{endpointTransferSeries_eq_exp_subst}, and
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_eq_partitionExpSum}.  The finite partition
engine and its equality with the recursive exponential coefficients are
\lean{weightedPartitions}, \lean{partitionExpSum}, and
\lean{partitionExpSum_eq_expCoeff} in \path{ExponentialPartition.lean} and
\path{ExponentialBell.lean}.  Evaluation and series naturality are
\lean{aeval_endpointTransferPolynomial} and
\lean{map_endpointTransferSeries}; the four identities in
\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-first-four} are
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_zero},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_one},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_two}, and
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_three}.

All statements in this subsection are finite or formal.  They do not identify
the series analytically with \(\exp(X)(1-Xt)^{1/t}\), construct a tilted law,
or prove a Taylor-window, tail, or endpoint-remainder estimate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Recursive elementary re-expansion of the phase}
\label{sec:elementary-asymptotic}

The exact Lambert phase can itself be expanded to arbitrary order.  With
\[
 T(x)=-\log_2x,
\]
write \(\mathcal L_N(x)\) for
\lean{fabiusLambertLogarithmicApproximation}\((N,x)\).  This approximation is built from the
recursively defined polynomials
\lean{dyadicLambertDisplacementPolynomial}.  For every \(N\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)-\mathcal L_N(x)
 =\BigO\!\left(T(x)^{-N}\right)
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                     \label{eq:lambert-recursive-all-orders}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_sub_logarithmicApproximation_isBigO}; the equivalent dyadic-scale
form is \lean{dyadicLambertAllOrderRemainder_isBigO}.  Explicit coefficient polynomials
are proved through index two, while higher orders are represented by the formal recursion.

The periodic term cannot simply be discarded from a sharp estimate.  If
\(\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Wiki}}\) denotes the uncorrected elementary expression
\lean{fabiusWikipediaElementaryMain}, then Lean proves that
\(x\mapsto\log F(x)-\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Wiki}}(x)\) is not
\(\BigO((-\log x)^{-1})\) as \(x\downarrow0\).
This is \lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaElementaryMain_not_isBigO} in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.  The obstruction is therefore a theorem, not a
numerical diagnosis.

\subsection{Three endpoint consequences already formalized}

On the dyadic logarithmic scale, let
\(g(t)=-\log F(2^{-t})\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{g(t)}{t^2}=\frac{L}{2}.             \label{eq:dyadic-quadratic-law}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusLogProfile_normalized_tendsto} in
\path{FabiusLogSquaredAsymptotic.lean}.  On the original small-argument scale, for every
\(c>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \exp(-c/x)=o(F(x))\qquad(x\downarrow0).                     \label{eq:exp-inverse-little-o}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{exp_neg_div_isLittleO_fabius} in
\path{FabiusDecayComparison.lean}.  At the opposite endpoint, the exact global
symmetry already proved in \eqref{eq:shape-symmetry-global} gives only the identity
\begin{equation}
 1-F(1-x)=F(x).                                             \label{eq:right-endpoint-sym}
\end{equation}
No additional right-endpoint asymptotic-transfer theorem is asserted here.

\section{The Fourier--Legendre expansion of \texorpdfstring{\(\RvachevUp\)}{up}}
\label{sec:legendre}

Let \(P_n\) be the Legendre polynomial normalized by \(P_n(1)=1\), and define
\begin{equation}
 u_n=\frac{4n+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)P_{2n}(x)\Differential x.      \label{eq:un-legendre}
\end{equation}
Odd Legendre coefficients vanish by
\lean{rvachevFullLegendreCoefficient_odd_eq_zero}.  The even coefficients have the exact
finite formula
\begin{align}
 u_n={}&4^{-n}(4n+1)\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+k}
 \binom{2n}{n+k}\binom{2n+2k}{2n}                         \notag\\
 &\hspace{5em}\times(2k)!\,2^{\binom{2k+1}{2}}
 F(2^{-2k-1}).                                             \label{eq:legendre-exact-coeff}
\end{align}
This is \lean{rvachevLegendreCoefficient_eq_fabius_sum} in
\path{FabiusLegendreCoefficients.lean}.

The even series
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_nP_{2n}(x)\qquad(-1\le x\le1) \label{eq:legendre-series}
\end{equation}
converges absolutely and uniformly in the formal development.  The exact declarations are
\lean{summable_abs_rvachevLegendreCoefficient},
\lean{hasSum_rvachevLegendreSeries}, and
\lean{hasSum_rvachevLegendreSeries_uniform} in
\path{FabiusLegendreSeries.lean}.  Its \(N\)-th polynomial partial sum minimizes squared
\(L^2([-1,1])\) error among all polynomials of degree at most \(2N+1\), and every distinct
competitor has strictly larger error.  These are
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSum_least_squares} and
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSum_strict_least_squares} in
\path{FabiusLegendreLeastSquares.lean}.

At compiled source checkpoint \texttt{a3854643d},
\path{FabiusLegendreTranslateBlocks.lean} specializes
\eqref{eq:rvachev-polynomial-synthesis-finite} to the Legendre family.  Its six
public definitions are \lean{rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{rvachevLegendreScale}, \lean{rvachevLegendreIndexSet},
\lean{rvachevLegendreAtomCoefficient},
\lean{rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock}, and \lean{rvachevTranslateGram}.  Its seven
public theorems are
\lean{natDegree_rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{leadingCoeff_rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{eval_legendrePolynomial_eq_sum_rvachevUp},
\lean{eval_legendrePolynomial_even_eq_sum_rvachevUp},
\lean{rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock_eq_rvachevLegendreBlock},
\lean{intervalIntegral_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock_mul}, and
\lean{sum_rvachevLegendreAtomCoefficient_mul_gram}.

Writing \(Q_d=\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}P_d\), the two degree theorems give
\begin{equation}
 \deg Q_d=d,\qquad \operatorname{lc}(Q_d)
 =2^{-d}\binom{2d}{d},                                    \label{eq:legendre-rvachev-degree}
\end{equation}
so deconvolution preserves the exact Legendre degree and its explicit leading
coefficient.  The two synthesis theorems then give
\begin{align}
 P_d(x)&=2^{-d}\sum_{-2^{d+1}<k<2^{d+1}}
 Q_d(k/2^d)\RvachevUp(x-k/2^d),                                  \label{eq:legendre-rvachev-synthesis}\\
 P_{2n}(x)&=4^{-n}\sum_{-2\cdot4^n<k<2\cdot4^n}
 Q_{2n}(k/4^n)\RvachevUp(x-k/4^n),                               \label{eq:legendre-rvachev-even-synthesis}
\end{align}
for \(-1\le x\le1\).  More explicitly, let
\[
 M=4^n.
\]
Setting \(x=0\) in the even synthesis gives the exact central cancellation
\begin{equation}
 Q_{2n}(0)+2\sum_{0<k<M}Q_{2n}(k/M)\RvachevUp(k/M)
 =(-1)^n\binom{2n}{n}.                                   \label{eq:legendre-central-sum}
\end{equation}
The exhaustive public surface of
\path{RvachevLegendreCentralSum.lean} consists of no definitions and the
three theorems \lean{eval_legendrePolynomial_even_zero},
\lean{eval_rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial_even}, and
\lean{rvachevLegendreCentralSum}.  The last theorem proves
\eqref{eq:legendre-central-sum} for every \(F:\lean{BoundedFabius}\) satisfying
\lean{IsFabius}\(F\), a harmless strengthening from the canonical function,
and includes \(n=0\).

Indeed, begin with \eqref{eq:legendre-rvachev-even-synthesis} at zero.
Compact support removes every term with \(M\leq |k|<2M\).  The deconvolved
even mode is even by
\lean{eval_rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial_even}, while
\(\RvachevUp\) is even, so the surviving nonzero indices pair as \(k,-k\).
The central term uses \(\RvachevUp(0)=1\).  Finally clear the mesh \(M=4^n\)
and insert
\[
 P_{2n}(0)=(-1)^n4^{-n}\binom{2n}{n},
\]
which is \lean{eval_legendrePolynomial_even_zero}.  Thus the representation
frontier's \nolinkurl{cor:leg-central-sum} is \emph{Exact/Complete}.  This
finite argument supplies no Jacobi closed form, all-degree decoder parity or
rationality, reverse spectral closure, mesh minimality, or larger Lagrange
right inverse.

The subsequent compiled leaf
\path{RvachevLegendreBiorthogonality.lean} has exactly one public definition
and one theorem.  Its normalized translated analysis kernel is
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_m(c)=\frac{2m+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}
 \RvachevUp(x-c)P_m(x)\Differential x,
 \qquad
 \Lambda_m=\lean{rvachevLegendreAnalysisKernel}(F,m,\cdot),       \label{eq:rvachev-legendre-analysis-kernel}
\end{equation}
and \lean{rvachevLegendreBiorthogonality} proves, for every
\(F:\lean{BoundedFabius}\) satisfying \(\lean{IsFabius}\,F\), natural
\(M\ne0\), arbitrary \(\ell,m\), and
\(\ell\le\TwoAdicValuation(M)\),
\begin{equation}
 \frac1M\sum_{-2M<k<2M}Q_\ell^-(k/M)\Lambda_m(k/M)
 =\begin{cases}1,&m=\ell,\\0,&m\ne\ell.\end{cases}             \label{eq:rvachev-legendre-biorthogonality}
\end{equation}
The open integer block and normalization are literal, and there is no degree
restriction on the analysis index \(m\).  The proof inserts the already exact
degree-\(\ell\) finite Rvachev synthesis on \([-1,1]\), multiplies by
\((2m+1)P_m/2\), interchanges the finite sum with the interval integral, and
applies Legendre orthogonality.  Thus the representation label
\nolinkurl{def:leg-Lambda} and theorem
\nolinkurl{thm:leg-biorthogonality} are \emph{Exact/Complete}, including
\(\ell=0\) and \(m=0\).  The larger support, smoothness, parity, origin-value,
and Fourier--Bessel package \nolinkurl{thm:leg-Lambda} and
\nolinkurl{cor:leg-biorthogonal-matrices} remain Partial: this leaf does not
formalize the reverse finite projector, its rank and trace, or the
Cauchy--Binet identity, and it makes no rationality claim for \(\Lambda_m\).

More explicitly, let
\[
 M_n=4^n,\qquad I_n=\{k\in\IntegerNumbers:-2M_n<k<2M_n\},\qquad
 a_{n,k}=\frac{u_n}{M_n}Q_{2n}(k/M_n),
\]
and define the literal finite translate block
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde B_n(x)=\sum_{k\in I_n}a_{n,k}\RvachevUp(x-k/M_n).
 \label{eq:legendre-translate-block}
\end{equation}
Then \(\widetilde B_n(x)=u_nP_{2n}(x)\) throughout \([-1,1]\), and these
finite trains satisfy the same complete orthogonality formula as the polynomial
blocks.  With
\[
 G_{m,n}(k,\ell)=\int_{-1}^{1}
 \RvachevUp(x-k/M_m)\RvachevUp(x-\ell/M_n)\Differential x,
\]
the fully expanded atom-Gram identity is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in I_m}\sum_{\ell\in I_n}a_{m,k}a_{n,\ell}G_{m,n}(k,\ell)
 =\delta_{mn}\frac{2u_n^2}{4n+1}.                         \label{eq:legendre-atom-gram}
\end{equation}

At compiled source checkpoint \texttt{a3854643d}, the focused-build module
\path{FabiusLegendreTranslateSeries.lean} closes the literal outer series and
the common-mesh partial sums.  Its five public
definitions are \lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumAtomCoefficient},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlock},
\lean{rvachevLegendreTranslateBlockOnInterval}, and
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlockOnInterval}.  Its twenty-five
public theorems are
\lean{natDegree_rvachevLegendrePartialSumDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{leadingCoeff_rvachevLegendrePartialSumDeconvolutionPolynomial},
\lean{summable_norm_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock},
\lean{summable_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock},
\lean{hasSum_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock},
\lean{tsum_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumDeconvolutionPolynomial_eq_sum},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumAtomCoefficient_eq_sum},
\lean{eval_rvachevLegendrePartialSumPolynomial_eq_tsum_rvachevUp},
\lean{eval_rvachevLegendrePartialSumPolynomial_eq_sum_rvachevUp},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlock_eq_eval_partialSumPolynomial},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlock_eq_sum_translateBlock},
\lean{rvachevLegendreTranslateBlockOnInterval_apply},
\lean{rvachevLegendreTranslateBlockOnInterval_eq_smul},
\lean{summable_norm_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlockOnInterval},
\lean{summable_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlockOnInterval},

\lean{tsum_rvachevLegendreTranslateBlock_uniform},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlockOnInterval_apply},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlockOnInterval_eq_eval_partialSumPolynomial},
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlockOnInterval_eq_sum},
\lean{tendsto_rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlockOnInterval},

\lean{tendsto_rvachevLegendrePartialSumTranslateBlock}.

Consequently the literal finite trains in
\eqref{eq:legendre-translate-block} are absolutely summable both pointwise and
in the supremum norm on the interval, and their pointwise and uniform sums are
exactly
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde B_n(x),
 \qquad -1\le x\le1.                                      \label{eq:legendre-translate-block-series}
\end{equation}
For the existing polynomial partial sum
\(S_N=\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_nP_{2n}\), define
\begin{equation}
 C_N:=\mathscr D_{\RvachevUp}S_N
     =\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_nQ_{2n}.                             \label{eq:legendre-partial-sum-deconvolution}
\end{equation}
The equality is
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSumDeconvolutionPolynomial_eq_sum}.  With
the same hypotheses, the two new triangularity wrappers prove
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{natDegree}(C_N)=\operatorname{natDegree}(S_N),\qquad
 \operatorname{lc}(C_N)=\operatorname{lc}(S_N),            \label{eq:legendre-partial-sum-degree}
\end{equation}
including degenerate partial sums whose visible degree drops.  With
\(M_N=4^N\), the global theorem uses a compactly supported \texttt{tsum} for
every real input, while on the Legendre interval its exact finite form is
\begin{equation}
 S_N(x)=\frac1{M_N}\sum_{-2M_N<k<2M_N}
 C_N(k/M_N)\RvachevUp(x-k/M_N).                                  \label{eq:legendre-fixed-scale-partial-sum}
\end{equation}
On $[-1,1]$, the named fixed-scale train is equal pointwise both to \(S_N\)
and to the finite sum of the separately scaled blocks.  The latter statement
does not identify the two coefficient vectors.  Bundled in
\(C([-1,1])\), these common-mesh partial trains converge to \(\RvachevUp\) in the
supremum norm.  The API also provides the raw
\texttt{TendstoUniformlyOn} statement, convergence of the supremum-norm error
to zero, and the pointwise corollary at every \(x\in[-1,1]\).  It asserts no
rate, coefficientwise limit, or uniform convergence outside this interval.

The nine modules \path{RvachevMomentAppell.lean},
\path{RvachevPolynomialSynthesis.lean},
\path{RvachevSuperconvergentSynthesis.lean},
\path{LagrangeRvachevSynthesis.lean},
\path{RvachevAppellHasse.lean},
\path{LagrangeRvachevMatrix.lean},
\path{CompositeMeshSharpness.lean},
\path{FabiusLegendreTranslateBlocks.lean}, and
\path{FabiusLegendreTranslateSeries.lean} therefore have public
definition/theorem inventories $6/33$, $0/5$, $1/8$, $2/7$, $1/14$, $4/6$,
$1/7$, $6/7$, and $5/25$, respectively: exactly $138$ public declarations.
The seventh module proves
least-mesh sharpness only for universal exactness on the complete polynomial
space through a degree cutoff.  It does not prove minimality for an individual
Legendre polynomial or a particular partial sum.  The finite Hasse expansion,
odd reciprocal-moment vanishing, and geometric closed decoder are now proved.
The nine modules still do not prove decoder optimality,
range/kernel/rank/trace/characteristic-polynomial or Cauchy--Binet results for the
coefficient-space projector, rationality of the atom rows, equality of the fixed-scale and separately
scaled coefficient vectors, an unconditional
\(\operatorname{natDegree}(S_N)=2N\) theorem or nonvanishing of the top
Legendre coefficient, or the later refinement, projector, and asymptotic
layers.

At compiled source checkpoint \texttt{ca66a1247}, the polynomial-form Legendre blocks

 \int_{-1}^{1}B_m(x)B_n(x)\Differential x

\end{equation}
This is \lean{intervalIntegral_rvachevLegendreBlock_mul}, for the definition

 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)^2\Differential x

 \int_{-1}^{1}\bigl(\RvachevUp(x)-S_N(x)\bigr)^2\Differential x
   &=\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\frac{2u_n^2}{4n+1}.              \label{eq:legendre-exact-tail}
\end{align}
The full-series and shifted-tail \texttt{HasSum} declarations are

\lean{integral_sq_eval_rvachevLegendrePartialSumPolynomial}.  Finally, writing

 A_2:=\int_0^1F(t)^2\Differential t,

so that \(A_2=\lean{fabiusSquareEnergy}\ F\), the bridge and series declarations are

At compiled source checkpoint \texttt{b9b240bc0},

 A_2

The sharpness declarations show that \(2^d\), and hence \(4^N\) at cutoff

low-degree values of \(Q_d\); rationality of the atom rows; equality of the
